# Proverbs

## 1:1 Introduction
[Philipp Melanchthon, Explicatio Proverbiorum Salomonis (Wittenberg, 1552). English: machine translation, The Faith Received (Mere Orthodoxy); the original follows each comment.]
PREFACE OF PHILIP MELANCHTHON TO THE PROVERBS OF SOLOMON.

Latin:

PRAEFATIO PHILIPPI ME-LANTHONIS IN PROVERBIA SALOMONIS.

## 1:1 Argument
CHAPTER I.

Latin:

CAPUT I.

## 1:3
To understand, &c.

The proposition is, Hence you shall learn Wisdom and discipline, &c. The phrasing is foreign to Latin custom, but construct it thus: The sentences of Solomon written for this utility, that hence you may learn wisdom. And let us be content with this simple and grammatical explanation. Afterwards let us consider the words, which even if there are some differences, yet there is a certain proximity.

Wisdom signifies the acknowledgment of God, and especially fear and faith. Discipline or erudition is generally preparation or instruction.

Prudence signifies the governance of morals, Intelligence, discernment, where with many probable things proposed, choice is difficult. As when Jeremiah advises surrender against the opinion of others. Justice signifies universally obedience according to all the commandments. Judgment, accusation, chastisement, punishment. Rectitude is opposed to hypocrisy, as if I were to say sincerity. It is also equality of judgment without respect of persons &c. In sum, this heap of appellations signifies that admonitions are proposed, which may rule us toward God and toward men.

Latin:

Ad intelligenda, &c.

Propositio est, Hinc disces Sapientiam & disciplinam, &c. Phrasis aliena est a consuetudine latina, sed sic contexito, Sententiae Salomonis scriptae ad hanc utilitatem, ut hinc discas sapientiam. Et hac simplici & grammatica enarratione contenti simus. Postea vocabula consideremus, quorum & si sunt aliqua discrimina, tamen vicinitas quaedam est.

Sapientia significat agnitionem Dei, ac praecipue timorem ac fidem. Disciplina seu eruditio generaliter est praeparatio seu institutio.

Prudentia significat gubernationem morum, Intelligentia, diiudicationem, ubi multis probabilibus propositis difficilis est electio. Ut cum leremias suadet deditionem contra aliorum opinionem. Iusticia significat universaliter obedientiam iuxta omnia praecepta. Iudicium, accusationem, castigationem, poenam. Rectitudo, opponitur hypocrisi, ac si dicerem sinceritas. Est & aequalitas iudicii sine acceptione personarum &c. In summa, hic acervus appellationum significat proponi admonitiones, quae regant nos erga Deum & erga homines.

## 1:4
Astuteness.

Craftiness is opposed to stupidity, and signifies docility. Knowledge is choice, discernment of things, places, times.

Latin:

Astutiam.

Calliditas opponitur stoliditati, & significat docilitatem. Scientia delectus, discretio rerum, locorum, temporum.

## 1:5
The Wise man will hear and will profit &c.

II. He adds to the proposition an admonition, what kind of person the hearer ought to be. A wise hearer will profit as Christ says. To him who has, it shall be given. Likewise concerning the seed that falls into good ground. Likewise, He will give the Holy Spirit to those who ask, that is, not to those who despise, not to those who resist. Thus he says here that the despisers of God, such as Epicureans and the like, do not profit, but others, in whom there are beginnings of the fear of God, as he says immediately afterwards, The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom.

Latin:

Audiet Sapiens& proficiet &c.

II. Addit propositioni admonitionem, qualem oporteat auditorem esse. Sapiens auditor proficiet sicut Christus inquit. Habenti dabitur. Item de semine quod cadit in terram bonam. Item, Dabit Spiritum sanctum petentibus, id est, non contemnentibus, non repugnantibus. Ita hic dicit non proficere contemptores Dei, ut Epicureos & similes, sed alios, in quibus initia sunt timoris Dei, ut statim postea dicit, Timor Domini, initium est sapientiae.

## 1:7
The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom.

III. There follows the first sentence, which is the doctrine of the first commandment in the Decalogue concerning the fear and acknowledgment of God, according to the law divinely handed down. Beginning signifies not only the commencement, but also the principal part. And some explain it thus, The fear of the Lord is the highest Wisdom. Understand, however, the fear as the true fear which is perceived by sense, which is to tremble at the thought of the wrath of God, And with this fear or dread is joined faith, embracing the whole Creed, acknowledging the mediator and the remission of sins and approaching God and worshipping Him with true invocation, and thus the heart is ruled by fear and faith so that it may not rush against the commandments of God. This whole worship of God he usually names the Fear of God, Just as the Psalm also says, Blessed are all who fear the Lord, Therefore the name of Fear generally signifies the whole true worship of God, in which there is also the true confidence of a child of God. For without this acknowledgment of the child of God, no worship pleases God.

Latin:

Initium sapientiae timor Domini.

III. Sequitur prima sententia, quae est doctrina primi praecepti in Decalogo de timore & agnitione Dei, iuxta legem divinitus traditam. Initium non tantum significat inchoationem, sed etiam principalem partem. Et aliqui sic enarrant, Timor Domini summa est Sapientia. Intelligas autem timorem de vero timore qui sensu percipitur, qui est expavescere cogitatione irae Dei, Et Cum hoc timore seu pavore sit coniuncta fides, amplectens totum Symbolum, agnoscens mediatorem & remissionem peccatorum & accedens ad Deum & vera invocatione eum colens, ac ita regatur cor timore & fide ne ruat contra mandata Dei. Hunc totum cultum Dei usitate nominat Timorem Dei, Sicut & Psalmus inquit, Beati omnes qui timent Dominum, Plerunque igitur nomen Timoris significat totum cultum Dei verum, in quo & vera fiducia est filii Dei. Nam sine hac agnitione filii Dei nulli cultus Deo placent.

## 1:8
Hear, my son, the instruction of your father and do not forsake the law of your mother.

I said before that Solomon began from the first commandment of the Decalogue, and embraces universal obedience. Now he expressly inserts the fourth commandment concerning parents. For it is necessary in every assembly that teachers be set in charge. Here, therefore, as in domestic custom, or as in pedagogy, he attributes to parents the duty of teaching. And it ought to be the chief care of parents that they rightly instruct their children about God, just as Paul also writes to the Ephesians, Bring up your children in the doctrine and discipline of the Lord. In turn, let children also know that it is the will of God that we be submissive to our parents and reverently obey them. But consider diligently here what he commands; he leads the listener to a certain kind of doctrine, he orders the father to be heard in this people, who teaches the law concerning the fear of God, as I said above.

Latin:

Audi fili eruditionem patris tui & matris tuae legem ne di-mittas.

Dixi prius exorsum esse Salomonem a primo praecepto Decalogi, & complecti universalem obedientiam. Nunc expresse inserit quartum preceptum de parentibus. Necesse est enim in omni coetu praefici doctores. Hic igitur, ut in domestica consuetudine, seu ut in paedagogia, attribuit parentibus munus docendi. Ac praecipua esse cura parentum debet, ut natos recte erudiant de Deo, Sicut & Paulus ad Ephesios scribit, Educate liberos in doctrina & disciplina Domini. Vicissim & nati sciant voluntatem Dei esse ut parentibus simus subrecti & reverenter obediamus. Diligenter autem hic, consideres, quid praecipiat, deducit auditorem ad certum genus doctrinae, iubet audiri patrem in isto populo, qui docet legem de timore Dei, ut supra dixi.

## 1:8
Do not forsake the law of your mother.

That is, that very law of which I spoke before, which teaches about the fear of the Lord, namely the entire doctrine handed down to this people by God with certain testimonies. This law of your mother, namely that which your mother sounds forth in the people of God, you should hear, learn, and obey. And it figuratively signifies the voice of the Church sounding forth the doctrine of God, so that we may know that we are led to a certain kind of doctrine, which command is very often repeated. As John 14 If anyone loves me, he will keep my commandments, and my father will love him, etc. But just as Christ added there the highest of all promises to this commandment concerning the profession of doctrine, so here Solomon adds a promise, which, so that it may be more pleasing, is taken from childish ornaments: This obedience, he says, will be a crown to your head and a necklace for your neck.

A crown signifies a kingdom or presence. And the meaning is, God will place you among salutary governors, and will promote, and will aid your counsels so that they may be salutary to you and to the people, so that you may be a vessel of mercy, like Samuel, David, Nathan, and not be a vessel of wrath, like Saul, Absalom, Ahithophel.

Ornaments of the neck signify eloquence, that is, salutary doctrine and salutary counsels; with these huge gifts God will adorn you, he says, if you have heard the law of your mother.

Latin:

Ne dimittas legem matris tuae.

ID est, Illam ipsam legem de qua antea dixi, quae docet de timore Domini, videlicet doctrinam integram a Deo certis testimoniis traditam huic populo. Hanc legem matris tuae, videlicet quam sonat mater tua in populo Dei, audias, discas, ei obtemperes. Ac figurate significat vocem Ecclesiae sonantis doctrinam Dei, Ita sciamus nos ad certum genus doctrinae deduci, quod mandatum saepissime repetitum est. Ut Ioh. 14. Si quis diligit me, mandata mea servabit, & pater meus diliget eum &c. Ut autem Christus promissionem omnium summam ibi addidit huic praecepto de professione doctrinae, Ita hic Salomon addit promissionem, quae ut sit blandior metaphora sumpta est a puerilibus ornamentis, Haec obedientia, inquit, erit corona capiti & torques collo tuo.

Corona significat Regnum seu praesentiam. Estque sententia, Deus collocabit te inter salutares gubernatores, & provehet, & iuvabit consilia ut sint salutaria tibi & populo, ut sis vas misericordiae, sicut Samuel, David, Nathan, non sis vas irae, sicut Saul, Absolon, Achitophel.

Ornamenta colli significant eloquentiam, hoc est, salutarem doctrinam & salutaria consilia, his ingentibus donis Deus te ornabit, inquit, si legem matris tuae audieris.

## 1:10
If sinners entice you, etc.

After he proposed the sum of doctrine, and set a teacher over us, he immediately adds an admonition about avoiding the enticements which lead minds away from the higher commandments, from God and parents. And there are two chief scandals which corrupt very many: the multitude and the prosperous circumstances of the impious. These two turn the minds of many away from God, as the examples of all times show.

Against these scandals he immediately serves this admonition here, If the impious entice you do not consent, that is, you see that the multitude of Gentiles and other impious people is huge, and you perceive that these flourish in wealth and glory, therefore these thoughts will excite great tumults in your mind. Perhaps all things are carried by chance without God, or certainly our law is not more true than others. Because it is not consistent that so many wise men, excelling in virtue, power, and glory, should be neglected by God.

Driven by this imagination, many slip away from true doctrine to the examples of the multitude, and afterwards they seek wealth and power, as they say here. Let us lie in wait for blood, because this is the customary way, to grow by the oppression of others, as it is said, Unless a serpent eats serpents, it does not become a Dragon.

This is the sum of this prosopopoeia. But Solomon adds a discourse about punishments as a refutation; they err, he says, when they think that they will attain those things which they hope for by these methods. For they will quickly be overturned and will perish, and this very thing will happen which is said: Bad counsel is worst for the counselor.

Latin:

Si lactaverint te peccatores &c.

Postquam doctrinae summam proposuit, & doctorem nobis praefecit, mox addit admonitionem, de cavendis illecebris, quae abducunt mentes a superiorioribus preceptis, a Deo & parentibus. Ac duo sunt scandala praecipua, quae plurimos corrumpunt, multitudo & res secundae impiorum. Haec duo plurimorum animos a Deo avertunt, ut omnium temporum exempla ostendunt.

Adversus haec scandala statim hic inservit admonitionem, Si te impii invitabunt non accedas, Id est, vides ingentem esse multitudinem Ethnicorum & aliorum impiorum, & hos florere cernis opibus & gloria, ideo hae cogitationes magnos tumultus in animo tuo excitabunt. Fortassis omnia casu feruntur sine Deo, aut certe nostra lex non magis vera est quam aliae. Quia tam multos sapientes, praestantes virtute, potentia, gloria, negligi a Deo non est consentaneum.

Hac impulsi imaginatione multi a vera doctrina ad exempla multitudinis dilabuntur, Et postea quaerunt opes & potentiam, sicut hic dicunt. Insidiemur sanguini, quia haec est via usitata, oppraessione aliorum crescere, Sicut dicitur, Serpens nisi comedat serpentes, non fit Draco.

Haec est summa huius Prosopopaeiae. sed addit Salomon concionem de poenis tanquam refutationem, Errant, inquit, cum istis modis consecuturos se ea quae sperant, existimant. Cito enim evertentur & peribunt, & hoc ipsum accident quod dicitur. Malum consilium consultori pessimum.

## 1:17
The net is cast in vain &c.

A metaphor is inserted signifying vain attempts, just as a bird-catcher is frustrated when he does not hide himself with skill. The application of the similitude is this: As it is necessary for a bird-catcher to be hidden, so the impious think they escape the eyes of God, and that no judgment will follow, no punishments, but they themselves are deceived, because God the inspector and avenger will repress them, and shortly after will punish them. In this sentiment, with respect to the eyes of God, he says in this place that the impious do not escape, and immediately concerning the punishment he adds that they bring destruction upon themselves. This is a known common discourse, which is often repeated, from the very beginning of the human race, that God inspects all the deeds of men, and punishes the impious, Psal. 57. Truly there is a God judging men on earth. Psal. 61. You render to each one according to his works. Indeed, experience also compelled the heathens to confess that atrocious crimes are punished divinely in this life, as God the avenger sees all things. Item The powers above look upon mortal things with just eyes.

Latin:

Frustra iacitur rete &c.

Inserta est Metaphora significans irritos conatus, sicut auceps frustatur, cum se non arte abscondit. Accommodatio similitudinis haec est, Ut aucupem latere necesse est, Ita impii putant se fallere oculos Dei, & nullum secuturum esse iudicium, nullas poenas, sed falluntur ipsi, quia Deus inspector & vindex reprimet eos, & paulo post puniet. In hanc sententiam respectu oculorum Dei, hoc loco dicit, impios non latere, & statim de poena subiicit, sibiipsis eos accersere exitium. Nota est autem haec communis concio, quae saepe repetitur, inde usque ab initio generis humani, Deum inspicere omnia hominum facta, & punire impios, Psalmo 57. Utique est Deus homines iudicans in terra. Psal. 61. Tu reddis unicuique iuxta opera sua. Imo & Ethnicos experientia fateri coegit divinitus in hac vita atrocia scelera puniri, ut Cernit Deus omnia vindex. Item Aspiciunt oculis superi mortalia iustis.

## 1:20
Wisdom cries out in the streets.

AS he previously delivered an admonition to the pious, not to be deceived by the scandals of the multitude and the power of the impious, so now a general rebuke is added, accusing all who are negligent and who despise the divine voice, and threatening them with punishments, among which this last one is the saddest: Then they will call and I will not hear. He signifies, however, that punishments are deferred, and in the meantime men are often called to repentance, and for those who are amended punishments are remitted, as is said in Isaiah the first. If your sins shall be as scarlet, you shall be white as snow, but when amendment is not made, and contumacy grows, harsher punishments follow, as is said concerning the Fig tree. Lucae 13. Behold I come the third year, cut it down, &c. And then invocation is made in vain, that is, before the punishment anger can be appeased, when we are already punished with the torments of bodies, miseries are not immediately taken away, but yet for those doing penance at any time both bodily punishments are mitigated, and eternal punishments are remitted, as to the thief on the cross, to David, to Manasseh, and to many similar ones. Thus before eternal punishment it can be appeased in this way, afterwards cries are vain, as Judas would lament in vain after the punishment. The explanation of the saying is not difficult, if a fitting accommodation is made to the different punishments.

But what he says, Wisdom cries out, I understand the word of God sounding with the voice of ministers, of all the Prophets, of Christ, of the Apostles and of all who teach rightly from the very beginning of the human race. This word of God or voice of the Gospel, preaching repentance and the remission of sins in the whole human race, in all empires, in all nations cries out &c.

The prosperity of fools.

IN the end he adds an epiphonema, which contains an antithesis of different objects and the outcome.

These two specious objects deceive men, the multitude and the prosperous affairs of the impious. On the contrary, the fewness and the cross of the pious deter many, but the outcomes are different , and from the final outcome, namely from eternity, judgment must be made. Even if Kings, Princes, Pontiffs, enemies of the Gospel flourish for some time, yet at length they will be oppressed by horrible punishments, on the contrary God will adorn the Church with true and eternal glory. He recites this comparison, saying, The allurements of the simple, that is, the specious opinions and authority of the powerful and of the multitude, invite the simple, but these very allurements are for them a destruction. Item the prosperity of fools invites many to imitation, and yet this very prosperity is for them a destruction, as is commonly said. Fortune makes him a fool whom she favors too much, as already in many enemies of the Gospel whose obstinacy and pride are confirmed by successes, but eventually God will punish them and bring aid to His Church and His truth. He adds, therefore, here another sentiment. He who is obedient to the word of God will be secure and tranquil, that is, he will be saved and defended by God, as is often said elsewhere. They who trust in the Lord shall not be moved, like Mount Zion. Likewise, The Lord will not leave the rod of sinners upon the lot of the righteous.

Latin:

Sapientia in plateis clamitat.

UT antea tradidit admonitionem piis, ne decipiantur scandalis multitudinis & potentiae impiorum, Ita nunc generalis obiurgatio additur, accusans omnes negligentes & contemnentes vocem divinam, & minitans eis poenas, inter quas haec postrema tristissima est, Tunc invocabunt & non exaudiam. Significat autem differri poenas, et interea saepe vocari homines ad paenitentiam, & iis qui emendantur remitti poenas, ut Esaiae primo dicitur. Si peccata vestra erunt sicut coccinum, eritis candidi sicut nix, Sed cum non fit emendatio, & crescit contumatia, sequuntur poenae duriores, ut de Ficu dicitur. Lucae 13. Ecce veni anno tertio, erue eam, &c. Et tunc frustra fit invocatio, id est, ante poenam ira placari potest, cum iam punimur suppliciis corporum, non mox tolluntur miseriae, sed tamen agentibus paenitentiam quocunque tempore & mitigantur poenae corporales, et remittuntur aeterne poenae, Ut latroni in cruce, Davidi, Manasse, & multis similibus. Sic ante aeternam poenam placari ita potest, postea sunt irriti clamores, ut Iudas post poenam frustra lamentaretur. Non difficilis est explicatio dicti, si fiat concinna accommodatio ad diversas poenas.

Quod autem dicit. Sapientia clamitat, intelligo verbum Dei sonans voce ministrorum, omnium Prophetarum, Christi, Apostolorum & omnium recte docentium inde usque ab initio generis humani. Hoc verbum Dei seu vox Evangelii, praedicans paenitentiam & remissionem peccatorum in toto genere humano, in omnibus imperiis, in omnibus gentibus clamitat &c.

Prosperitas stultorum.

IN fine addit Epiphonema, quod continet antithesin diversorum obiectorum & eventum.

Haec duo obiecta speciosa fallunt homines, multitudo & res secundae impiorum. E contra paucitas & crux piorum deterrent multos, sed exitus diversi sunt, & a postremo exitu videlicet ab aeternitate iudicium faciendum est. Etiamsi florent aliquamdiu Reges, Principes, Pontifices, inimici Evangelii, tamen tandem horribilibus poenis opprimentur, E contra ornabit Deus Ecclesiam vera & aeterna gloria. Hanc collationem recitat, inquiens, Illecebrae rudium, id est, speciosae opiniones & autoritas potentium & multitudinis, invitant rudes, sed hae ipsae illecebrae sunt eis exitio. Item prosperitas stultorum multos invitat ad imitationem, & tamen haec ipsa prosperitas est eis exitio, ut vulgo dicitur. Fortuna quem nimium fovet stultum facit, Sicut iam in multis hostibus Evangelii pertinatia & superbia successibus confirmatur, sed aliquando post Deus eos puniet & Ecclesiae ac veritati suae opem feret. Addit igitur hic alteram sententiam. Obediens verbo Dei erit securus & tranquillus, id est, servabitur, & defendetur a Deo, ut saepe alias dicitur. Qui confidunt in Domino non commovebuntur, sicut Mons Sion. Item, Non relinquet Dominus virgam peccatorum super sortem iustorum.

## 2:1 Argument
CHAPTER II.

The whole second Chapter is an exhortation to learn and to love the doctrine handed down by God, which is a sermon very often repeated in all the Prophets, and it agrees with this eternal command, This is my beloved son, hear him. Likewise. If anyone loves me, he will keep my word. God binds us to this doctrine, which He has revealed in the Church, and He wills that we learn and embrace it, and for this zeal He promises great rewards, as is said elsewhere, Seek first the kingdom of God, and the rest shall be added unto you. Let us propose this command and this consolation to ourselves, so that we may confirm ourselves against Epicurean judgments, just as Solomon also inserts here an admonition, lest we be deceived by an adulterous woman, by which he figuratively understands wisdom departing from the word of God, as are the judgments of the Epicureans and others, who mock or persecute the true doctrine.

But you should observe that especially, that He not only commands that we learn, nor does He only console us with added promises, but He also indicates how we can make progress, and He joins two causes, our zeal, and God's help, If you seek her, then you will find the knowledge of God, because the Lord will give wisdom. Thus he says, that God will be present to us, and will give wisdom and salutary counsels, if we hear, learn, and love His doctrine, and if we ask to be ruled and taught by Him. This admonition must be diligently considered, because one's own wisdom is a great impediment to many, who dream that they have enough counsel for themselves in governing their morals and life, and only ask God for the goods of the body. But let us learn that salutary counsels, right judgment, the true knowledge of God, and the governance of morals ought to be sought, as it is said, How much more will your heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to those who ask? And the Lord's prayer begins with the petition for the true knowledge of God, and for governance. Afterwards, the petition concerning bodily goods is added, Give us this day our daily bread, etc.

Latin:

CAPUT II.

Totum secundum Caput adhortatio est, ad discendam & amandam doctrinam a Deo traditam, quae concio saepissime in omnibus Prophetis repetita est, & congruit cum hoc aeterno mandato, Hic est filius meus dilectus, hunc audite. Item. Si quis diligit me, sermonem meum servabit. Alligat nos Deus ad hanc doctrinam, quam in Ecclesia patefecit, & vult ut eam discamus & amplectamur, & pro hoc studio premia magna promittit, sicut alibi dicitur, Primum quaerite regnum Dei, & caetera adiicientur vobis. Hoc mandatum & hanc consolationem nobis proponamus, ut nos contra iudicia Epicurea confirmemus, sicut & hic Salomon inserit admonitionem, ne a muliere adultera decipiamur, qua figurate intelligit sapientiam discedentem a verbo Dei, ut sunt iudicia Epicureorum & aliorum, qui derident aut persequuntur veram doctrinam.

Illud autem praecipue observes, quod non solum praecipit ut discamus, nec tantum consolatur nos additis promissionibus, sed etiam indicat quomodo proficere possimus, & coniungit duas causas, nostrum studium, & Dei auxilium, Si quaesieris eam, tunc scientiam Dei invenies, quia Dominus dabit sapientiam. Ita inquit, Deum nobis affuturum esse, & daturum sapientiam & consilia salutaria, si nos doctrinam eius audiamus, discamus, amemus, & si nos regi, & doceri ab ipso petamus. Haec admonitio diligenter consideranda est, quia magnum impedimentum est multis propria sapientia, qui somniant satis consilii sibi esse in regendis moribus & vita, tantum petunt a Deo bona corporis. Sed discamus consilia salutaria, rectum iudicium, veram Dei agnitionem & gubernationem morum peti oportere, ut dicitur, Quanto magis Pater vester caelestis dabit Spiritum sanctum petentibus? Et precatio dominica orditur a petitione verae notitiae Dei, & a gubernatione. Postea additur petitio de bonis corporalibus, Panem nostrum cotidianum &c.

## 3:1 Argument
CHAPTER III.

The beginning of the third chapter is a common exhortation to the love of doctrine, and to obedience. And promises of present and future goods are added, because at the end he says clearly, You will find grace and prosperity before God and men. He signifies, therefore, that God does well to the obedient both in this life and after this life, And a little before he says, Mercy and truth will not forsake you, Which saying I understand to be a promise, and to signify that the mercy and benefits of God will not be lacking to the obedient. For it is a customary phrase concerning God and men, He has done mercy and truth, that is, he was beneficent toward someone and faithful. That is. True benevolence, not feigned, he loved faithfully and constantly.

And this is also remarkable, that he promises that obedience will be pleasing not only to God, but also to men, although it is a rule that all the wicked oppose the saints, and friends, if there are any, are no protection, as happened to Jeremiah, the Baptist, and Christ. And the complaints are well known.

If the times are cloudy, you will be alone.

And in the Psalm it is much sadder πάθος, My father and mother have forsaken me, but the Lord has taken me up. But he answers, How many saints are subject to the cross, yet they do many things beneficial to men. As Jeremiah, although he is most miserable, yet he preserves the flower of the people. He does therefore good things before men, as is said here. And although he is killed, yet the Church is grateful to him. Indeed, while he lives, God mixes consolations with hardships and mitigates evils, he bends the mind of the Babylonian king and the prefects so that they defend him, etc.

These two things therefore Solomon embraces, that the just do things beneficial to men, and are vessels of mercy, and that some pious people judge rightly of them. But the unjust judgments of enemies and calumniators are not to be cared for. As Christ says about the Pharisees, Let them alone. Likewise, Blessed will you be when they shall revile you, lying. Meanwhile, however, the right judgments of the just are to be cared for, just as Paul also warns, You will be approved by God and men. Likewise Proverbs 22. A good name is better than great riches.

Latin:

CAPUT III.

Initium tertii capitis est adhortatio communis, ad amorem doctrinae, & ad obedientiam. Et adduntur promissiones bonorum praesentium & futurorum, quia in fine diserte dicit, Invenies gratiam, & prosperitatem coram Deo & hominibus. Significat igitur Deum obedientibus bene facere & in hac vita & post hanc vitam, Et paulo ante inquit, Misericordia & veritas non deserent te, Quod dictum intelligo promissionem esse, & significare Dei misericordiam & beneficia non defutura esse obedientibus. Usitata enim phrasis est de Deo & hominibus, Misericordiam & veritatem fecit, id est, fuit beneficus erga aliquem & fidelis. Id est. Vera benevolentia, non simulata, fideliter & constanter dilexit.

Est & hoc insigne, quod non solum promittit Deo placituram esse obedientiam, sed etiam hominibus, cum tamen regulare sit, ut sanctis omnes mali adversentur, Et amici, si qui sunt, non sint praesidio, ut accidit leremiae, Baptistae, Christo. Et notae sunt querelae.

Tempora si fuerint nubila solus eris.

Et in Psalmo multo tristius πάθος est, Pater & mater dereliquerunt me, Dominus autem suscepit me. Sed respondet, Quanti sancti sunt subiecti cruci, tamen salutaria hominibus multa faciunt. Ut Ieremias, etsi miserrimus est, tamen servat florem populi. Facit igitur bona coram hominibus, sicut hic dicitur. Et quanquam interficitur, tamen Ecclesia ei grata est. Imo donec vivit, miscet Deus aerumnis consolationes & mittigat mala, flectit animum regis Babylonici & praefectorum ut eum defendant, &c.

Haec duo igitur complectitur Salomon, Iustos salutaria hominibus facere, & esse vasa misericordiae, & aliquos pios recte de eis iudicare. Sed iniusta iudicia hostium & calumniatorum non sunt curanda. Ut de Pharisaeis dicit Christus, Omittite eos. Item, Beati eritis cum maledicent vobis mentientes. Interea tamen recta iudicia iustorum curanda sunt, sicut & Paulus monet, Eris probatus Deo & hominibus. Item proverbiorum 22. Melius est nomen bonum quam divitiae multae.

## 3:5
Trust in the Lord with all your heart.

Three singular precepts follow, the first concerning the acknowledgment of God and faith, and concerning the fear of God. The second, concerning helping the ministry of the Gospel. The third, concerning the Cross. Afterwards he returns to the general exhortation.

But these three precepts must be diligently considered. The first commands concerning faith, and an antithesis is added, Do not lean on your own understanding. Here again, as before, he leads us to the certain doctrine handed down by God, he wants this one thing to be an immovable norm for us in the acknowledgment of God and in the governing of morals and counsels. For he embraces these three things, So acknowledge God, as this doctrine handed down by Him teaches. I do not want you to wander, like the heathens, following your own opinions. As Simonides, asking what God is, confesses that he cannot find out. Then, I want you to do in morals what is commanded, not to follow your own judgment as the heathens do, who allowed themselves lusts. Thirdly, in the counsels of useful things, I want you to follow my precepts and do necessary things, but not forbidden ones, or πολυπραγμόνικα, and commend the outcomes to God, As it is said in the Psalm, Be subject to God and pray to him, I do not want you, because of human opinion about outcomes, to depart from the precepts, as those who flee confession do, who omit necessary things, and excuse themselves with this pretext, that they are consulting for common peace, concord, etc. Nor do I want you to move unnecessary things by foolish hope or fear.

He commands also concerning judgment and trust. Let judgment follow the norm of the word of God and when we follow that norm, that is, when we think rightly and do necessary things, then let trust expect help from God and not be broken even when we are destitute of human aids.

But promises are added, so that against human judgments we may have consolation, even if dangers hang over those who think rightly, and do necessary things, yet God will govern them, and will give happy outcomes, as it is said in the Psalm, Cast your care upon the Lord, and he will sustain you. And there are sweet figures here, wisdom will be the irrigation of your bones, by which effect consolation and a joyful outcome are described. For just as dread and grief hinder the generation of blood and vital spirits, and thus consume the juice, and at length extinguish life, So on the contrary a tranquil heart generates good blood and vital spirits, and that joy born from faith is the beginning of eternal life.

Latin:

Confide Domino in toto corde tuo.

Sequuntur tria singularia praecepta, Primum de agnitione Dei & fide, & de timore Dei. Secundum, de iuvando ministerio Evangelii. Tertium, de Cruce. Postea redit ad generalem adhortationem.

Diligenter autem consideranda sunt haec tria praecepta. Primum praecipit de fide, & addita est antithesis, Ne innitaris prudentiae tuae. Hic rursus, ut antea, deducit nos ad certam doctrinam a Deo traditam, hanc unam vult nobis normam esse immotam in agnitione Dei & in gubernatione morum & consiliorum. Haec tria enim complectitur, Sic agnoscas Deum, ut haec doctrina ab ipso tradita docet. Nolo te vagari, ut Ethnicos, sequentem tuas opiniones. Ut Simonides quaerens quid sit Deus, fatetur se non posse invenire. Deinde, Volo te in moribus facere quae praecepta sunt, non sequi tuum iudicium ut Ethnici faciunt, qui sibi permiserunt libidines. Tertio in consiliis rerum utilium, Volo te sequi mea praecepta & necessaria facere, non autem prohibita, aut πολυπραγμόνικα, & eventus Deo commendare, Sicut in Psalmo dicitur, Subditus esto Deo & ora eum, Non volo te propter opinionem humanam de eventibus, discedere a praeceptis, ut faciunt fugientes confessionem, qui omittunt necessaria, & se excusant hoc praetextu, quod consulant communi paci, concordiae &c. Nec volo te non necessaria movere stulta spe aut metu.

Praecipit autem & de iudicio & de fiducia. Iudicium sequatur normam verbi Dei & cum sequimur eam normam, id est, cum recte sentimus & necessaria facimus, deinde fiducia expectet a Deo auxilium non frangatur etiam cum destituimur humanis praesidiis.

Adduntur autem promissiones, ut contra humana iudicia consolationem habeamus, etiamsi pericula impendent recte sentientibus, & necessaria facientibus, tamen Deus ea gubernabit, & dabit felices exitus, sicut in Psalmo dicitur, Iacta curam tuam in Dominum, & ipse te sustentabit. Suntque hic dulces figurae, sapientia erit irrigatio ossium tuorum, quo effectu describitur consolatio & laetus exitus. Ut enim pavor & dolor impediunt generationem sanguinis & spirituum vitalium, & ita succum absumunt, & tandem extinguunt vitam, Ita e contra cor tranquillum gignit bonum sanguinem & spiritus vitales, & laeticia illa a fide orta, est inchoatio vitae aeternae.

## 3:9
Honor God with your substance.

According to the commandment, it mandates that the ministry of the Gospel be supported from our resources. And a most sweet promise is added, which agrees with the saying of Christ, If anyone shall give to one of the least of mine, even a drink of water on account of the doctrine, he shall have his reward. The example of the widow of Zarephath pertains to this.

Of the chastisement of God.

The third commandment contains the doctrine concerning the cross, about which I shall not speak at length here, because elsewhere the sum of this doctrine has been recited in such a way that the principal members which it is necessary to know have been collected into one place, namely, what are the causes of punishments & afflictions. Item, Whether they signify that a man has been rejected by God. Item, What kind of obedience is necessary. Item, That faith & hope ought to be added to that obedience. From there a longer explanation can be taken. But the matter becomes clearer, by comparing the doctrine of the Church to Philosophy. The philosopher says that matter is the cause of death & of diseases, or the wills of men, as when Catiline brings evil upon himself or when others harm others, as wicked citizens did to Socrates. These things are not falsely said concerning proximate causes. But the Church seeks more remote causes, whence this evil is in matter & in wills, and then when punishments follow, whether these are governed by providence & what they signify. Here Solomon answers briefly, that punishments & chastisements, whether they be τιμορίαι, or δοκιμασίαι,, do not happen by chance or without the counsel of God. Therefore he calls them the discipline of the Lord. Just as Paul also says, When we are judged, we are chastened by the Lord, that we may not be condemned with this world. Item, All the hairs of your head are numbered. And that they do not signify rejection he affirms afterwards, saying, Whom he loves, he chastens. Just as he says elsewhere. As I live, I do not desire the death of the sinner, &c. This consolation is necessary for the Church, for men to know that they are not rejected on account of calamities. And when grief is softened by this consolation, then some reason for good is perceived in miseries, namely the good will of God recalling us to repentance and wishing to receive us.

Here minds begin to desire obedience in some way, & they do not grow angry at God, but call upon him, & hope for mitigation, as David indeed grieves when driven into exile, but in some way he wills the punishment, & moderates his grief, he does not procure death for himself, like Saul, nor does he grow angry at God, but calls upon him & hopes for mitigation, as it is written, Call upon me in the day of tribulation & I will deliver you. In such an image let us consider in some way what the tolerance of the pious is like, and let us remind ourselves of the will of God in our own hardships from this doctrine of the Church concerning the cross. The commandment therefore orders us to obey God in afflictions, that is, to bear grief moderately on account of these two causes.

I. Because obedience is owed to God, & afflictions do not happen by chance, but by the counsel of God.

II. Because the final cause is not that we should perish, but that, having been recalled to repentance, we should be saved.

And do not abhor his correction.

The judgment concerning miseries & the grief & the raging of the mind is significantly expressed, when he says, Do not consider chastisement to be a thing to be abhorred. And what kind of Emphasis this is, can be seen most of all in the example of the passion of Christ. The Jews, seeing Christ hanging on the cross, considered him to be altogether a portent to be abhorred, & rejected by God. Item, thus it is said in the Tragedy. Depart from me, for let my shadow be a hindrance to the good. It is certain, however, that human judgments without the word of God judge thus concerning hardships, & rage, as if at the sight of a thing to be abhorred, but the word of God leads us away from that hideous appearance to the consideration of the good will of God &c. I shall not add a longer interpretation here, because it has been spoken of more copiously elsewhere concerning the whole matter.

Latin:

Honora Deum de substantia tua.

Secundum praeceptum mandat de facultatibus nostris iuvari ministerium Evangelii. Et addita est dulcissima promissio, quae congruit cum dicto Christi, Si quis dederit uni ex minimis meorum, vel potum aquae propter doctrinam, habebit mercedem. Huc pertinet exemplum viduae Sareptanae.

Castigationem Dei.

Tertium praeceptum doctrinam continet de cruce, de qua hic non prolixe dicam, quia alibi summa huius doctrinae ita recitata est, ut praecipua membra quae scire necesse est, in unum locum collecta sint, videlicet, quae sint causae poenarum & afflictionum. Item, An significent hominem a Deo reiectum esse. Item, Qualis obedientia necessaria sit. Item, Quod fidem & spem in ea obedientia accedere oporteat. Inde sumi potest explicatio longior. Fit autem res illustrior, collata doctrina Ecclesiae ad Philosophiam. Philosophus dicit materiam esse causam mortis, & morborum, vel voluntates hominum, ut cum sibi ipsi accersit malum Catilina vel cum alii nocent aliis, ut Socrati cives improbi. Haec de propinquis causis non falso dicuntur. Sed Ecclesia quaerit remotiores causas, unde hoc malum sit in materia & in voluntatibus, ac deinde cum sequuntur poenae, an hae regantur providentia & quid significent. Hic breviter respondet Salomon, poenas & castigationes sive sint τιμορίαι, sive δοκιμασίαι, non accidere casu aut sine consilio Dei. Ideo nominat eas disciplinam Domini. Sicut & Paulus inquit, Cum iudicamur, a Domino corripimur, ne cum hoc mundo damnemur. Item, Omnes capilli capitis vestri numerati sunt. Et quod non significent reiectionem postea affirmat, inquiens, Quem diligit, corripit. Sicut & alibi inquit. Vivo ego nolo mortem peccatoris, &c. Haec consolatio necessaria est Ecclesiae, scire homines non reiectos esse propter calamitates. Et cum lenitur hac consolatione dolor, iam aliqua boni ratio in miseriis conspicitur, videlicet bona Dei voluntas revocantis nos ad paenitentiam & volentis nos recipere.

Hic incipiunt animi velle obedientiam aliquo modo, & non irascuntur Deo, sed invocant eum, & sperant mitigationem, ut dolet quidem David pulsus in exilium, sed aliquo modo vult poenam, & moderatur dolorem, non consciscit sibi mortem, ut Saul, nec irascitur Deo, sed invocat eum & sperat mitigationem, ut scriptum est, Invoca me in die tribulationis & ego eripiam te. In tali imagine consideremus utcunque, qualis sit tolerantia piorum, & nos ipsos de voluntate Dei in nostris aerumnis commonefaciamus ex hac doctrina Ecclesiae de cruce. Praeceptum igitur iubet obedire Deo in afflictionibus, id est, moderate ferre dolorem propter has duas causas.

I. Quia Deo debetur obedientia, & afflictiones non accidunt casu, sed consilio Dei.

II. Quia causa finalis est non ut pereamus, sed ut revocati ad paenitentiam servemur.

Et non abomineris correptionem.

Iudicium de miseriis & dolor & fremitus animi significanter exprimitur, cum inquit, Ne ducas castigationem esse rem abominandam. Et qualis sit Emphasis, maxime conspici potest in exemplo passionis Christi. Iudaei videnter Christum pendentem in cruce, duxerunt eum prorsus esse portentum abominandum, & a Deo reiectum. Item sic in Tragoedia dicitur. Discedite a me, nam umbra mea obsit bonis. Constat autem humana iudicia sine verbo Dei, sic de aerumnis iudicare, & fremere, tanquam conspectu rei abominandae, sed verbum Dei abducit nos ab illa tetra specie ad considerationem bonae voluntatis Dei &c. Non addam hic longiorem interpretationem, quia alibi copiosius de tota re dictum est.

## 3:13
Blessed is the man.

He returns to common exhortations, and commands that we learn the doctrine handed down by God, and obey it, and he introduces promises and threats, Blessed is he who finds wisdom, namely revealed, that is, the Word handed down by God. This is the simplest meaning, to understand these sayings principally concerning revealed wisdom, that is, concerning the Word of God sounding in the Church, which agrees with the divine mind that created things, and testifies what kind of God He is, namely wise, good, just, truthful, chaste. Therefore, from the revealed Word he passes to the wisdom which is in God, and says that God is wise, and that the testimonies of wisdom are shown in creation.

For the order of many things, of bodies and celestial motions, of laws in the human mind, of punishments, testify that God is wise, good, just, truthful, chaste, etc. And with this wisdom in His own mind agrees the revealed Word, which narrates what kind of God He is, but that is to be considered first, that these praises of the wisdom of God are a praising of the Evangelical ministry, and a thanksgiving for the revealed Word, and for the preservation of the ministry. The Arians abused these sayings about Wisdom, and twisted them to a person, namely to the Son of God. And although the Arians can be refuted in many ways, yet now it is enough to warn the reader, so that he may distinguish things properly said about the Son of God, John 1. from other narrations, where it is spoken about the revealed voice, or the ministry.

Do not withhold doing good.

Above he recited the precepts pertaining to the first table of the Decalogue, here he adds precepts more in agreement with the second table. And it must be diligently seen to which common places, and to which virtues or vices the precepts are to be referred, which when it has been shown, then there is no need for a long explanation.

The precept, Do not withhold etc., pertains to the topic of vocation, that you do not hinder another's vocation, but faithfully serve your own. Now another's vocation is hindered either by emulation, or by hatred, or πολυπραγμοσύνῃ, or by petulance, as Antony hinders Augustus by emulation, Dathan and Abiram hinder Aaron, the Devil hinders the Church by hatred, and at all times ever since Cain the instruments of the Devil, because they burn with hatred of God and the Church, hinder good government. Pericles, Demosthenes sin πολυπραγμοσύνῃ, when they attract wars without necessity. Alcibiades moves the Republic by petulance alone. Such causes are to be considered so that they may be avoided with greater care and vigilance.

On the contrary, however, it is commanded that everyone faithfully obey his own vocation. As Paul says, Moreover it is required that a minister be faithful. So here he says, If it is in your hands, do these things yourself. That is, If your vocation offers your hands the occasion and the ability to do good.

And although to the rude and ignorant of public counsels this precept seems to be empty, yet in life it extends most widely. For there are many in any government who hinder good counsels and few who help, and many vessels of wrath, few vessels of mercy. As in the army of Saul, many leaders were impious, few were salutary, like David and Jonathan. At Rome many were disturbers of the Republic, Cassius, Brutus, Lepidus, Antony and their companions, on the contrary Augustus was useful to the fatherland. But nothing is sadder than for a man to be a vessel of wrath, and pernicious to himself and to many others. Therefore let us pray that God may make us vessels of mercy, and let us ourselves add our diligence, so that everyone may do his own duty, and not move things that are not necessary.

Latin:

Beatus homo.

Redit ad communes adhortationes, ac praecipit ut discamus doctrinam a Deo traditam, & ei obediamus, Et inferit promissiones & comminationes, Beatus qui invenit sapientiam, videlicet revelatam, id est. Verbum a Deo tradirum. Haec est simplicissima sententia, intelligere haec dicta principaliter de sapientia revelata, id est, de verbo Dei sonante in Ecclesia, quod congruit cum mente divina conditrice rerum, et testatur qualis sit Deus, scilicet sapiens, bonus, iustus, verax, castus. Ideo a revelato verbo transit ad sapientiam, quae est in Deo, & dicit Deum esse sapientem, & sapientiae testimonia in creatione ostendere.

Nam ordo plurimarum rerum, corporum & motuum caelestium, legum in mente humana, poenarum, testantur Deum esse sapientem, bonum, iustum veracem, castum, &c. Et cum hac sapientia in ipsius mente congruit verbum revelatum, quod narrat qualis sit Deus, illud autem in primis est considerandum, has laudes sapientiae Dei, laudationem esse ministerii Evangelici, & gratiarum actionem pro revelato verbo, & pro conservatione ministerii. Ariani abusi sunt his dictis de Sapientia, & detorserunt ea ad personam scilicet ad filium Dei. Et si autem Ariani multipliciter refutari possunt, tamen nunc satis est monere lectorem, ut discernat proprie dicta de filio Dei, Ioan. 1. ab aliis narrationibus, ubi de voce revelata, vel ministerio dicitur.

Ne prohibeas benefacere.

Supra recitavit praecepta ad primam tabulam Decalogi pertinentia, hic addit praecepta magis ad secundam tabulam congruentia. Ac diligenter videndum est ad quos locos communes, & ad quas virtutes aut vitia referenda sint praecepta, quod cum monstratum est, deinde non est opus longa enarratione.

Praeceptum. Ne prohibeas &c. pertinet ad locum de vocatione, ne alienam vocationem impedias, tuae vero fideliter inservias. Impeditur autem aliena vocatio aut aemulatione, aut odio, aut πολυπραγμοσύνῃ, aut petulentia, ut Antonius aemulatione impedit Augustum, Dathan & Abyron impediunt Aaronem, Diabolus odio impedit Ecclesiam, & omnibus temporibus inde usque a Cain organa Diaboli, quia ardent odio Dei & Ecclesiae, impediunt bonam gubernationem. Pericles, Demosthenes peccant πολυπραγμοσύνῃ, cum attrahunt bella sine necessitate. Alcibiades sola petulantia movet Rempublicam. Tales causae considerandae sunt ut maiore cura & vigilantia vitentur.

E contra vero praecipitur ut suae quisque vocationi fideliter obtemperet. Sicut Paulus inquit, Caeterum hoc requiritur ut minister fidelis sit. Ita hic inquit, Si est in manibus tuis, facito haec ipse. Id est, Si vocatio manibus tuis offert occasionem & facultatem benefaciendi.

Etsi autem rudibus & ignatis consiliorum publicorum videtur hoc praeceptum ieiun um esse, tamen in vita latissime patet. Multi enim in qualibet gubernatione sunt, qui impediunt bona consilia & pauci qui adiuvant, & multa vasa irae, pauca vasa misericordiae. Ut in exercitu Saulis, multi duces impii fuerunt, pauci salutares, ut David & Ionathas. Romae multi turbatores Reipublicae Cassius, Brutus, Lepidus, Antonius & eorum comites, econtra Augustus fuit utilis patriae. Nihil autem tristius est, quam hominem esse vas irae, & sibi & multis aliis perniciosum. Ideo oremus ut Deus nos faciat vasa misericordiae, & nos ipsi diligentiam nostram addamus, ut suum quisque officium faciat, nec moveat non necessaria.

## 3:28
Do not say to your neighbor, go.

This precept pertains properly to liberality, which does not assist fastidiously, nor morosely, nor proudly, nor by some captiousness, but with true love and for the sake of God. As it is written, God loves a cheerful giver. Likewise, He gives twice who gives quickly. Likewise, Seneca says concerning the powerful, Injuries are headlong, benefits are slow. Likewise, Grace is absent from a duty which delay retards.

You shall not do an injury.

As I said above, in this beginning of the book the first precepts of the Decalogue are repeated, so also here let the reader know that the two sentences which follow pertain to the precepts, You shall not kill, and those following, because they are prohibitions of injuries, just as those most well-known laws of nature, Do harm to no one, Give to each his own.

Nor do they need a long declaration, since they are sentences known by nature. And God himself declares these laws with horrible punishments, when he punishes atrocious crimes in this life. There is, however, a double prohibition. The one speaks of manifest violence. The other of judgments, where under a false pretext of law and judgments snares are laid for others, just as John also embraces these two species, Do not extort from anyone, nor accuse falsely. But it becomes the studious to consider what the demonstrations of these laws are.

This one is philosophical. Every nature is ordered so that it may be a preserver of its own species, and therefore στοργή is implanted in parents, so that it may incite them to the preservation of their offspring. Injuries dissipate and destroy the species. Therefore they are to be avoided.

The theological one is much more illustrious. Man is created so that he may be the image of God, and God wills that the knowledge and virtues in men be testimonies that He is God and what kind He is, and that He is about to judge. There are, however, virtues in God: justice, beneficence, truth, chastity. Therefore let there be in man a likeness of the archetype.

Latin:

Ne dicas proximo tuo vade.

Hoc praeceptum proprie ad liberalitatem pertinet, quae non fastidiose, non morose, non superbe, non captatione aliqua opitulatur, sed vero amore & propter Deum. Sicut scriptum est, Hilarem datorem diligit Deus. Item, Bis dat qui cito dat. Item de potentibus inquit Seneca, Iniuriae praecipites sunt, lenta beneficia. Item, Gratiaque officio, quod mora tardat abest.

Non facies iniuriam.

UT supra dixi in hoc initio libri repeti primum Decalogi praecepta, Ita & hic sciat lector duas sententias, quae sequuntur, pertinere ad praecepta, Non occides, & sequentia, Quia sunt prohibitiones iniuriarum, sicut illae notissimae leges naturae, Neminem ledito, Suum cuique tribuito.

Nec indigent longa declaratione, cum sint sententiae natura note. Et Deus ipse horribilibus poenis declarat has leges, cum in hac vita punit atrocia delicta. Est autem duplex prohibitio. Altera de manifesta violentia loquitur. Altera de iudiciis, ubi falso praetextu iuris & iudiciorum aliis struuntur insidiae, Sicut & Ioannes has duas species complectitur, Neminem Concutiatis neque Calumniemini, Illud autem decet studiosos cogitare, quae sint demonstrationes harum legum.

Philosophica haec est. Omnis natura ordinata est, ut sit conservatrix suae speciei, ideoque indita est στοργή parentibus, ut incitet ad conservationem natorum. Iniuriae dissipant & delent speciem. Ergo vitandae sunt.

Theologica est multo illustrior. Homo est conditus ut sit imago Dei, & vult Deus noticias & virtutes in hominibus testimonia esse quod sit Deus & qualis sit, & quod iudicaturus sit. Sunt autem in Deo virtutes, iusticia, beneficentia, veritas, castitas. Ergo sit in homine similitudo archetypi.

## 3:31
Do not emulate, etc.

The precept is concerning scandals, that is, concerning not imitating the impious on account of their successes, and it agrees with the whole Psalm, Do not emulate the malignant. And it is a sentence very often repeated, Do not be moved by the prosperous affairs of the impious, so that on account of that you might imitate them. As, do not be moved by the glory and successes of the Turkish empire, or of other impious men, so that on account of that you might think the doctrine concerning God, which God himself has revealed by illustrious testimonies in the Church, to be fabulous.

For although the impious flourish for a little while, yet afterwards they will be overturned, as is said several times in the same Psalm. But in order that we may retain the constancy of the true sentence concerning God, it is necessary to know the doctrine, why God wills the Church to be subjected to the cross. And here at the end it is said concerning the prosperous affairs of the impious, The exaltation of fools is ignominy, which is said in other words by the Poet.

They are lifted on high, that they may fall with a heavier crash. And below, Before ruin is exaltation, and before glory is humility.

Latin:

Non aemuleris&c.

Praeceptum est de scandalis, hoc est, de non imitandis impiis propter eorum successus, & congruit cum toto Psalmo, Noli aemulari in malignantibus. Estque sententia saepissime repetitia, Non movearis rebus secundis impiorum, ut propterea eos imiteris. Ut, non movearis gloria & successibus imperii Turcici, aut aliorum impiorum, ut propterea cogites fabulosam esse doctrinam de Deo, quam Deus ipse illustribus testimoniis in Ecclesia patefecit.

Quanquam enim impii aliquantisper florent, tamen postea evertentur, sicut in Psalmo eodem aliquoties dicitur. Ut autem constantiam verae sententiae de Deo retineamus, necesse est scire doctrinam, quare velit Deus Ecclesiam cruci esse subiectam. Et hic in fine dicitur de rebus secundis impiorum, Exaltatio stultorum ignominia, quod aliis verbis apud Poetam dictum est.

Tolluntur in altumUt lapsu graviore ruant.Et infra, Ante ruinam exaltatio, & ante gloriam humilitas.

## 4:1 Argument
CHAPTER IV.

The whole fourth chapter is an exhortation to learn the doctrine handed down by God, and to the study of obeying this doctrine, and of governing discipline, and to circumspection, lest we be deceived by the examples of the impious. And mention of rewards and punishments is inserted, as is customary in exhortations. Such discourses here and elsewhere teach, as has often been said already, that the Church is bound to this doctrine, which God has delivered, and that men are forbidden to invent with blind audacity opinions about the essence or will of God, but with firm faith to embrace this doctrine revealed by God, and with this shining before them, to acknowledge and call upon God, and obey Him. There are, however, in the text many sweet figures, which signify diligence, Search the heart, Flee impious speeches, Rule the eyes, the feet. This whole description exhorts that we be vigilant and diligent in governing discipline. Just as Paul also commands to the Ephesians: walk accurately, not as the foolish. And it has often been said elsewhere, To avoid sins is to avoid the occasions of sins.

Human powers can, however, in some way perform this diligence, and God wills that the reborn, by these very exercises, stir up and confirm faith, fear, invocation, and other virtues.

There is obscurity in one saying, The beginning of wisdom is to acquire wisdom, but it is a very plain sentence concerning the manner and the beginning of virtues. To hear the doctrine, and to truly seek it and ask for guidance from God according to the doctrine, is the very beginning of conversion, etc. As it is said elsewhere: He will give the Holy Spirit to those who ask, etc.

Latin:

CAPUT IIII.

Totum Caput quartum adhortatio est, ad discendam doctrinam a Deo traditam, & ad studium obediendi huic doctrinae, & regendae disciplinae & ad circumspectionem, ne decipiamur exemplis impiorum. Et inseritur mentio praemiorum & poenarum, sicut in adhortationibus usitatum est. Tales autem conciones hic & alibi docent, ut saepe iam dictum est, alligatam esse Ecclesiam ad hanc doctrinam, quam Deus tradidit, ac prohiberi ne homines caeca audicia fingant opiniones de essentia aut voluntate Dei, sed firma fide amplectantur hanc doctrinam a Deo revelatam, hac praelucente agnoscant & invocent Deum, & ei obtemperent. Sunt autem in textu multae dulces figurae, quae diligentiam significant, Sertia cor, Fuge sermones impios, Rege oculos, pedes. Haec tota descriptio hortatur, ut simus vigilantes & diligentes in regenda disciplina. Sicut & Paulus praecipit ad Ephesi. accurate ambulate non ut fatui. Et saepe alias dictum est, Vitare peccata est vitare occasiones peccatorum.

Hanc autem diligentiam aliquo modo humanae vires praestare possunt, & vult Deus renatos his ipsis exercitiis exuscitare et confirmare fidem, timorem, invocationem, & caeteras virtutes.

Obscuritas est in uno dicto, Principium sapientiae est acquirere sapientiam, sed planissima sententia est, de modo & inchoatione virtutum. Audire doctrinam, & eam vere expetere & petere gubernationem a Deo iuxta doctrinam, est ipsa inchoatio conversionis &c. Sicut alias dicitur. Dabit Spiritum sanctum petentibus &c.

## 5:1 Argument
CHAPTER V.

The fifth chapter pertains especially to two precepts of the Decalogue , You shall not commit adultery. And, You shall not steal. For the first prohibits adulteries and all wandering lusts, and adds a discourse about punishments, because without any doubt events respond to this divine rule, God will judge fornicators and adulterers, as private and public histories show, David, Troy, Thebes. And although God mitigates calamities for some who do penance, nevertheless some punishments always accompany them.

Inserted, however, is a most sweet discourse, which approves of marriage and mutual love, and the sweetness of spouses, and prohibits adulteries. The approval is of marriage and mutual love, when it says, May you be delighted with the wife of your youth. That is, whom you marry as a youth. Then it paints mutual love with a notable image. Let love be such as that of the stag and the doe, or as that of the roe and the fawn, that is, let it be an ardent love without simulation and without coercion, let it be sweet without moroseness and disgust, let it be sincere without suspicions, without πλεονεξιά, and let them acquiesce in mutual love, lest they be torn away or desire others, whether male or female.

A description of ardor exists in Oppian concerning Stags. The Stag himself with swift feet follows her, When she flees, and stops the beloved in his embraces.

The antitheses, however, declare examples. Tiberius unwillingly marries Julia; here simulation and coercion render the marriage unsweet. Quintus Cicero's Pomponia is too morose. There are also many natures of women and men suspicious without end. Saul's daughter proudly despises David. All such vices are removed when the sincere and sweet affection of the roe and the fawn, or the stag and the doe, is considered; there is nothing simulated in these, nothing of suspicion, nothing of moroseness or diffidence, nothing of pride, but it is a true and ardent love, and they acquiesce in mutual love, and each grieves that they are drawn away from the other, each wishes well to the other. Such marriages were those of Adam and Eve, Jacob and Rachel, David and Abigail.

The text adds, however, Let her breasts inebriate you, That is, let the love be true and let it not be wandering. The approval is, as I said above, both of love and of the conjugal covenant. Let all know, however, that the law concerning marriage was expressly delivered in Paradise, They shall be two in one flesh, That is, One man and one woman, they shall be joined by true mutual love and inseparably.

Since, however, God sanctioned this obedience immediately at the beginning with an express command, and willed that this work, namely chastity, should be even in an uncorrupted nature a distinguished exercise of the obedience owed to Him, it can be sufficiently understood that God most severely both demands chastity and punishes wandering lusts, just as He declares this will of His daily by manifest punishments in this mortal life, and for that reason chastity is to be guarded the more diligently, so that we may be able to call upon God with a good conscience.

And let us recognize more that virtue which makes an illustrious distinction between God, men, and devils, concerning which distinction one must think in every prayer: You call upon God the creator of things, wise, beneficent, truthful, just, chaste. The knowledge of these virtues was therefore delivered divinely, so that it might show the conspicuous distinction between God and men and devils, which distinctions, having been destroyed by this virtue, were lost among the Gentiles. And Paul to the Rom. and to the Ephe. signifies that these evils are joined together, ignorance of God and foul lusts, because the Devil drives many things at once when he impels miserable men to wandering lusts, he draws minds away from God, he irritates God so that He may dissipate whole nations with public punishments. Just as the punishment of David shows that the evils which accompany that punishment wander widely, and the whole of life is full of examples. Let honest minds, however, consider in this place both sentences: Wandering lusts are forbidden, and mention of punishments is added, The adulterer will lose his soul. And Paul says, Fornicators and adulterers will not possess the kingdom of God.

On the contrary, a sweet praise of true love between spouses is recited, when he says, Let her breasts inebriate you, he commands one to enjoy the love of a spouse. For breasts, on account of the chest and the embrace, signify love, and they were added to the chest in the human race so that milk might be better cooked by the neighboring heat and spirit of the heart, and so that the spirit might transfuse στοργὴν and love into the offspring. Just as God in us, with the Holy Spirit transfused, lights the light by which we recognize Him.

Latin:

CAPUT V.

Caput quintum ad duo praecepta Decalogi praecipue pertinet, Non moechaberis. Et, Non furtum facies. Primum enim prohibet adulteria & omnes vagas libidines, & addit concionem de poenis, quia sine ulla dubitatione huic divinae regulae respondent eventus, Scortatores & adulteros iudicabit Deus, ut historiae privatae et publicae ostendunt, David, Troia, Thebae. Et quanquam Deus mitigat calamitates aliquibus agentibus paenitentiam, tamen aliquae poenae semper comitantur.

Inserta est autem concio dulcissima, quae approbat coniugium & mutuum amorem, & suavitatem coniugum, & prohibet adulteria. Approbatio est coniugii & mutui amoris, cum inquit, Delecteris uxore adolescentiae tuae. Id est, quam adolescens ducis. Deinde pingit amorem mutuum insigni imagine. Talis sit amor, ut est cervi & cervae, seu ut est capreae & hinnuli, id est, sit ardens amor sine simulatione & sine coactione, sit suavis sine morositate & fastidio, Sit sincerus sine suspicionibus, sine πλεονεξιά, & acquiescant in mutuo amore, ne divellantur aut appetant alios, vel alias.

Extat ardoris descriptio apud Oppianum de Cervis.Ipse tamen Cervus pedibus velocibus ipsam,Cum fugit assequitur, sistitque amplexus amatam.

Antitheses vero declarant exempla. Tiberius invitus ducit Iuliam, hic simulatio & coactio reddit coniugium insuave. Quinti Ciceronis Pomponia est morosior. Sunt & multae naturae mulierum & virorum sine fine suspicaces. Filia Saul superbe despicit Davidem. Talia vitia omnia removentur considerato sincero & dulci affectu capreae & hinnuli, aut cervi & cervae, nihil est in his simulatum, nihil suspicionis, nihil morositatis aut diffidentiae, nihil superbiae, sed verus & ardens amor est, & acquiescunt in amore mutuo, & uterque dolet se ab altero abstrahi, uterque vult alteri bene esse. Talia coniugia fuerunt Adae & Evae, Iacob & Rahel, David & Abigail.

Addit autem textus, Ubera eius inebrient te, Id est, sit amor verus & non sit vagus. Approbatio est ut supra dixi & amoris & foederis coniugalis. Sciant autem omnes legem de coniugio expresse traditam esse in Paradiso, Erunt duo in carnem unam, Id est, Unus vir & una mulier, erunt iuncti vero amore mutuo & inseparabiliter.

Cum autem Deus hanc obedientiam statim initio sanxerit expresso mandato, & hoc opus, videlicet castitatem, voluerit etiam in natura integra insigne exercitium esse obedientiae ipsi debitae, satis intelligi potest Deum severissime & postulare castitatem, & punire vagas libidines, Sicut hanc suam voluntatem manifestis poenis in hac vita mortali quotidie declarat, eoque diligentius tuenda est castitas, ut bona conscientia Deum invocare possimus.

Et eam virtutem magis agnoscamus, quae facit illustre discrimen inter Deum, homines & Diabolos, de quo discrimine in omni precatione cogitandum est, Invocas Deum conditorem rerum sapientem, beneficium, veracem, iustum, castum. Harum virtutum notitia ideo divinitus tradita est, ut monstret conspicuum discrimen inter Deum & homines & diabolos, quae discrimina, hac virtute deleta, inter Ethnicos amissa fuerunt. Et Paulus ad Rom. & ad Ephe. significat haec mala coniuncta esse, Ignorantiam Dei, & tetras libidines, quia multas res simul agit Diabolus, cum impellit miseros homines ad vagas libidines, abstrahit mentes a Deo, irritat Deum, ut publicis poenis dissipet totas gentes. Sic ut poena Davidis ostendit, late vagari mala, quae comitantur illam poenam, & plena est tota vita exemplorum, Considerent autem honestae mentes hoc loco utramque sententiam, Prohibentur vagae libidines, & additur mentio poenarum, Adulter perdet animam suam. Et Paulus inquit, Scortatores & adulteri non possidebunt regnum Dei.

Econtra dulcis laudatio recitatur veri amoris inter coniuges, cum inquit, Ubera eius inebrient te, iubet frui amore coniugis. Ubera enim propter pectus & complexum significant amorem, & tantum in genere humano pectori addita sunt, ut lac vicino calore & spiritu cordis melius coquatur, & ut spiritus transfundat στοργὴν & amorem in natos. Sicut Deus in nobis transfuso spiritu sancto lucem accendit, qua eum agnoscimus.

## 5:1 Concerning the Second Precept. You Shall Not Steal
CONCERNING THE SECOND PRECEPT. You shall not steal.

Drink water from your own fountains. At the same time, this has embraced the precepts concerning particular justice abstaining from what is another's, concerning parsimony, and concerning liberality. And this place is to be observed diligently against the Monks and Anabaptists, who praise Platonic community. For just as the precept, You shall not steal, clearly sanctions the distinction of ownerships, so also Solomon here commands the Lord to retain the fountain, by which saying he approves the distinction of things. Then he commands one to abstain from what is another's, Drink water from your own cisterns, That is, you should abstain from the things of others, and enjoy your own things.

He adds also the precept concerning liberality, and concerning the manner of liberality, which is called parsimony, he commands the streams to be derived outward. That is, to bestow upon the needy from the fruits, but in such a way that the Lord is not shaken out of the estate. He establishes this manner for liberality, just as Paul also says, To give not so that there may be idleness for others and affliction for you. Likewise Christ, Give according to your means δότε ἐκ τῶν ἐνόντων.

He confirms the distinction of ownerships, and commands parsimony by the sentence placed above in the same chapter, Do not give your honor to others, nor your substance to a cruel one, lest strangers be filled with your faculties, etc. The matter shows in courts how many abuse the facility of lords, so also among the people many abuse the facility of friends; these leeches or harpies he calls cruel here, because they are unjust toward those to whom they owe both fidelity and gratitude, and they suck out their blood as it were; so also with a charming allusion Aristophanes said ταμίαι γάμιαι. That is, Which like screech-owls suck out the faculties of the people.

And therefore, in this whole passage, let us learn especially that the doctrine concerning the distinction of dominions is divinely approved, the distinction of dominions. Also, that there are neighboring virtues, liberality and parsimony, and that both please God, because God wills that we use His gifts reverently in the order which He instituted. He wills that we acknowledge the fruits and other things necessary for life to be His gifts. And then that we take something from there to be contributed to this use, so that the knowledge of God may be preserved on earth, namely for the ministry of the Gospel. Then for the defense of peace and of judgments. Thirdly, for nourishing ourselves and our own without luxury and intemperance. Finally, for helping others, according to the measure of our means, so that the Lord may not be shaken from the foundation. And that saying of Epicharmus was rightly stated, You rejoice in giving, you have a disease, χάιρεις διδούς, νόσον ἔχεις. because one must rejoice in duty, not in profusion.

Latin:

DE ALTERO PRAECEPTO. Ne furtum facias.

Bibe aquam dc tuis fontibus. Simul complexus est hic praecepta de iusticia particulari abstinente ab alieno, de parsimonia & de liberalitate. Estque diligenter hic locus observandus adversus Monachos & Anabaptistas, qui communionem Platonicam laudant. Ut enim praeceptum, Non furtum facies, perspicue sancit distinctionem dominiorum, ita & hic Salomon iubet Dominum retinere fontem, quo dicto approbat rerum distinctionem. Deinde iubet abstinere ab alieno, Bibe aquam ex tuis cisternis, Id est, abstineas ab alienis, & fruaris tuis rebus.

Addit & praeceptum de liberalitate, & de modo liberalitatis, qui vocatur parsimonia, iubet rivulos foras derivari. Id est, largiri egentibus de fructibus, sed ita ne Dominus ex fundo excutiatur. Hunc modum liberalitati constituit, sicut & Paulus inquit, Dare non ut aliis sit ignavia, vobis afflictio. Item Christus, Date pro facultatibus δότε ἐκ τῶν ἐνόντων.

Confirmat distinctionem dominiorum, & praecipit parsimoniam sententia supra posita in eodem capite, Ne des alienis honorem tuum, nec substantiam tuam crudeli, ne satientur alieni facultatibus tuis &c. Res ostendit in aulis quam multi facilitate dominorum abutantur, sic & in populo multi abutuntur facilitate amicorum, has irudines seu harpyas vocat hic crudeles, quia iniusti sunt erga eos, quibus & fidelitatem & gratitudinem debent, & tanquam sanguinem eorum exugunt, ita & venusta allusione Aristophanes dixit ταμίαι γάμιαι. Id est, Quae stores velut striges exugunt populi facultates.

Et toto igitur hoc loco discamus praecipue doctrinam de distinctione dominiorum approbatam esse divinitus distinctionem dominiorum. Item vicinas virtutes esse, liberalitatem, & parsimoniam & utramque Deo placere, quia Deus vult nos suis donis reverenter uti eo ordine, quem instituit, Vult ut agnoscamus fruges & caeteras res ad vitam necessarias sua dona esse. Et deinde aliquid inde sumamus conferendum ad hunc usum, ut noticia Dei in terris conservetur, videlicet ad ministerium Evangelii. Deinde ad defensionem pacis et iudiciorum. Tertio ad nos & nostros sine luxu & intemperantia alendos. Postremo ad alios iuvandos, pro modo nostrarum facultatum, ne Dominus ex fundo excuciatur. Et recte dictum est illud Epicharmi, Laetaris dando, morbum habes, χάιρεις διδούς, νόσον ἔχεις. Quia laetandum est officio non profusione.

## 5:1 Concerning Examples of the Multitude
CONCERNING EXAMPLES OF THE MULTITUDE.

IN this chapter, this notable saying concerning the examples of the multitude is also to be observed. I was almost in all evil, in the midst of the congregation. That is, the contagions of the people harm everyone. Because tender and weak minds are drawn by the consent of many and by custom, so that they embrace false opinions and many vices. And the length of time confirms these evils, wherefore afterwards they are pertinaciously retained as if they were good and sweet things. As it is said, Custom is a second nature. Also πᾶν σὺντροφον γλυκύ. And Aeschylus says, That in all things there is nothing worse than evil conversation, just as we see how much custom avails in the defense of false religions. Therefore, Solomon prudently teaches that those contagions are to be avoided and often recalls us to the norm handed down by God, namely, to the prophetic scripture, so that opinions and morals may be ruled by the word of God.

Observe here also the saying concerning providence, Before the eyes of the Lord are all the ways of a man. As in Psalm 32. He sees all the sons of men, etc.

Latin:

DE EXEMPLIS MULTITUDINIS.

IN hoc capite obsernandum est & hoc insigne dictum, de exemplis multitudinis. Pene fui in omni malo, in medio congregationis. Id est, Contagia populi nocent omnibus. Quia teneri & imbecilles animi trahuntur consensu multorum & consuetudine, ut falsas opiniones & vitia multa amplectantur. Et haec mala diuturnitas confirmat, quare postea tanquam res bonae & dulces pertinaciter retinentur. Ut dicitur, Consuetudo est altera natura. Item πᾶν σὺντροφον γλυκύ. Et Aeschylus inquit, In omnibus rebus nihil peius esse prava conversatione, sicut videmus quantum valeat consuetudo in defensione falsarum religionum. Docet igitur Salomon prudenter cavenda esse illa contagia & saepe revocat nos ad normam a Deo traditam, videlicet, ad scripturam propheticam, ut verbo Dei opiniones & mores regantur.

Observa hic & dictum de providentia, Coram oculis Domini omnes viae hominis. Ut Psalmo 32. Videt omnes filios hominum, &c.

## 6:1 Argument
CHAPTER VI.

IN the sixth chapter, the first sentence dissuades from suretyships, narrating that they are dangerous, because very many deceive the sponsors. Therefore, the saying that was written in the Delphic temple has been celebrated, Give a pledge, and harm is at hand. And Homer says.

Δειλαὶ τοῖ δειλῶν γε καὶ ἐγγύαι ἐγγύαασθαι.

But in general, this saying warns that faith in agreements is rare, or that few are sincere and faithful, on the contrary, many are inconstant, unfaithful, perfidious, etc.

But first, let us know concerning the doctrine about God, that one must not believe men asserting something outside the word of God by their own audacity. Then, in other arts, the doctrine is certain, on account of demonstrations and other causes of certainty. Because κριτήρια and demonstrations also are an order divinely instituted, which God wills to be perceived by human minds. But concerning the wills of men and the hope of help, let that virtue which is called candor govern judgment, the extremes of which are calumny and foolish credulity.

Candor is, however, a virtue neighboring to truth, approving the wills of others on account of a probable reason, and not nourishing malevolence, or κακοήθειαν. also bending certain ambiguous things into a better part, and indeed hoping well, but yet, as concerning mutable things, thinking that wills can be changed, and that a man can err concerning the will of another, since the hiding places of the human breast are not thoroughly seen. As David felt well concerning Jonathan, because he has a probable reason, he did not feel well concerning Ahithophel, because he has a probable reason, wherefore he may think his will to be alien to himself. To this opinion it was said 1. Corinth. 13. Love believes all things, That is, it is candid, it is not suspicious, it does not think evil of another without probable and grave reason. But the extremes are vicious, namely calumny, which is most familiar to suspicious natures, because they think evil of many without probable reason, and they invent many false things for themselves. For as they are malevolent concealers, dissemblers, and greedy, so from their own morals they imagine others, they fear snares everywhere, they think others are seeking power for themselves, they deprave the right deeds and ambiguities of others.

These calumnious judgments they call wisdom, prudence, circumspection, and caution, and they admire themselves because of that sharpness, but to these very people often happens the same thing that is said about the fox, The old fox is hardly caught but yet is caught. And many fables about the fox agree with this.

But since it is manifest that suspicious people are malevolent, and reason many things falsely and calumniously, this extreme is opposed both to justice and to truth, and is often the cause of great distractions and wars, as Pompey turned himself away from Julius because of false suspicions.

The other extreme is foolish credulity, which judges nothing, and without probable reason thinks all are faithful, sincere, and firm friends. As it is said, He who believes quickly is light of heart. Furthermore, it also attributes too much to friends who are not bad, It trusts in their wills, as if they were immovable demonstrations, and thinks there are certain and immutable protections, and because of the hope of help it moves great and unnecessary things, as happens almost in all alliances.

This false imagination is also a lie, and against this credulity and the immoderate trust in human protections, many sentences have been handed down divinely. In the Psalm, Do not trust in princes, in the sons of men in whom there is no salvation. Likewise Jeremiah 17. Cursed is he who trusts in man etc. And that most common one, which is cited from Epicharmus νῆφε καὶ μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν ταῦτα ἂρθρα φρενῶν. Which Quintus Cicero rendered thus. That the nerves and joints of wisdom are ἐπιχάρμειον that, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν. These sayings properly tax this extreme, namely foolish credulity believing everyone without probable reason, and trust in the protections of others in doubtful matters.
