Yet these sayings should not be understood as if they approve the other extreme, but the virtue must be retained, which moves between these extremes, whose name is Candor, which is joined with love, it acts with others faithfully, openly, and not πλεονεκτικῶς, It believes because of a probable reason, but in such a way, not that it assents as if to immovable demonstrations, but as to likely ones. David is neither suspicious nor foolishly credulous. He believes Jonathan, when he saw the most grave signs of a sincere heart, because Jonathan willingly yields the kingdom to him. But yet there was not such trust, as if the benevolence were immutable, but he was thinking that. All things are turned, certain loves are turned.

Therefore, in sum, the saying of Solomon warns about avoiding sponsorships, so that one may not believe others without probable and grave reason.

Latin:

CAPUT VI.

IN sexto capite prima sententia disuadet sponsiones, narrans esse periculosas, quia plurimi fallunt sponsores. Ideo celebratum est dictum quod in templo Delphico scriptum fuit, Sponde, damnum praesto est. Et Homerus inquit.

Δειλαὶ τοῖ δειλῶν γε καὶ ἐγγύαι ἐγγύαασθαι.

Sed in genere monet hoc dictum, in raris fidem pactorum esse, seu paucos synceros & fideles esse, Econtra multos inconstantes, infidos, perfidiosos, &c.

Primum autem sciamus de doctrina de Deo, non esse credendum hominibus asseverantibus aliquid extra verbum Dei sua audacia. Deinde in aliis artibus certa est doctrina, propter demonstrationes & alias causas certitudinis. Quia κριτήρια & demonstrationes etiam sunt ordo divinitus institutus, quem vult Deus conspici ab humanis mentibus. Sed de hominum voluntatibus & spe auxilii gubernet iudicium ea virtus quae vocatur candor, cuius extrema sunt calumnia & stulta credulitas.

Est autem Candor virtus vicina veritati, propter probabilem rationem approbans alienas voluntates, & non alens malevolentiam, seu κακοήθειαν. Quaedam etiam ambigua flectens in meliorem partem, ac bene quidem sperans, sed tamen ut de rebus mutabilibus, cogitans posse mutari voluntates, & posse hominem errare de aliena voluntate, cum non penitus aspiciantur latebrae humani pectoris. Ut David bene sensit de Ionatha, quia habet probabilem rationem, non bene sensit de Achitophel, quia probabilem rationem habet, quare illius voluntatem a se alienam esse existimet. In hanc sententiam dictum est 1. Corinth. 13. Dilectio credit omnia, Id est, est candida, non est suspicax, non male sentit de alio sine probabili & gravi ratione. Extrema autem sunt vitiosa, videlicet calumnia, quae familiarissima est naturis suspicacibus, quia male sentiunt de plurimis sine probabili ratione, & multa falsa sibi ipsis fingunt. Ut enim sunt malevoli ocultatores, fucati, pleonectici, ita ex suis moribus fingunt alios, metuunt ubique insidias, putant alios sibi potentiam quaerere, depravant aliorum recte facta & ambigua.

Haec calumniosa iudicia vocant sapientiam, prudentiam, circumspectionem, & cautionem, seque propter illud acumen admirantur, sed his ipsis saepe accidit idem, quod de vulpe dicitur, Vetula vulpes vix capitur sed tamen capitur. Et congruunt huc multi Apologi de vulpe.

Cum autem manifestum sit suspicaces malevolos esse, & multa falso & culumniose ratiocinari, hoc extremum oppositum est & iusticiae & veritati, & saepe causa est magnarum distractinum & bellorum, ut Pompeius avertit se a lulio propter falsas suspiciones.

Alterum extremum est stulta credulitas, quae nihil iudicat, & sine probabili ratione omnes existimat esse fideles, sinceros & firmos amicos. Ut dicitur. Qui cito credit, levis est corde. Deinde & amicis non malis nimium tribuit, Confidit eorum voluntatibus, tanquam demonstrationibus immotis, & putat esse certa & immutabilia praesidia, & spe auxilii res magnas & non necessarias movet, ut accidit fere in omnibus foederibus.

Est & haec falsa imaginatio mendacium, & contra hanc credu litatem & fiduciam humanorum praesidiorum immoderatam, multae sententiae divinitus traditae sunt. In Psal. Nolite confidere in principibus, in filiis hominum in quibus non est salus. Item leremiae. 17. Maledictus qui confidit in homine &c. Et illud usitatissimum, quod ex Epicharmo citatur νῆφε καὶ μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν ταῦτα ἂρθρα φρενῶν. Quod ita reddidit. Quintus Cicero. Nervos atque artus esse sapientiae, ἐπιχάρμειον illud, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν. Haec dicta proprie taxant hoc extremum, videlicet stultam credulitatem sine probabili ratione omnibus credentem, & fiduciam alienorum praesidiorum in rebus dubiis. Nec tamen ita intelligantur haec dicta, quasi alterum extremum approbent, sed retienda est virtus, quae inter haec extrema versatur, cui nomen est Candor, qui cum dilectione coniunctus est, agit cum aliis fideliter, aperte, & non πλεονεκτικῶς, Credit propter probabilem rationem, sed ita, non ut assentiatur tanquam immotis demonstrationibus, sed ut verisimilibus. David nec suspicax est nec stulte credulus. Credit autem lonathae, cum gravissima signa sinceri pectoris videret, quia Ionathas volens ei de regno cedit. Sed tamen non erat fiducia talis, quasi benevolentia immutabilis esset, sed cogitabat illud. Omnia vertuntur certi vertuntur amores.

Monet igitur in summa dictum Salomonis de vitandis sponsionibus, ne sine probabili & gravi ratione aliis credatur.

## 6:1 On Labor
ON LABOR.

IN this sixth chapter the second precept is about labor, which he also illustrates by an example . He commands us to look at the ants, and to imitate their industry. First, therefore, let us learn here that it is a command of God that each one should perform the labors of his own vocation, and indeed show industry in them, be diligent and faithful, as Paul says. Let each one labor etc. Likewise, let us be ambitious in this, that we may do our own things. The name of this virtue is industry, which is to will constantly and firmly for the sake of God and the common salvation to perform labors, suitable to one's vocation, as David is an industrious soldier, Paul is an industrious doctor.

The extremes are sloth, as Sardanapalus is a slothful king; the other extreme is πολυπραγμοσὺνη,, which is doing many unnecessary things or πάρεργα, and intruding oneself into the vocations of others, as Achas, Pericles, and Demosthenes, by doing unnecessary things, provided an occasion for pernicious wars. Thus, it is most common for the Church to be disturbed by unnecessary disputes or contentions, or when Doctors intrude themselves into civil affairs, as if having one foot in the court and the other in the temple. Let this suffice to have said briefly concerning the sum of the precept; now let us consider the ants.

God scattered images of virtues and vices in living creatures, so that they might be as it were pictures, which might invite us to virtue and dissuade us from vices. An honest school, in which the arts are taught in order, and there are some faithful and useful Doctors, is similar to hives, in which bees perform their tasks vigorously. A diligent Doctor is like a lively bee; the slothful are like drones, who, although they do not labor, yet eat together, but they brood like bees; but hornets are not only thieves, but also robbers, who devour the bees. Thus a tyrant is similar to a wolf.

In ants, as in bees, these images of virtues exist. The first is similar to prudence, because they collect and store up. Also, because they are astrologers, they cease for some days and nights around the conjunction of the moon and the sun. Conversely, at the full moon, they perform their daily and nightly tasks. Also, because they make little caverns, and because they bring out grains dampened by rains, so that when dried they do not rot. Then, it is of justice that they contribute to the common good without envy, and bring help to those overly burdened in the labor itself.

Fortitude is that they show assiduity in labor. Parsimony is that they frugally enjoy what has been produced, and guard the rest. Nor are they useless to men, but medicines are taken from ants, such as for wounds, and eggs instilled into the ears provide a cure.

Solomon, however, exaggerates the example, saying, The ant without an overseer shows diligence in its tasks, when men can be driven neither by laws nor by commands to perform their own tasks. In which description he deplores human misery. It is to be lamented that men alone do not obey the judgment divinely implanted in nature, nor maintain the order for which nature was founded, since other living creatures do not dissent from their own nature. But after nature in the fall of Adam cast off God as its ruler, this stubbornness of heart followed, fighting with the judgment of the mind, and such is the infirmity of the whole nature of men that even excellent talents often loosen the reins to desires and rush into shameful acts and sad falls, as Hercules, David, and countless others. Nor are the causes small why the Son of God assumed human nature; it is not a light disease, nor is the physician to be spurned. Let us therefore acknowledge the magnitude of our infirmity, and let us seek help from the Son of God, and let us strive that we may diligently govern our discipline.

Latin:

DE LABORE.

IN hoc sexto capite secundum praeceptum est de labore, quod exemplo etiam illustrat. Iubet nos intueri formicas, & earum sedulitatem imitari. Primum igitur hic discamus, mandatum Dei esse ut suae vocationis labores quisque faciat, & quidem in his praestet sedulitatem, sit diligens & fidelis, ut Paulus inquit. Laborer unusquisque &c. Item in hoc ambitiosi simus, ut propria faciamus. Huic virtuti nomen est sed ulitas, quae est constanter & firmiter velle propter Deum & communem salutem, labores facere, vocationi convenientes, ut David est sedulus miles, Paulus est sedulus doctor.

Extrema sunt ignavia, ut Sardanapalus est ignavus rex, alterum extremum est πολυπραγμοσὺνη, quae est multa non necessaria seu πάρεργα facere, & alienis se vocationibus ingerere, ut Achas, Pericles, Demosthenes, non necessaria agendo perniciosis bellis occasionem praebuerunt. Ita usitatissimum est turbari Ecclesiam non necessariis disputationibus, aut contentionibus, aut cum ingerunt se Doctores civilibus negociis, tanquam alterum pedem in curia, alterum in templo habentes. Haec de summa praecepti satis sit breviter dixisse, Nunc formicas consideremus.

Deus sparsit simulacra virtutum & vitiorum in animantibus, ut essent velut picturae, quae nos ad virtutem invitarent, & dehortarentur a vitiis. Honesta schola, in qua ordine traduntur artes, & sunt aliqui fideles & utiles Doctores, est similis alueolis, in quibus apes vegerae operas faciunt. Sedulus Doctor est veluti alacris apis, Ignavi sunt ut fuci, qui cum non laborent, tamen una pascuntur, sed incubant ut apes, At cabrones non tantum sunt fures, sed etiam latrones, qui devorant apes. Sic tyrannus est similis lupo.

In formicis ut in apibus haec virtutum simulacra sunt. Primum prudentiae simile est, quod convehunt & comparcunt. Item quod sunt Astrologae, cessant diebus ac noctibus aliquot circa coniunctionem lunae & solis. Econtra plena luna faciunt operas diurnas & docturnas. Item quod cavernulas faciunt, quod madefacta grana pluviis proferunt, ut siccata non putrescant. Deinde iusticiae est, quod in commune conferunt sine invidia, & in ipso labore nimium oneratis opem ferunt.

Fortitudo est, quod praestant assiduitatem in labore. Parsimonia, quod parto frugaliter fruuntur, & reliquum custodiunt. Nec sunt inutiles hominibus, sed medicamenta ex formicis sumuntur, ut vulnerum, Et ova instillata auribus medentur.

Exaggerat autem Salomon exemplum, inquiens, Formica sine exactore operarum praestat sedulitatem, cum homines nec legibus nec imperiis adigi possint, ut faciant suas operas. In qua descriptione deplorat humanam miseriam. Dolendum est solos homines non parere iudicio divinitus insito naturae, nec tueri ordinem ad quem natura condita est, cum caetera animantia non dissentiant a sua natura. Sed postquam in lapsu Adae natura excussit rectorem Deum, secuta est haec contumatia cordis, pugnans cum iudicio mentis, tantaque infirmitas est universae naturae hominum, ut etiam praestantia ingenia saepe laxent frenos cupiditatibus, & ruant in turpitudines, & tristes casus, ut Hercules, David, & alii innumerabiles. Nec parvae causae sunt, cur Filius Dei assumpserit humanam naturam, non levis morbus est, nec medicus aspernandus, Agnoscamus igitur magnitudinem nostrae infirmitatis, & a filio Dei auxilium petamus, & annitamur, ut disciplinam diligenter regamus.

## 6:1 Concerning the Authors of Contentions. The Rash Man
CONCERNING THE AUTHORS OF CONTENTIONS. The rash man.

He still dwells on the precept concerning diligence. And as he previously described sloth snoring and doing nothing, so now he describes the contrary vice, τὴν πολυπραγμοσύνην,, which is, with necessary things neglected that pertain to one's vocation, to do unnecessary things and to intrude oneself into the affairs of others. This is a common vice of many, but most pernicious in governors, many of whom, having omitted their own duty, seek unnecessary business and move many things at once, and disturb the republic and the Church without necessary causes. Such is in Greek history Cleon; the Old Comedy thus attacks Pericles, because when he could easily to make peace on conditions, yet φιλονεικία and such great stubbornness existed, that he did not want to seem to yield. Thus the φιλονεικια of Lysander is accused, who stirred up the Theban war, which was not necessary.

And here he described the Polypragmonic with beautiful figures, he says it is sophistic, and to seek quarrels. These two things are most truly in all Polypragmonics, just as it is written about Agesilaus, that he was accustomed to undermine treaties and pacifications ὑπορύττειν. Therefore he says here, this man is rash, that is, not remaining within the boundaries of his office, to speak perverse things. That is, to devise tricks and sophisms, for disturbing business. And there are certain sophistic natures, which know how to pervert even things said most simply and most correctly, and to corrupt them with some stratagem, and they are by nature quarrelsome, therefore they stir up great tumults in the Republic.

But all these things are seen more and do more harm in the Church, where great and sad lacerations arise from these vices, when either false or unnecessary struggles are moved, by a desire for contentions, and are defended by the masks of sophisms, as our age, alas, has seen and sees many examples. The Greek text rendered the sentence with illustrious words, ἐν παντὶ καιρῷ ὁ τοιοῦτος ταραχὰς συνίστησι At every time, or by whatever occasion such a one excites huge tumults for the city, just as it is said about Pericles, κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἑλλάδα.

The gestures of such Clamorers are also feigned here, that in speaking they gesticulate with their fingers and make noise with their feet, just as they write that Cleon was the first accustomed in public assemblies to shout, to tear his garment, to strike his thigh, to make noise with his feet, and to run about on the pulpit.

Let us, however, diligently observe the vituperations of these vices, so that we may cast bridles upon ourselves, and follow the precept of Paul, In this let us be ambitious, that we may be tranquil, and do our own things, that is, things necessary and convenient to our vocation.

But soon he adds to this precept a vituperation of neighboring vices, which are universally in the quarrelsome, pride, sophistry, cruelty, and contentiousness. Therefore he says these six the Lord hates, but the seventh is an abomination before the Lord.

After he instituted the description of the restless citizen and quarrelsome person, first he attributes pride or ambition to him. And although the description of pride lies wide open in the doctrine of the Church, and can be understood with difficulty, yet here mention is made of foolish and Thrasonic ambition, which can easily be recognized. And although all proud people cloak their vice with this honorable title, that they seek glory, yet the rule must be held. That which is just, you shall pursue justly. Furthermore, the proud and ambitious do not seek true glory, but the shadow of glory and a lie. It is necessary, however, to hold the definition of true glory. Glory is the approval of a conscience judging rightly, and of others judging rightly.

That this glory is to be sought, Paul also teaches, saying. This is our boasting, the testimony of our conscience. Item to the Galatians. Let each one test his own work and thus he will have glory in himself and not from others. That is, let him do the duties of his vocation rightly, and consider them diligently, and if he has done them faithfully and diligently, he will have a good conscience, and will be adorned with the testimonies of the better ones judging rightly.

This glory is true and to be sought, about which Paul says, You will have glory within yourself. Then what he adds, not from others, he prohibits depending on the applause of those not judging rightly, he prohibits also seeking glory by the detraction of others. The virtue therefore which keeps itself within boundaries in this kind, in Latin custom is called modesty, namely, not to attribute more to oneself than according to one's strength, nor to undertake anything outside one's vocation, nor to despise others, but in the fear of God and faith diligently to perform the duties of your vocation while maintaining a Geometric proportion, so that you do not impede the course of others who serve rightly in their own place. As Jonathan is modest, and neither desires the kingdom nor impedes David's vocation. The opposite vice is Ambition.

Impudence is to despise the judgments of God and of all men, and without regard for the judgment of one's own conscience, or of other sound men who do not judge foolishly, to loosen the reins on all desires, or to bend oneself to every opportunity, from wherever gain is shown. It has been said of such people.

He is light, nor does the honor of glory stir him, Or as Catullus says. And the rumors of the more severe old men, Let us value them all at a single penny.

The other extreme is ambition, which is depicted in Thraso, who attributes more to himself than is in accordance with his strengths, or as Antony desires great things outside his vocation, and neither understands the duties of his own vocation, nor strives to perform them rightly. Therefore, in the Greek verse of Menander concerning the desire for glory, precepts about circumstances have been added.

Pursue glory, neither all of it nor too much; do not hunt for empty glory, nor for new.

It is, however, universal for ambitious people to sow discords of one kind or another, because they do not remain within the boundaries of their own place, and they desire things greater than their place, and they attempt to press others down. Therefore it is said below, Among the proud there is contention, just as when Pompey is burned by the glory of Julius, Antony wants to rule alone with Augustus cast off. The little verses are well known.

There is no faith in the partners of a kingdom, and all power, Will be impatient of a consort. Item. All things are a care to men as to who should be the commander.

But when discords have been moved, one fights either by sophistry or by manifest violence, or in both ways; therefore he here attributes to this ambitious person a perverse tongue, that is, sophistry and cruelty. Examples are everywhere obvious in the whole of life, for both princes and private individuals disturb a well-constituted state with ambitions.

Although discord is very pernicious in empires, yet it is much more pernicious in the Church . Arius, grieving that he was not made Bishop, stirs up a struggle concerning the Son of God. And this evil has always spread in the Church, and will spread, namely, emulation among colleagues, the source of which is pride or ambition. For emulation is to fight about preeminence, just as Cain grieves that his brother is preferred, Aaron the holy man is burned by the glory of Moses. Jason snatches the high priesthood from his brother Onias. Concerning such evils he says here that God hates the ambitious and that these, armed with sophistry and cruelty, disturb the Church.

There is, however, a singular emphasis when he says that God abominates the brawler, the one who sows discords, for πάθος of abomination signifies a more vehement hatred. But the precepts concerning the pursuit of Concord have been repeated often. And the Son of God in his agony praying for the Church asks for these three things: that truth may remain in the Church, that discords may not arise among the teachers, and that eternal life may be given to the Church. And Paul says, Ephe. 4. Being diligent to preserve the unity of the spirit. Let us obey these precepts, and let us prudently take care that we do not disturb the concord of the Church of God.

But here it is asked again, if concord is not to be disturbed, will it not be permitted to rebuke errors and faults? when yet it has been said, If anyone loves father or mother more than me, etc. Item. I have not come to send peace, but a sword, etc.

Concerning this question let us know that, just as with other virtues, so the pursuit of concord is between vicious extremes. The extremes are adulation and φιλονεικία.. Between these is the mean . The virtue is the pursuit of concord, which is justice constantly taking care that one does not fight over uses that are not very necessary, lest the Church be distracted, or invocation be disturbed, and wars and devastations follow. And it is necessary wisdom to see where one must fight, and where anger must be curbed. These divine rules govern this wisdom. Flee idols. Item, If anyone teaches another Gospel, let him be anathema. Item, If anyone denies me before men, I will deny him before the heavenly Father. Item, It is necessary to obey God rather than men. Item in Ezekiel, If you do not speak, I will require his soul from your hand, etc. The vice of flattery fights against these rules, which approves manifest idols, not light errors, and foul crimes for the sake of the powerful. These evils must be censured, according to the rules which I have just reviewed. But the contrary vice is φιλονεικία contentiousness, or monstrosity of nature, or moroseness, or ambition, or for whatever cause, to fight about false or unnecessary things, or light errors, because among the good who hold the foundation, some infirmity must be tolerated. And here that saying is valid. κακόν οὐ κείμενον μή κινητὲον.

But let the younger ones also know this, since it is difficult to maintain the mean, that it is closer to virtue to do less than to exceed. As Isocrates says. Just as in geometric proportion, a smaller number is closer to the middle than a larger one. 8. 4. 2.

Latin:

DE CONTENTIONUM AUTORIBUS. Homo temerarius.

Adhuc commoratur in praecepto de sedulitate. Ac ut antea descripsit ignaviam stertentem, & nihil agentem, ita nunc contrarium vitium describit, τὴν πολυπραγμοσύνην, quae est neglectis necessariis, quae ad vocationem pertinent, facere non necessaria, & ingerere se alienis negociis. Commune vitium est hoc multorum, sed maxime perniciosum in gubernatoribus, quorum multi omisso proprio munere, quaerunt negotia non necessaria, & multa simul movent, & turbant sine necessariis causis rempublicam & Ecclesiam. Talis est in Graeca historia. Cleon, Vetus comoedia & Periclem sic exagitat, quod cum facile posset aequis conditionibus pacem facere, tamen φιλονεικία & pertinatia tanta fuerit, ut noluerit videri cedere. Ita Lysandri φιλονεικια accusatur, qui movit Thebanum bellum non necessarium.

Et hic pulchris figuris descripsit Polypragmonicum, dicit esse sophisticum, & quaerere rixas. Haec duo verissime sunt in omnibus Polypragmonicis sicut de Agesilao scriptum est, solitum foedera & pacificationis suffodere ὑπορύττειν. Ideo hic dicit, hunc virum temerarium, Id est, non manentem intra metas sui officii loqui perversa. Id est, tegnas & sophismata excogitare, ad turbanda negocia. Et sunt quaedam naturae sophisticae, quae etiam simplicissime & rectissime dicta norunt pervertere, & aliqua stropha corrumpere, & caedem naturae sunt rixatrices, ideo cient magnos tumultus in Republica.

Sed haec omnia magis conspiciuntur & plus nocent in Ecclesia, ubi magnae & tristes dilacerationes oriuntur ex his vitiis, cum aut falsa aut non necessaria certamina moventur, cupiditate contentionum, & fucis sophismatum defenduntur, Ut nostra aetas, proh dolor, multa vidit & videt exempla. Graecus textus illustribus verbis reddidit sententiam, ἐν παντὶ καιρῷ ὁ τοιοῦτος ταραχὰς συνίστησι Omni tempore, seu quacunque occasione talis ingentes tumultus civitati excitat, sicut de Pericle dictum est, κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἑλλάδα.

Gestus hic etiam finguntur talium Clamatorum, quod in dicendo digitis gesticulentur, & strepant pedibus, sicut scribunt Cleonem primum in concionibus solitum vociferari, lacerare vestem, percutere femur, strepere pedibus, & discurrere in pulpito.

Diligenter autem vituperationes horum vitiorum observemus, ut nobis ipsi frenos iniiciamus, & praeceptum Pauli sequamur, In hoc simus ambitiosi, ut simus tranquilli, & propria faciamus, id est, necessaria, convenientia vocationi.

Mox autem huic praecepto addit vituperationem vicinorum vitiorum, quae universaliter sunt in rixosis, superbiae, sophistices, crudelitatis & contentiositatis. Ideo dicit sex ista odit Dominus, septimum autem abominatio est coram Domino.

Postquam instituit descriptionem inquieti civis & rixatoris, primum tribuit ei superbiam vel ambitionem. Etsi autem in doctrina Ecclesiae late patet descriptio superbiae, & difficulter intelligi potest, tamen hic mentio fit stolidae & Trasonicae ambitionis, quae facile agnosci potest. Et quanquam superbi omnes pretexant suo vitio hunc honestum titulum, quod gloriam appetant, tamen tenenda est regula. Quod iustum est, iuste persequaris. Praeteria superbi & ambitiosi non appetunt veram gloriam, sed umbram gloriae & mendacium. Necesse est autem verae gloriae definitionem tenere. Gloria est approbatio conscientiae recte iudicantis, & aliorum recte iudicantium.

Hanc gloriam appetendam esse & Paulus docet, inquiens. Haec est gloriatio nostra, testimonium conscientiae nostrae. Item ad Gal. Probet opus suum unusquisque & sic gloriam habebit in seipso & non ex aliis. Id est, suae vocationis officia recte faciat, & ea diligenter consideret, ac si fideliter & diligenter ea fecerit, habebit bonam conscientiam, & ornabitur testimoniis meliorum recte iudicantium.

Haec gloria vera est & appetenda, de qua dicit Paulus, Habebis gloriam apud te ipsum. Deinde quod addit non ex aliis, prohibet pendere ex applausu non recte iudicantium, prohibet item quaerere gloriam obtrectatione aliorum. Virtus igitur quae se intra metas continet in hoc genere, latina consuetudine vocatur modestia, videlicet, non sibi plus tribuere, quam pro viribus, nec extra vocationem aliquid suscipere, nec despicere alios, sed in timore Dei & fide diligenter facere officia vocationis servata proportione Geometrica, ne aliorum cursum, qui suo loco recte serviunt, impedias. Ut Ionathas modestus est, nec appetit regnum, nec impedit Davidis vocationem. Vicium oppositum est Ambitio.

Impudentia est contemnere Dei & omnium hominum iudicia, & sine respectui iudicii conscientiae propriae, aut aliorum sanorum non stulte iudicantium omnibus cupiditatibus frenos laxare, vel flectere se ad omnes occasiones, undecunque lucrum ostenditur. De talibus dictum est.

Levis est, nec illum stimulat gloriae decus,Vel ut Catullus inquit.Rumoresque senum severiorum,Omnes unius aestimemus assis.

Alterum extremum est ambitio, quae pingitur in Thrasone, qui sibi plus tribuit, quam pro viribus, vel ut Antonius magna appetit extra vocationem, & suae vocationis officia nec intelligit, nec recte studet facere. Ideo in Graeco versu Menandri de appetitione gloriae addita sunt praecepta de circumstantiis.

δόξαν δίωκε, μήτ' ἅπασαν μήτ' ἄγανμή τὴν κενήν θήρσυε, μήτε τὴν νέαν.

Universale est autem ambitiosos serere discordias alios in alio genere, quia non manent intra metas sui loci, & appetunt maiora sua loco, & alios premere conantur. Ideo dicitur infra, Inter superbos est contentio, ut cum Pompeius uritur gloria lulii, Antonius solus dominari vult excusso Agusto. Noti sunt versiculi.

Nulla fides regni sociis, omnisque potestas,Impatiens consortis erit.Item.Omnia cura viris uter esset induperator.

Motis autem discordiis, aut sophistica, aut manifesta violentia, vel utroque modo pugnatur, ideo hic tribuit huic ambitioso linguam perversam, id est, sophisticam & crudelitatem. Exempla in tota vita ubique obvia sunt, Nam & principes & privati turbant bene constitutum statum ambitionibus.

Quanquam autem in imperiis valde perniciosa est discordia, tamen Ecclesiae multo perniciosior est. Arrius dolens se non factum esse Episcopum, movet certamen de Filio Dei. Et semper in Ecclesia hoc malum grassatum est, & grassabitur, videlicet, aemulatio inter collegas, cuius fons est superbia vel ambitio. Est enim aemulatio pugnare de antecellentia, ut Cain dolet anteferri fratrem, Aaron vir sanctus uritur gloria Moysi. Iason eripit summum sacerdotium Oniae fratri. De talibus malis hic dicit, Deum odisse ambitiosos & hos inquit sophistica & crudelitate armatos turbare Ecclesiam.

Est autem singularis emphasis cum inquit, Deum abominari rixatorem, serentem discordias, Nam πάθος abominationis significat vehementius odium. Saepe autem repetita sunt praecepta de studio Concordiae. Et Filius Dei in agone orans pro Ecclesia haec tria petit, ut maneat veritas in Ecclesia, ne inter doctores discordiae oriantur, & ut Eccleisae detur vita aeterna. Et Paulus inquit, Ephe. 4. Solliciti servare unitatem spiritus. His praeceptis obtemperemus, & prudenter caveamus, ne turbemus concordiam Ecclesiae Dei.

Sed hic rursus quaeritur, si non est turbanda concordia, non licebit reprehendere errores & delicta? cum tamen dictum sit, Si quis plus diligit patrem aut matrem quam me &c. Item. Non veni mittere pacem, sed gladium &c.

De hac quaestione sciamus, ut caeteras virtutes, ita studium concordiae inter extrema vitiosa esse. Extrema sunt adulatio & φιλονεικία. Inter haec est medis virtus studium concordiae, quod est iusticia constanter cavens ne pugnet de usare non valde necessaria, ne Ecclesia distrahatur, aut turbetur invocatio, & sequantur bella & vastationes. Et necessaria sapientia est, videre ubi pugnandum sit, & ubi frenanda sit iracundia. Hanc sapientiam regunt hae regulae divinae. Fugito idola. Item, Si quis aliud Evangelium docet anathema sit. Item, Si quis negaverit me coram hominibus, negabo eum coram patre caelesti, Item, Oportet Deo magis obedire quam hominibus. Item apud Ezechielem, Si non dixeris, requiram animam eius de manu tua, &c. Cum his regulis pugnat vitium adulatio, quae approbat in gratiam potentum manifesta idola, errores non leves, & tetra delicta. Haec mala taxari necesse est, iuxta regulas quas iam recensui, Sed vitium contrarium est φιλονεικία pugnacitate, vel monstrositate naturae, vel morositate, vel ambitione, vel quacunque de causa, pugnare de falsis aut non necessariis rebus, aut levibus erroribus, quia in bonis qui fundamentum tenent, aliqua infirmitas toleranda est. Et hic valet illud. κακόν οὐ κείμενον μή κινητὲον.

Iuniores autem & hoc sciant, cum dissicile sit mediocritatem tueri, Propius virtutem esse minus facere, quam excedere. Ut inquit Isocrates. Sicut in Geometrica proportione, minor numerus propior est medio quam maior. 8. 4. 2.

## 7:1 Argument
CHAPTER VII. ON CHASTITY.

The remaining part of the sixth chapter, and the whole seventh Chapter, preach about chastity, which the Church knows to be a necessary virtue by divine command. The heathens, because they had lost the doctrine handed down by the voice of God, imagined this virtue to be less necessary, and permitted vague, multiple lusts which were followed by horrible punishments; but yet some, however much reminded by the light of nature, saw that this virtue was a great honor. Whence these verses are of the old Poet Bacchylides, whose Greek sentiment the Emperor Julian is said to have often recited,

As when a beautiful face is removed from a statue, the trunk has no beauty in the remaining body, so the remaining morals will remain spurned without honor, unless they are adorned by the praise of modesty.

I. But let us most firmly retain the commands of God, which teach both what chastity is that pleases God, and which prohibits all vague lusts.

Chastity is either to piously observe the laws of marriage handed down divinely. According to that saying, They shall be two in one flesh, That is, one male and one female inseparably joined, Or it is to live celibate by a singular gift without any commixture, and without any contamination of the body.

II. Then let us know that this virtue is necessary, and indeed commanded by the express voice of God in Paradise, where God immediately distinguished marriages from the vague commixture of beasts, That the human race might learn to understand chastity, and might think that God is a chaste mind, and might serve Him in this obedience. For by this voice, They shall be two in one flesh, That is, inseparably joined, all vague lusts are prohibited, as can be easily understood.

III. Thirdly, let us consider the wrath of God, declared against wandering lusts both by many sentences and examples of all times. Because it is most certain that in this life also, individuals who pollute themselves are punished by public and private calamities, whether more or less according to the magnitude of the offenses, as David is horribly punished on account of adultery and the other sins that followed; princes are hanged in Israel on account of whoredom, as chapter 25 in Numbers narrates; almost the whole tribe of Benjamin is destroyed on account of the defiled wife of the Levite. How sad were the punishments of the Sodomites, the Canaanites, Oedipus, the Ptolemies, and very many who were polluted by incestuous custom.

Let us know, therefore, that the universal rule is: God will punish fornicators and adulterers. And Deut. 27. Cursed is he who has intercourse with the wife of his neighbor. And 1. Corinth. 6. Do not err, fornicators, adulterers, etc., shall not possess the kingdom of God, etc. To the Ephesians and Romans, he signifies that lusts are a singular cause of great blindness and a punishment for the worship of idols, just as monstrous lusts have for the most part accompanied the worship of idols. And now the state of the mass-priests, who defend impious worship of idols, is greatly contaminated with obscenity. And let there be a certain norm: It is impossible for these to be the Church of God, who, having been admonished, pertinaciously defend lusts, because Paul manifestly affirms that devils are the authors of the prohibition of marriage ordained by God.

Therefore, let both the commandments of God, and the sayings about punishments, and the examples of punishments always be in sight, and let us render obedience to God in maintaining chastity, which must be done with even greater care because a conscience having the purpose of sinning cannot call upon God, as is said concerning those not doing penance: God does not hear sinners. Furthermore, let us love chastity all the more, so that in prayer, addressing God, we may distinguish Him from devils by the thought of chastity. For since we do not perceive God with the eyes of the body, it is necessary that His manifestations and properties be thought of by mind and faith, so that He may be distinguished from creatures and from devils. But in order that we may be able to maintain chastity, the occasions of allurements must be avoided, and the incitements of lusts, idleness, gluttony, evil companionships, etc. The eyes must be governed, as is said here in the text also concerning those incitements.

Latin:

CAPUT VII. DE CASTITATE.

Reliqua pars sexti capitis, & septimum Caput totum concionantur de castitate, quam Ecclesia scit ex mandato divino necessariam virtutem esse. Ethnici, quia amiserant doctrinam traditam voce Dei, minus necessarium esse hanc virtutem imaginati sunt, & permiserunt vagas libidines multiplices quas secutae sunt poenae horrendae, sed tamen aliqui, utcunque luce naturae commonefacti, viderunt magnum decus esse hanc virtutem, Unde hi versus sunt veteris Poetae Bacchylidis, quorum Graecam sententiam Iulianus imperator saepe recitasse dicitur,

Ut cum de statua facies formosa remota est,Non decus in reliquo corpore truncus habet,Sic reliqui mores spreti sine honore manebunt.Ni sint ornati laude pudiciciae.

I. Sed nos firmissime retineamus mandata Dei, quae docent, & quae sit castitas Deo placens, & prohibens omnes vagas libidines.

Est autem castitas, aut leges coniugii divinitus traditas pie servare. Iuxta illud, Erunt duo in carnem unam, Id est, unus mas & una femina inseparabiliter iuncti, Aut est singulari dono sine commixtione ulla, & sine contaminatione corporis caelibem vivere.

II. Deinde sciamus hanc virtutem necessariam esse, & quidem expressa voce Dei mandatam in Paradiso, ubi statim distinxit Deus coniugia, a vagcommixtione bestiarum, Ut genus humanum disceret intelligere castitatem, & cogitaret Deum mentem esse castam, & in hac obedientia ei serviret. Nam hac voce, Erunt duo in carnem unam, Id est, inseparabiliter iuncti, omnes vagae libidines prohibentur, ut facile intelligi potest.

III. Tertio iram Dei consideremus, adversus vagas libidines declaratam & multis sententiis & exemplis omnium temporum. Quia certissimum est & in hac vita publicis & privatis calamitatibus puniri singulos, qui se polluunt, seu magis seu minus pro magnitudine delictorum, ut David horribiliter propter adulterium & caetera peccata quae accesserunt, punitur, Suspenduntur principes in Israel propter scortationem, ut narrat caput. 25. in Numeris, fere tota tribus Beniamin deletur propter stupratam Levitae coniugem, Quam tristes poenae fuerunt Sodomorum, Cananaeorum, Oedipi, Ptolemaeorum, & plurimorum qui incesta consuetudine polluti fuerunt.

Sciamus igitur regulam universalem esse, Scortatores & adulteros puniet Deus. Et Deut. 27. Maledictus qui habet consuetudinem cum uxore proximi sui. Et 1. Corinth. 6. Nolite errare, scortatores, adulteri &c. non possidebunt regnum Dei &c. Ad Ephesios & Romanos significat libidines singularem causam esse magnae caecitatis & poenam cultus idolorum, sicut plerumque ad cultum idolorum accesserunt portentosae libidines. Et nunc sacrificulorum status, qui defendunt impios cultus idolorum, valde contaminatus est obscoenitate. Sitque norma certa, Impossibile est hos Ecclesiam Dei esse, qui admoniti pertinaciter defendunt libidines, quia Paulus manifeste affirmat. Diabolos esse autores prohibitionis coniugii a Deo ordinati.

Semper itaque in conspectu sint & mandata Dei, & dicta de poenis, & exempla poenarum, Et Deo obedientiam praestemus in tuenda castitate, quod eo etiam maiore cura faciendum est, quia conscientia habens propositum delinquendi, non potest invocare Deum, Ut dicitur de non agentibus paenitentiam. Peccatores non exaudit Deus. Deinde & eo magis amemus castitatem, ut in precatione alloquentes Deum cogitatione castitatis discernamus eum a Diabolis. Cum enim non cernamus Deum oculis corporis, necesse est mente & fide cogitari ipsius patefactiones & proprietates, ut a creaturis & a Diabolis discernatur. Ut autem tueri castitatem possimus, vitandae sunt illecebrarum occasiones, & irritamenta libidinum, ocium, helluationes, mala sodalatia, &c. Regendi oculi, ut hic in textu etiam de irritamentis illis dicitur.

## 8:1 Argument
CHAPTER VIII. AND IX.

Up to this point he has repeated the doctrine of the Decalogue; now he again adds a general exhortation to learn wisdom, that is, to learn the word of God. For when he names wisdom, he does not understand the craftiness by which men seek and maintain wealth, power, or similar advantages or protections, but he recalls human minds to the one eternal norm of the divine mind expressed in the word by which God has manifested Himself; he wishes our judgments, counsels, morals, actions, and life to conform to this norm, and he calls this one norm wisdom, as he said above, The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom. And here, The fear of the Lord hates evil.

He binds us, therefore, to the acknowledgment of this God who manifested Himself in Israel; He wishes this One to be acknowledged, invoked, feared, and He wishes us to obey Him. He names this wisdom, which He wishes us to retain and prefer to all human opinions, nor does He wish us to be led away from it by favorable or adverse things.

He adds, therefore, promises which teach that the outcomes of this wisdom are prosperous and happy, even if often many things must be endured, but the of this wisdom struggle through. the hearers at last from calamities. And this very wisdom is salutary to the hearers themselves, and to the Church, and to the rest of men. Therefore he says, With me is counsel and success, Item, I am intelligence, and with me is efficacy, or victory, That is, where the word of God governs counsels, successes are prosperous, and wisdom is happy for the Church, and for all nations, and will be victorious, even if for a little while great hardships must be borne.

And this passage is to be diligently considered, concerning the distinction of happy and unhappy wisdom, Pericles, Demosthenes, Crassus, Pompey, and many similar ones, were wise, That is, they saw the arts of increasing power, but this wisdom was unhappy for themselves and the citizens. And for the most part the errors of those who excel most in wisdom are notable, because pride and πολυπραγμοσύνη bring evils upon themselves and others. But David, Hezekiah, Jeremiah, and some similar ones, were both wise and happy, and remained within the boundaries of their calling and the divine word. Let us therefore consider the distinction of happy and unhappy wisdom. And let us pray, that God may make us vessels of mercy, and that we may be vessels of mercy, let us know that we must remain within the boundaries of the word of God and our calling.

After he has distinguished between happy and unhappy wisdom, he adds the saying. By me Kings reign, &c. Signifying that the Church, empires, and salutary affairs are thus constituted, when minds are governed by this wisdom, of which he speaks, And that counsels, however specious, which are undertaken without divine light by human curiosity or desire, are not salutary. Just as the counsel of Crassus and Pompey was unhappy.

Latin:

CAPUT VIII. ET IX.

Hactenus repetivit doctrinam Decologi, nunc rursus addit generalem adhortationem, ad discendam sapientiam, id est, ad discendum verbum Dei. Cum enim nominat sapientiam, non intelligit calliditatem, qua homines quaerunt & tuentur opes, potentiam, aut similia commoda vel praesidia, sed revocat mentes humanas ad unam aeternam normam mentis divinae expressam in verbo, quo se patefecit Deus, huic normae vult congruere nostra iudicia, consilia, mores, actiones & vitam, & hanc unam norman vocat sapientiam, ut supra dixit, Timor Domini initium sapientiae. Et hic, Timor Domini odit malum.

Alligat nos igitur ad agnitionem huius Dei, qui se in Israel patefecit, hunc vult agnosci, invocari, timeri, & huic vult nos obedire. Hanc nominat sapientiam, quam vult nos retinere & anteferre omnibus opinionibus humanis, nec vult nos ab ea abduci rebus secundis aut adversis.

Addit igitur promissiones, quae docent huius sapientiae exitus prosperos & felices esse, etiamsi saepe multa ferenda sunt, sed eluctantur huius sapientiae auditores tandem ex calamitatibus. Et haec ipsa sapientia est salutaris ipsis auditoribus, & Ecclesiae, & reliquis hominibus. Ideo dicit, Penes me est consilium & successus, Item, Ego sum intelligentia, & penes me est efficacia, seu victoria, Id est, ubi gubernat consilia verbum Dei, successus prosperi sunt, & sapientia felix est Ecclesiae, & totis gentibus, & erit victrix, etiamsi aliquantisper magnae aerumnae ferendae sunt.

Estque hic locus diligenter considerandus, de discrimine felicis & infoelicis sapientiae, Pericles, Demosthenes, Crassus, Pompeius, & similes multi, fuerunt sapientes, Id est, viderunt artes augendae potentiae, sed haec sapientia fuit infoelix ipsis & civibus. Ac plerunque insignia sunt errata eorum qui maxime exellunt sapientia, quia superbia & πολυπραγμοσύνη accersunt mala sibi & aliis. Sed David, Ezechias, Ieremias, & aliqui similes, fuerunt & sapientes & felices, & manserunt intra metas suae vocationis & verbi divini. Consideremus igitur discrimen felicis & infoelicis sapientiae. Et oremus, ut Deus nos vasa misericordiae faciat, & ut simus vasa misericordiae, sciamus manendum esse intra metas verbi Dei & vocationis.

Postquam autem distinxit inter felicem & infoelicem sapientiam, addit dictum. Per me Reges regnant, &c. Significans ita constitui Ecclesiam, imperia, res salutares geri, quando gubernantur animi hac sapientia, de qua loquitur, Nec salutaria esse consilia quamlibet speciosa, quae sine luce divina suscipiuntur humana curiositate vel cupiditate. Sicut infoelix consilium fuit crassi et Pompeii.

## 8:22
The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way.

Above I warned readers, that this saying, The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his way, has been twisted by the Arians to the Son of God, but it is manifest that Solomon is speaking of revealed wisdom, that is, of the word of God, which sounds in the Church, just as he says most sweetly below, Wisdom plays in the world, and her delights are with the sons of men, where he testifies that he is preaching that wisdom which is acknowledged by the hearers in the Church, and rules and heals them, and begins eternal life in them. And it is an efficacious consolation in the greatest sorrows, and afterwards he says, Wisdom has built her house, she has prepared her table. Let these sayings be understood concerning the Church and the ministry of the Gospel, And this very word which sounds in the Church, is the eternal wisdom of God, Just as it is established that the law is the norm of the divine mind. And that the Gospel has been brought forth with immense goodness from the bosom of the eternal Father &c.

It is fitting therefore what he says, The Lord possessed me in the beginning of his ways. That is, this wisdom is the eternal counsel of God, which he himself revealed to the human race, so that he might gather an eternal Church for himself.

But let the reader diligently remember these sweetest words, Playing in the world, and her delights are with the sons of men, as if he were saying, that this wisdom alone, divinely handed down, exhibits to men efficacious consolations, in the greatest evils, against sins, against the wrath of God &c.

In the end he adds the Antithesis of the hearers. Epicureans become more furious, but the pious, docile, fearing God, make progress. Therefore he repeats here the sentence, The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom. He consoles the pious, however, so that they may not depart from the word of God, because of the multitude and the judgments of the impious, who hate or despise the doctrine handed down by God. Since, however, it is difficult to retain the firmness of faith against these scandals, let us learn to confirm ourselves both by the divine voice, and by the testimonies which God has added, namely by the thought of miracles, and of Prophetic and Apostolic examples, &c.

The final particle, A foolish woman is clamorous &c. is an allegory of wisdom contrary to the word of God, &c.

Latin:

Dominus possedit me initio viae suae.

Supra monui lectores, hoc dictum, Dominus possedit me initio viae suae, ab Arianis detortum esse ad Filium Dei, sed manifestum est, Salomonem loqui de sapientia revelata, id est, de verbo Dei, quod sonat in Ecclesia, sicut infra dulcissime inquit, Sapientia ludit in orbe terrae, & delectationes eius cum filiis hominum, ubi testatur, se illam sapientiam praedicare, quae agnoscitur ab auditoribus in Ecclesia, & regit ac sanat eos, & inchoat in eis vitam aeternam. Et est efficax consolatio in summis doloribus, & postea dicit, Sapientia aedificavit domum suam, paravit mensam suam. Haec dicta de Ecclesia & de ministerio Evangelii intelligantur, Et hoc ipsum verbum quod sonat in Ecclesia, est sapientia aeterna Dei, Sicut constat legem esse normam mentis divinae. Et Evangelium immensa bonitate prolatum esse ex sinu aeterni Patris &c.

Convenit igitur quod dicit, Dominus possedit me initio viarum suarum. Id est, haec sapientia est aeternum consilium Dei, quod ipse patefecit generi humano, ut aeternam Ecclesiam sibi colligeret.

Diligenter autem lector meminerit haec dulcissima verba, Ludens in orbe terrae, & delectationes eius cum filiis hominum, quasi dicat, hanc solam sapientiam divinitus traditam exhibere hominibus efficaces consolationes, in summis malis, adversus peccata, adversus iram Dei &c.

In fine addit Antithesin auditorum. Epicurei fiunt furiosiores, sed pii dociles timentes Deum proficiunt. Ideo repetit hic sententiam, Initium sapientiae timor Domini. Consolatur autem pios, ne discedant a verbo Dei, propter multitudinem & iudicia impiorum, qui oderunt aut contemnunt doctrinam a Deo traditam. Cum autem difficile sit retinere firmitatem fidei adversus haec scandala, discamus nos confirmare & voce divina, & testimoniis quae Deus addidit, videlicet cogitatione miraculorum, & exemplorum Propheticorum & Apostolicorum, &c.

Postrema particula, Mulier stulta garrula &c. allegoria est sapientiae contrariae verbo Dei, &c.

## 10:1 Argument
CHAPTER X.

Above he recited the precepts of the decalogue, which are the fountains of all honest laws. Now he adds brief admonitions, which are called γνώμαι or sentences.

They are either precepts concerning morals, and most are recited with mention of rewards and punishments, as A false balance is an abomination before the Lord.

Or they are narrations about events, As, Though you should pound a fool in a mortar, yet his folly will not be taken away from him.

Or they are promises or threats, as, The blessing of the Lord makes rich . Nor is it a wonder that the same sentences are often repeated in gnomes. But that these sentences may be understood more dexterously and accommodated to use, it must be considered in each one to what common place, and to what precept of the Decalogue they are to be referred. As in this place the first sentence, A wise son makes his father glad, is referred to the fourth precept, Honor thy father and thy mother. And the common places are, στοργὴ of parents, and the piety owed to parents.

Although the precept, Be obedient to your parents, is not directly recited, yet in the thing itself this is commanded, and the effects which are added warn how great a good obedience is, and on the contrary how atrocious an evil disobedience is, as if he were saying. He who is not obedient is a parricide of his parents, because there is no greater pain for men in life than that which arises from the calamity or crimes of their offspring, such as was the pain of the first parents, or of David in the crimes of his sons.

Although it is a brief saying, and seems to speak of a common matter, yet it preaches about a very great matter, he wishes the younger to be reminded of the magnitude of the pain in parents, and of the atrocity of the evil when they are offended, so that he may increase the care of obeying, here many things could be said about the duties of parents and children if we wished to linger, but let each one contemplate in his mind the doctrine of the fourth precept which ought always to be in sight, and let us always carry around the most severe law. He who shall speak evil of father or mother shall die the death.

The younger must also always be reminded of that, as often as it is spoken of στοργοῦ of parents, that are implanted in the nature of στοργάς men, so that they may be reminders of God. Let us know that a similar and true love is in God towards his offspring, by which thought in well-behaved men faith and invocation are often kindled.

Euripid.

Whoever honors his parents in life, he is dear to the gods both living and dead. There is no fairer reward for children than this, to have been born of a noble and good father, and to render worthy honor to those who begat them.

The sweetest praise of parents to their offspring.

There is nothing sweeter for me to hear, than a word spoken by a father to his son containing praise.

Epaminondas said.

That he derived the greatest fruit of his virtues and praises, because he had his parents as spectators.

Latin:

CAPUT X.

Supra decalogi praecepta recitavit, quae omnium honestarum legum fontes sunt. Nunc addit breves admonitiones, quae vocantur γνώμαι vel sententiae.

Sunt autem vel praecepta de moribus, & pleraque cum mentione praemiorum & poenarum recitantur, ut Statera dolosa abo minatio est apud Domium.

Aut sunt narrationes de eventibus, Ut, Etiamsi stultum in mortario contundas, tamen non auferetur ab eo stultia.

Aut sunt promissiones vel comminationes, ut, Benedictio Domini divites facit. Nec mirum est in gnomis easdem sententias saepe repeti. Ut autem dexterius intelligi & ad usum accommodari hae sententiae possint, in singulis considerandum est ad quem locum communem, & ad quod praeceptum Decalogi referendae sint. Ut hoc loco prima sententia, Filius sapiens exhilarat patrem, refertur dictum ad quartum praeceptum, Honora patrem & matrem. Et loci communes sunt, στοργὴ parentum, & pietas debita parentibus.

Etsi autem non directe recitatur praeceptem, Sitis morigeri parentibus, tamen reipsa hoc praecipitur, & effectus qui additi sunt, monent quantum bonum sit obedientia, & econtra quam atrox malum sit inobedientia, quasi dicat. Non obediens est parricida parentum, quia nullus est hominum maior dolor in vita, quam qui oritur ex calamitate aut sceleribus natorum, qualis fuit dolor primorum parentum, aut Davidis in sceleribus filiorum.

Etsi autem breve dictum est, & videtur de re vulgari loqui, tamen de re maxima concionatur, vult iuniores commonefieri de magnitudine doloris in parentibus, & de atrocitate mali cum offenduntur, ut augeat curam obdiendi, lam hic multa dici possent de officiis parentum & liberorum si immorari vellemus, sed singuli doctrinam quarti praecepti mente intueantur quae semper in conspectu esse debet, & semper circumferamus severissimam legem. Qui male dixerit patri aut matri morte morietur.

Illud etiam semper monendi sunt iuniores, quoties de στοργοῦ parentum dicitur, insitas esse στοργάς hominum naturae, ut sint commonefactrices de Deo. Sciamus similem & verum amorem in Deo esse erga natos, qua cogitatione in hominibus bene moratis saepe fides & invocatio accenditur.

Euripid.

ὅστις δὲ τοὺς τέκοντας ἐν βίῳ σέβει,ὅδ' ἐστὶ καὶ ζῶν καὶ θανὼν θεοῖς φίλος.οὐκ ἐστι παισὶ τούτου κάλλιον γέρας,Ἡ πατρὸς ἐσθλοῦ κἀγαθοῦ πεφυκέναι,Καὶ τοῖς τεκοῦσιν ἀξίαν τιμὴν νέμειν.

Dulcissima natis laus parentum.

οὐκ ἐστ' ἄκους μ' ἥδιον, ἤ ῥηθεὶς λόγοςΠατρὸς πρὸς ὑιὸν περιέχων ἐγκώμιον

Epaminundas dixit.

SE maximum fructum capere suarum virtutum & laudum, quod haberet parentes spectatores.

## 10:2
Treasures will not profit.

This opinion has often been repeated, that justice is a firmer protection than wealth unjustly acquired. It is a common topic, Justice, and the punishments of injustice, according to the rule, All atrocious injuries are punished divinely in this life, according to that, He who takes up the sword, shall perish by the sword. Item, Woe to you who plunder, because you shall be plundered. And these sayings pertain to the commandments, You shall not kill, You shall not commit theft, etc.

This light remains even in the Gentiles, to which experience also bears witness, when they see atrocious injuries punished divinely. Hence are those sayings, Evil gains are equal to losses. Ἡδὲ κακὴ βουλή τῷ βουλεὺσαντι κακίστη.

Latin:

Non proderunt thesauri.

HAec sententia saepe re petita est, iusticiam firmius praesidium esse, quam opes iniuste partas. Locus communis est, Iusticia, & poenae iniusticiae, iuxta regulam, Omnes atroces iniurias in hac vita divinitus puniri, iuxta illa, Qui acceperit gladium, gladi peribit. Item, Vae qui spolias, quia spoliaberis. Et pertinent haec dicta ad praecepta, Non occides, Non furtum facies &c.

Manet haec lux etiam in Ethnicis, cui & experientia testimonium perhibet, cum vident atroces iniurias divinitus puniri. Hinc sunt illa dicta, Mala lucra aequalia damnis. Ἡδὲ κακὴ βουλή τῷ βουλεὺσαντι κακίστη.

## 10:3
The Lord will not allow the soul of the just to hunger.

It is a promise pertaining to the first commandment. Now, concerning these promises of bodily goods, it must be known why they were delivered, and how they are to be used. They were delivered, first, That they might teach us about providence, and refute Epicurean and other opinions, which imagine that men are not a care to God, or certainly that these bodily things are not a care to God, and that they are provided only by our diligence, and that God does nothing without secondary causes. Against these false imaginations God published His promises, and affirms that those who call upon Him are a care to Him, that those who call upon Him are heard and helped, even when they are destitute of secondary causes, because since He is a most free agent, He moderates many things in secondary causes, etc. Therefore it is expressly said, Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God. Item Psalm 71. He delivers the poor who had no helper. Item. To the orphan you will be a helper. Item, Call upon me in the day of trouble, and I will deliver you.

Secondly, let us know that it is necessary to accept these promises by faith. It is not enough to know this, that they were delivered, so that they might tax Epicurean and Stoic opinions, but it is necessary that we also embrace them by faith, and sustain anxious minds with this consolation in true dangers, that God truly regards our groans, that He is about to help those who call upon Him, that this hope is not vain, And this faith, hope, invocation, petition, and expectation of help are true worship of God proper to the Church and unknown to the impious.

Nor does this faith forbid seeking protections ordered divinely, but it shows God in such a way that we may know He will be present to us even then, when we are destitute of human aids. Let all counsels therefore be governed by the word of God, and let us diligently perform the duties of our calling, and let us commend to God the events which can neither all be foreseen nor governed by our counsels. As the Psalm says, Wait for the Lord and keep His way. This is the chief rule of life. Do the necessary things commanded by God, and commend the events to God, and seek and expect aids from God.

Thirdly, to this faith concerning bodily goods, it is necessary that the recognition of the Son of God should shine forth, and the faith which accepts the remission of sins, and reconciliation. Because the mind without faith in the remission of sins flees from God. And it is written. No one can come to the Father, except through the Son, And in the Lord's prayer, immediately after the petition for bodily things, it is added, And forgive us our debts.

Let us know therefore that the promises of bodily things are reminders of the other promise, because God bestows bodily goods, so that He may preserve in this life some assemblies, in which the knowledge of Him, and true invocation may shine. According to that, The dead will not praise you, O Lord. And He wishes faith and thanksgiving to be exercised in many ways in the petition for bodily things, He wishes His presence to be seen in various deliverances, in which human counsels and aids were lacking. Indeed, among the reasons why the Church is subjected to the cross, this also is one, that it may learn to depend on God by faith and to discern the presence of God, and not be governed only by human counsels, as it is said 2. Corinth. 1., Therefore we had the sentence of death in ourselves, that we might not have confidence in ourselves. He also commands many to pray for themselves, so that many may give thanks.

Finally, a heart not exercising faith in the petition for bodily things and in the giving of thanks is altogether without true faith that calls upon and expects something from God. And the mind is either Epicurean, or Academic, or Stoic. Let us therefore obey the commandment of God, Call upon me in the day of trouble &c. And at the same time, in the petition for bodily things, let us exercise the faith which seeks the remission of sins.

A slack hand causes poverty. But the hand of the diligent will be rich. He who gathers in harvest is prudent. He who sleeps in summer is put to shame.

Above he recited the divine promise, here he adds an economic commandment concerning labor. Because divine help, doing the works of one's calling, and seeking and expecting help by faith, all concur for a happy outcome. But it has often been said elsewhere that labor is commanded by God, as it is said in the beginning in Genesis. And Paul says, That you may do this diligently, that you may be quiet, and that each one may do the duties of his own office. This virtue is called diligence, which is the virtue of doing the duties of one's own office diligently and faithfully, for the sake of God and the common utility.

The opposite vices are sloth and πολυπραγμοσύνη,, both of which Paul forbids, when he commands us to be quiet and to do the duties of our office. And here in Proverbs both vices are censured.

Latin:

Non sinet esurire Dominus animam iusti.

Promissio est pertinens ad primum praeceptum. Iam de his promissionibus bonorum corporalium sciendum est, quare traditae sint, & quomodo eis utendum sit. Sunt autem traditae, primum, Ut doceant nos de providentia, & refutent Epicureas & alias opiniones, quae fingunt Deo non esse curae homines, aut certe haec corporalia non esse curae Deo, & parari ea tantum nostra diligentia, & Deum nihil facere sine causis secundis. Contra has falsas imaginationes Deus edidit suas promissiones, & affirmat sibi curae esse invocantes ipsum, exaudiri & iuvari invocantes, etiam cum destituuntur a causis secundis, quia cum sit agens liberrimum, multa moderatur in causis secundis &c. Ideo expresse dictum est, Non in solo pane vivit homo, sed in omni verbo quod procedit ex ore Dei. Item Psalmo 71. Liberat pauperem cui non erat adiutor. Iem. Orphano tu eris adiutor. Item, Invoca me in die tribulationis, & ego eripiam te.

Secundo, sciamus & fide oportere accipi has promissiones. Non satis est hoc scire, traditas esse, ut taxent Epicureas & Stoicas opiniones, sed necesse est nos quoque fide eas amplecti, & sustentare anxias mentes hac consolatione in veris periculis, quod vere respiciat Deus gemitus nostros, quod sit opitulaturus invocantibus, quod haec spes non sit irrita, Et haec fides, spes, invocatio, petitio & expectatio auxilii sunt veri cultus Dei proprii Ecclesiae & ignoti impiis.

Nec prohibet haec fides quaerere praesidia ordinata divinitus, sed monstrat Deum ita, ut sciamus eum nobis affuturum esse tunc quoque, cum destituimur humanis auxiliis. Regantur igitur consilia omnia verbo Dei, & faciamus diligenter officia vocationis, & eventus qui nec prospici omnes possunt, nec regi nostris consiliis, Deo commendemus. Sicut Psalmus inquit, Expecta Dominum & custodi viam eius. Haec est regula vitae praecipua. Facito necessaria a Deo mandata, & eventus Deo commendato, & pete ac expecto auxilia a Deo.

Tertio, Huic fidei de corporalibus bonis praelucere oportet agnitionem filii Dei, & fidem quae accipit remissionem peccatorum, & reconciliationem. Quia mens sine fide remissiones peccatorum fugit Deum. Et scriptum est. Nemo potest accedere ad Patrem, nisi per Filium, Et in precatione dominica mox additur petitioni rerum corporalium, Et remitte nobis debita nostra.

Sciamus igitur promissiones rerum corporalium esse commonefactiones de altera promissione, quia largitur Deus bona corporalia, ut servet in hac vita aliquos coetus, in quibus luceat ipsius noticia, & vera invocati. Iuxta illud, Non mortui laudabunt te Domine. Vultque in petitione corporalium multipliciter exerceri fidem, & gratiarum actionem, vult conspici praesentiam suam in variis liberationibus, in quibus deerant humana consilia & auxilia. Imo inter causas cur Ecclesia subiecta sit cruci haec quoque est, ut discat fide a Deo pendere & cernere Dei praesentiam, nec tantum regatur humanis consiliis, ut 2. Corinth. 1. dicitur, Ideo responsum mortis in nobis habuimus, ne fiduciam nostri haberemus. Iubet & multos pro se petere, ut multi gratias agant.

Denique cor non exercens fidem in petitione corporalium, & gratiarum actione, est prorsus sine vera fide invocante & expectante aliquid a Deo. Et est mens vel Epicurea, vel Academica, vel Stoica. Obtemperemus igitur praecepto Dei, Invoca me in die tribulationis &c. Et simul in petitione corporalium exerceamus fidem, quae petit remissionem peccatorum.

Egestatem efficit manus ignava.Et manus sedulorum dives erit.Congregans in messe prudens est.Dormitans in aestate confunditur.

Supra recitavit promissionem divinam, hic addit praeceptum oeconomicum de labore. Quia ad felicem eventum concurrunt auxilium divinum, facere opera vocationis, & fide petere & expectare auxilium. Saepe autem alias dictum est, praecipi laborem a Deo, ut initio in Genesi dicitur. Et Paulus inquit, Hoc studiose faciatis, ut sitis quieti, et unusquisque sui muneris officia faciat, Haec virtus dicitur sedulitas, quae est virtus diligenter & fideliter faciendi proprii muneris officia, propter Deum & communem utilitatem.

Vitia opposita sunt, Ignavia & πολυπραγμοσύνη, quae ambo prohibet Paulus, cum iubet quietos esse, & nostri muneris officia facere. Et hic in Proverbiis utrumque vitium taxatur.

## 10:4
A deceitful hand causes poverty.

That is, one which is slothful and a shirker in its office, and meanwhile curiously thrusts itself into the affairs of others. Then there is the antithesis,

The hand of the diligent makes rich.

Where in the Latin version it is read, The hand of the strong, but it properly signifies the diligent and keen. And below in chapter 12, in the same way he says, The hand of the diligent will rule &c. But above, more has been said copiously concerning diligence. When, however, he adds, He who gathers in harvest, he warns that we should prudently observe the times, occasions, and order of actions.

And concerning Order there are many economic commandments in Xenophon and Aristotle. Xenophon says, Nothing is more beautiful nor more useful than order. And Aristotle says, It was a Laconian custom, ἕκαστον σκεῦος ἐν ἄυτου χώρᾷ κεῖσθαι that each instrument be placed in its own place.

Blessings will be upon the head of the just. But violence will overwhelm the wicked from what they themselves have conceived.

General things have been said, and consolations against the scandal of the prosperity of the wicked, who flourish for a little while, but in the end it will be well for the just, and ill for the wicked.

Latin:

Egestatem efficit manus frustratrix.

ID est, quae ignava est & cessatrix in suo officio, & interim curiose ingerit se alie nis negociis. Deinde Antithesis est,

Manus sedulorum ditat.

Ubi in latina versione legitur, Manus fortium, sed proprie significat sedulos & acres. Et infra cap. 12. eodem modo dicit, Manus sedulorum dominabitur &c. Supra autem de sedulitate copiosius dictum est. Cum autem addit, Congregans in messe, Monet ut tempora, occasiones, & ordinem actionum prudenter observemus.

Et de Ordine multa sunt praecepta oeconomica apud Xenophontem & Aristotelem. Xenophon ait, Nihil pulchrius neque utilius est ordine. Et Aristoteles inquit, Laconicum fuisse, ἕκαστον σκεῦος ἐν ἄυτου χώρᾷ κεῖσθαι unumquodlibet instrumentum suo loco positum esse.

Benedicetur capiti iusti. At impios iniuria ab ipsis concepta obruet.

Generalia dicta sunt, & consolationes contra scandalum felicitatis impiorum, qui aliquantisper florent, sed tandem iustis bene erit, & malis male.

## 10:8
The wise in heart accepts admonition.

That is, the teachable amend evil counsels; the stubborn, who manifestly oppose the truth, will stick in punishments.

Latin:

Sapiens corde admittit admonitionem.

ID est, Dociles emendant mala consilia, Pertinaces, qui manifeste veritati adversantur, haerebunt in poenis.

## 10:9
He who walks in simplicity.

The word is (Tam,) which signifies whole or uncorrupted, as, Blessed are the undefiled, That is, whole and uncorrupted, That is, having faith without corruption, and a good conscience, according to the Gospel. Such men walk securely, That is, first they have a tranquil conscience in themselves. Then they are also sustained by the hope of divine help.

Latin:

Ambulans in simplicitate.

Vocabulum est (Tam,) quod significat integrum seu incorruptum, ut, Beati immaculati, Id est, integri & incorrupti, Id est, habentes fidem sine corrupte lis, & bonam conscientiam, iuxta Evangelium. Tales ambulant secure, Id est, primum in sese conscientiam tranquillam habent. Deinde & spe auxilii divini sustentantur.

## 10:10
He who winks with the eyes &c.

That is, hypocrites feigning countenances, simulating blandishments or sadness, are to be avoided. As it is said, No faith in the face.

Latin:

Annuens oculis &c.

ID est, Hypocritae fingentes vultus, simulantes blanditias vel tristitiam, cavendi sunt. Ut dicitur, Fronti nulla fides.

## 10:12
Hatred stirs up strife. But love covers all offenses.

There is a commandment concerning ἐπιείκεια and candor. Just as justice is a virtue between two vices, between remission and cruelty, so a species of justice is equity, which mitigates severe justice, that is, it either punishes more leniently or remits offenses for a probable reason, as Julius Victor had the right of killing Cicero, M. Marcellus, and similar men, but spared them for a probable reason. Sulla was cruel, who killed even those who were quiet in the state, just as Juvenal says of him, The medicine exceeded the limit. Arcadius is sluggish, and sins by remission, because he does not even punish atrocious offenses.

But just as God commands severity in punishing atrocious offenses, as in unjust slaughter, which was done by ambush. Likewise, in punishing blasphemers, perjurers, those polluted by incestuous lust. So afterwards in other lighter offenses He commands ἐπιείκειαν, especially where they are curable by nature. But in private offenses, necessary defense is granted, but where there is no need for defense, He commands that offenses be remitted and buried in oblivion, according to that, Forgive, and it will be forgiven you. For God wishes this forgiveness to be done, so that we may think that we too are in need of divine and human remission. Likewise, so that we may be reminded of divine ἐπιεικεία. Likewise, so that public peace may not be disturbed by private quarrels. Likewise, so that your invocation and that of others may not be disturbed by kindled hatreds. And the verses of Nazianzen are worthy of memory,

Ειδ' ὄιδας ὄφλων, καὶ πρόχρησο το πρᾶον,Οἴκτῳ γὰρ ἆκτος καὶ θεῷ στατμίζεται.

The meaning of the commandment is therefore that the ---cts of others are to be covered by equity or love, and that by such moderation the common tranquility is to be maintained. On the contrary, minds often irritated by small injuries stir up civil wars, and cause horrendous devastations, as in the war of 28 years in Greece, in which Athens was finally captured, the causes were most trivial, because a certain grove was violated. Likewise, because Aspasia was treated with insult.

Let us therefore know that it is commanded to us that we should forget many injuries, lest the public peace be disturbed, A-nd this commandment is most necessary in the Church, where the good are irritated by many proud, malevolent, envious people. Often the injuries are not light, which if one -e--to repel, an occasion is provided for new distractions of the Churches and greater dissensions, as many examples of all times show. Here wisdom and moderation are necessary, to prefer public peace to private pain. This is the sum of this saying of Solomon.

But the younger ones are to be warned not to admit a false interpretation, who adapts this saying to the error which feigns that our works merit the remission of sins.

Love covers offenses, Therefore your love covers your offenses before God.

The consequence must be denied, Because the saying is political, and speaks of forgiveness between men, and of covering the offenses of others, and of repressing the desire for vengeance. As Levit. 19. it is commanded, You shall not seek vengeance, nor be mindful of injury against your citizens. For it speaks by name about citizens, so that it signifies that for the sake of common concord many injuries are to be dissembled, according to our pride and impatience, when the Son of God not only remits to us many and great offenses, but also derived the punishment onto Himself, prostrate before the Father, and sustaining wrath against our sins, as if He Himself had polluted Himself with our crimes.

The fourth pride is our unwillingness to appear inferior to our equals, and a shameful impatience, our unwillingness to endure any pain that would be profitable to the common peace. But just as virtues are neighbors, severe justice and ἐπιείκεια., so too are virtues neighbors, truth judging severely concerning the words or deeds of others, and candor. The extremes are flattery and calumny. Now just as ἐπιείκεια ought to moderate severe justice, so let candor moderate the truth judging concerning the words or deeds of others. Often something is said inconveniently by good writers; to soften that with a true interpretation consistent with the author's perpetual meaning is a virtue useful for peace.

In the lips of the wise wisdom is found. And a rod on the back of the foolish.

Latin:

Odium excitat rixas. Et omnia delicta tegit dilectio.

Praeceptum est de ἐπιείκεια & candore. Ut, Iusticia est virtus inter duo vitia, inter remissionem & crudelitatem, ita species iusticiae est aequitas, quae mitigaseveram iusticiam, Id est, aut punit lenius, aut remittit offensiones probabili ratione, ut Iulius victor habebat ius interficiendi Ciceronem, M. Marcellum, & similes, sed pepercit eis propter probabilem rationem. Sylla crudelis fuit, qui etiam in civitate quietos interfecit, sicut de eo inquit Iuvenalis, Excessit medicina modum. Arcadius lentus est, & peccat remissione, quia nec atrocia delicta punit.

Ut autem praecipit Deus severitatem in atrocibus delictis puniendis, ut in caede iniusta, quae facta est ex insidiis. Item, in puniendis blasphemis, periuris, pollutis incesta libidine. Ita postea in aliis levioribus delictis praecipit ἐπιείκειαν, praesertim ubi naturae sunt sanabiles. In privatis vero offensionibus conceditur necessaria defensio, Sed ubi defensione non est opus, praecipit remitti offensiones, & obrui oblivione, iuxta illud, Remittite, & remittetur vobis. Vult enim Deus hanc condonationem fieri, ut cogitemus nos quoque indigere divina & humana remissione. Item, ut commonefiamus de divina ἐπιεικεία. Item, ne pax publica turbetur privatis rixis. Item, ne invocatio tua & aliorum turbetur accensis odiis. Et versus sunt Nazianzeni digni memoria,

Ειδ' ὄιδας ὄφλων, καὶ πρόχρησο το πρᾶον,Οἴκτῳ γὰρ ἆκτος καὶ θεῷ στατμίζεται.

Sententia est igitur praecepti, aequitate seu dilectione tegenda esse aliorum ---cta, eaque moderatione retineri tranquillitatem communem. Econtra, saepe parvis iniuriis irritati animi movent civilia bella, & efficiunt horrendas vastationes, ut belli annorum 28. in Graecia, in quo Athenae tandem captae sunt, levissimae causae fuerunt, quod Lucus quidam violatus est. Item, quod Aspasia contumelia affecta fuit.

Sciamus igitur praecipi nobis, ut multas iniurias obliviscamur, ne pax publica turbetur, I-que praeceptum in Ecclesia maxime necessarium est, ubi irritantur boni a multis superbis, malevolis, invidis. Saepe iniuriae non leves sunt, quas si -e--depellere, praebetur occasio novis distractionibus Ecclesiarum & maioribus dissidiis, Ut multa exempla omnium temporum ostendunt. Hic necessaria sapientia & moderatio est, anteferre privato dolori publicam pacem. Haec est summa huius dicti Salomonis.

Monendi autem sunt iuniores, ne admittant falsam interpretationem, qui accommodat hoc dictum ad errorem, qui fingit nostra opera mereri remissionem peccatorum.

Delicta tegit caritas,Ergo tua dilectio tegit tua delicta coram Deo.

Neganda est consequentia, Quia dictum est politicum, & loquitur de condonatione inter homines, & de alienis delictis tegendis, & de reprimenda cupiditate vindictae. Ut Levit. 19. praecipitur, Non quaeras vindictam, nec sis mem-iniuriae adversus cives tuos. Nominatim enim de civibus loquitur, ut signific- propter communem concordiam multas iniurias dissimulandas esse, iuxta hu-strae superbiae & impatientiae, cum Filius Dei non solum remittat nobis multa & magna delicta, sed etiam poenam in se derivaverit, prostratus coram Patre, ac iram adversus nostra peccata sustinens, quasi ipse se nostris sceleribus polluisset.

Tetra superbia est, nos nolle videri inferiores aequalibus, & flagiciosa impacientia, nos nolle dolorem aliquem perferre profuturum communi paci. Sicut autem virtutes vicinae sunt iustitia severa, & ἐπιείκεια. Ita sunt vicinae virtutes, veritus severe iudicans de alienis dictis aut factis, & candor. Extrema sunt assentatio & calumnia, Iam sicut ἐπιείκεια moderari debet severam iusticiam, sic candor moderetur veritatem iudicantem de alienis dictis aut factis. Saepe aliquid incommode in bonis scriptoribus dictum est, id lenire vera interpretatione congruente ad perpetuam autoris sententiam, virtus est paci utilis.

In labiis sapientis invenitur sapientia.Et virga in tergo stulti.

## 10:12 Concerning the Vices of the Tongue
CONCERNING THE VICES OF THE TONGUE.

Many things are said in Solomon concerning speech, or concerning bridling the tongue. Now the vices proper to the tongue are: Lying, Garrulity, Precipitation, Curiosity, Evil-speaking, Reviling or petulance of the tongue, Vanity or boasting, Irony, δυσωπία, Accismus, Impudence, φιλονεικία, Scurrility, Being mocking, Futility.

Lying sins in the whole matter, because it says things contrary to the truth.

Garrulity sins properly in that it does not observe moderation.

Precipitation sins in time, concerning which it was said by Bias, Do not speak quickly, for it is a sign of insanity.

Curiosity does not discern necessary objects from non-necessary ones, and with ambition seeks new speeches.

Evil-speaking is unjust, and desirous of doing harm, just as was said concerning Polycrates, who, when asked why he spoke evil of everyone, replied, Because he could not do evil. Calumnies also pertain to this.

Petulance of the tongue or reviling is to revile easily and willingly.

Vanity or boasting is to preach gloriously about oneself, etc.

Irony, dissimulation or concealment is that which is vicious when a confession is necessary, or where it was necessary for something to be said more openly.

Δυσωπία is excessively great bashfulness, and to be hindered by rustic shame from speaking when there is need.

Ακκισμος is to simulate shame, as if you do not want what you most desire, concerning which vice there is the proverb, The Scythian donkey-feasts, σκύθης ὄνιον δαῖτα.

Impudence is contrary to bashfulness, to say what you do not know, and to promise great things -c. φιλονεικεία, Desire for contentions or quarrels.

Scurrility is to say obscene or insulting jokes.
