To be mocking is to rebuke petulantly with a gesture or a biting remark.

Futility is garrulity that is entirely idle and empty.

Flattery assumes much from all these. So many are the vices of the tongue, which, so that we may learn to avoid them, must be considered, and the mind must be accustomed with singular diligence to avoiding these plagues. But often in this kind, many vices run together . For all garrulous people are futile, liars, precipitate, curious, and vain. But often garrulity is the cause of great evils, both to the garrulous themselves and to other men, as daily examples show. These sayings of Solomon warn of this, all of which can be comprehended by this saying of Euripides,

Τῶν ἄχαλινων στομάτων τέλος δυστυχία. The end of unbridled tongues is calamity.

But it must be known that these vices are not only pernicious in private morals, but harm much more publicly in the Church. There, an unbridled, garrulous tongue, armed with impudence and sophistry, stirs up horrible tumults, and truly κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἐκκλησίαν, as was said concerning Pericles, κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἑλλάδα.

But it is the greatest of all crimes to corrupt the doctrine handed down by God, according to that saying, If anyone shall teach another Gospel, let him be anathema.

But just as an unbridled tongue is the cause of huge evils, so on the contrary there are huge benefits of a truthful and modest tongue. Because not only is this mortal life for the most part governed by speech, doctrine, and counsels, but also the knowledge of God and eternal life are begun by the voice, which sounds in the ministry of the Gospel. We discern or apprehend God by no other thing, except by the thought-out voice, by which He has revealed Himself.

Latin:

DE LINGUAE VITIIS.

Multa sunt dicta in Salomone de sermone, seu de lingua frenanda. Sunt autem vitia linguae propria, Mendacium, Garrulitas, Praecipitantia, Curiositas, Maledicentia, Conviciatio seu petulantia linguae, Vanitas seu iactantia, Ironia, δυσωπία, Accismus, Impudentia, φιλονεικία, Scurrilitas, Scopticum esse, Futilitas.

Mendacium tota re peccat, quia contraria veritati dicit.

Garrulitas proprie eo peccat, quod non servat modum.

Praecipitantia tempore peccat, de qua Biantis dictum est, Ne cito loquaris, est enim insaniae indicium.

Curiositas non discernit obiecta necessaria a non necessariis, & ambitione quaerit novos sermones.

Maledicentia iniusta est, & cupida nocendi, Sicut de Pholycrate dictum est, qui interrogatus, cur omnib, malediceret, Respondit, Quia malefacere non posset. Huc & calumniae pertinent.

Petulantia linguae seu conviciatio est facile & libenter conviciari.

Vanitas seu iactantia est gloriosa de se praedicare &c.

Ironia, dissimulatio seu occultatio est, quae tunc viciosa est, cum necessaria est confessio, seu ubi necesse erat aliquid dici apertius.

Δυσωπία est nimis magna verecundia, & rustico pudore impediri, ne loquaris, cum opus est.

Ακκισμος est simulare pudorem, quasi nolis quod maxime expetis, de quo vitio proverbium est, Scytha asininas epulas, σκύθης ὄνιον δαῖτα.

Impudentia, contraria est verecundiae, dicere quae nescis, et magna polliceri -c. φιλονεικεία, Cupiditas contentionum seu rixarum.

Scurrilitas, dicere iocos obscoenos aut contumeliosos.

Scopticum esse, est gestu vel mordaci dicto petulanter reprehendere.

Futilitas, est garrulitas prorsus ociosa & inanis.

Assentatio multa assumit ex his omnibus. Tam multa sunt vitia linguae, quae ut discamus vitare, consideranda sunt, & singulari diligentia mens assuefacienda ad cavendas has pestes. Plerunque autem in hoc genere simul concurrunt multa vitia. Nam garruli omnes, futiles, mendaces, praecipites, curiosi & vani sunt. Saepe autem garrulitas magnorum malorum causa est, & ipsis garrulis & aliis hominibus, ut quotidiana exempla ostendunt. Id monent haec dicta Salomonis, quae comprehendi omnia possunt hoc dicto Euripidis,

Τῶν ἄχαλινων στομάτων τέλος δυστυχία. Linguae effrenis finis est calamitas.

Sciendum est autem non solum in privatis moribus haec vitia perniciosa esse, sed multo plus publice nocere in Ecclesia. Ibi lingua effrenis, garrula, armata impudentia & sophistica, horribiles tum ultus ciet, & vere κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἐκκλησίαν, ut de Pericle dictum est, κυκᾶ πᾶσαν ἑλλάδα.

Summum est autem omnium scelerum, corrumpere doctrinam a Deo traditam, luxta illud, Si quis aliud Evangelium docuerit, anathema sit.

Sicut autem effrenis lingua causa est ingentium malorum, ita econtra beneficia sunt ingentia linguae veracis & modestae. Quia non solum haec vita mortalis magna ex parte regitur sermone, doctrina & consiliis, sed etiam agnitio Dei & vita aeterna inchoantur voce, quae sonat in ministerio Evangelii. Non alia re cernimus vel apprehendimus Deum, nisi cogitata voce, qua se patefecit.

## 10:12 Concerning the Insolence of the Rich
CONCERNING THE INSOLENCE OF THE RICH.

The substance of the rich is a fortified city. The work of the just brings life, the produce of the wicked sin, That is, the just use their faculties well, the wicked abuse them.

Latin:

DE INSOLENTIA DIVITUM.

Substantia divitum est urbs munita.Opus iusti adfert vitam, Proventus impii peccatum,Id est, Iusti bene utuntur facultatibus, mali abutuntur.

## 10:12 Concerning Those Listening to One Admonishing Well. He Walks Toward Life, Who Hears Correc
CONCERNING THOSE LISTENING TO ONE ADMONISHING WELL. He walks toward life, who hears correction.

A necessary virtue is docility, and it is near to modesty and truthfulness. For docility is that by which minds are curbed, so that they do not think arrogantly of themselves, nor despise the judgments of others out of admiration for their own wisdom, but listen, and when convinced by true arguments, yield. This is the second degree of those worthy of praise according to Hesiod. Many sayings are to be referred here, which disparage stubbornness. For there are extremes, Stubbornness and ταχυπύθεια foolish credulity. He who believes quickly is light of heart. Item, To err is human, to persevere in error is diabolical. Thus also in the Church there is a distinction of those who err, some are weak and docile, others are stubborn blasphemers. Sophocles in the Tyrant, If you think that stubbornness without a mind is a noble thing, you do not think rightly.

Latin:

DE AUSCULTANTIB. BENE MONENTI. Ambulat ad vitam, audiens emendationem.

Necessaria virtus est docilitas, et vicina est modestiae & veracitati. Est enim docilitas, qua frenantur animi, ne arroganter de se sentiant, nec admiratione propriae sapientiae contemnant aliena iudicia, sed audiant, & convicti veris argumentis cedant. Hic est secundus gradus laude dignorum apud Hesiodum. Huc dicta multa referantur, quae vituperant pertinaciam. Sunt enim extrema, Pertinacia & ταχυπύθεια stulta credulitas. Qui cito credit, levis est corde. Item, Errare humanum est, perseverare in errore diabolicum. Sic & in Ecclesia discrimen est errantium, alii sunt infirmi dociles, alii pertinaces blasphemi. Sophocles in Tyranno, Si putas pertinaciam sine mente rem praeclaram esse, non recte sentis.

## 10:18
A lying lip covers hatred.

CONCERNING backbiting, That is, he who does not admonish nor castigate an erring friend, and otherwise backbites and detracts from his reputation, acts unjustly.

Latin:

Tegit odium labium mendax.

DE obtrectatione, Id est, qui non admonet nec castigat amicum errantem, & alio qui obtrectat & detrahit eius existimationi, iniuste facit.

## 10:22
The blessing of the Lord makes rich.

I said above that the promises of bodily goods were handed down for 4 reasons. First, that they may teach us about providence, so that we may truly believe that those goods are not brought about by chance only, or only by physical causes, but are also governed by the counsel of God. As the Son of God says, All the hairs of your head are numbered.

Secondly, so that faith and invocation and hope may be exercised by the petition and expectation of such goods, and the presence of God may become more visible to us, when He helps those who invoke Him. As He says, Invoke me in the day of tribulation, and I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me &c.

Thirdly, so that they may be reminders of the spiritual promise, because it is always necessary in the petition of bodily things for faith to shine forth, which seeks and receives the remission of sins, according to that saying, How shall they invoke unless they believe?

The fourth reason is, so that we may know that God will preserve the Church even in this life, and that we may not be broken by despair when the wicked attempt to destroy the Church. As the Son of God consoles us before the agony Luke 21. They will betray you parents, and some will be killed, and you will be hated by all for my name's sake &c. A hair from your head will not perish &c.

To this common doctrine concerning the promises let this saying also be referred, The blessing of the Lord makes rich without sorrow, That is, some with great labor achieve nothing, or if anything is acquired, it is not stable, as the labor of Ahab in his whole government is unhappy. Others with moderate labor achieve much, as Isaac found a hundredfold in the harvest. Let us know that the true and principal cause of this distinction is the will of God, who by His goodness most freely helps the labors of some. Therefore, this saying teaches us about providence at the same time, and it is a promise, And it warns, lest relying on our own diligence alone, we remove God, and omit invocation, since it has been said, Cursed is he who makes flesh his arm.

Latin:

Benedictio Domini facit divites.

Dixi supra traditas esse promissiones bonorum corporalium, propter 4. causas. Primum, ut doceant nos de providentia, ut vere credamus non casu tantum, aut tantum Physicis causis bona illa obiici, sed etiam gubernari Dei consilio. Sicut inquit Filius Dei, Omnes capilli capitis vestri numerati sunt.

Secundo, ut fides & invocatio & spes exerceantur petitione & expectatione talium bonorum, & praesentia Dei nobis fiat conspectior, cum opitulatur invocantib. Sicut inquit, Invoca me in die tribulationis, & eripiam te, & glorificabis me &c.

Tertio, ut sint commonefactiones de promissione spirituali, quia semper in petitione corporalium necesse est praelucere fidem, quae petit & accipit remissionem peccatorum, luxta illud, Quomodo invocabunt nisi credent?

Quarta causa est, ut sciamus et in hac vita Deum servaturum esse Ecclesiam, nec frangamur desperatione, cum impii conantur delere Ecclesiam. Sicut consolatur nos Filius Dei ante agonem Luc. 21. Prodent vos parentes, & interficientur aliqui, & eritis odio omnibus propter nomen meum &c. Capillus de capite vestro non peribit &c.

Ad hanc communem doctrinam de promissionibus referatur & hoc dictum, Benedictio Domini divites facit sine angustia, Id est, Aliqui magno labore nihil proficiunt, aut si quid partum est, non est stabile, ut labor Achas in tota gubernatione est infelix. Alii mediocri labore multum proficiunt, ut Isaac in messe invenit centuplum. Huius discriminis veram & principalem causam sciamus esse voluntatem Dei, qui sua bonitate liberrime adiuvat aliquorum labores. Simul igitur hoc dictum de providentia nos docet, & promissio est, Et monet, ne sola nostra diligentia freti, removeamus Deum, & invocationem omittamus, cum dictum sit, Maledictus qui ponit carnem brachium suum.

## 10:26
As vinegar to the teeth and smoke to the eyes, so is the sluggard to those who sent him.

CONCERNING messengers it is manifest that speed is pleasing and slowness is hateful, whence also that saying of Plautus comes, A slow messenger stirs up bile in the nostrils. And the figure in this saying of Solomon indicates that this sentiment was proverbial. Just as many other things concerning messengers were proverbial, by which either industry was praised or stupidity was censured. As that Homeric one, ἐσθλὸν καὶ τὸ τέτυκται ὅτ' ἄγγελος ἄισιμα ἔιδη, Good is this also, when a messenger knows what is fitting.

Generally, however, the saying of Solomon can be applied to the doctrine, often repeated, concerning diligence, the extremes of which are vicious, sloth, and πολυπραγμοσύνη.

The remaining sentences in this chapter are for the most part common sayings, which promise rewards to the just and threaten punishments to the unjust, all of which pertain universally to the rule. Atrocious crimes are punished, and in this life with atrocious punishments.

Latin:

Sicut acetum dentibus & fumus oculis, Sic piger his, qui mi-serunt eum.

DE nunciis manifestum est, gratam esse celeritatem, & odiosam tarditatem, unde & Plautinum illud est, Tardus nuncius bilem ciet in naribus. Et figura in hoc dicto Salomonis indicat, hanc sententiam fuisse proverbialem. Sicut & alia multa de nunciis proverbialia fuerunt, quibus vel industria laudata est, vel taxata stoliditas. Ut Homericum illud, ἐσθλὸν καὶ τὸ τέτυκται ὅτ' ἄγγελος ἄισιμα ἔιδη, Bonum & hoc est, quando nuncius scit convenientia.

Generaliter autem dictum Salomonis accommodari potest ad doctrinam, saepe repetitam de sedulitate, cuius extrema viciosa sunt, ignavia, et πολυπραγμοσύνη.

Reliquae sententiae in hoc capite pleraeque sunt dicta communia, quae promittunt praemia iustis, & minantur poenas iniustis, quae omnia pertinent universaliter ad regulam. Atrocia delicta puniuntur, & in hac vita atrocibus poenis.

## 11:1 Argument
CHAPTER XI.

Latin:

CAPUT XI.

## 11:1
A false balance will the Lord abhor.

It is a precept concerning particular justice and concerning truth in contracts. It pertains, however, to the precept of the Decalogue, You shall not steal. And let the threat, often repeated, be understood to be repeated here also. Atrocious crimes are punished and in this life with atrocious punishments. Therefore also Isaiah says, Woe to you who plunder, because you will be plundered. Let that also be referred here, What is ill-gotten perishes ill.

Latin:

Stateram iniustam abominabitur Dominus.

Praeceptum est de particulari iusticia, & de veritate in contractibus. Pertinet autem ad praeceptum Decalogi, Non furtum facies. Et comminatio saepe repetita, hic quoque repeti intelligatur. Atrocia delicta puniuntur & in hac vita atrocibus poenis. Ideo & Esaias inquit, Vae qui spolias, quia spoliaberis. Huc & illud referatur, Male partum male disperit.

## 11:2
Where there is humility, there is wisdom.

IN prosperous circumstances, care, invocation, and the fear of God gradually languish, and in many are extinguished altogether, as many sentences warn. As, The people sat down to eat and to drink, and rose up to play. Item, Minds often grow wanton in prosperous circumstances. And when the fear of God has departed, men who are puffed up desire many and great things outside their calling, and loosen the reins to desire, and inflict injuries upon others. Whence it is said, κὸρος ὕβριν τίκτει, Satiety begets insolence.

Therefore punishments follow universally. For this reason the Greeks imagined that there was a certain deity ἄδραστειαν, Nemesis, who punishes the proud. Whence also that saying of Theognis is, ὑβρις, καὶ μάγνητας ἄπωλεσε καὶ κολοφῶνα. Pride destroyed the Magnetes.

Item, They are lifted on high, That they may fall with a heavier crash.

Thus here Solomon says that the proud bring punishments upon themselves. Conversely, it is most wisely said, Where there is humility, there is wisdom.

It is, however, true ταπεινοφροσύνη, to fear God, and to acknowledge our infirmity, and not to desire things greater than one's calling. Meanwhile, however, with trust in divine aid, to do diligently the things fitting to one's calling. But when a man restrains himself within the bounds of his calling, and especially when the fear and invocation of God are present, labors are happy, as it has been said, Commit your way to the Lord, and he will act. Item, Who makes you to will, will make you to perform. Item, Your labor is not in vain in the Lord. This, however, is true and salutary wisdom, to acknowledge God rightly, and then to see the bounds of one's office, within which while the pious remain, they do salutary things. The wisdom of Demosthenes is unhappy, because it is πολυπραγμοσύνη restless without a calling and without necessity, repeatedly provoking the Macedonian kings. Therefore he brings destruction upon himself and others.

Even the Gentiles saw that it is great wisdom to contain oneself within the bounds of one's office. Whence it was said, You have obtained Sparta, adorn this. But this wisdom is greater, when the acknowledgment of God, fear, faith, and the invocation of God are added, as in Jonathan not desiring the kingdom.

Latin:

Ubi est humilitas, ibi est sapientia.

IN rebus secundis cura, invocatio & timor Dei paulatim languefiunt, & in multis omnino extinguuntur, ut multae sententiae monent. Ut, Sedit populus manducare & bibere, & surrexit ludere. Item, Luxuriant animi rebus plerunque secundis. Et cum discessit timor Dei, homines elati concupiscunt multa & magna extra vocationem, & laxant frenos cupiditati, & alios iniuriis afficiunt. Unde dicitur, κὸρος ὕβριν τίκτει, Saturitas insolentiam parit.

Sequuntur igitur poenae universaliter. Ideo Graeci finxerunt numen quoddam esse ἄδραστειαν, Nemesin, quod puniat superbos. Unde & illud est Theognidis, ὑβρις, καὶ μάγνητας ἄπωλεσε καὶ κολοφῶνα. Superbia perdidit magnetas.

Item, Tolluntur in altum,Ut lapsu graviore ruant.

Ita hic dicit Salomon, superbos accensere sibi poenas. Econtra sapientissime dicitur, Ubi humilitas est, ibi sapientia est.

Est autem vera ταπεινοφροσύνη, timere Deum, & agnoscere nostram infirmitatem, & non appetere maiora vocatione. Interea vero fiducia auxilii divini, diligenter facere convenientia vocationi. Cum autem cohercet se homo intra metas vocationis, & praesertim cum adsunt timor & invocatio Dei, labores sunt felices, sicut dictum est, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet. Item, Qui facit ut velitis, faciet ut perficiatis. Item, Non est inanis labor vester in Domino. Haec est autem vera & salutaris sapientia, recte agnoscere Deum, & deinde videre metas officii, intra quas dum manent pii, faciunt salutaria. Demosthenis sapientia infelix est, quia est πολυπραγμοσύνη inquieta sine vocatione & sine necessitate, subinde irritans Macedonicos reges. Ideo sibi & aliis accersit exicium.

Etiam Ethnici viderunt magnam sapientiam esse, continere se intra metas officii. Unde dictum est, Spartam nactus es, hanc orna. Sed haec sapientia maior est, cum accedunt agnitio Dei, timor, fides, invocatio Dei, ut in Ionatha non appetente regnum.

## 11:3
The simplicity of the just shall uphold them.

The six following sentences contain a common doctrine concerning providence: it will be well for the just , and ill for the wicked, just as those common sayings also warn: God, the avenger, discerns all things, θεὸς οἰδε δικάζειν. God knows how to judge. And Psal. 67. he says, Surely there is a God judging men on the earth.

This reminder is repeated very often because, since in such great confusion of human life many contrary things seem to happen—so that many impious men flourish in glory and hold empires, and indeed kill the pious, as Cain kills his brother—the weak are moved to doubt whether human affairs are a care to God, as many voices of doubters exist in poems. And Epicurus and many others with horrible fury deny that there is providence. Against this madness, God confirms us not only with words but also with manifest examples and testimonies of His revelation, and indeed even by the very order of nature. All these things must be thought upon often, so that assent may be confirmed.

First, think upon the most beautiful order of nature itself, which testifies that there is a God the Creator, and look upon yourself. Consider the inborn notions, which warn what kind of God there is, and conscience testifies that a judgment will follow. The very preservation of human society, in such great tumults, is a testimony of the presence of God, and is a reminder of the judgment of God, because atrocious crimes are universally accompanied by atrocious punishments even in this life.

To these arguments taken from nature, let divine revelation be added: in the exodus from Egypt, in the resurrection of the dead, and in other miracles. And let divine sayings be fixed in the minds, so that we may firmly establish that God is the inspector and judge of all human actions. And let us fear the true and horrible wrath and punishments of God, and on the contrary, let us rouse ourselves to obedience, invocation, and hope of divine aid, liberation, and happy outcomes.

Afterwards, the solution to the opposing argument must also be added: Since there is providence, why do such great dissipations of the Church occur, and why is it in such great miseries? This doctrine is not taught in philosophy, but has only been revealed divinely. And there are many causes of the miseries of the Church, but the first and principal one is sin, which clings to the nature of men. The other causes have been repeated often elsewhere. Hence, let the solution to the argument be taken.

The order of justice demands that it be well for the just; the Church is just; therefore, it is well for the Church.

I answer first to the minor premise: the Church is just, namely, by imputation and beginning, but yet the remnants of sin still cling to it, and many fall horribly, as Aaron, David, and countless others, who even if they repent, yet God wishes His wrath against sins to be seen in punishments.

Now I answer to the major premise: the order of justice demands that it be well for the just, namely, according to the declaration of the Gospel. For since this saying, 'It is well for the just,' is a voice of the law, which condemns all, it is necessary to learn from the Gospel how some are just, and why they are heard and saved.

This whole doctrine concerning providence, and concerning consolation in the cross and all calamities, unknown to philosophy, must be diligently and rightly learned from the doctrine of the Church, in which it has been properly revealed. And to these reminders and consolations, let these sayings be accommodated: The justice of the upright shall save them; that is, it will be well for the upright. And although they are in miseries, yet they are heard, guarded, and upheld by God, and finally liberated. As it is written in Isaiah, God will dwell among the afflicted. On the contrary, the impious bring destruction and punishments upon themselves. And although they flourish for a little while, yet afterwards they fall into eternal miseries. This doctrine is also copiously taught in the Psalm, 'Fret not thyself': I have seen the impious exalted like the cedars of Lebanon; I passed by, and behold, he was not.

Let this suffice to have warned once about all such sayings, but God partly declares judgment in this life, and partly defers it. However, the rule is universally true: Atrocious crimes are accompanied by atrocious punishments in this life. These are not yet punishments equal and fitting for the impious, but they are the beginnings of punishments, and they are signs reminding us of the wrath of God, through which some are recalled to repentance.

Since, therefore, God is just, it is necessary that another judgment remain, in which Herod and the Baptist, Nero and Paul, and those like them will be distinguished.

Latin:

Simplicitas iustorum sustentabit eos.

Sex proximae sententiae continent communem doctrinam, de providentia, Iustis bene erit, malis male, sicut & illa communia dicta monent, Cernit Deus omnia vindex, θεὸς οἰδε δικάζειν. Deus novit iudicare. Et Psal. 67. inquit, Certe est Deus iudicans homines in terra.

Ideo autem saepissime repetita est haec commonefactio, quia cum in tanta confusione vitae hominum multa contraria accidere videantur, ut multi impii florrent gloria, & tenent imperia, & quidem interficiunt pios, ut Cain interficit fratrem, moventur imbecilles, ut dubitent an Deo curae sint humana, ut multae extant in poematis dubitantium voces. Et Epicurus & multi alii horribili furore negant esse providentiam. Contra hanc amentiam confirmat nos Deus non solum dictis, sed etiam manifestis exemplis, & testimoniis suae patefactionis, imo & ipso naturae ordine. Haec omnia saepe cogitanda sunt, ut assensus confirmetur.

Primum cogita ipsum naturae ordinem pulcherrimum, qui testatur esse Deum opificem, ac teipsum aspice, Considera notitias impressas, quae mone-qualis sit Deus, Et conscientia testatur secuturum esse iudicium. Ipsa etiam conservatio societatis humanae, in tantis tumultibus, testimonium est praesentiae Dei, & commonefactio est de iudicio Dei, quod universaliter atrocia delicta comitantur atroces poenae etiam in hac vita.

His argumentis a natura sumptis adiungatur patefactio divina, in exstu ex Aegypto, in resuscitatione mortuorum, & aliis miraculis, Et dicta divina infigantur animis, ut certo statuamus, Deum esse inspectorem & iudicem omnium humanarum actionum. Ac timeamus veram ac horrendam Dei iram & poenas, & econtra exuscitemus nos ad obedientiam, invocationem, spem auxilii divini, liberationis ac felicium eventuum.

Postea adiungenda est & solutio argumenti oppositi, Cum sit providentia, cur tantae dissipationes Ecclesiae fiunt, & cur est in tantis aerumnis? Haec doctrina non traditur in philosophia, sed tantum divinitus patefacta est. Et multae causae sunt aerumnarum Ecclesiae, sed prima & principalis est, peccatum, quod haeret in natura hominum. Caeterae causae alibi saepe repetitae sunt. Hinc solutio argumenti sumatur.

Iustis bene esse ordo iusticiae postulat,Ecclesia est iusta,Ergo Ecclesiae bene sit.

Respondeo primum ad minorem, Ecclesia est iusta, scilicet, imputatione & inchoatione, sed tamen adhuc haerent in ea reliquiae peccati, & multi horribiliter labuntur, Ut Aaron, David, & alii innumerabiles, qui & si resipiscunt, tamen Deus in poenis vult conspici iram suam adversus peccata.

Nunc ad maiorem respondeo, Iustis bene esse ordo iusticiae postulat, scilicet iuxta declarationem Evangelii. Cum enim hoc dictum, Iustis bene sit, vox sit legis, quae omnes condemnat, necesse est ex Evangelio disci, quomodo aliqui sint iusti, & cur exaudiantur & serventur.

Haec tota doctrina de providentia, & de consolatione in cruce, & omnibus calamitatibus, ignota Philosopiae, diligenter & recte discenda est ex doctrina Ecclesiae, in qua proprie revelata est. Et ad has commonefactiones & consolationes accommodentur haec dicta, Iusticia rectorum servabit eos; id est, rectis bene erit. Et quanquam sunt in aerumnis, tamen exaudiuntur, custodiuntur & sustentantur a Deo, & tandem liberantur. Sicut scriptum est apud Esaiam, Habitabit Deus in afflictis. Econtra accersunt sibi impii exitium & poenas. Et quanquam aliquantisper florent, tamen postea ruunt in aeternas miserias. Haec doctrina & in Psalmo, Noli aemulari, copiose tradita est, Vidi impium exaltatum ut cedros Libani, Praeterii, & ecce non erat.

Haec satis sit semel de talibus dictis omnibus monuisse, sed partim Deus iudicium in hac vita declarat, partim differt. Regula autem universaliter vera est, Atrocia delicta comitantur atroces poenae in hac vita. Hae nondum sunt poenae aequales convenientes impiis, sed sunt initia poenarum, & sunt signa commonefacientia nos de iram Dei, per quae aliqui revocantur ad paenitentiam.

Cum igitur Deus sit iustus, necesse est restare aliud iudicium, in quo discernentur Herodes & Baptista, Nero & Paulus, & similes.

## 11:9
With his mouth the hypocrite destroys his friend, but by knowledge the righteous shall be delivered.

The admonition is that disguised friends should be prudently avoided. And many sayings pertain to this, The way is safe and frequent to deceive under the name of a friend,

Although the way may be safe and frequent, it holds a crime.

But simulation is especially harmful, that is, sophistry in the corruptions of doctrine. For always in sophisms almost some splendid and plausible opinion is proposed, so that the approach may be attractive and invite the listeners. Afterwards, false and evil consequences are attached. As when it is said, One must not depart from the Church, which, since it is most true, can be splendidly and magnificently adorned, because according to God the highest reverence is owed next to the Evangelical ministry and the Church. But afterwards an evil consequence is attached, a false minor premise: Bishops, Canons, Monks are the Church, when indeed they are enemies of the Gospel and parricides. Therefore, the other member of Solomon's saying warns that we should be vigilant.

Knowledge delivers the righteous, that is, discernment learnedly, rightly, prudently, and skillfully done, uncovers the lie and reveals the truth. Moreover, there is need of many virtues for the discernment of sophisms: love of truth, erudition, prudence, sagacity, candor, and dexterity in judging, which virtues ought to be in good and learned men, according to the commandment, You shall not bear false witness.

Latin:

Ore simulator evertit amicum suum, Scientia iusti liberabuntur.

Admonitio est, ut prudenter caveantur fucati amici. Et pertinent huc multa dicta, Tuta frequensque via est per amici fallere nomen,

Tuta frequensque licet sit via, crimen habet.

Praecipue vero nocet simulatio, hoc est, sophistica in corruptelis doctrinae. Semper enim in sophismatibus fere aliqua splendida & plausibilis sententia proponitur, ut aditus sit speciosus, & invitet auditores. Postea falsa attexuntur mala consequentia. Ut cum dicitur, Non discedendum est ab Ecclesia, Quod cum sit verissimum, splendide & magnifice ornari potest, Quia secundum Deum proxime summa reverentia debetur Evangelico ministerio & Ecclesiae. Sed postea mala consequentia attexitur falsa minor, Episcopi, Canonici, Monachi sunt Ecclesia, cum quidem sint hostes Evangelii & Parricidae. Ideo alterum membrum dicti Salomonis monet, ut simus vigilantes.

Scientia liberat iustos, Id est, Diiudicatio erudite, recte, prudenter, dextre, concinne facta retegit mendacium, & patefacit veritatem. Opus est autem multis virtutibus ad diidicationem sophismatum, amore veritatis, eruditione, prudentia, sagacitate, candore & dexteritate in iudicando, quas virtutes in bonis & doctis viris esse oportet, iuxta praeceptum, Non dicas falsum testimonium.

## 11:11
By the blessing of the upright the city is made fortunate. When the righteous flourish, cities flourish.

Government ought to imitate the will of God, to protect justice, and to punish the wicked, as is clearly said in Rom. 13., and it is said below, Justice establishes the throne, etc. When these things are done, the state is salutary, but they are done then when the righteous are in charge. But the contrary happens in tyranny, as when the wicked are in charge, truth, justice, chastity, modesty, etc., are oppressed. As the tyrants say in the Tragedy,

Sanctity, piety, faith, are private goods, let them go where it pleases Kings. He who confuses his friend is a fool. And a prudent man covers up disgraces.

He prohibits evil-speaking, and warns that one must spare the reputation of another, according to the commandments, You shall not be a whisperer, etc. Likewise, Do not judge, that you may not be judged. Likewise, You shall not bear false witness.

Here let the old saying be learned: I have need of a good conscience for myself, a good reputation for my neighbor. God wants us to protect both our own reputation and that of others. First, because God wants to distinguish fame and infamy, the honest and the dishonest. Therefore, so that there may be a place for us among the honest, a good reputation must be protected, and indeed it must be protected by doing right, not just by empty clamors.

Secondly, because God wants a good reputation to be an example to others, so that they too may be invited to virtue. Therefore, reputation is truly said to be a huge good, as is said below in chapter 22, A good name is better than great riches, better than gold and silver. Therefore, let us be guardians of a good reputation for ourselves, and let us beware of harming the reputation of others. But where crimes are manifest, there let judgments pronounce concerning punishment and infamy.

Latin:

Benedictione rectorum fortunatur civitas. Florentibus iustis florent civitates.

Gubernatio debet imitari voluntatem Dei, iusticiam tueri, & punire sceleratos, ut Rom. 13. perspicue dicitur, & infra dicitur, Iusticia stabilit thronum &c. Haec cum fiunt, status est salutaris, fiunt autem tunc, cum praesunt iusti. Contrarium vero fit in tyrannide, ut cum scelerati praesunt, opprimuntur veritas, iusticia, castitas, modestia &c. Ut Tyranni in Tragedia dicunt,

Sanctitas, pietas, fides, Privata bona sunt, Qua iuvat Reges eant. Confundens amicum suum stultus est. Et vir prudens tegit probra.

Maledicentiam prohibet, & monet famae alienae parcendum esse, iuxta praecepta, Non sis susurro &c. Item, Nolite iudicare, ne iudicemini. Item, Non dicas falsum testimonium.

Hic discatur vetus dictum, Bona conscientia mihi opus est, bona fama proximo. Vult Deus nos & nostram & alienam famam tueri. Primum enim, quia vult Deus famam & infamiam discernere, honestos & inhonestos. Ideo ut locus sit nobis inter honestos, bona fama tuenda est, & quidem tuenda est recte faciendo, non tantum inanibus clamoribus.

Secundo, quia vult Deus bonam famam aliis exemplo esse, ut invitentur & ipsi ad virtutem. Ideo fama vere dicitur esse ingens bonum, ut infra capite 22. dicitur, Melius est nomen bonum quam divitiae magnae, Melius auro & argento. Quaere & bonae famae pro nobis ipsis custodes simus, & caveamus aliorum famam laedere. Sed ubi scelera manifesta sunt, ibi iudicia pronuncient de poena & infamiae.

## 11:13
He who walks deceitfully, reveals a secret.

This saying condemns perfidy that discloses secrets, and it agrees with the customary saying,

It is an exceptional virtue to maintain silence on matters, but on the contrary, it is a grave fault to speak of things that should be kept silent.

And that saying of Seneca is worthy of memory, Faith is the most sacred good of the human heart.

Without magistrates the people will fall.

God wills the human race to be ruled by legitimate governments, therefore neither ἀναρχία, nor tyranny is useful to the human race, and both evils are condemned in this saying. The first part condemns ἄναρχίαν.. The second part prohibits tyranny. There is safety where there are many counsels, that is, where all things are not done according to the will and desire of one person, but where the authority of faithful men, who are able to judge, prevails.

Latin:

Qui ambulat fraudulenter, revelat arcanum.

Hoc dictum damnat perfidiam patefacientem arcana, & congruit cum usitato dicto,

Eximia virtus praestare filentia rebus, At contra gravis est cuipa tacenda loqui.

Et dignum memoria est illud Senecae, Fides sanctissimum humani pectoris bonum est.

Absque magistratibus ruet populus.

Deus vult genus humanum regi legitimis imperiis, ideo nec ἀναρχία, nec tyrannis utilis est generi humano, & utrunque malum damnatur in hoc dicto. Prior pars damnat ἄναρχίαν. Altera pars prohibet tyrannidem. Ibi est salus, ubi multa sunt consilia, id est, ubi non ad unius arbitrium & cupiditatem omnia fiunt, sed ubi valet autoritas fidelium hominum, qui iudicare possunt.

## 11:15
He will be badly afflicted who gives surety for a stranger.

It warns that suretyships are dangerous, as has been said above, and at the same time it signifies that one should not universally believe rashly, according to that saying, He who believes quickly is light of heart.

Avoid those whom you believe to be faithful, you will be safe. Faith is nowhere safe.

Quintus Cicero to his brother, Hold fast to that Epicharmian saying, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν that they are the nerves and joints of wisdom.

Latin:

Male affligetur, qui spondet pro extraneo.

Monet sponsiones periculosas esse, ut supra dictum est, ac simul significat universaliter non temere credendum esse, iuxta illud, Qui cito credit, levis est corde.

Quos credis fidos effuge tutus eris. Nusquam tuta fides.

Quint. Cicero ad fratrem, Teneto illud Epicharmium, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν nervos atque artus esse sapientiae.

## 11:16
A woman who guards her honor is gracious.

The saying warns that good morals are a greater recommendation than beauty, and that diligence excels riches.

The beneficent man [troubles] his own spirit.

It condemns ἄστοργίαν, just as it is also condemned in Romans, chapter one. Because God has imprinted a singular benevolence upon nature, and commands that we first embrace those of our own household. But στόργαι were imprinted upon the nature of men for this reason, that they might be reminders of the love of the eternal Father toward the Son and toward us.

The impious man does a perverse work. Although the wicked conspire among themselves, they will not escape; the seed of the just will escape.

It is a Hebrew phrase. Even if hand be joined to hand, or hand inserted into hand, yet the wicked will not be unpunished. Even if the impious make leagues, and as many as possible join themselves against truth and justice, yet they will finally be overturned. And the saying pertains to providence. It warns that the just are a care to God, even if they are sometimes afflicted, but in the end the wicked are divinely oppressed with punishments, according to many similar discourses, I have seen the impious exalted like the cedars of Lebanon, I passed by, and behold he was not. I have not seen the just forsaken.

A golden ornament in a swine's snout.

The sentiment is, good morals are a greater ornament than beauty.

SAYINGS CONCERNING LIBERALITY. Some by giving liberally yet become rich. Some by living sparingly yet become poor.

THAT is, It will be given to the giver. However, the doctrine concerning the promises of bodily goods handed down above must be repeated in this place, and faith must be confirmed, that they are not encountered by chance, and that they are to be sought and expected from God, and to be distributed just as God has commanded.

Latin:

Mulier quae tuetur honorem, gratiosa est.

Monet dictum, Bonos mores maiorem esse commendationem quam formam, Sedulitatem antecellere divitiis.

Beneficus suum ipsius genium.

Damnat ἄστοργίαν, Ut & Rom. primo damnatur. Quia Deus impressit naturae singularem benevolentiam, & praecipit, ut primum domesticos complectamur. Ideo autem στόργαι hominum naturae impressae sunt, ut sint commonefactiones de amore aeterni Patris erga Filium & erga nos.

Impius facit opus perversum. Mali quanquam inter se conspirant, non elabentur, semen iustum evadet.

Hebraica Phrasis est. Etiamsi manus ad manum sit, Vel manus manui inserta, tamen mali non erunt impuniti. Etiamsi impii faciant foedera, & quam plurimi se coniungant contra veritatem & iusticiam, tamen tandem evertentur. Et pertinet dictum ad providentiam. Monet Deo curae esse iustos, etiamsi aliquandis affliguntur, tandem vero divinitus opprimi poenis sceleratos, iuxta similes conciones multas, Vidi impium exaltatum, ut cedros Libani, Praeterii, & ecce nos erat. Non vidi iustum derelictum.

Ornamentum aureum in nare porci.

Sententia est, maius ornamentum sunt boni mores quam forma.

DICTA DE LIBERALITATE. Aliqui liberaliter dando tamen ditescunt. Aliqui parce vivendo tamen pauperes fiunt.

ID est, Danti dabitur. Repetenda est autem hoc loco doctrina de promissionibus bonorum corporalium supra tradita, & fides confirmanda, quod non casu obi+ciantur, & quod sint petenda & expectanda a Deo, & collocanda sicut Deus praecepit.

## 11:26
He who does not share grain, a curse will be upon him among the people, but a blessing will be upon him who sells.

Observe that a singular promise is added to him who sells grain in the time of famine to those in need. Observe also that contracts are confirmed.

Latin:

Qui non communicat frumenta, maledicetur ei in populo, & benedicetur vendenti.

Observa singularem promissionem additam esse vendenti frumentum tempore famis egentibus. Observa etiam confirmari contractus.

## 11:27
He who seeks good will find good, he who seeks evil will find evil.

The same sentiment is, Ἡ δὲ κακή βουλὴ τῷ βουλεύσαντι κακίστη.

Latin:

Quaerens bonum inveniet bonum, Quaerens malum inveniet malum.

Eadem sententia est, Ἡ δὲ κακή βουλὴ τῷ βουλεύσαντι κακίστη.

## 11:29
He who troubles his own house will inherit the wind. And the fool will be servant to the wise.

This is a precept against the morose, the stubborn, and the contentious, who, since they are unwilling to overlook and tolerate any errors of their own people and any offenses, stir up many unnecessary conflicts, and discord often grows horribly from the slightest beginnings, according to the saying of Homer, which Virgil rendered in these words,

Small at first through fear, soon it lifts itself into the air, and walks upon the ground, and hides its head among the clouds.

Therefore, ἐπιείκεια, must be cultivated, which is a moderation that forgives some faults and errors, and does not recall everything to the utmost bitterness of the law. Concerning this virtue it has been said, Forgive and it will be forgiven you. Nor can any society be long-lasting unless we mutually forgive many offenses, for the sake of common tranquility.

The meaning is therefore, He who troubles his own house, that is, the morose, the contentious, the stubborn, inherits the wind, that is, incurable discords, which are the destruction of both private families and Republics, according to that, For other kingdoms

Luxury turns with vices and hatreds and pride. The following saying is the antithesis of the preceding one.

Latin:

Turbans domum suam accersit ventos. Et stultus serviet sapienti.

Praeceptum est contra morosos, praefractos & contentiosos, qui cum nulla errata suorum & nullas offensiones dissimulare & tolerare volunt, multa non necessaria certamina movent, & saepe ex levissimis iniciis crescunt discordiae horribiliter, iuxta dictum Homeri, quod Vergilius his verbis reddidit,

Parva metu primo mox sese attollit in auras,Ingrediturque solo, & caput inter nubila condit.

Colenda est igitur ἐπιείκεια, quae est moderatio condonans aliqua delicta & errata, nec omnia ad summam acerbitatem iuris revocans. De hac virtute dictum est, Remittite & remittetur vobis. Nec potest ulla societas diuturna esse, nisi multas offensiones mutuo condonemus, propter communem tranquillitatem.

Est ergo sententia, Turbans domum suam, id est, morosus, contentiosus, praefractus, accersit ventos, id est, discordias insanabiles, quae sunt exitio & privatis familiis & Repuspublicis, Iuxta illud, Nam caetera regna

Luxuries vitiis odiisque superbia vertit.Sequens dictum est antithesis praecedentis.

## 11:30
The fruit of the righteous is a tree of life, and he who is wise wins souls.

That is, the counsels or actions of the righteous are salutary, like a tree of life. For such a one, namely the righteous and wise governor, by his moderation and gentleness retains concord, and heals many errors, just as it is said, Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the earth.

Latin:

Fructus iusti est lignum vitae, Et capit animas sapiens.

ID est, consilia seu actiones iusti sunt salutares, ut lignum vitae. Talis enim, videlicet iustus, & sapiens gubernator, sua moderatione & lenitate retinet concordiam, & multa errata sanat, sicut dictum est, Beati mites, quia possidebunt terram.

## 11:31
If the righteous in the earth is repaid, how much more the wicked and the sinner?

This admonition is to be numbered among the chief maxims of this book. And Peter recites it, For it is time that judgment begin from the house of God, but if first from us, what is the end of those who do not believe the Gospel of God?

It is, however, a doctrine concerning Providence, and a consolation concerning the scandal of the cross, affirming that a judgment remains, in which, without any doubt, the wicked are certainly going to pay the penalties. And an argument is instituted from the lesser, If the righteous are punished in this life, who nevertheless are amended, how much more will the wicked, who contumaciously and furiously persist in crimes, be punished? It is necessary, therefore, that after this life another judgment remains.

There are, however, two rules, of which one illuminates providence, the other obscures it.

The former is, Atrocious crimes are punished in this life with atrocious penalties. When this happens regularly, assent concerning providence is confirmed. And this rule is more clear, because it manifestly agrees with the law of God, and is the guide of discipline, because the law establishes penalties for contumacy.

But the other rule seems to disturb the order, namely, that the righteous in this life are for the most part oppressed by the unjust. Here human minds dispute in this way,

Providence distributes rewards to the righteous, penalties to the unjust, We see, however, that the righteous are oppressed, Therefore there is no providence.

This argument is explained especially in the Church of God, what God has revealed there, why the Church is subjected to the cross.

The major premise is true. Providence distributes rewards to the just, punishments to the unjust, namely in its own order. And indeed, so that the justice of God may be more illustrious, rewards and punishments are not momentary things, but perpetual and durable.

But to the minor premise it must be answered: In this life the just are oppressed, namely for a brief moment, and yet the rule is preserved regarding others. Soon those who oppress are punished, such as Pharaoh, Saul, and the like. But that God wills the just to be afflicted for some time, He has revealed the cause, because sin clings in these also, and especially God wills His wrath to be acknowledged in the Church against sins. Therefore He subjects it to the cross. There is therefore equality; God is angry at sin in His own assembly and in His enemies. Hence follows the inversion in Solomon. Since there is providence, and God is equal, and punishes the just in this life, much more will He punish the impious, even if they flourish for some time.

It is useful to think upon this doctrine concerning providence and the equality of God, and the testimonies of providence must be diligently collected, both as proposed in nature and in demonstrations, and in the examples of liberations in the Church, as in the leading out from Egypt, in the leading back from Babylon, etc. Finally, in the preservation of the universal Church of all times, which is nevertheless miraculously preserved amidst the horrible tumults of empires and domestic confusions.

Latin:

Cum iustus in terra persolvat, Quanto magis impius & peccator?

HAec admonitio inter praecipuas sententias huius libri numeranda est. Et recitat eam Petrus, Quia tempus est, ut incipiat iudicium a domo Dei, Si autem primum a nobis, quis finis est eorum, qui non credunt Evangelio Dei?

Est autem doctrina de Providentia, & consolatio de scandalo crucis, affirmans restare iudicium, in quo sine ulla dubitatione impii certissimas poenas daturi sunt. Et instituitur argumentum a minore, Cum iusti in hac vita puniantur, qui tamen emendantur, quanto magis impii contumaciter & furenter in sceleribus perseverantes punientur? Necesse est igitur post hanc vitam restare aliud iudicium.

Duae sunt autem regulae, quarum altera illustrat providentiam, altera obscurat.

Prior est, Atrocia delicta puniuntur in hac vita atrocibus poenis. Hoc cum ita regulariter fiat, confirmatur assensio de providentia. Estque magis perspicua haec regula, quia manifeste congruit cum lege Dei, & est rectrix disciplinae, quia lex constituit poenas contumaciae.

Sed altera regula videtur turbare ordinem, videlicet, quod iusti in hac vita plerumque opprimuntur ab iniustis. Hic disputant humanae mentes hoc modo,

Providentia distribuit iustis praemia, iniustis poenas,Videmus autem iustos opprimi,Non est igitur providentia.

Hoc argumentum praecipue in Ecclesia Dei explicatur, quid ibi patefecit Deus, quare Ecclesia cruci subiecta sit.

Maior vera est. Providentia distribuit premia iustis, poenas iniustis, scilicet suo ordine. Et quidem ut illustrior sit iustitia Dei, Praemia & poenae non sunt res momentaneae, sed perpetuae & durabiles.

Ad minorem vero respondendum est, In hac vita iusti sunt oppressi, scilicet brevi momento, & tamen servatur regula de aliis. Mox puniuntur ii, qui opprimunt, ut Pharao, Saul & similes. Quod autem iustos aliquandiu vult affligi Deus, causam patefecit, quia in his quoque haeret peccatum, & praecipue vult Deus agnosci iram suam in Ecclesia adversus peccata. Ideo subiicit eam cruci. Est igitur aequalitas, Irascitur Deus peccato in suo coetu & in hostibus. Hinc sequitur inversio in Salomone. Cum sit providentia, & Deus sit aequalis, ac puniat iustos in hac vita, multo magis puniet impios, etiamsi aliquandiu florent.

Hanc doctrinam de providentia & de aequalitate Dei cogitare utile est, & diligenter colligenda sunt testimonia providentiae, cum in natura & in demonstrationibus proposita, tum in exemplis liberationum in Ecclesia, Ut in eductione ex Aegypto, in reductione ex Babylone &c. Denique in universae Ecclesiae omnium temporum conservatione, quae inter horribiles tumultus imperiorum & confusiones domesticas mirabiliter tamen servatur.

## 12:1 Argument
CHAPTER XII.

Latin:

CAPUT XII.

## 12:1
HE who loves correction will be more prudent, but he who hates discipline is a fool.

This admonition concerning the utility of correction, and concerning avoiding obstinacy, is often repeated. Amendment in doctrine pertains to the virtue which is named truth. A lover of truth hears others, and yields when he understands himself to be overcome by true arguments. On the contrary, the proud value their own κενοδοξίαν, truth more. Thus also φιλόνεικοι they indulge their own nature more than they love the truth.

But let us know that it is the command of God to yield to the truth, as the law says, You shall not speak false testimony. Likewise, A lie is an abomination before the Lord. Likewise in the doctrine of the Gospel, He who shall have spoken blasphemy against the Holy Spirit, it shall not be forgiven him, etc.

Thus in punishments one must yield to justice, as it is written, Be humbled under the mighty hand of God. As it is justice when a citizen who has offended complies in punishment. Thus God wills David to obey in his punishment, and David, understanding that that obedience is just, wills the punishment, and bears it more moderately. As Daniel says, To You, O Lord, is justice, but to us is confusion of our face.

But as it is necessary to yield to truth and justice, so on the contrary it is a command to parents, magistrates, teachers, likewise friends, that each one in his own place should admonish those who err, and that they should rule discipline severely, as Solomon often commands concerning discipline in this very book. And Paul says, The law is a pedagogue unto Christ.

Latin:

QUI diligit castigationem, prudentior erit, At qui odit disciplinam, stultus est.

SAepe repetitur haec admonitio de utilitate castigationis, & de vitanda pertinacia. Emendatio in doctrina pertinet ad virtutem, quae nominatur veritas. Amans veritatis audit alios, & cedit, cum se veris argumentis vinci intelligit. Econtra, superbi pluris faciunt suam κενοδοξίαν, veritatem. Sic & φιλόνεικοι magis indulgent suae naturae, quam amant veritatem.

Sciamus autem mandatum Dei esse, cedere veritati, ut lex inquit, Non dicas falsum testimonium. Item, Mendacium abominatio est coram Domino. Item in doctrina Evangelii, Qui dixerit blasphemiam contra Spiritum sanctum, non remittetur ei &c.

Sic in poenis cedendum est iustitiae, sicut scriptum est, Humiliamini sub potenti manu Dei. Ut iustitia est, cum civis, qui deliquit, obtemperat in poena. Sic vult Deus obedire Davidem in sua poena, & David intelligens eam obedientiam iustam esse, vult poenam, & fert moderatius. Sicut inquit Daniel, Tibi Domine iustitia, nobis autem confusio faciei nostrae.

Ut autem necesse est cedere veritati & iustitiae, ita econtra mandatum est parentibus, magistratibus, docentibus, Item amicis, ut suo quisque loco moneat errantes, & ut disciplinam severe regant, ut saepe de disciplina in hoc ipso libro Salomon praecipit. Et Paulus inquit, Lex est paedagogus in Christum.

## 12:2
The good man will draw pleasure from God.

Some sayings subsequently repeat the common admonition concerning the providence and judgment of God, that it will be well for the just, the unjust will be punished. Which discourse is repeated so often for this reason, that minds may be well confirmed against the scandal of the cross, and against Epicurean arguments. Therefore let faith and the expectation of divine judgment shine in those who do rightly, so that their minds may not be broken on account of present miseries, but may expect liberation, and know that the prosperous affairs of the impious will not be long-lasting, according to the Psalm, Do not emulate.

Latin:

Bonus hauriet voluptatem a Deo.

Aliquot dicta deinceps repetunt communem admonitionem de providentia & iudicio Dei, Iustis bene erit, iniusti punientur. Quae concio ideo toties repetita est, ut bene confirmentur animi contra scandalum crucis, & contra Epicurea argumenta. Luceat igitur fides & expectatio divini iudicii in recte facientibus, ne frangantur animi propter praesentes aerumnas, sed expectent liberationem, & sciant res secundas impiorum non fore diuturnas, iuxta Psalmum, Noli aemulari.

## 12:5
The thoughts of the righteous are judgments. The industry of the wicked is fraud. Better is a lowly man who works for himself, than a boastful man who is in want.

Neighboring vices are boasting and vanity in speech, and ostentation in increasing displays beyond measure. Now, boasting is to praise oneself immoderately, and it fights against modesty, like Thraso in Terence. Vanity or arrogance is to praise false things, and it fights against truth. Just like the braggart soldier in Plautus, who on the same day in Cilicia and Macedonia routed many legions. Now, the sentence is well known, A lie is an abomination before the Lord. Wherefore this vanity is also a sin.

Neighboring is ostentation in displays, when preparations are increased beyond measure, so that others may think us, on account of this very appearance cast before the eyes, more powerful, wiser, stronger, or more honorable than we are. And a poor man who is glorious, or an ostentatious man, or a boastful man is called in Greek πτωχαλάζων.. Now, this ostentation fights against modesty and frugality. All these vices are condemned in Romans 1, when Paul lists the proud and the boastful; let us know that the same vices are prohibited here. A more useful citizen is a modest man, although lowly and working in his own place, Lucius Marcius, who after the Scipio brothers were killed retained Spain, than Antonius, who carries himself in a Thraso-like manner and disturbs many things.

Latin:

Cogitationes iustorum iudicia sunt. Industria impiorum fraus est. Melior est ignobilis laborans sibi, quam iactabundus inops.

Vicina vitia sunt iactantia & vanitas in sermone, & ostentatio in augendis pompis supra modum. Est autem iactantia sese immodice praedicare, & pugnat cum modestia, Ut Thraso apud Terentium. Vanitas seu arrogantia est falsa praedicare, & pugnat cum veritate. Sicut miles gloriosus apud Plautum, qui eodem die in Cilicia & Macedonia fudit multas legiones. Nota est autem sententia, Mendacium abominatio est coram Domino. Quare & haec vanitas peccatum est.

Vicina est ostentatio in pompis, quando supra modum augentur apparatus, ut alii putent nos propter hanc ipsam speciem offusam oculis, potentiores, sapientiores, fortiores aut honestiores esse sumus. Ac pauper gloriosus vel ostentator, vel iactabundus graece dicitur πτωχαλάζων. Pugnat autem haec ostentatio cum modestia & frugalitate. Omnia haec vitia damnantur Rom. primo, cum Paulus recenset superbos & iactabundos, eadem vicia & hic prohiberi sciamus. Vtilior civis est vir modestus, quanquam ignobilis & laborans suo loco, Lucius Marcius, qui interfectis fratribus Scipionibus retinuit Hispaniam, quam Antonius, qui thrasonice se effert, & multa turbat.

## 12:10
The righteous man acknowledges the souls of his beasts. But the bowels of the wicked are cruel.

Just as God commands justice, so on the contrary He prohibits cruelty, which is opposed to justice. Now, justice and beneficence are neighboring virtues, and it is necessary that both be exercised. But goodness, or beneficence, or equity adds something to justice, and sometimes softens the rigor of the law. And in the Epistle of James it is said, Mercy triumphs over judgment, that is, the rigor of the law. Thus here Solomon says that in a righteous man there is goodness and mercy, not only toward equals, but also toward inferiors, nay even toward beasts, for we ought to use, not abuse, even these as gifts of God.

Now, he signifies rightly that mercy is to be exercised toward servants. And let the sentence be transferred to governments. For although in this old age of the world governments are more squalid, and servitude is sadder, yet it is to be wished that some Princes and powerful men might agree with this sentence, that is, be affected by the misery of the people, and not exercise too great harshness. Just as Xenophon warns, saying, A good prince differs in nothing from a good father.

But Solomon signifies the causes. The wicked man, he says, is cruel, because the wicked man does not acknowledge God, nor does he use things in the order which God commands; he refers power to his own desire or utility, not to the salvation of the people. He does not consider that God is equal to the powerful and the lowest, and the avenger of injuries, nor does he acknowledge his own infirmity; therefore he rules proudly and harshly. In a righteous prince there are contrary causes.

Latin:

Agnoscit iustus iumentorum suorum animas. At viscera impiorum crudelia sunt.

Sicut praecipit Deus iusticiam, ita econtra prohibet crudelitatem, quae opposita est iusticiae. Sunt autem vicinae virtutes iusticia & beneficentia, & utramque exerceri necesse est. Addit autem aliquid ad iustitiam bonitas, seu beneficentia, seu aequitas, ac lenit interdum rigorem iuris. Et in Epistola Iacobi dicitur, Misericordia superet iudicium, id est, rigorem iuris. Ita hic dicit Salomon, In viro iusto bonitatem & misericordiam esse, non solum erga pares, sed etiam erga inferiores, imo & erga iumenta, nam & his, ut donis Dei, uti, non abuti debemus.

Significat autem recte, misericordiam erga servos exercendam esse. Et transferatur sententia ad imperia. Etsi enim in hac senecta mundi squalidiora sunt imperia, & tristior est servitus, Tamen optandum est, ut aliqui Principes & potentes congruant cum hac sententia, hoc est, afficiantur miseria populi, & non exerceant nimis magnam asperitatem. Sicut monet Xenophon, inquiens, Nihil differt bonus princeps a patre bono.

Sed Salomon causas significat. Impius, inquit, est crudelis, quia impius non agnoscit Deum, nec utitur rebus eo ordine, quo Deus praecipit, Refert potentiam ad suam cupiditatem vel utilitatem, non ad salutem populi. Non considerat Deum aequalem esse potentibus & infimis, & vindicem iniuriarum, nec agnoscit suam infirmitatem, ideo superbe & duriter imperat. In principe iusto contrariae causae sunt.

## 12:11
He who works his land will be satisfied with bread. He who follows vanities is a fool.

He repeats the precept concerning diligence, about which it has often been spoken. And it is useful for this doctrine to be inculcated to all, so that they may know that it is necessary for even this external life to be ruled by the word of God, and that it is a command of God that we have a choice of labors, so that a way of life may be instituted with a good conscience, and invocation may be able to be added, because labors are not happy, either undertaken outside of one's calling, or which are sustained without the invocation of God, according to that, Commit your way to God, and He will do it. Likewise, Without me you can do nothing.

Sedulity is a virtue which inclines us to do diligently what is necessary according to our calling. The extremes are sloth, which ceases entirely, and πολυπραγμοσύνη, which undertakes or stirs up many, various, unnecessary things outside of one's calling. This evil, even if it seems to have the appearance of a virtue, is nevertheless a great and pernicious --cium, highly displeasing to God, and in many ways disturbing the life of men, just as Pericles, Alcibiades, Brutus, Cassius, and countless similar people disturbed their states with unnecessary things. Therefore, let us keep ourselves within the boundaries which God has surrounded us with, namely, that we do our duty diligently, and indeed in great matters do only what is necessary. As Paul commands in Thessalo.: In this be ambitious, that you be quiet, and do your own things. Thus he joins two neighboring virtues, the pursuit of quietness and sedulity.

For the pursuit of quietness φιλησυχία is a virtue which keeps itself within the boundaries of its own of- fice, and does not rush into another's function. Neighboring this virtue is sedulity, which adds care, vigilance, diligence, and intention in one's own office. And although it is a great plague πολυπραγμοσύνη, in private affairs and in empires, it is nevertheless much more pernicious in religions, where correctly established doctrine is changed by curious and restless people.

The evil man is caught by perverse lips, But the just man escapes from adversity.

Some of the following sayings are precepts about truth, and condemnations of lying, and sophistries. Let this summary of many similar sentences be fixed in your minds: A lying lip is an abomination before the Lord. Let all Sophistry be understood, for lies painted with the appearance of truth are more pernicious than those which are manifest. But it must be diligently considered that he used an atrocious word when he says, It is an abomination before the Lord. For abomination signifies not just a simple rejection, but indignation mixed with some pain.

Latin:

Exercens terram suam, satiabitur panibus. Ad sectans inania, stultus est.

Repetit praeceptum de sedulitate, de qua saepe dictum est. Et utile est inculcari hanc doctrinam omnibus, ut sciant oportere etiam hanc externam vitam regi verbo Dei, & mandatum Dei esse, ut delectum habeamus laborum, ut bona conscientia institui genus vitae possit, & accedere possit invocatio, quia labores non sunt felices, vel extra vocationem suscepti, vel qui sustinentur sine invocatione Dei, luxta illa, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet. Item, Sine me nihil potestis facere.

Est autem Sedulitas virtus, quae inclinat, ut iuxta vocationem necessaria diligenter faciamus, Extrema sunt ignavia, quae prorsus cessat, & πολυπραγμοσύνη quae multa, varia, non necessaria extra vocationem suscipit aut movet. Hoc malum, & si speciem virtutis habere videtur, tamen magnum & perniciosum --cium est, valde displicens Deo, & multipliciter turbans vitam hominum, sicut Pericles, Alcibiades, Brutus, Cassius, & similes innumerabiles suas civitates motis non necessariis rebus turbarunt. Ideo contineamus nos intra metas, quas Deus nobis circundedit, videlicet ut nostrum officium diligenter faciamus, & quidem in rebus magnis tantum necessaria faciamus. Sicut Paulus praecipit ad Thessalo. In hoc ambiciosi sitis, ut quieti sitis, & propria faciatis. Ita duas vicinas virtutes coniungit, studium quietis & sedulitatem.

Est enim studium quietis φιλησυχία virtus, quae continet se intra metas sui of- cii, & non irruit in alienam functionem. Huic vicina virtus est sedulitas, quae addit curam, vigilantiam, diligentiam, intentionem in proprio officio. Etsi autem magna pestis est πολυπραγμοσύνη, in privatis rebus & in imperiis, tamen in religionibus multo perniciosior est, ubi recte constituta doctrina a curiosis et inquietis mutatur.

Pravis labiis capitur malus, Sed evadit ex adversitate iustus.

Aliquot sequentia dicta sunt praecepta de veritate, & vituperationes mendacii, & sophismatum. Sit autem infixa animis haec summa multarum similium sententiarum, Abominatio coram Domino labium mendax. Intelligatur autem omnis Sophistica, nam perniciosiora sunt mendacia specie veritatis fucata, quam quae manifesta sunt. Diligenter autem considerandum est, quod atroci verbo usus est, cum inquit, Abominatio coram Domino est. Nam abominatio significat non simplicem tantum reiectionem, sed indignationem mixtam cum dolore aliquo.

## 12:23
A prudent man conceals knowledge.

He forbids boasting and the ostentation of wisdom. There are various degrees of this vice . Some proclaim themselves openly, like Thraso in the Comedy. Others, even if they do not proclaim themselves openly, nevertheless show off their wisdom while they inject themselves into business, and think they see more than others, judging and reforming everything, as many now take upon themselves the judgment of controversies of dogmas which they do not understand. Likewise, there are many inept writers . On the contrary, the modest man is neither a herald of himself, nor injects himself into business, nor takes judgment upon himself, unless where his calling demands it, and concerning things rightly investigated and truly known, because such a person understands the dangers, how easy it is to slip and err, and what great evils often follow a not very great slip in appearance. Concerning this he says, The prudent man conceals knowledge, That is, he does not judge unless when it is necessary, and concerning things investigated and known.

Latin:

Homo astutus celat scientiam.

Prohibet iactantiam & ostentationem sapientiae. Huius vicii gradus varii sunt. Alii sese palam praedicant, ut Thraso in Comoedia. Alii etiamsi se ipsi palam non praedicant, tamen ostentant sapientiam, dum ingerunt se negociis, & putant se plus videre aliis, omnia iudicant & reformant, ut nunc multi sumunt sibi iudicium de dogmatum controversiis, quas non intelligunt. Item, multi inepti scriptores sunt. Econtra, modestus, nec praeco sui est, nec ingerit se negociis, nec sumit sibi iudicium, nisi ubi postulat vocatio, & de rebus recte investigatis, & vere cognitis, quia talis intelligit pericula, quam facile sit labi & errare, Et quanta mala sequantur saepe non magnum lapsum in speciem. De hoc dicit, Astutus celat scientiam, Id est, non iudicat, nisi cum necesse est, & de rebus inquisitis, & notis.

## 13:1 Argument
CHAPTER XIII.

Latin:

CAPUT XIII.

## 13:7
THERE is one who is rich, and has nothing. Another is poor, who nevertheless has a surplus.

This is an economic saying. For he distinguishes between economists and non-economists, that is, those who are frugal, using their things in order for necessity, from others who, although they have great resources, nevertheless are always in need and are in debt, either because they pour out much on useless pomp, or because they do not apply this diligence to make only necessary expenses, but consume without order from what is present, They also allow many things to slip away through negligence. These vices must be considered, because profusion and negligence also displease God, who wishes us to use His gifts reverently. And it is commanded above, Let your fountains be dispersed abroad, and you remain the master of them. Do not give your resources to the cruel.

Latin:

EST dives, & nihil habet. Alius pauper, cui tamen superest.

Oeconomicum dictum est. Discernit enim oeconomos & non oeconomis, hoc est, frugales, ordine utentes suis rebus ad necessitatem, ab aliis, qui quanquam habent magnas facultates, tamen semper egent & sunt obaerati, vel quia multa profundunt ad inutiles pompas, vel quia non adhibent hanc diligentiam, ut tantum necessarios sumptus faciant, sed sine ordine ex praesenti consumunt, multa etiam dilabi per negligentiam sinunt. Haec vitia consideranda sunt, quia etiam Deo displicent profusio & negligentia, qui vult nos suis donis reverenter uti. Et supra praeceptum est, Fontes tui deriventer foras, & tu Dominus eorum maneto. Ne des crudeli facultates tuas.

## 13:10
Contention among the proud.

He shows the source of many pernicious contentions. For he warns that there are some proud natures, that is, those who wish to excel others in some way; these, wherever they think their reputation is in danger, stir up struggles, and they are not ignited by the magnitude or smallness of things. Indeed, often the things are not at all worthy, for the sake of which tranquility is disturbed, but they fear for their own kingdom, and when struggles are stirred up, they do not yield, but rather inflame discords more and more, as it is said.

Small at first through fear, soon it lifts itself into the breezes, and walks upon the ground and hides its head among the clouds.

But where there are many such, there more and more atrocious and pernicious struggles arise, as between Marius and Sulla, Pompey and Julius. Concerning such, the Proverb says, Two mountains do not mix.

But since we know that God commands the pursuit of concord, and we know that discord is the source of the greatest calamities, let us not rashly stir up struggles. On the contrary, he says that wisdom is among those deliberating, that is, the modest, seeking the truth with a sincere heart for the sake of the glory of God, acquiesce in the truth after it has been revealed, and the vanquished obeys God, yields, and confesses his error, and both the victor and the vanquished give thanks to God for the truth having been illustrated, and they transfer it to use. In this way, God commands that there be disputing and deliberating, and he says that wisdom is found among such.

Resources are diminished by prodigality. But they are increased by thrift.

It is an economic precept that is not obscure, and it agrees with the saying of Cicero. Thrift is a great revenue. But let us know that thrift must be practiced not only for the sake of utility, but much more for the sake of the will of God, because God wishes us to use His gifts reverently. And it is written above, Do not give your resources to the cruel.

The instruction of the wise, etc.

The following sentences speak much about discipline, that is, about the universal governance of morals, according to the word of God, and about curbing wandering desires. As Paul says, Walk accurately, not as the foolish. And these precepts pertain in general to the title, concerning discipline, or concerning universal justice, and to the rule, Atrocious crimes are accompanied by atrocious punishments in this life. Afterwards, consolations are added here, that the just are to have rewards, even if they are delayed for some time. According to the Psalm, Do not be envious, etc.

Much food in the furrows of the poor.

There follows again an economic saying, Much food in the furrows of the poor, and others accumulate without measure. But he commands concerning diligence and moderation, according to that, Praise large farms, cultivate a small one. And the sentiment is, It is better to possess moderate resources, and to protect these with diligence and industry, than to buy many estates, or to acquire them in any way, which you cannot cultivate, etc.

But let the saying be transferred from economy to moderation in other things, that in governance we may be content with a humble or moderate position, and in this we may show faith and diligence. That, without any doubt, is much more useful than to seek superior positions. Just as the little verse also warns, which Aristotle cites.

Πολλὰ μεσοῖσιν ἄριστα, μέσος θέλω ἐν πόλει ἐῖναι

Let that also be accommodated here, You have obtained Sparta, adorn this.

Latin:

Inter superbos contentio.

Ostendit fontem multarum contentionum perniciosarum. Monet enim esse superbas aliquas naturas, id est, quae volunt antecellere aliis quo quomodo, hae ubicunque periclitari existimationem putant, movent certamina, nec rerum magnitudine vel parvitate accenduntur, Imo saepe res minime dignae sunt, quarum causa turbetur tranquillitas, sed regno suo metuunt, & motis certaminibus non cedunt, sed magis magisque inflammant discordias, Ut dicitur.

Parva metu primo mox sese attollit in auras,Ingrediturque solo & caput inter nubila condit.

Ubi autem multi sunt tales, eo plura & atrociora & perniciosiora certamina existunt, ut inter Marium & Syllam, Pompeium & Iulium. De talibus Proverbium inquit, Duo montes non miscentur.

Cum autem sciamus Deum praecipere studium concordiae, & sciamus discordiam esse fontem maximarum calamitatum, non moveamus temere certamina. Econtra dicit sapientiam esse inter deliberantes, hoc est, Modesti sincero corde veritatem quaerentes propter gloriam Dei, acquiescunt in veritate, postquam patefacta est, & victus obtemperat Deo, cedit & fatetur errorem, & simul victor & victus agunt Deo gratias, propter illustratam veritatem, & eam ad usum transferunt. Hoc modo praecipit Deus disputari & deliberari, & inter tales inquit sapientiam inveniri.

Facultates prodigalitate minuuntur. Parsimonia vero augentur.

Praeceptum oeconomicum est non obscurum, & congruit cum dicto Ciceronis. Parsimonia magnum vectigal. Sciamus autem non solum propter utilitatem, sed multo magis propter voluntatem Dei, parsimoniam praestandam esse, quia vult Deus nos reverenter uti donis suis. Et supra scriptum est, Ne des crudelibus facultates tuas.

Institutio sapientis &c.

Sequentes sententiae multae de disciplina loquuntur, Id est, de gubernatione morum universali, iuxta verbum Dei, & de frenandis vagis cupiditatibus. Sicut Paulus inquit, Ambulate accurate non ut fatui. Et pertinent in genere haec praecepta ad titulum, de disciplina, vel de iusticia universali, & ad regulam, Atrocia delicta comitantur atrocespoenae in hac vita. Postea hic additae sunt consolationes, quod iusti habituri sint praemia, etiamsi aliquandiu differuntur. Iuxta Psalmum, Noli aemulari &c.

Multum escae in sulcis pauperum.

Sequitur rursus dictum oeconomicum, Multi cibi in sulcis pauperum, & alii sine modo cumalant. Praecipit autem de diligentia & de mediocritate, iuxta illud, Laudato ingentia rura, Exiguum colito. Estque sententia, Melius esse possidere mediocres facultates, & has diligentia & industria tueri, quam multos fundos emere, aut quoquo modo parare, quos non possis colere &c.

Transferatur autem dictum ab oeconomia, ad mediocritatem in aliis rebus, Ut in gubernatione simus contenti humili aut mediocri loco, & in hoc praestemus fidem & diligentiam. Id sine ulla dubitatione multo est utilius, quam appetere superiora loca. Sicut & versiculus monet, quem citat Aristoteles.

Πολλὰ μεσοῖσιν ἄριστα, μέσος θέλω ἐν πόλει ἐῖναι

Huc & illud accommodetur, Spartam nactus es, hanc orna.

## 14:1 Argument
CHAPTER XIV.

Latin:

CAPUT XIIII.

## 14:3
In the mouth of the fool is the rod of pride.

Just as it behooves a musician to know the distinctions of his instruments, so in life a consideration is necessary to see what kind of people those are with whom we live, whether they are partners in the same business, or adversaries, or governors, so that we may consider where we can use the help of others and where we cannot. Let us also see who are most to be avoided and shunned, etc.

Here, however, Solomon speaks of avoiding a certain kind, which is called μωρόκακον,, from which there is much danger. It is such a kind, moreover, that is not sound in judgment, and does not rightly understand those very disputes about which it contends, but is fascinated by certain opinions of its own. Furthermore, it has a persuasion of wisdom and admires itself, and carries itself far above others. And if anyone disagrees, it rages with pride and hatred, nor does it seek the truth or the public welfare, but contends either for its own glory or for some advantage of its own, and is savage, malevolent, slanderous, and burns with a desire to do harm. There are many such monsters, which can be recognized from coarser examples.

But in empires there have been such fools, proud and cruel, Rehoboam, Claudius. Concerning such governors, partners, and adversaries, μωροκάκοις is said here: In the mouth of the fool is the rod of pride. That is, men not sound in judgment and arrogant are tyrannical, they command and demand harsh things, and have a desire to do harm, etc. And the same sentiment is soon repeated: If you meet a fool you will not find the lips of knowledge, that is, responding with discernment. That is, he who contends or does anything with a foolish person will receive blows. And this is the sum: Flee far from such as you see do not understand their own disputes, and are fascinated by evil persuasions, and yet trust in themselves greatly and carry themselves high; you will experience the savagery and desire to do harm of these if you do not avoid them.

Latin:

In ore stulti virga superbiae existit.

Sicut Musicum oportet nosse discrimina instrumentorum suorum, sic in vita necessaria est consideratio, videre quales sint, quibus cum vivimus, vel socii eorundem negociorum, vel adversarii, vel gubernatores, ut ubi aliorum auxiliis uti possimus, ubi non possimus, consideremus, Videamus etiam qui maxime vitandi & fugiendi &c.

Hic autem loquitur Salomon de vitando certo genere, quod vocatur μωρόκακον, unde multum est periculi. Est autem tale genus, quod non est sanum iudicio, & illas ipsas disputationes, de quibus certat, non recte intelligit, sed fascinatum est suis quibusdam opinionibus. Deinde habet persuasionem sapientiae & sese admiratur, ac longe se effert supra alios. Et si quis dissentit, furit superbia & odio, nec quaerit veritatem, aut publicam salutem, sed vel de sua gloria dimicat, vel de aliquo suo commodo, & est saevum, malevolum, maledicum, & ardet cupiditate nocendi. Talia monstra multa sunt, quae ex crassioribus exemplis agnosci possunt.

Sed in imperiis tales fuerunt stulti, superbi, immites, Roboam, Claudius. De talibus gubernatoribus sociis & adversariis μωροκάκοις hic dicitur, In ore stulti virga superbiae. Id est, Homines non sani iudicio & arrogantes sunt Tyrannici, dura imperant, & postulant, & habent nocendi cupiditatem &c. Ac eadem sententia mox repetita est, Si occurres stulto non invenies labia scientiae, id est cum delectu respondentia, Id est, qui cum fatuo contendit aut agit aliquid, plagas accipiet. Et summa haec est, Procul fugito tales, quos vides non intelligere suas disputationes, & fascinatos esse malis persuasionibus, & tamen sibi valde confidere et sese efferre, horum experieris saeviciam, & cupiditatem nocendi si eos non vitabis.

## 14:4
Where there are no oxen, the manger is empty.

This is a commendation of labor and the selection of persons. For the metaphor of oxen often signifies those who labor, that is, those performing a certain duty. This sentiment therefore warns that suitable men must be chosen for any business whatsoever, which, when it is not done, the administration is unhappy, as if it were said: A school does not flourish without suitable and faithful readers. Likewise, churches and cities are disturbed when governors are neither suitable nor faithful. But someone might say: Since all government is a most difficult thing, who will be suitable? etc. Here it must be answered, according to Paul: Moreover, it is required that we be faithful.

When, however, Paul names faithfulness, he embraces two things: the will to do rightly, and moderate industry, because it is also a matter of perverse will, and contrary to faithfulness, to bring affected ignorance. We therefore call him suitable who brings moderate industry and a good will.

Then to these two, to good will and moderate industry, must be added faith seeking and expecting the help of God, because without God no government is happy.
