As the Psalm says: Unless the Lord builds the house. Likewise: Without me you can do nothing. And Paul consoles us, saying: Your labor is not in vain in the Lord. Likewise: Our suitability is from God. That is, God helps those who obey their calling and keep themselves within the bounds of their calling and add invocation. This admonition is necessary in all of life, and it represses curiosity, and on the other hand consoles those who diligently serve their calling.

When the heart is tormented, it is not helped by consolations that are offered from the outside.

It is a description of a fact, that when the heart is occupied by true sadness, external consolations do not delight, which do not remove the proper cause of this pain, as to a needy anxious person, sadness is not much eased unless money is given. He warns, however, that the tranquility of the heart must be retained, because without this other things do not delight. Many other sayings pertain to this, as below, A sad spirit dries up the bones. And Ecclesiasticus 30. Give not sadness to your soul. Item, Sadness kills many, & there is no profit in it. Item, The gladness of the heart is life. Item, ὼ μῶρε, θύμος ἐν κακοῖς ὁυ σύμφερον.

But how is the tranquility of the mind or heart retained? Here the whole doctrine concerning consolations must be known. And this saying pertains to the virtue which is called Patience. These Philosophical consolations are distributed into 5. chapters.

The first is Necessity, which is resisted in vain.

The second, Virtue is to be preferred to fortune & pain. As Cato does not act rightly, losing virtue, namely justice, when he inflicts death upon himself.

The third, A good conscience, according to that, It is something for great crimes to be absent from evils.

The fourth, Examples, which warn that we should bear things similar to those which other honest men have borne, lest we seem to demand inequality.

The fifth, Final causes, lest pain hinder better counsels. Item, Lest it harm the Republic.

These causes all somehow repress sadness, but the object of joy is a good thing, therefore pain conquers, until help or liberation is shown.

These things, however, in great pains, are not shown in philosophy, but in divine promises. Let us learn therefore to flee to the consolations which God, not without grave cause, has published through the Son & wishes to be valid & effective.

These Evangelical consolations are distributed into 7. chapters.

The first, is Necessity on account of the command of God, according to that, Humble yourselves under the mighty hand of God.

The second, Virtue is to be preferred to fortune & pain.

The third, A good conscience.

The fourth, The help & presence of God. This Evangelical consolation shows good things. Therefore pain is sensibly conquered, & we feel that we are sustained and able to bear torments.

The fifth, The hope of liberation or at least of mitigation. According to that, Many are the tribulations of the just, but the Lord will deliver them out of them all.

The sixth, Examples, We ought to be made conformable to the image of the Son of God.

The seventh, Final causes, lest through impatience we accumulate sins &c.

These sources of consolations are often to be thought upon, & Philosophy is to be compared with the Gospel, and it must be known that by these three things the tranquility of the heart is chiefly retained, a good conscience, faith, and the hope of divine help, and of mitigation or liberation. Concerning this good it is said in the next chapter, A good heart is a continual feast.

It must be known, however, that the tranquility of the heart is to be sought not only on account of utility, because sadness is destructive, but also because the Tranquility of the heart is a virtue, namely, either joy in God, & thanksgiving, or patience. For in sadness there is for the most part Impatience, which is indignation & roaring against God or men, complaining that God is harsher than his immense goodness should be, or that he is unequal, It fights also with humility, Saul thinks that he deserved better things &c. Finally, in impatience many vices run together. Therefore Paul He says Coloss. 3. Let the peace of God rule your hearts, that is, the tranquility born from a good conscience, faith, and hope.

There is a way that seems right before a man, but the end thereof is the way of death.

There are two precepts concerning diligence or prudence. In the one, he warns that we should take care not to be ruled by errors. In the other, that we should not be ruled by the allurements of desires. Now, the causes of errors in the governance of morals are either to be entirely ignorant of the rule of truth, namely the doctrine handed down by God, or to know it but to have it corrupted by opinions taken from long custom, or from the multitude, or from imaginations which we ourselves, admiring our own wisdom, fashion. As many tricksters, heretics, and others corrupt the truth handed down divinely with certain imaginations of their own, and, fascinated by their own persuasions, they either sow or confirm errors. Furthermore, in deliberations, many things move men without necessity, incited by the appearance of justice, just as Pericles moves a war that is not necessary, etc.

To all these kinds belongs the saying which Plato cites from Simonides, δόξα βιάζεται τὴν ἀληθείαν. Opinion brings force to bear upon truth.

And this evil dominates in the greatest part of the human race, because very many neglect the sources of doctrine, or follow custom, or beget certain imaginations of their own, which they love most tenderly.

Since, therefore, there are very many errors in life, and these are the cause of great calamities, Solomon warns that we should be cautious, that we should seek the truth according to the rule. Thy word is a lamp to my feet, O Lord. And in deliberations, we should not be driven by our own desires, or opinions, or hopes, but we should do only what is necessary according to our calling. And indeed, let us hear the counsels of the experienced, and let us add faith and invocation, so that God may show us counsels and rule the outcomes. According to those sayings, Without me you can do nothing. Item, Be subject to God, and pray to him. Item, Commit thy way to God, and he will act.

The precept pertains to diligence or prudence, the parts of which are especially the care of seeking the truth and the care of maintaining one's calling.

Laughter will be mingled with sorrow, and the end of joy will be grief.

After inordinate joy follows sorrow.

He warns that we should not, driven by the allurements of pleasures, bring calamities upon ourselves. Many sayings agree with this sentiment.

Avoid pleasures; pleasure bought with pain is harmful.

Grief is the companion of pleasure. Mart. Joys do not remain, but fly away as fugitives.

The multitude of the people is the glory of the prince. And without the people is the confusion of the prince.

Let the end for the head of a household be that he may justly rule his family and do good to it, and when he does that, he is loved by his own. And two things signify that he is loved. When he has been away from home, all run to him upon his return, and joyfully surround him. Item, The bond of concord is the love of the head of the household. Conversely, when a bad head of a household returns home, all shudder and flee, and the very walls seem sadder. This image is to be well considered and applied to princes. Let this also be their end, that they may justly rule and do good to the human race, according to that saying, Justice establishes the throne. And Archytas he said, ἀρχὴν δεῖ οὐ μόνον δίκαιαν ἀλλὰ καὶ φιλάνθρωπον εἶναι And when he does this, he is loved. And the signs of love are that the citizens run to him, wherever there is either an occasion or a necessity, they rejoice at his arrival, and in turn they strive to show signs of benevolence, and they are affected by his dangers as if they were their own, and they willingly protect him, and by authority and benevolence he retains concord. Paul describes such a governor, saying, Who is my crown if not you, and who is your crown if not I?

A sound heart is the life of the flesh.

This is the praise of a good conscience. Just as he says later in chapter 15, A good heart is a continual feast, so here he says, A sound heart is the life of the flesh. It is necessary, however, that the whole doctrine concerning not wounding the conscience always be before everyone, in which doctrine this must first be known, that it is a most severe command of God that a rational creature should not act against conscience, that is, that he should not violate the law of God knowingly, because a violation committed against conscience is a contempt of God. Therefore Paul says, Wage a good warfare, holding faith and a good conscience.

Next, you should consider the order which, even in depraved nature, still remains, that in all men there follow horrible pains after offenses, which are sadder than death itself, and in the impious they are the beginning of eternal punishments.

Thirdly, you should know for certain that as long as the wound of the conscience is not healed, God is angry with such people who have wounded consciences, and they live without God and without invocation, and they are unable to ask for and expect help from God.

These huge evils accompany falls against conscience. On the contrary, however, a good conscience, and indeed one raised up by faith, is joyful, and invoking God, it can rest in God, ask for and expect help, governance, and defense, etc. As John says, If our heart does not condemn us, we dare to ask, that is, if we do not have the purpose of sinning. This common doctrine concerning conscience ought to be most known to everyone, and let it be the guide of life, etc.

Latin:

Ubi non sunt boves, praesepe vacuum est.

Commendatio est laboris & delectus personarum. Nam metaphora bovum saepe significat laborantes, hoc est, certo munere fungentes. Monet igitur haec sententia, deligendos esse idoneos homines ad quaelibet negocia, quod cum non fit, infoelix est administratio, ut si dicatur, Schola sine idoneis & fidelibus lectoribus non floret. Item, Ecclesiae, civitates turbantur, quando gubernatores nec idonei sunt, nec fideles. Sed dicat aliquis, Cum omnis gubernatio res difficilima sit, quis erit idoneus? &c. Hic respondendum est, iuxta Paulum, Caeterum hoc requiritur fideles ut simus.

Cum autem Paulus nominat fidelitatem, duo complectitur, voluntatem recte faciendi, & mediocrem industriam, quia hoc quoque perversae voluntatis est, & contra fidelitatem, afferre affectatam ignorantiam. Vocamus igitur Idoneum, qui mediocrem iudustriam adfert, & bonam voluntatem.

Deinde ad haec duo, ad bonam voluntatem, & mediocrem industriam, accedere debet fides petens & expectans auxilium Dei, quia sine Deo nulla gubernatio est felix. Sicut Psalm. dicit, Nisi Dominus aedificaverit domum. Item, Sine me nihil potestis facere, Et Paulus consolatur nos inquiens, Non est irritus labor vester in Domino. Item, Idoneitas nostra ex Deo est. Id est, Deus adiuvat obtemperantes vocationi, & se intra metas vocationis continentes & addentes invocationem, Haec admonitio in omni vita necessaria est, & reprimit curiositatem, & econtra consolatur eos, qui suae vocationi diligenter serviunt.

Cum cor angitur, non iuvatur solatiis quae extrinsecus offe-runtur.

Facti descriptio est, quando cor vera moesticia occupatum est, non delectant externa solatia, quae non tollunt propriam causam huius doloris, Ut egenti anxio, non multum lenitur moesticia nisi data pecunia. Monet autem retinendam esse cordis tranquillitatem, quia sine hac non delectant res caeterae, Huc multa alia dicta pertinent, ut infra, Spiritus tristis exiccat essa. Et Ecclesiastici 30. Tristiciam ne des animae tuae. Item, Multos occidit tristicia, & non est utilitas in ea. Item, Iucunditas cordis est vita. Item, ὼ μῶρε, θύμος ἐν κακοῖς ὁυ σύμφερον.

Sed quomodo retinetur animi seu cordis tranquillitas? Hic tota doctrina de consolationibus scienda est. Et pertinet hoc dictum ad virtutem, quae vocatur Patientia. Sunt autem Philosophicae consolationes hae distributae in 5. capita.

Primum est Necessitas, cui frustra repugnatur.

Secundum, Virtus anteferenda fortunae & dolori. Ut Cato non recte facit, amittens virtutem, scilicet iusticiam, cum sibi mortem consciscit.

Tertium, Bona conscientia, iuxta illud, Est aliquid magnis crimen abesse malis.

Quartum, Exempla, quae monent ut feramus similia eorum, quae alii honesti homines tulerunt, ne inaequalitatem postulare videamur.

Quintum, Causae finales, ne dolor impediat meliora consilia. Item, Ne noceat Reipublicae.

Hae causae omnes utcunque reprimunt moesticiam, sed obiectum gaudii est res bona, ideo vincit dolor, donec non ostenditur auxilium, aut liberatio.

Haec autem in magnis doloribus, non ostenduntur in philosophia, sed in promissionibus divinis. Discamus igitur confugere ad consolationes, quas Deus non sine gravi causa per Filium edidit & vult ratas & efficaces esse.

Sunt autem hae consolationes Evangelicae distributae in 7. capita.

Primum, est Necessitas propter mandatum Dei, iuxta illud, Humiliamini sub potenti manu Dei.

Secundum, Virtus anteferenda fortunae & dolori.

Tertium, Bona conscientia.

Quartum, Auxilium & praesentia Dei. Haec Evangelica consolatio ostendit res bonas. Ideo dolor sensibiliter vincitur, & sentimus nos sustentari et perferre posse cruciatus.

Quintum, Spes liberationis aut certe mitigationis. Iuxta illud, Multae tribulationes iustorum, sed ex omnibus liberabit eos Dominus.

Sextum, Exempla, Oportet nos fieri conformes imaginis Filii Dei.

Septimum, Causae finales, ne per impatientiam cumulemus peccata &c.

Hi fontes consolationum saepe cogitandi sunt, & conferenda Philosophia cum Evangelio, ac sciendum, his tribus rebus praecipue retineri tranquillitatem cordis, bona conscientia, fide, & spe auxilii divini, & mitigationis aut liberationis. De hoc bono dicitur in proximo capite, Bonum cor iuge convivium est.

Sciendum est autem, non solum propter utilitatem appetendam esse cordis tranquillitatem, quia moestitia est exitialis, sed etiam, quia Tranquillitas cordis virtus est, scilicet, vel laeticia in Deo, & gratiarum actio, vel patientia. Nam in moestitia plerumque est Impatientia, quae est indignatio & fremitus adversus Deum aut homines, conquerens Deum esse duriorem quam decebat immensam bonitatem, aut esse inaequalem, Pugnat & cum humilitate, cogitat Saul se meliora meritum esse &c. Denique in impatientia concurrunt multa vitia. Ideo Paulus inquit Coloss. 3. Pax Dei gubernet corda vestra, id est, Tranquillitas orta a bona conscientia, fide & spe.

Est via recta coram homine, cuius exitus tamen est via mortis.

Dvo praecepta sunt de diligentia vel prudentia, In altero monet, ut caveamus ne regamur erroribus. In altero ne regamur illecebris cupiditatum, Causae autem errorum in gubernatione morum sunt, vel prorsus nescire regulam veritatis, scilicet doctrinam a Deo traditam, vel nosse eam, sed corruptam opinionibus sumptis a longa consuetudine, vel multitudine, vel imaginationibus, quas ipsi admirantes nostram sapientiam fingimus, Ut multi praestigiatores, haeretici, & alii veritatem divinitus traditam, suis quibusdam imaginationibus corrumpunt, & fascinati suis persuasionibus, vel serunt, vel confirmant errores. Praeterea in deliberationibus multa movent homines sine necissitate incitati specie iuris, ut Pericles movet bellum non necessarium &c.

Ad haec genera omnia pertinet dictum, quod plato ex Simonide citat, δόξα βιάζεται τὴν ἀληθείαν. Opinio vim adfert veritati.

Et hoc malum dominatur in maxima parte generis humani, quia plurimi negligunt fontes doctrinae, vel sequuntur consuetudinem, vel gignunt suas quasdam imaginationes, quas tenerrime amant.

Cum igitur plurimum sit errorum in vita, & hi causa sint magnarum calamitatum, monet Salomon, ut cauti simus, quaeramus veritatem, iuxta regulam. Lucerna pedibus meis verbum tuum Domine, Et in deliberationibus, non nostris cupiditatibus, aut opinionibus, aut spebus impellamur, sed tantum necessaria iuxta vocationem faciamus. Et quidem audiamus consilia peritorum, & addamus fidem & invocationem, ut Deus monstret consilia, & regat exitus. Iuxta illa dicta, Sine me nihil potestis facere. Item, Subditus esto Deo, & ora eum. Item, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet.

Pertinet praeceptum ad diligentiam, vel prudentiam, cuius praecipue partes sunt, cura quaerendae veritatis, & cura tuendae vocationis.

Risus dolore miscebitur, & extrema gaudii luctus occupabit.

Post inordinatam laeticiam sequitur dolor.

Monet ne illecebris voluptatum impulsi, accersamus nobis calamitates. Cum hac sententia multa dicta congruunt.

Serne voluptates, nocet empta dolore voluptas.

Voluptatis comes moeror est.Mart.Gaudia non remanent, sed fugitiva volant.

Frequentia populi est decus principis. Et absque populo confusio principis.

Patrifamilias finis sit, ut familiam iuste regat, & ei benefaciat, idque cum facit, diligitur a suis. Et diligi eum duae res significant. Cum abfuit domo, redeunti occurrunt omnes, & laeti circundant. Item, Concordiae vinculum est amor patrisfamilias. Econtra cum malus paterfamilias domum redit, cohorrescunt omnes & diffugiunt, & ipsi parietes iam tristiores videntur. Haec imago bene consideranda est, & ad principes accommodanda. Sit his quoque finis, ut iuste regant & benefaciant generi humano, Iuxta illud Iusticia stabilit thronum. Et Architas dixit, ἀρχὴν δεῖ οὐ μόνον δίκαιαν ἀλλὰ καὶ φιλάνθρωπον εἶναι Et cum hoc facit, diligitur. Et signa dilectionis sunt, quod ad eum concurrunt cives, ubicunque vel occalio vel necessitas est, laetantur eius adventu, & vicissim ostendere signa benevolentiae conantur, & periculis eius afficiuntur ut suis, & libenter eum tuentur, & authoritate & benevolentia retinet concordiam. Talem gubernatorem describit Paulus inquiens, Quis est corona mea nisi vos, & quis est corona vestra nisi ego?

Vita carnis est cor sanum.

Laus est bonae conscientiae. Sicut postea dicit cap. 15. Bonum cor est iuge convivium, Ita hic inquit, Sanum cor est vita carnis. Necesse est autem semper in conspectu omnibus esse totam doctrinam de non saucianda conscientia, in qua doctrina, hoc primum sciendum est, severissimum Dei mandatum esse, ne creatura rationalis contra conscientiam faciat, Id est, ne violet legem Dei sciens, quia violatio contra conscientiam facta contemptus Dei est. Ideo Paulus inquit, Milita bonam militiam, retinens fidem & bonam conscientiam.

Deinde consideres ordinem, qui ad huc, quamvis in depravata natura, manet, quod in omnibus hominibus sequuntur horrendi dolores post delicta qui sunt tristiores ipsa morte, & in impiis sunt initium aeternarum poenarum.

Tertio, scias certo, donec non sanatur vulnus conscientiae, Deum irasci talibus, qui habent sauciatas conscientias, & vivere eos sine Deo & sine invocatione, & non posse a Deo petere & expectare auxilium.

Haec ingentia mala comitantur lapsus contra conscientiam. Econtra vero bona conscientia, & quidem erecta fide, laeta est, & invocans Deum, potest acquiescere in Deo, petere & expectare auxilium, gubernationem & defensionem &c. Ut Ioannes inquit, Si cor nostrum non reprehenderit nos, audemus petere, Id est, si non habemus propositum peccandi. Haec communis doctrina de conscientia notissima omnibus esse debet, & sit rectrix vitae &c.

## 14:32
The just man hopes in his death.

In this place an express sentence is placed concerning the life that follows after this mortal life, and concerning the future judgment. For he affirms that the just have hope even then, when they are killed by tyrants, because after this life punishments of the impious will follow, and the Church will be in the fellowship of God and in glory, etc.

Here, therefore, the distinction between temporal and eternal promises must be considered. And the promise of eternal salvation must be held, even when deliverances do not happen in this mortal life, for the promises of temporal deliverances are conditional, if God so wills. As David says, If he wills, he will bring me back, etc., but the promise of reconciliation and eternal salvation is not conditional, but it is a divine command that we should always embrace it by faith. Therefore Job also says, Even if he kills me, I will hope in him. Nevertheless, I will argue my ways before -o , and he himself will be my savior, nor will hypocrites enter before him. That is, even if I lose everything in this life, and this very bodily life, yet I know it is the will of God that I hold this faith, that I am a care to him, that I am received into grace, that I am an heir of eternal salvation, and by this faith and hope I sustain myself. And indeed this very consolation is the beginning of eternal life. I do not judge concerning the will of God from these calamities of mine, but from his promise. I know that calamities are not signs of rejection.

After this consolation he adds a confession, I will argue my ways before him. That is, I acknowledge that I am a sinner, and in his judgment guilty. And yet, trusting in his promise and mercy, I know he will be a savior to me.

Latin:

Sperat in morte sua iustus.

Hoc loco expressa sententia ponitur de vita secutura post hanc mortalem vitam, & de futuro iudicio Affirmat enim iustos spem habere etiam tunc, cum interficiuntur a Tyrannis, quia post hanc vitam poenae secuturae sint impiorum, & Ecclesia futura in consuetudine Dei & in gloria &c.

Hic igitur considerandum est discrimen temporalium & aeternarum promissionum. Ac promissio aeternae salutis retinenda est, etiam cum liberationes in hac mortali vita non contingunt, nam promissiones liberationum temporalium sunt conditionales, si ita velit Deus. Sicut inquit David, Si volet, reducet me &c. sed promissio reconciliationis & aeternae salutis non est conditionalis, sed mandatum divinum est, ut semper eam fide amplectamur. Ideo & Iob inquit, Etiamsi me occiderit, sperabo in eum. Veruntamen vias meas coram -o arguam, & ipse erit salvator meus, nec hypocritae intrabunt coram eo. Id est, Etiamsi amitto omnia in hac vita, & hanc ipsam vitam corporalem, tamen scio voluntatem Dei esse, ut hanc fidem retineam, me ipsi curae esse, me recipi in gratiam, me esse haeredem aeternae salutis, & hac fide & spe me sustento. Et quidem haec ipsa consolatio initium est vitae aeternae. Non iudico de voluntate Dei ex istis calamitatibus meis, sed ex promissione ipsius. Scio calamitates non esse signa reiectionis.

Post hanc consolationem addit confessionem, Vias meas arguam coram eo. Id est, agnosco me esse peccatorem, & in ipsius iudicio reum. Et tamen confisus eius promissione & misericordia scio eum mihi fore salvatorem.

## 15:1 Argument
CHAPTER XV.

Latin:

CAPUT XV.

## 15:1
A soft answer mitigates wrath, a harsh word provokes wrath.

He gives instruction concerning the pursuit of peace and the manner of maintaining peace. And pertains to the saying, Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called the children of God. But here is properly condemned the vice which is called φιλονεικία, which is the desire for contention, which easily for any cause moves struggles on account of moroseness or irascibility, arisen from pride or insolence, and easily irritates others and nowhere yields to the one who irritates. Nor does it only rush against enemies, but also against friends, as it is said of Pericles, that he acted as Peleus did, who, seeking a wild beast, killed his friend. Such are many restless and quarrelsome oratorical natures, wherefore they also ignite great public discords, therefore the saying exists in Stobaeus in these words, τὰ μεγάλα κακά κακά οὐκ ἄλλωσι ἔκ φιλονεικίας συνίσταται.

This vice must be curbed and repressed, therefore Solomon prohibits the inflaming of the angry and warns that wrath must be softened by a softer answer, just as it is also signified in the Pythagorean saying. Do not stir the fire with a sword. And the verses of Euripides are praised.

Of two speaking, when one is angry, he who does not contend against the words is the wiser.

But here many cry out in opposition, Reprove in season, out of season, etc. And zeal is used as a pretext for private diseases, pride, moroseness, irascibility, the desire for vengeance. But let good minds know that one must not fight for all causes, and let them hold the rule . Ready to give an account of the Doctrine, to all who ask. etc.

That is, let Doctors teach rightly in their place, and indeed the necessary things, which conduce to edification, let them not litigate about side-issues. Let hearers asked about doctrine answer. Let us modestly purge ourselves when accused of atrocious crimes. But let us dissimulate many lighter insults with great patience, and overwhelm them with silence, and let us take care that they be refuted by life and morals themselves, not by words. As Cicero says, It goes badly with me, if my speech purges me rather than my life.

Often also unnecessary disputes should be omitted. According to that, Bear one another's burdens. Which saying warns that the infirmities of others must be borne, not pernicious ones. Likewise, Careful to keep the unity of the spirit, through humility and gentleness and patience.

Finally, this whole Doctrine concerning the pursuit of peace, and the manner of maintaining peace, has been often repeated in divine precepts. But the manners are, as Paul says, Humility, gentleness and patience, which Solomon comprehends here, when he says, A soft answer. Therefore the desire for contention must be curbed, and it must be wisely seen where, when, and about what matters it is necessary to fight.

And in Solomon the following sentence is an interpretation of the former, The tongue of the wise makes knowledge good. That is, Knowledge, admonition, doctrine, profits more, becomes more pleasing and more commendable, when the wise man sees where, at what time, among what hearers, it is necessary or useful for an admonition or doctrine to be proposed.

Latin:

Mollis responsio mitigat iram, Sermo asper provocat iram.

Praecipit de studio pacis & de modo retinendae pacis. Et pertinet ad dictum, Beati pacifici, quia filii Dei vocabuntur. Proprie autem damnatur hic vitium, quod vocatur φιλονεικία, quod est cupiditas contentionis, quae facile quavis de causa movet certamina propter morositatem vel iracundiam, ortam ex superbia vel insolentia, & facile irritat alios & nusquam cedit irritanti. Nec tantum in hostes, sed etiam in amicos incurrit, ut de Pericle dictum est, facere eum sicut Peleus fecit, qui petens feram, amicum interfecit. Tales sunt multae oratoriae naturae inquietae & rixatrices, quare & magnas discordias publicas accendunt, Ideo dictum apud Stobeum extat his verbis, τὰ μεγάλα κακά κακά οὐκ ἄλλωσι ἔκ φιλονεικίας συνίσταται.

Hoc vitium frenandum & reprimendum est, ideo Salomon prohibet incendi iratos & monet leniendam esse iram molliore responsione, sicut & in Pythagorico dicto significatum est. Ignem gladio ne fodito. Et laudantur versus Euripidis.

δυοῖν λεγόντωιν θατέρου θυμουμένου,ὁ μὴ ἀντιτείνων τοῖς λογοις σοφώτερος

Sed hic contra vociferantur multi, Increpa opportune, importune &c. Et praetexitur zelus privatis morbis, superbiae, morositati, iracundiae, cupiditati vindictae. Sed sciant bonae mentes non de omnibus causis pugnandum esse, & regulam teneant. Parati ad rationem reddendam de Doctrina, omnibus qui interrogant. &c.

Id est, Doctores suo loco recte doceant, & quidem necessaria, quae ad aedificationem conducunt, non litigent de Parergis. Auditores interrogati de doctrina respondeant. Accusati de atrocibus criminibus modeste nos purgemus. Multa vero convicia leviora, magna patientia dissimulemus, & silentio obruamus, & curemus, ut ipsa vita & moribus refutentur, non verbis. Sicut Cicero inquit, Male mecum agitur, si me oratio potius quam vita mea purgat.

Saepe etiam omittantur non necessariae disputationes. Iuxta illud, Alter alterius onera portate. Quod dictum monet, ferendas esse alioquorum infirmitates, non perniciosas. Item, Solliciti servare unitatem spiritus, per humilitatem et lenitatem & patientiam.

Denique tota haec Doctrina de studio pacis, & modo retinendae pacis saepe repetita est in praeceptis divinis. Sunt autem modi, ut Paulus inquit, Humilitas, lenitas & patientia, quos hic comprehendit Salomon, cum inquit, Mollis responsio. Frenanda est igitur cupiditas contentionis, & sapienter videndum, ubi, quando, de quibus rebus necesse sit pugnare.

Et in Salomone sequens sententia interpretatio est prioris, Lingua sapientum scientiam facit bonam. Id est, Scientia, commonefactio, doctrina, plus prodest, fit gratior & commendatior, cum sapiens videt, ubi, quo tempore, apud quos auditores, necessarium aut utile sit proponi commonefactionem aut doctrinam.

## 15:8
The sacrifice of the wicked is an abomination to the Lord. But the prayer of the upright pleases him.

There are three degrees of worship: Ceremonies, honest moral works, and faith. It is necessary that the distinctions and use of these degrees be rightly understood in the Church. God wills that there be some Ceremonies, and He Himself instituted certain ones, so that the external signs of the promises might be seen, and the promise might sound openly among the human race. For God does not will that the Church be in hiding, but He wills that it be recognized and distinguished from other nations by voice and ceremonies. Furthermore, since there is intellect and a love of order in human nature, some distinction of exercises is ordered.

Therefore, it must be prudently considered why Ceremonies are necessary and how they should be used. But because men are prone to false worship, and especially to the abuse of ceremonies, having lost the knowledge of the promises and with the light of faith extinguished, they turn their eyes to Ceremonies, and they think that these merit the remission of sins before God, and are Righteousness. This persuasion wanders most widely among the human race at all times. Thus the Heathens felt about their sacrifices, thus a great part of the people of Israel felt, thus the Pharisees taught, thus in the Church the Monks taught concerning the Mass, concerning vows, and other ceremonies.

The Prophets, however, most sharply tax this common error, as in Hosea 6: I desire mercy and not sacrifice, and the knowledge of God more than burnt offerings. Likewise, The true worshippers will worship the Father in spirit and truth, That is, in true fear and true faith. Indeed, even the Heathens reproved this superstition, which observes ceremonies without good moral works, as Plato says, It is necessary to worship God οὐ σχήμασι τεχνάξοντας, ἄλλα ἀλήθεια τιμῶντας ἄρετην. Although, however, it is true that the righteousness of a good conscience must be present at the same time, yet this legal doctrine does not yet suffice, but the knowledge of the divine promise and faith must shine forth, that is, trust in the Mediator, so that hearts may not indulge in doubt, but may approach God, and we may truly establish that we are received and heard on account of the Son, even though we are unworthy, and hearts may acquiesce in this consolation, and await the help of God.

God demands this faith and hope in invocation, as Matth. 21. Whatever you ask in prayer, believing, you will receive. Likewise, Pray without anger and without hesitation. Likewise, he who trusts in the Lord will not be confounded, etc.

Then, therefore, invocation and sacrifices are pleasing, when faith shines forth, and the righteousness of a good conscience is present, That is, repentance or a good purpose. When faith does not shine forth, Ceremonies are empty, and when the righteousness of a good conscience is not present, God is mocked by the simulation of Ceremonies. Therefore it is said here, The sacrifice of the wicked is an abomination before the Lord.

Observe, however, the diligence in the Antithesis; concerning the sacrifices of the wicked, he retains the name signifying the external thing, The sacrifice of the wicked. But concerning the true worship of those rightly invoking, he says, The prayer of the upright. For the invocation of the wicked is an empty sound. Only those invoke who bring repentance and faith to God. As Paul says, How will they invoke, if they do not believe? Likewise, Having such a high priest, let us approach with confidence to the throne of grace.

This distinction concerning true and false worship must be rightly known, and minds must be aroused to true invocation. Let us look upon the mediator, and let us approach God by the trust of the mediator, and let us bring the hope of divine help, and let us not indulge in doubt, let us not think that our groans and our vows are void, but let us truly establish that they are received on account of the mediator. We shall experience in the thing itself that such a prayer is effective. Observe, however, in this saying, also a confirmation that such an invocation is effective, because it is added, Such a prayer pleases God. Since it pleases, it signifies that it is effective. Just as the Lord says, Ask and you shall receive. Likewise, How much more will your heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to those who ask him.

A joyful mind gladdens the countenance. A good heart is a continual feast.

The precept concerning maintaining a good conscience is repeated often, because it ought to be most known to all, that it is a command of God that we should not violate the law of God against conscience, just as it is written, Wage a good warfare, holding faith and a good conscience, Likewise, The end of the commandment is love from a pure heart, and a good conscience, and faith unfeigned. Let these sayings be fixed in our minds, and let them rule us, so that we do not act against conscience, That is, so that we do not knowingly violate the law of God. Because such a violation, which is done by one who knows, is a contempt of God. And concerning these it is said, He who commits sin is of the Devil. Likewise, The works of the flesh are manifest, That is, known to your conscience, or such things as can be judged by your conscience, which those who do are not heirs of eternal life.

It must be known, therefore, that lapses against conscience are atrocious crimes, and that God is truly angry at them, and that such a purpose and the Holy Spirit and faith cannot exist in the heart at the same time. For there are two contradictory assents: to want, while knowing, to act against God, and to want repentance, without which faith cannot exist. For the oath of God embraces both, As I live, I do not desire the death of the sinner, but that he may be converted and live.

He demands conversion by an oath, That is, to cast away the evil purpose. It is necessary that these things be known in the Church, and they are inculcated by the Prophets and Apostles. As in Ezekiel, When the righteous man shall sin, I will not be mindful of his righteousness. Likewise, Do not err, God is not mocked, fornicators, adulterers, etc., shall not possess the kingdom of God. Let men understand, therefore, how miserable is the man having a wound of conscience not yet healed, that is, still retaining an evil purpose, Such a one displeases God, he invokes God in vain, nay, he cannot even invoke Him, because without repentance and faith, one does not approach God, he cannot ask and hope for the help of God, he lives as one deserted by God, and a captive of the Devil.

Then, without any doubt, punishments follow even in this life. And unless conversion occurs, sadder ones follow after this life. Likewise, Not all who have lapsed are restored, but many rush from other crimes into others, because the Devil weaves a long web, he is not content with the first wound, but he does this, that he may afterwards drive the wounded one and others into eternal destruction.

Although, however, secure minds do not consider these huge and innumerable evils, yet in truth such is the misery of those rushing against conscience, Furthermore, in the secure, however, this is added, by divine ordination some natural pain and dread always accompanies crimes in man, committed against conscience. Wherefore, even if the sense of the wrath of God has not yet horribly oppressed the mind, Yet the voice of the law always brings some pain. Therefore, the Gentiles also said many things about the pains of a wounded conscience, and about the tranquility of an intact conscience. According to these verses, It is something to be free from crime in great evils. Likewise,

As each one's conscious mind is, so it conceives within, hope and fear in the breast according to its merits.

But this pain grows immensely, when at the same time God oppresses the terrified mind with the sense of His wrath, as in Judas, Saul, and similar ones, who cry out, Fall upon us, mountains, etc. Let the consideration of these huge evils remind us, so that with greater care and vigilance we may avoid lapses against conscience.

On the other hand, let good things also exhort us to maintain the integrity of conscience. The commandment of God,

The retention of faith. The faculty of invocation. The hope of help and of divine protection. Tranquility of heart. Mitigation and liberation in calamities.

These huge goods, coupled with a good conscience in a pious heart, must be guarded with great care. For consider what a great thing it is not to be torn away from God, and to be able to approach God, to invoke, to ask, and to hope for help. As it is written in the Epistle of John, If our heart does not condemn us, we have confidence to ask, etc.

Then let us be the more diligent, because not all who have fallen are restored; indeed, many rush from other sins into others, as was said above. Therefore Peter says, Watch, because your adversary as a roaring lion goes about seeking whom he may devour, whom resist, being strong in the faith. But briefly, the saying of Solomon has embraced the goods of an integral conscience, saying, A good heart is a continual feast. By which description let us embrace that whole heap of goods which I have recounted, and on the other hand let us consider the evils. Let the most sweet saying of Nazianzen also be held, Οὐδὲν ὅυτως ἐυφραίνει ἡμᾶς ἤ ὁταν τὸ συνεδός καθαρὸν ἀγαθαί ἐλπιδες. Nothing exhilarates us so much as a pure conscience and a good hope.

Finally, this is the chief rule of life, in all actions, that the conscience should agree with the word of God, and that nothing should be done or approved against conscience, and when such integrity of conscience is retained, the mind rests, retains faith, and remains in grace. But when it is wounded, it is torn away from God, and punishments certainly follow. As Job says, I feared all my works, knowing that you do not spare the delinquent.

Latin:

Victima impiorum abominatio Domino. Et oratio rectorum delectat eum.

Tres sunt gradus cultuum, Caeremoniae, honesta opera moralia, & fides. Horum gradvum discrimina & usum, necesse est recte intelligi in Ecclesia. Deus vult aliquas esse Caeremonias, & ipse certas instituit, ut externa signa promissionum conspiciantur, & promissio palam sonet in genere humano. Non enim vult Deus Ecclesiam in latebris esse, sed vult eam agnosci & discerni ab aliis gentibus, voce & ceremoniis. Praeterea cum in natura humana sit intellectus, & amor ordinis, distinctio aliqua exercitiorum ordinata est.

Itaque cur necessariae sint Caeremoniae & quomodo eis utendum sit, considerandum est prudenter. Sed quia homines proni sunt ad falsos cultus, & praesertim ad abusum caeremoniarum, amissa noticia promissionum & extincta luce fidei, flectunt oculos ad Caeremonias, & has cogitant mereri remissionem peccatorum coram Deo, & esse lusticiam, Haec persuasio in genere humano omnibus temporibus latissime vagatur. Sic de suis sacrificiis Ethnici sentiebant, sic in populo Israel magna pars sentiebat, sic docebant Pharisaei, sic in Ecclesia Monachi de Missa, de votis, & aliis caeremoniis docuerunt.

Hunc autem communem errorem accerrime taxant Prophetae, Ut Oseae. 6. Misericordiam volo non sacrificium, & agnitionem Dei magis quam holocaustum. Item, Veri adoratores adorabunt patrem in spiritu & veritate, Id est, in vero timore & vera fide. Imo & Ethnici reprehenderunt hanc superstitionem, quae caeremonias servat, sine bonis operibus moralibus, ut Plato dicit, Oportet colere Deum οὐ σχήμασι τεχνάξοντας, ἄλλα ἀλήθεια τιμῶντας ἄρετην. Quanquam autem verum est, oportet iusticiam bonae conscientiae simul esse, tamen haec legalis doctrina nondum sufficit, sed praelucere oportet noticiam promissionis divinae & fidem, id est, fiduciam Mediatoris, ut non indulgeant corda dubitationi, sed accedant ad Deum, & vere statuamus nos recipi & exaudiri propter Filium, etiamsi sumus indigni, & acquiescant corda in hac consalatione, & expectent auxilium Dei.

Hanc fidem & spem in invocatione postulat Deus, ut Matth. 21. Quicquid petieritis in oratione credentes accipietis. Item, Orate sine ira & sine haesitatione. Item, qui confidit in Dominum non confundetur &c.

Tunc gitur placent invocatio & sacrificia, cum praelucet fides, & adest iusticia bonae conscientiae, Id est, paenitentia seu bonum propositum. Non praelucente fide Caeremoniae sunt inanes, & cum non adest iusticia bonae conscientiae, simulatione Caeremoniarum deridetur Deus. Ideo hic dicitur, Abominatio coram Domino victimae impiorum.

Observa autem diligentiam in Antithesi, de sacrificiis impiorum retinet nomen rem externam significans, Victima impiorum. Sed de vero cultu recte invocantium inquit, Oratio rectorum. Nam impiorum invocatio est inanis sonitus. Illi tantum invocant, qui paenitentiam & fidem ad Deum afferunt. Ut Paulus inquit, Quomodo invocabunt, si non credent? Item, Habentes talem pontificem, accedamus cum fiducia ad thronum gratiae.

Hoc discrimen de veris & falsis cultibus recte cognoscendum est, & mentes ad veram invocationem exuscitandae sunt. Intueamur mediatorem, & accedamus ad Deum fiducia mediatoris, & afferamus spem auxilii divini, nec indulgeramus dubitationi, non cogitemus nostros gemitus, & nostra vota irrita esse, sed vere statuamus recipi ea propter mediatorem. Talem precationem efficacem esse re ipsa experiemur. Observes autem in hoc dicto, etiam confirmationem, quod invocatio talis sit efficax, Quia additum est, Placet Deo talis oratio. Cum placeat, significat efficacem esse. Sicut Dominus inquit, Petite & accipietis. Item, Quanto magis Pater vester caelestis dabit Spiritum sanctum petentibus.

Laetus animus exhilarat vultum. Bonum cor iuge convivium.

SAepe repetitur praeceptum de bona conscientia tuenda, quia notissimum omnibus esse debet, mandatum Dei esse ne legem Dei violemus contra conscientiam, sicut scriptum est, Milita bonam militiam, retinens fidem & bonam conscientiam, Item, Finis mandati est dilectio ex corde puro, & conscientia bona, & fide non fictta. Haec dicta sint infixa animis, & regant nos, ne contra conscientiam faciamus, Id est, ne scientes legem Dei violemus. Quia talis violatio, quae fit a sciente, est contemptus Dei. Et de his dicitur, Qui facit peccatum ex Diabolo est. Item, Manifesta sunt opera carnis, Id est, nota tuae conscientiae, seu talia, quae possunt iudicari a tua conscientia, quae qui faciunt non sunt haeredes vitae aeternae.

Sciendum est igitur lapsus contra conscientiam atrocia delicta esse, ac Deum vere eis irasci, nec simul esse in corde tale propositum, & Spiritum sanctum, ac fidem. Sunt enim duae contradictoriae assensiones, velle scientem facere contra Deum, & velle paenitentiam, sine qua fides esse non potest. Nam iuramentum Dei complectitur utrumque, Vivo ego nolo mortem peccatoris, sed ut convertatur & vivat.

Postulat iureiurando conversionem, Id est, abiici malum propositum. Haec in Ecclesia sciri necesse est, & sunt inculcata a Prophetis & Apostolis. Ut apud Ezechielem, Cum iustus deliquerit, non ero memor iusticiae eius. Item, Nolite errare, Deus non irridetur, scortatores, adulteri &c. non possidebunt regnum Dei. Intelligant igitur homines, quam miser sit homo habens vulnus conscientiae nondum sanatae, id est, adhuc retinens malum propositum, Talis displicet Deo, frustra invocat Deum, imo ne quidem potest invocare, quia sine paenitentia & fide, non acceditur ad Deum, non potest petere & sperare auxilium Dei, ita vivit tanquam desertus a Deo, & captivus Diaboli.

Deinde sine ulla dubitatione sequuntur etiam in hac vita poenae. Et nisi fiat conversio, sequuntur tristiores post hanc vitam. Item, Non omnes lapsi restituuntur, sed multi ex aliis delictis in alia ruunt, quia Diabolus longam telam texit, non est primo vulnere contentus, sed hoc agit, ut postea impellat vulneratum & alios in aeternum exitium.

Quanquam autem haec ingentia & innumerabilia mala securae mentes non considerant, tamen revera talis est miseria ruentium contra conscientiam, Praeterea in securis tamen hoc accedit, semper ordinatione divina in homine aliquis naturalis dolor & pavor comitatur scelera, contra conscientiam facta. Quare etiam si sensus irae Dei nondum horribiliter oppressit mentem, Tamen vox legis semper aliquem affert dolorem. Ideo Ethnici quoque multa de doloribus sauciatae conscientiae, & de tranquillitate integrae conscientiae dixerunt. iuxta hos versus, Est aliquid magnis crimen abesse malis. Item,

Conscia mens ut cuique sua est, ita concipit intra,Pectora pro meritis spemque metumque suis.

Sed hic dolor crescit in immensum, cum simul Deus sensu irae suae opprimit perterrefactam mentem, sicut in Iuda, Saul, & similibus, qui vociferantur, Ruite super nos montes, &c. Horum ingentium malorum consideratio, commonefaciat nos, ut maiore cura & vigilantia vitemus lapsus contra conscientiam.

Econtra & bona hortentur nos, ad tuendam integritatem conscientiae. Mandatum Dei,

Retentio fidei.Facultas invocationis.Spes auxilii, & protectionis divinae.Tranquillitas cordis,Mitigatio & liberatio in calamitatibus.

Haec ingentia bona, copulata cum bona conscientia in corde pio, magna cura tuenda sunt. Cogita enim quanta res sit, non esse avulsum a Deo, & posse ad Deum accedere, invocare, petere & sperare auxilium. Ut in Epistola loannis scriptum est, Si cor nostrum non reprehenderit nos, habemus fiduciam petendi &c.

Deinde eo simus diligentiores, quia non omnes lapsi restituuntur, imo multi ex aliis peccatis in alia ruunt, ut supra dictum est. Ideo Petrus inquit, Vigilate, quia adversarius vester tanquam Leo rugiens circumit quaerens quem devoret, cui resistite fortes in fide. Breviter autem dictum Salomonis integrae conscientiae bona complexum est, inquiens, Bonum cor iuge convivium. Qua descriptione complectamur illum totum cumulum bonorum quem recensui, & econtra consideremus mala. Teneatur etiam dulcissimum dictum Nazianzeni, Οὐδὲν ὅυτως ἐυφραίνει ἡμᾶς ἤ ὁταν τὸ συνεδός καθαρὸν ἀγαθαί ἐλπιδες. Nihil exhilarat nos ita, ut conscientia pura & spes bona.

Denique haec est regula praecipua vitae, in omnibus actionibus, ut conscientia congruat cum verbo Dei, nec agatur quidquam, aut approbetur contra conscientiam, & cum retinetur talis integritas conscientiae, mens acquiescit, retinet fidem, & manet in gratia. Cum autem sauciata est, avellitur a Deo, & certo comitantur poenae. Sicut Iob inquit, Verebar omnia opera mea, sciens quod non parcis delinquenti.

## 15:30
The light of the eyes exhilarates the heart, a good report fattens the bones.

Above, it was spoken of a good conscience; now it must be spoken of glory and a good reputation. Glory is the approval of our conscience judging rightly, and of others judging rightly.

A good reputation is the approval of others judging rightly. Moreover, this little book also testifies that glory and a good reputation are to be sought. As it is said below in Chapter 22, A good name is better than many riches. And it was very learnedly said by Augustine, I have need of a good conscience for myself, a good reputation for my neighbor. Hither also let many rightly said things be referred, An honest rumor is another patrimony. He is light, nor does the glory of honor stimulate him.

But why are glory and a good reputation good and desirable things?

The first cause is this, because God wills that the distinction of virtues and vices should stand out and be understood by all, so that we may know what He Himself is like, and what His will is. Therefore He wills that judges and other men should approve virtue and vituperate baseness. Moreover, approval is a good reputation, just as true vituperation is a bad reputation. And God severely commands this rectitude of judgments, according to that of Isaiah. Woe to those who call evil good, and good evil. And in the first of Corinthians, the sixth, it is said, Neither the revilers, nor the extortioners shall possess the kingdom of God. And Ecclesiast. 10. A whisperer is like a serpent, which bites while hiding. Since, therefore, the approval of virtue is a thing commanded divinely, and for that reason is good, it is to be sought in its own place, and to be guarded by just means.

The other cause is more clear, but it arises from the former. It is a divine commandment that we should invite others by good examples and avoid scandals, as it is written, Let your light shine before men and your heavenly Father be glorified. Likewise, Woe to the man through whom scandal comes.

Since, therefore, the way of men is for the most part governed by examples, it is necessary to have regard for reputation, because reputation is a judgment concerning examples, whether good or bad. These things must be considered, so that the madness of many may be refuted, of whom some pretend to neglect reputation and glory through feigned humility, others neglect them in reality through a certain fury and impudence, which the Greeks call ἄδιατρεψιαν, whom this very thing delights, to insult men as if by the reputation of a new crime, as Cambyses killed the son of a Prince for sport.

Such furies arise from the Devil, the light of the divine word having been extinguished in the minds of men. Wherefore, as Paul says, let us walk accurately, not as fools, etc. And let us learn to beware of and to ward off the impulses of the Devil, and let the rule be held, The word of God is a lamp to my feet, which first commands that you serve God in faith and integrity of conscience, then it also commands you to seek a good reputation, so that God may be celebrated, and others may be invited by good examples. Let these rules always be in sight, as Paul says, Fight the good fight, keeping faith and a good conscience. And elsewhere he adds the judgments of men. Rom. 14. In these things, serving, he pleases God, and is approved by men. Item, Woe to the man through whom scandal comes.

Latin:

Lux oculorum exhilarat cor, Bona fama saginat ossa.

Supra dictum est de bona conscientia, nunc de gloria & bona fama dicendum est. Gloria est approbatio nostrae conscientiae recte iudicantis, & aliorum recte iudicantium.

Bona fama est approbatio aliorum recte iudicantium. Testatur autem & hic libellus gloriam & bonam famam appetendam esse. Ut infra Cap. 22. dicitur, Melius est nomen bonum, quam divitiae multae. Et valde erudite dictum est ab Augustino, Bona conscientia mihi opus est, bona fama proximo. Huc & multa recte dicta referantur, Honestus rumor alterum patrimonium est. Levis est, nec illum stimulat gloriae decus.

Sed cur gloria & bona fama sunt res bonae & expetendae?

Prima causa haec est, quia Deus vult extare & intelligi ab omnibus discrimen virtutum & vitiorum, ut sciamus qualis & ipse sit, & quae sit eius voluntas. Ideo vult iudices & alios homines conprobare virtutem, et vituperare turpitudinem. Conprobatio autem est bona fama, sicut & vituperatio vera est mala fama. Et severe praecipit hanc rectitudinem iudiciorum Deus, luxta illud Esaiae. Vae illis qui dicunt malum bonum, & bonum malum. Et Primae Corinth. sexto dicitur, Nec maledici, nec raptores regnum Dei possidebunt. Et Ecclesiast. 10. Susurro similis est serpenti, qui latitans mordet. Cum igitur comprobatio virtutis sit res mandata divinitus, eamque ob causam bona sit, suo loco appetenda est, & iustis modis tuenda.

Altera causa magis perspicua est, sed ex priore oritur. Mandatum divinum est, ut bonis exempplis invitemus alios & vitemus scandala, Ut scriptum est, Luceat lux vestra coram hominibus & glorificetur Pater vester caelestis. Item, Ve homini per quem venit scandalum.

Cum igitur via hominum magna ex parte regatur exemplis, necesse est famae rationem habere, quia Fama est iudicium de exemplis, seu bonis, seu malis. Haec consideranda sunt, ut refutetur amentia multorum, quorum alii simulant se negligere famam & gloriam ficta humilitate, alii reipsa negligunt furore quodam, & impudentia, quam Graeci vocant ἄδιατρεψιαν quos hoc ipsum delectat, novi scleris fama velut insultare hominibus, ut Cambyses per ludum interfecit filium Principis.

Tales furores a Diabolo oriuntur, extincta luce verbi divini in mentibus hominum. Quare ut Paulus inquit, accurate ambulemus, non ut fatui, &c. Et cavere & arcere Diaboli impulsiones discamus, & regula teneatur, Lucerna pedibus meis verbum Dei, quod primum iubet, ut in fide & integritate conscientiae servias Deo, Deinde iubet etiam te famam bonam expetere, ut Deus celebretur, & alii bonis exemplis invitentur, Haec regulae semper sint in conspectu, Ut Paulus inquit, Milita bonam militiam, retinens fidem & bonam conscientiam. Et alibi addit hominum iudicia. Rom. 14. In his serviens placet Deo, & probatus est hominibus. Item, Vae homini per quem venit scandalum.

## 15:33
Before glory is humility.

Both sayings have been frequently repeated, that many, infatuated by successes, afterwards move unnecessary matters, in which they fall, as Claudian said.

They are lifted on high, That they may fall with a heavier ruin.

As Pompey, Antony, and many others, infatuated by favorable circumstances, afterwards moved unnecessary matters in which they were overwhelmed. Because the rule is true. God resists the proud, On the contrary, judgment begins from the house of God, therefore the Saints are first tamed, and afterwards are adorned with glory, as it is also said here. Before glory is humility.

Latin:

Ante gloriam humiliatio.

Utrunque dictum crebro repetitum est, Multos infatuatos successibus, postea, movere res non necessarias, in quibus concidunt, ut dixit Claudianus.

Tolluntur in altum,Ut lapsu graviore ruant.

Ut Pompeius, Antonius, & alii multi infatuati rebus secundis, postea moverunt res non necessarias in quibus oppressi sunt. Quia vera est regula. Deus superbis resistit, Econtra, ludicium a domo Dei incipit, ideo Sancti prius domantur, & postea ornantur gloria, Ut & hic dicitur. Ante gloriam humiliatio.

## 16:1 Argument
CHAPTER XVI.

Latin:

CAPUT XVI.

## 16:1
It is for man to prepare the heart, But from the Lord is the answer of the tongue.

This saying does not take away the freedom of the will, but teaches that the invocation of God must be joined, so that God may help us, Because even if there is some choice, and some freedom, yet men often err, and easily hallucinate. Furthermore, neither are successful outcomes present unless God helps. According to those sayings, Unless the Lord builds the house. Item, I know, O Lord, that the way of man is not his own. That is, a calling cannot be governed by human counsels alone, nor is it successful unless God helps. And yet our diligence is necessary, let us not ourselves disturb the calling, rushing against conscience, and let there be a zeal for doing rightly, and a good purpose, and let the invocation of God be added.

Solomon confesses that there is some human deliberation, and that the human mind and will do something, but lest it err, and so that successes may be good, there is need of the governance and help of God, as David, when fighting, does something, But the battle is successful in this way, when the hands are helped by God.

Latin:

Hominis est praeparare cor, Sed a Deo est responsio linguae.

Hoc dictum non tollit libertatem voluntatis, sed docet adiungendam esse invocationem Dei, ut Deus nos adivuct, Quia & si est aliqua electio, & aliqua libertas, tamen saepe errant homines, & facile hallucinantur. Deinde nec felices eventus sunt nisi Deo iuvante. Iuxta illa dicta, Nisi dominus edificaverit domum. Item, Scio Domine non est hominis via eius. Id est, Vocatio non potest regi solis humanis consiliis, nec felix est nisi Deo iuvante. Et tamen nostra diligentia necessaria est, non turbemus ipsi vocationem, ruentes contra conscientiam, & sit studium recte faciendi, & bonum propositum, & accedat invocatio Dei.

Fatetur Salomon aliquam esse humanam deliberationem, & aliquid agere humanam mentem, & voluntatem, sed ne erret, & ut successus boni sint, gubernatione & auxilio Dei opus est, ut David praelians agit aliquid, Sed ita est felix pugna, cum manus a Deo iuvantur.

## 16:2
All the ways of a man are clean in his own eyes, The Lord is the governor of spirits.

He says there is a distinction between hypocrites and wholesome instruments. Which he recites for this reason, that we may seek the help of God. The course of Ahithophel is glorious, and for a long time successful, yet at length his hypocrisy is laid open. Thus the course of many teaching in the Church, even if it is glorious in the beginning, yet afterwards the hypocrisy is uncovered, when they receive false dogmas, either through ambition, or through adulation, or through the levity of their wits.

Therefore it signifies that there is a distinction between salutary instruments and hypocrites. But how we may be made salutary instruments, the following sentence teaches, which is as it were a consequence of the two nearest ones, Commit your works to God, and your plans will be firm, Here he expressly commands that help be sought, and expected with true faith and hope from God, but it is explicitly added, Your works, or, Your way, so that we may know that we are restrained within the limits of our calling, and prohibited from πολυπραγμοσύνην.

But let the whole doctrine concerning invocation always be in sight, and let us determine that our petitions are not in vain. Indeed, that this is the chief worship of God, to seek and expect help from Him, as is said more copiously in the doctrine concerning invocation. And let us remember that for this very reason the Church is involved in great dangers and calamities, so that we may learn that it is preserved not only by human diligence, but divinely, and let us seek help, so that we may more acknowledge and celebrate the goodness of God. Indeed, for this reason the Son of God assumed human nature, so that there might exist an evident testimony that He cares for human nature, who indeed says, Come to me all you who labor and are burdened, &c. This whole doctrine becomes more illustrious when we exercise invocation.

God does all things for His own sake, Even the wicked for punishment.

The previous sentences were precepts concerning the help of God. This saying preaches concerning providence, that human affairs are not carried on by chance, but that God is the inspector, ordainer, and governor, to this end, that He Himself may be acknowledged. For He founded the human race for this reason, and revealed Himself, so that the knowledge of Him might shine in the human race. Moreover, He ordains and governs and preserves good things by His help, such as the order of nature, the turns of summer and winter, the fertilization of the earth, generation in individual species, the society of the human race, the political order, the Church, and in the good minds of individuals His own good things, and many liberations of those who invoke Him.

That these things are done by God and are ordained and good, and testify concerning providence, can be understood in whatever way . But what about evils, does He also effect impiety in man? By no means, but so that punishments may testify concerning providence, he makes mention here also of evils, so that he may refute the objection, which argues that there is no providence, since many evils happen, Indeed, he says, because the wicked are drawn back to punishment, it is necessary that there be providence.

Therefore the word, does, is to be referred to punishment, The wicked, who sins by his own will, God ordains to punishment, here let the word does be referred to the predicate, As when I say, The sculptor makes a figure of Alexander out of marble, here the sculptor does not beget the marble, but is the maker of the figure, Thus God is by no means the cause of impiety, but after the wicked man has sinned by his own will, He ordains him to punishment, and punishment is an illustrious testimony of the justice of God, for punishments universally accompany offenses. Thus he affirms that there is providence, because it is manifest that good things are ordained by God.

Then also it is certain that punishments are from God. Therefore there is Providence. Wherefore the Cyclopean opinions are false and to be detested, and the true sentence is to be confirmed in minds, that there is a God, the creator of nature, an inspector and judge, and that He is to be acknowledged, feared, and invoked, and that He is to be obeyed, and that wisdom, truth, goodness, justice, mercy, and other virtues are to be attributed to Him.

Latin:

Omnes viae viri mundi in oculis suis, Gubernator spirituum est Dominus.

Discrimen esse dicit inter hypocritas & salutaria organa. quod ideo recitat, ut petamus auxilium Dei. Cursus Achitophel est gloriosus, & diu felix, tamen tandem patefit hypocrisis. Sic cursus multorum docentium in Ecclesia, etiamsi initio gloriosus est, tamen postea retegitur hypocrisis, cum falsa dogmata, vel ambitione, vel adulatione, vel levitate ingeniorum recipiunt.

Ideo significat discrimen esse inter salutaria organa & hypocritas. Quomodo autem efficiamur salutaria organa, sequens sententia docet, quae quasi consequens est duarum proximarum, Commenda Deo opera tua, & erunt firma consilia, Hic expresse iubet peti, & vera fide & spe expectari auxillium Dei, sed diserte additum est, Opera tua, vel, Viam tuam, ut sciamus coherceri nos intra metas vocationis, & prohiberi πολυπραγμοσύνην.

Semper autem in conspectu sit tota doctrina de invocatione, ac statuamus non esse irritas nostras petitiones. Imo hunc praecipuum cultum Dei esse, petere & expectare ab ipso auxilium, ut copiosius dicitur in doctrina de invocatione. Ac meminerimus hanc ipsam ob causam in magnis periculis & calamitatibus versari Ecclesiam, ut discamus non tantum humana diligentia eam servari, sed divinitus, & petamus auxilium, ut Dei bonitatem magis agnoscamus, & celebremus. Imo ideo Filius Dei assumpsit humanam naturam, ut extet evidens testimonium curae ei esse humanam naturam, qui quidem inquit, Venite ad me omnes qui laboratis & onerati estis, &c. Haec tota doctrina fit illu strior cum exercemus invocationem.

Omnia facit Deus propter sese, Etiam impium ad poenam.

Superiores sententia fuerunt praecepta de auxilio Dei. Hoc dictum concionatur de providentia, quod scilicet non casu ferantur res humanae, sed quod Deus sit inspector, ordinator & gubernator, ad hunc finem, ut ipse agnoscatur. Ideo enim condidit genus humanum, & sese patefecit, ut noticia ipsius luceat in genere humano. Ordinat autem & gubernat & servat res bonas suo auxilio, ut naturae ordinem, vices aestatis & hyemis, fecundationem terrae, generationem in singulis speciebus, societatem generis humani, ordinem politicum, Ecclesiam, & in bonis mentibus singulorum sua bona, & multas liberationes invocantium.

Haec a Deo fieri & ordinata ac bona esse, & testificari de providentia, utcunque intelligi potest. Sed quid de malis, num etiam efficit impietatem in homine? Nequaquam, sed ut poenae testificentur de providentia, hic mentionem etiam malorum facit, ut refutet obiectionem, quae disputat non esse providentiam, cum multa mala fiant, Imo, inquit, quia mali retrahuntur ad poenam, necesse est esse providentiam.

Ideo verbum, facit, referendum est ad poenam, Impium, qui propria voluntate peccat, ordinat Deus ad poenam, hic referatur verbum facit ad praedicatum, Ut cum dico, Statuarius facit ex marmore figuram Alexandri, hic statuarius non gignit, marmor, sed figurae fabricator est, Ita Deus nequaquam est causa impietatis, sed postquam impius propria voluntate peccavit, ordinat eum ad poenam, & poena est illustre testimonium iusticiae Dei, universaliter enim comitantur poenae delicta. Ita providentiam esse affirmat, quia bona ordinari a Deo manifestum est.

Deinde & poenas esse a Deo certum est. Est igitur Providentia. Quare Cyclopycae opiniones falsae & detestandae sunt, & confirmanda est vera sententia in mentibus, esse Deum conditorem naturae, inspectorem ac iudicem, & hunc agnoscendum esse, timendum, & invocandum, & ei obtemperandum, & tribuendum ei esse sapientiam, veritatem, bonitatem, iusticiam, misericordiam & alias virtutes.

## 16:6
By mercy and truth iniquity is expiated.

This saying is opposed to the persuasion concerning sacrifices, which was, is, and will be of all ages and nations. For very many dream that sins are compensated by sacrifices and ceremonies. And many indulge themselves all the more, because they think afterwards that they can appease God with sacrifices.

To this error he opposes this saying concerning the justice of the heart, concerning the fear of God, and true love. It is, however, a Synecdoche, embracing faith and other virtues, which ought to follow faith . The Hebrew phrase by mercy and truth signifies beneficence and faithfulness. That is, not a deceptive beneficence but a constant one, just as Jonathan performed mercy and truth with David. That is, he was beneficent toward him, and indeed faithfully, that is, without deception, and constantly.

Latin:

Misericordia & veritate expiatur iniquitas.

Hoc dictum opponitur persuasioni de sacrificiis, quae fuit, est, & erit omnium aetatum & gentium. Plurimi enim somniant peccata compensari sacrificiis & coeremoniis. Et multi eo magis sibi indulgent, quia postea existimant se posse Deum placare sacrificiis.

Huic errori opponit hoc dictum de iusticia cordis, de timore Dei, & vera dilectione. Est autem Synecdoche, complectens fidem & alias virtutes, quae fidem sequi debent. Hebraica phrasis per misericordiam & veritatem, significat beneficentiam, & fidelitatem. Id est, non fucosam beneficentiam sed constantem, ut Ionathas fecit misericordiam & veritatemcum Davide. Id est, fuit beneficus erga eum, & quidem fideliter, hoc est, sine fuco, & constanter.

## 16:9
The heart of man thinks out his way.

He establishes two causes of good and happy actions in man, the human will and the help of God. Just as it is often said elsewhere. And the customary sayings agree. With grace going before, with the will accompanying.

Let us know, therefore, that it is necessary for our diligence and the invocation of God to concur, and both vices are to be detested and avoided, trust in ourselves and sloth. For God commands diligence in the labors of one's calling, but promises help so that they may be prosperous and happy. And he often testifies that this is the chief worship, to seek and expect help and divine governance by faith. According to that, Commit your way to God, and he will do it. Item, Ask and you shall receive. Item, Without me you can do nothing.

Latin:

Cor hominis cogitat viam suam.

Dvas causas constituit bonarum & felicium actionum in homine, volunta tem humanam & auxilium Dei. Sicut alias saepe dicitur. Et congruunt usitata dicta. Praeeunte gratia, comitante voluntate.

Sciamus igitur oportere concurrere nostram diligentiam & invocationem Dei, & utrumque vitium detestandum & fugiendum est, Fiducia nostri & ignavia. Praecipit enim Deus diligentiam in laboribus vocationis, sed ut sint fausti & felices promittit auxilium. Et saepe testatur hunc praecipuum cultum, esse fide petere & expectare auxilium, & gubernationem divinam. Iuxta illud, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet. Item, Petite & accipietis. Item, Sine me nihil potestis facere.

## 16:14
A wise man will appease the wrath of a king.

He preaches about due obedience. A wise man appeases the indignation of a king, that is, he complies in due duties, and rightly keeps the limits of that obedience, and avoids just punishments, nor does he incur the indignation of magistrates through negligence, nor does he petulantly despise them, because God wants us to acknowledge that the political order is a huge good ordained by him. And he forbids this order to be disturbed. Concerning this due obedience, there exists a saying most worthy of memory in Ecclesiastes chapter 8. I observe the mouth of the King, and the oath of God, do not hasten to depart from his face, nor persist in an evil work, because there is no evasion in war, nor will the wicked be delivered. He says explicitly, The mouth of the king, That is, the edicts of magistrates or the voice of the laws. And he adds the oath of God, because he wants the mouth of the king and the oath not to dissent, That is, the law of God.

For this also must be observed, that the law is called an oath, so that we may know that God testifies by an oath that he will be an avenger and will punish offenses.

He says lastly concerning the punishment of the contumacious, that there will be no evasion, because God is the guardian of discipline and the political order, and drags the guilty to punishment, even if for some time they seemed to have escaped from the hands of the magistrates, as is manifestly apparent in the punishments of murderers, concerning whom it is said. He who takes the sword, will perish by the sword. Furthermore, Paul commands that honor be given to magistrates.

Honor, however, is universally the approval of another, attributing to him excellence on account of some divine good, just as Nebuchadnezzar treats Daniel with honor, who acknowledges that wisdom was given to him divinely above common grasp. For honor is principally referred to God, and to the proper works of God, wisdom, virtue, defenses, and liberations. As honor is to be attributed to a Physician, that is, it must be acknowledged that the medical art is a gift of God, and that the driving away of diseases and the preservation of life are benefits of God, which he exhibits through this art and the artisan, and this approval of the art and the artisan ought to embrace the giving of thanks to be attributed to God, and then to the minister. Thus, to attribute honor to a magistrate at the beginning signifies that divine goods are attributed to him, that is, that the political order is a divine thing, and wisdom and justice are ordained by God, and that the magistrate is the guardian of such great goods, on account of which both thanks are to be given to God, and the minister is to be loved, etc. Afterwards, this same honor, since it is the preservation of the political order, embraces the obedience of the heart, and external duties, so that that order may be preserved.

These things must often be thought upon, so that we may acknowledge that the political order is a gift and work of God, that we may love it, and that we may defend it with all duties. Nor should we think that men are born to wander without any bonds, just as birds fly in the air. For the nature of men was by no means created for such liberty, but for order and bonds, which God has placed around us. For Liberty properly signifies the faculty of doing rightly and holding one's own, and not being compelled to do things against justice.

Latin:

Sapiens placabit iram regis.

Concionatur de debita obedientia. Sapiens placat indignationem regis, id est, obtemperat in officiis debitis, & metas illius obedientiae recte servat, & vitat iustas poenas, nec aut negligentia incurrit in indignationem magistratuum, aut petulanter contemnit eos, quia vult nos Deus agnoscere, politicum ordinem esse ingens bonum ab ipso ordinatum. Et hunc ordinem turbari prohibet. De hac debita obedientia extat dictum memoria dignissimum in Ecclesiaste cap. 8. Ego os Regis observo, & iuramentum Dei, ne festines recedere a facie eius, neque permaneas in opere malo, quia non est evasio in bello, nec eripietur impius. Diserte inquit, Os regis, Id est, edicta magistratuum seu vocem legum. Et addit luramentum Dei, quia vult non disserntire os regis, & iuramentum, Id est, legem Dei.

Nam & hoc observandum est, legem dici iuramentum, ut sciamus Deum iuramento testari, se vindicem fore & puniturum delicta.

Postrema de poena contumacium dicit, non fore evasionem, quia Deus custos est disciplinae & politici ordinis, & retrahit sontes ad poenam, etiamsi aliquandiu visi sunt effugisse ex manibus magistratuum, ut in homicidarum poenis manifeste apparet, de quibus dicitur. Qui gladium acceperit, gladio peribit. Praeterea Paulus iubet honorem tribui magistratibus.

Est autem universaliter honos comprobatio alterius, tribuens ei praestantiam propter divinum aliquot bonum, ut Nebucadnezar honore afficit Danielem, qui agnoscit divinitus ei datam esse sapientiam supra communem captum. Nam honos principaliter refertur ad Deum, & ad opera Dei propria, sapientiam, virtutem, defensiones & liberationes. Ut Medico tribuendus est honos, id est, agnoscendum est artem medicam Dei donum esse, & depulsionem morborum & conservationem vitae, esse beneficia Dei, quae per hanc artem & artificem exhibet, & haec comprobatio artis & artificis, complecti debet gratiarum actionem Deo tribuendam, & deinde ministro. Sic magistratui tribui honorem initio significat tribui ei bona divina, id est, quod ordo politicus sit res divina, & sapientia & iusticia a Deo ordinata, & quod magistratus tantorum bonorum custos sit, propter quae & gratiae Deo agendae sunt, & minister diligendus est &c. Postea hic ipse honos cum sit conservatio politici ordinis complectitur obedientiam cordis, & officia externa, ut ille ordo conservetur.

Haec saepe cogitanda sunt, ut agnoscamus politicum ordinem esse donum & opus Dei, amemus eum, & omnibus officiis tueamur. Nec existimemus nasci homines, ut sine ullis vinculis vagentur, sicut aves in aere volitant. Nam natura hominum nequaquam ad talem libertatem condita est, sed ad ordinem & vincula, quae Deus nobis circundedit. Nam Libertas proprie significat facultatem recte faciendi & tenendi suum, & non cogi, ut faciamus contra iusticiam.

## 17:1 Argument
CHAPTER XVII.

Latin:

CAPUT XVII.

## 17:5
He who mocks the poor reproaches his Maker.

Above it was also said, The rich and the poor dwell together, the Lord makes them both. Item, By the blessing of the Lord the rich are made. These sayings and many others testify that wealth is not scattered by chance, nor is it acquired only by human diligence, but that increases are truly aided by God, who by a certain counsel of his own wills there to be a dissimilarity in the gift of all means, so that life may be associated by many little bonds, and that some may need the help of others, and that the stronger members may bring aid to the weaker. Therefore, let us remember here that both are said, that God is the author of that dissimilarity, so that some are poor, others rich, and that it is the command of God that the weaker poor be spared, both in government and in private custom. To this also pertain those things, To spare the subjects and to subdue the proud. Item, μηδ' ἱκέτας ἀδικειυ, ἱκέται ἱεράτε καὶ ἁγνοὶ.

To this also pertain the sayings of the Psalms. Blessed is he who understands concerning the needy and the poor. To this must also be added the doctrine concerning the promises of bodily goods, which have been handed down.

First, that they may teach us that bodily or temporal goods are not scattered by chance, but by God governing. And therefore God wills that these goods also be sought from him.

Second, that faith may be exercised in this petition.

Third, that these promises may be reminders concerning the spiritual promise.

Fourth, the promises of bodily goods testify that God will preserve the Church, even in this life, according to that, The dead shall not praise you, O Lord.

And he who is glad at another's calamity &c.

The antithesis is concerning ἐπιχαιρεκακῷ, who rejoices at the calamity of the good, such as is especially the Devil, who rejoices at the calamity of the Church, and indeed without any utility of his own. But this very thing alone delights him, to destroy the works of God out of hatred for the Mediator God. But a threat is added, ἐπιχαιρέκακος he shall not be unpunished.

Latin:

Subsannas pauperem exprobrat factori eius.

Supra quoque dictum est, Simul habitant dives & pauper, utrunque facit Dominus. Item, Benedictione Domini divites fiunt. Haec dicta & pleraque alia testantur, non casu spargi facultates, nec tantum humana diligentia parari, sed incrementa vere iuvari a Deo, qui suo quodam consilio omnium mediorum dono rum vult esse dissimilitudinem, ut vita sit consociata multis viriculis, & alii aliorum ope indigeant, & firmiora membra opem ferant infirmioribus. Ideo meminerimus hic utrunque dici, Deum esse autorem dissimilitudinis illius, ut alii sint pauperes, alii divites, & mandatum Dei esse, ut parcatur imbecillioribus pauperibus, & in gubernatione, & in privata consetudine. Huc & illa pertinent, Parecer subiectis & debellare superbos. Item, μηδ' ἱκέτας ἀδικειυ, ἱκέται ἱεράτε καὶ ἁγνοὶ.

Huc & Psalmi dicta pertinent. Beatus qui intelligit super egenum & pauperem. Huc addenda est etiam doctrina de promissionibus bonorum corporalium, quae traditae sunt.

Primum, ut doceant nos, non casu spargi bona corporalia seu temporalia, sed gubernante Deo. Ideoque Deus haec bona etiam a se peti vult.

Secundo, ut fides exerceatur in hac petitione.

Tertio, ut hae promissiones sint commonefactiones de promissione spirituali.

Quarto, testantur promissiones corporalium bonorum, Deum servaturum esse Ecclesiam, etiam in hac vita, iuxta illud, Non mortui laudabunt te Domine.

Et cui calamitas alterius voluptati &c.

Antithesis est de ἐπιχαιρεκακῷ, qui laetatur calamitate bonorum, qualis maxime est Diabolus, qui laetatur calamitate Ecclesiae, & quidem sine aliqua sua utilitate. Sed tantum hoc ipsum delectat, odio Dei Mediatoris destruere opera Dei. Sed est addita comminatio, ἐπιχαιρέκακος non erit impunitus.

## 17:6
The crown of old men is the children's children.

From no human thing do parents take greater grief than from the calamity or disgrace of their children. And on the contrary, virtue brings great joy. Item, The prosperous affairs of children. And this must be mutual in the offspring, let this saying of Epaminondas be held, who said that he took this greatest fruit of his victory, that he still had his parents as spectators of his glory.

Latin:

Corona senum filii filiorum.

Nulla ex re humana parentes maiorem dolorem capiunt, quam ex calamitate aut turpitudine liberorum. Et econtra magnam laetitiam affert virtus. Item, Res secundae filiorum. Et hoc mutuum debet esse in natis, Hic dictum Epaminondae teneatur, qui dixit, se hunc maximum fructum capere suae victoriae, quod haberet adhuc parentes spectatores suae gloriae.

## 17:7
Proud lips do not become a fool. Nor lying lips a prince.

There are two precepts, the one concerning modesty or avoiding arrogance, the other concerning truth or avoiding sophistry. However, it is established that both vices are very common, and that in government there are two chief plagues, arrogance or πολνπραγμοσύνκην and sophistry.

Modesty is a virtue which, in our opinion of ourselves and in our speech, takes care that we do not attribute to ourselves more than the reality is, and which in our behavior maintains an order suitable to reason.
