The virtue closest to modesty, and its root, is the Fear of God, which is to tremble at the recognition of our own weakness and of divine judgment; when this recognition restrains and bridles our spirits, we do not take upon ourselves more than we are able to perform. Therefore, it warns here that vices opposed to modesty are to be avoided, namely arrogance, πολυπργμοσύνην, petulance, because they truly fight against the fear of God. And he says that arrogant people are fools, and even if they often seem wise in their own judgment and have others applauding them, yet tragic outcomes follow, which because they do not foresee, they are truly fools, as it is written, God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble.

The other saying is more obscure, Sophistry does not befit princes, for especially the highest governors in the Church and in empires ought to be guardians of the truth divinely handed down. Likewise, of truth in judgments. Because sophistry in religions and in judgments horribly disturbs the life of men.

He who hides another's fault acquires friendship. But he who makes it public, discord.

Let the saying be accommodated to ἐπιείκειαν. And the sentiment is, The blemishes of friends are to be covered, but there is a measure to this matter. If it is an error of doctrine or a manifest fault, it cannot be covered. But these things must be healed in a just order. First, the author is to be admonished, who, if he is corrected, his own conversion heals the scandal. But if it is not a manifest or atrocious evil, and the medication is dangerous, it is to be tolerated and covered, according to that,

We see some wounds made worse by curing, which it would have been better not to have touched.

Likewise, Love covers all sins. Likewise, Forgive, and it will be forgiven you. And in this matter let us consider the immense mercy of God, who even in the saints tolerates a great mass of filth and errors, and when he punishes, yet he does not kindle all his wrath, as is said in the Psalm. Likewise, If you, O Lord, should mark iniquities, Lord, who shall stand? This admirable goodness is to be thought upon daily in prayer, and at the same time our uncleanness is to be deplored, and thanks are to be given to God who covers and heals, and for his sake let us also forgive others.

But Solomon adds, The defamer separates princes, because dissensions, which were small at the beginning, little by little separate both the multitude and the governors into different factions, as Homer says about strife,

Small at first through fear, soon it raises itself into the air, and walks upon the ground, and hides its head among the clouds.

But lest Solomon seem by this ἐπιεικεία to remove correction entirely, he soon adds another saying about admonition. And he distinguishes those to be admonished; some are curable, others are incurable.

Latin:

Non decent stultum labia superba. Nec principes labia mendacia.

Dvo sunt praecepta, Alterum de modestia seu vitanda arrogantia, Alterum de veritate seu vitanda sophistica. Constat autem utrumque vitium valde commune esse, & in gubernatione duas praecipuas pestes esse, arrogantiam sive πολνπραγμοσύνκην & sophisticam.

Est autem Modestia virtus, quae in opinione de nobis & sermone cavet, ne plus tribuamus nobis, quam res est, & quae in gestu ordinem rationi convenientem servat.

Est autem proxima virtus modestiae, & eius radix Timor Dei, qui est expavescere agnitione nostrae infirmitatis, & iudicii divini, quae agnitio cum compescit & frenat animos, non sumimus nobis plus, quam possumus praestare. Monet igitur hic vitanda esse vicia opposita modestiae, videlicet arrogantiam, πολυπργμοσύνην, petulantiam, quia revera pugnant cum timore Dei. Et arrogantes inquit esse fatuos, & si saepe suo iudicio ulade videntur sapere, & habent applaudentes alios, sed sequuntur exitus tragici, quos quia non praevident, revera sunt fatui, Ut scriptum est, Deus superbis resistit, humilibus autem dat gratiam.

Alterum dictum obscurius est, Principes non decet sophistica, Praecipue enim summi gubernatores in Ecclesia & in imperiis debent esse custodes veritatis divinitus traditae. Item, veritatis in iudiciis. Quia sophistica in religionibus & in iudiciis horribiliter perturbat hominum vitam.

Qui celat alienum delictum, amicitiam acquirit. Qui autem palam facit, dissidium.

Accommodetur dictum ad ἐπιείκειαν. Estque sententia, Tegendi sunt naevi amicorum, sed modus est eius rei. Si error doctrinae aut delictum manifestum est, non potest tegi. Sed iusto ordine haec sananda sunt. Primum autor admonendus est, qui si emendatur, ipsius conversio medetur scandalo. Sed si non est manifestum aut atrox malum, & medicatio est periculosa, tolerandum & tegendum est, iuxta illud,

Curando fieri quaedam peiora videmus Vulnera, quae melius non tetigisse fuit.

Item, Universa delicta operit dilectio. Item, Remittite, & remittetur vobis. Et in hac re immensam Dei misericordiam cogitemus, qui etiam in sanctis tolerat magnam molem sordium & errorum, & cum punit, tamen non accendit totam iram suam, ut in Psalmo dicitur. Item, Si iniquitates observaveris Domine, Domine quis sustinebit? Haec admiranda bonitas quotidie in invocatione cogitanda est, & simul deploranda nostra immundicies, & Deo tegenti & sananti gratiae agendae sunt, & propter ipsum aliis quoque remittamus.

Addit autem Salomon, Infamatorem distrahere principes, quia dissensiones, quae initio parvae fuerunt, paulatim distrahunt & multitudinem, & gubernatores, in diversas factiones, Ut Homerus de lire inquit,

Parva metu primo mox sese attollit in auras,Ingrediturque solo, & caput inter nubila condit.

Ne autem Salomon hac ἐπιεικεία prorsus tollere videatur correctionem, mox addit aliud dictum de admonitione. Et discernit admonendos, alii sunt sanabiles, alii sunt insanabiles.

## 17:11
An evil man seeks only quarrels, And a cruel messenger will meet him.

Contentious men are censured, whether ἐριστικοὶ, or ἀντιλογικοἰ, who wrangle without end, when they either have no causes, or ones too light to have been the reason for moving contentions, as when antiquity contended about the day of Easter, or about leavened bread.

Therefore this sentiment warns that we should not fight about all matters, but the foundation, that is, the Articles of faith, should be kept uncorrupted, and the worship of idols should be censured. In other matters, where one should fight or should not fight, it is a singular wisdom to judge,

And in all our life let us keep in sight, and always carry around with us, and often remember the most ardent prayer of the Son of God made in the Agony, in which he embraces two greatest things, Father, make it so that they may be one in us. Item, Father, sanctify them in the truth, your word is truth. He wishes, therefore, that the first care be of retaining true doctrine, the next of protecting concord. Let us direct our whole life to this norm. Furthermore, let him who is about to litigate consider this, lest he rashly disturb his own and another's invocation, Because immediately after hatreds or factions have flared up, invocation is disturbed.

And it is to be diligently observed in this place, that he names the contentious man an evil, because he moves contentions, inflamed by private affections, rather than because of the magnitude of the matters. Mention of punishment is also added, A cruel messenger will meet him, according to that, With what measure you have measured, it will be measured to you. Item, From another you should expect what you have done to another. Item, Do not judge, lest you be judged.

Latin:

Tantum iurgia quaerit malus, Et nuncius crudelis occurret ei.

Taxantur homines contentiosi, seu ἐριστικοὶ, seu ἀντιλογικοἰ, quisine fine rixanur, cum aut nullas causas habent, aut leviores, quam ut propter eas certamina movenda fuerint, Ut cum vetustas de die Paschatis contendit, vel de fermentato.

Monet igitur haec sententia, ne pugnemus de omnibus materiis, sed fundamentum, id est, Articuli fidei retineantur incorrupti, & taxetur cultus idolorum. In aliis materiis, ubi pugnandum sit, aut non pugnandum, singularis sapientia est iudicare,

Ac in omni vita teneamus in conspectu, & semper nobiscum circumferamus, ac saepe recordemur ardentissimam precationem Filii Dei factam in Agone, in qua duas res maximas complectitur, Pater fac, ut in nobis unum sint. Item, Pa ter sanctifica eos in veritate, sermo tuus est veritas. Vult igitur primam esse curam retinendae verae doctrinae, proximam tuendae concordiae. Ad hanc normam dirigamus totam vitam. Praeterea illud cogitet litigaturus, ne temere suam & alienam invocationem turbet, Quia statim postquam exarserunt odia vel factiones, turbatur invocatio.

Ac diligenter hoc loco observandum est, quod contentiosum nominat malum, quia movet certamina, accensus privatis affectibus, potius quam propter rerum magnitudinem. Additur & poenae mentio, Crudelis nuncius occurret ei, iuxta illud, Qua mensura mensi fueritis, eadem metietur vobis. Item, Ab alio expectes alteri quae feceris. Item, Nolite iudicare, ne iudicemini.

## 17:12
It is better to meet a bear robbed of her cubs, than a fool.

Docility is a necessary virtue, because it is necessary to hear the divine voice. And it has been said about others, Test the spirits whether they are from God. The opposite vices are, Excessive credulity, about which it is said, He who believes quickly is light of heart, and Pertinacity, which is an assertion, even when an error is recognized, or at least the mind is convinced, so that it ought to recognize the error. Moreover, it violates the second commandment and the eighth, You shall not speak false testimony, as the Anabaptists, when they are convicted and persist in error, violate the second commandment, because they cloak their error with the name of God. Such fury is a horrible sin. Therefore it is commonly said, To err is human, to persist in error is diabolical.

Now there is a question, how a man is convinced. In forensic affairs he is convinced by the voice of a legitimate judge. In Philosophy he is convinced by the evidence of principles, experience, and demonstrations. In the doctrine of the Church the mind is convinced by the proper and immovable testimonies of the divine voice, Because concerning all necessary articles, or concerning the foundation, firm and immovable testimonies exist in divine doctrine. That some, however, do not wish to be convinced, happens through their own perversity. Just as the Jews did not wish to be convinced, even though they saw the dead being raised. And because those pertinacious ones do not yield to the judgments of the true Church, Ecclesiastical dissensions remain, until God himself judges, having destroyed the impious, just as he destroyed the Jewish polity, just as he gradually destroyed the Arians.

Latin:

Melius est occurrere ursae orbatae catulis, quam stulto.

Virtus necessaria est docilitas, quia necesse est audire vocem divinam. Et de aliis dictum est, Probate spiritus an ex Deo sint. Opposita vitia sunt, Nimia credulitas, de qua dicitur, Qui cito credit, levis est corde, & Pertinacia, quae est asseruratio, etiam cum agnoscitur error, vel certe mens convincitur, ut errorem agnoscere deberet. Violat autem secundum praeceptum & octavum, Non dicas falsum testimonium, ut Anabaptistae, cum convicti perseverant in errore, violant secundum praeceptum, quia errori suo praetexunt nomen Dei. Talis furor est horribile peccatum. Ideo usitate dicitur, Errare humanum est, perseverare in errore diabolicum.

Iam est quaestio, quomodo convincatur homo. In forensibus negociis convincitur voce legitimi iudicis. In Philosophia convincitur evidentia principiorum, experientiae, & demonstrationum. In doctrina Ecclesiae convincitur mens propriis & immotis testimoniis vocis divinae, Quia de omnibus necessariis articulis, seu de fundamento, extant firma & immota testimonia in doctrina divina. Quod autem aliqui convinci nolunt, fit perversitate ipsorum. Sicut Iudaei nolebant convinci, etiamsi videbant resuscitari mortuos. Et quia iudiciis verae Ecclesiae pertinaces illi non cedunt, manent dissidia Ecclesiastica, donec Deus ipse diiudicat, deletis impiis, sicut delevit politiam Iudaicam, sicut paulatim Arrianos delevit.

## 18:1 Argument
CHAPTER XVIII.

He who loves discords, moves quarrels on any occasion whatsoever.

THERE is also this reproach of a contentious nature, and it signifies that contentions often arise from the φιλονεικία, of men, not from the magnitude of matters, and that for contentious people any light occasion whatsoever is the cause of a great fire. Let this sentence be added to the previous ones, concerning contentious people, the chief of which was, He who loves quarrels, loves sin.

Let us know, therefore, that there are two highest commandments, by which life is chiefly to be ruled, namely, that the purity of doctrine be retained, and that concord not be disturbed. These pertain to these laws, You shall not have strange Gods. Item, You shall love your neighbor. And Christ embraces both commandments in his prayer, Father, sanctify them in the truth, etc. The order of these commandments teaches where one must fight, and where one must not fight. When the first commandment is violated, that is, when the true doctrine concerning God is corrupted, it is necessary to oppose, or when the foundation is shaken, concerning which Paul says, No other foundation can be laid than that which has been laid, which is Christ. Item, If anyone has taught another Gospel, let him be anathema.

But concerning other matters outside the foundation, one must judge prudently where struggles are to be stirred up, and where ἐπιείκεια, is to be applied, and some small inconveniences are to be overlooked and pardoned for the sake of public harmony and the infirmity of men, just as it is often commanded concerning the toleration of the weak. Nor was it poorly said.

We see some things become worse by being cured, Wounds which it were better not to have touched.

Latin:

CAPUT XVIII.

Qui amat discordias, quacunque occasione movet lites.

EST & haec reprehensio naturae contentiosae, & significat saepe oriri certamina ab hominium φιλονεικία, non a rerum magnitudine, & contentiosis quamlibet levem occasionem causam esse magni incendii. Adiungatur haec sententia ad superiores, de contentiosis, quarum praecipua fuit, Diligens rixas, diligit peccatum.

Sciamus igitur duo summa praecepta esse, quibus praecipue vita regenda est, videlicet, ut retineatur doctrinae puritas, & ne concordia turbetur. Haec pertinent ad has leges, Non habebis Deos alienos. Item, Diliges proximum. Et utrumque praeceptum Christus in precatione complectitur, Pater, sanctifica eos in veritate &c. Horum praeceptorum ordo docet, ubi pugnandum sit, & ubi non pugnandum sit. Quando primum praeceptum violatur, hoc est, cum vera doctrina de Deo corrumpitur, necesse est adversari, seu quando fundamentum labefactatur, de quo Paulus inquit, Fundamentum aliud poni non potest praeter id quod positum est, quod est Christus. Item, Si quis aliud Evangelium docuerit, Anathema sit.

Sed de aliis materiis extra fundamentum prudenter iudicandum est, ubi sint movenda certamina, & ubi adhibenda sit ἐπιείκεια, & aliqua parva incommoda dissimulanda sint, & condonanda publicae concordiae, & infirmitati hominum, sicut saepe praecipitur de tolerandis infirmis. Nec male dictum est.

Curando fieri quaedam peiora videmus,Vulnera, quae melius non tetigisse fuit.

## 18:17
The just man is at the beginning the accuser of himself, Afterwards he inquires into another.

All men in this weakness of nature are proud, we please ourselves, we flatter ourselves, we love ourselves too much, we admire ourselves, we assent to ourselves, and we love κολακίας,, but toward others we act with bitter censure.

Hence there is the fable about the two wallets, concerning which Catullus says. Truly we are all deceived in the same way, Nor is there anyone, whom you could not see to be a Suffenus in some matter. To each his own error is attributed. But we do not see the part of the wallet that is on our back. And Horace says. When you look at your own evils with bleary, anointed eyes, why do you see so sharply in the vices of friends, as either an eagle, or an Epidaurian serpent?

And in the Gospel hypocrisy is censured, of one who, having a beam in his eye, wishes to take the splinter out of another's. To this pride and perversity of judgments this sentence is applied. The just man is in the beginning the accuser of himself, he inquires into himself, he acknowledges his own infirmity, because even the just have many blemishes and falls, according to that, There is no just man on earth, who though he does good, does not also sin. Thus also Paul leads us to the inspection of ourselves, saying, Let each one prove his own work, and so he will have glory from himself, not from others. In Greek the sentence is elegantly rendered, The just man is the accuser of himself, in πρωπολογία.. For it alludes to the custom of the courts, where πρωπολογία is called the speech of the plaintiff.

Latin:

Iustus initio accusator est sui, Postea inquirit in alium.

Omnes homines in hac imbecillitate naturae sumus superbi, nobis ipsi placemus, nobis blandimur, nos ipsos nimium amamus, nos ipsos admiramur, nobis assentamur, & amamus κολακίας, in alios vero acerbe censuram agimus.

Hinc Apologus est de duabus manticis, de quibus inquit Catullus.Nimirum idem omnes fallimur, Neque est quisquam,Quem non in aliqua re videre SuffenumPossis. Suus cuique attributus est error.Sed non videmus manticae quod in tergo est.Et Horatius inquit.Cum tua pervideas oculis mala lippus inunctis,Cur in amicorum vitiis tam cernis acutum.Quam aut aquila, aut serpens Epidaurius?

Et in Evangelio taxatur hypocria, qui habens trabem in oculo, vult alteri festucam eximere. Huic superbiae & perversitati iudiciorum apposita est haec sententia. Iustus in principio accusator est sui, inquirit in sese, agnoscit suam infirmitatem, quia etiam iusti multos naevos & lapsus habent, Iuxta illud, Non est iustus in terra, qui quanquam benefacit, non etiam peccet. Sic & Paulus deducit nos ad inspectionem nostri, inquiens, Probet unusquisque suum opus, & sic habebit gloriam a sese, non ex aliis. Graece eleganter est reddita sententia, Iustus accusator est sui, in πρωπολογία. Alludit enim ad morem iudiciorum, ubi πρωπολογία dicitur actoris oratio.

## 18:22
He who finds a wife, finds a good thing, And will draw pleasure from the Lord.

This is a praise of marriage, and it is signified that a happy and peaceful marriage is a singular gift of God. For to find signifies to marry happily, Finding, that is, happily obtaining or happily marrying, or in Greek ἐυτυχῶς ἐυτυχὼν..

Moreover, in praises of marriage it is most appropriate to begin them from the divine institution, and from the causes of the divine counsel. The institution is expressed in Genesis, where it is written. The Lord said, it is not good for man, etc. He said, that is, by his secret counsel he ordained, and by wonderful wisdom decreed to establish and preserve nature in this way, and he revealed this counsel, and wishes it to be valid. He wishes the union of male and female, and the procreation of the human race, to be such. Therefore the contrary law is to be execrated, the Pontiff said, not legitimate marriages.

But why God ordained it, the cause of the counsel should also be considered, because he created the human race for society, and he surrounded this society with certain boundaries. He prohibits wandering lusts, and punishes them horribly. For he wishes the virtue which is called chastity to be understood in the human race. So that we may know that God himself is a chaste mind, and by the thought of this virtue we may separate him in invocation from all unclean natures. For it is necessary for us to think what we invoke, where, and what that is which we invoke. But since we do not embrace God with our arms, as if he were a corporeal mass, we behold him with the mind shown in a certain way, namely, as sounding his own voice, and revealing himself by having sent the Son. And what sort he is, must be thought upon, namely wise, good, just, a judge, chaste, truthful, most free, merciful, etc.

These things must be thought upon in every invocation, so that you may discern God from all fictitious deities, from idols, and from devils.

Let us know, therefore, that the law concerning chastity has been established, so that we may acknowledge God to be a chaste mind, and may invoke him in chastity, because he wills that individual families be Churches invoking and celebrating him. Nor can a mind polluted by wandering lusts, persisting in that contamination, invoke him. As it is written, Without sanctification no one will see God.

Here let us also consider the horrible examples of punishments of all times, and the divine threat, God will judge fornicators and adulterers.

But why, says Solomon, will he draw pleasure from the Lord?

There is consolation in the marriage of the pious, to be opposed to the magnitude and variety of public and private dangers , which can neither all be foreseen nor avoided by human diligence. Because it is true what Menander said, He who takes a wife sails into the middle of the sea in the most savage storms.

Private dangers are such as diseases, poverty, unhappy births, the stubbornness of children. Public ones are such as when wars scatter or destroy families, or force miserable parents to go into exile with small children.

To these such great dangers Solomon warns that the presence of God must be opposed, who is the guardian both of your Church and of the public Church. As it is written, All your hairs are numbered. Item, I will be a wall of fire in your circuit. Item, Commend your way to God, and he will act.

Thus God defended the families of Zechariah, Anna, Mary, and many others, when indeed the armies of the worst nations were wandering through Judea, and constant robberies were taking place. Among such dangers God always covers some gatherings of the pious. Therefore Solomon proposes this consolation to the Churches of spouses, They will draw pleasure from the Lord. And at the same time let us know this, that it is necessary for a family to be a Church, to sound the voice of God, to invoke God rightly, and to serve him with true duties, etc.

And such a Church without any doubt will be a care to God, in the midst of the waves and however great the tumults of empires.

Latin:

Inveniens uxorem, invenit bonum, Et hauriet voluptatem a Domino.

Laus est coniugii, & significatur singulare Dei donum esse felix & placidum coniugium. Nam invenire significat feliciter ducere, Inveniens, id est feliciter nactus seu feliciter ducens, seu Graece ἐυτυχῶς ἐυτυχὼν.

Caeterum in laudationibus coniugii aptissimum est ordiri eas ab institutione divina, et a causis divini consilii. Institutio expressa est in Genesi, ubi scriptum est. Dixit Dominus, non est homini bonum, &c. Dixit, id est, suo arcano consilio sanxit, & mirabili sapientia decrevit hoc modo condere & conservare naturam, & hoc consilium patefecit, & vult ratum esse. Vult talem esse copulationem maris & feminae, & procreationem generis humani. Execranda est igitur contraria lex, Dixit Pontifex, ne legitima coniugia.

Sed cur sanxerit Deus, causa consilii etiam consideretur, quia condidit genus humanum ad societatem, & huic societati certas metas circundedit. Prohibet vagas libidines, & horribiliter punit. Vult enim in genere humano intelligi virtutem, quae vocatur castitas. Ut sciamus ipsum Deum esse mentem castam, & huius virtutis cogitatione seiungamus eum in invocatione ab omnibus naturis immundis. Necesse est enim nos cogitare quid invocemus, ubi, & quid sit illud quod invocamus. Cum autem non complectamur brachiis Deum, velut molem corpoream, mente eum intuemur certo modo ostensum, Videlicet sonantem suam vocem, & patefacientem se misso Filio. Et qualis sit, cogitandum est, videlicet sapiens, bonus, iustus, iudex, castus, verax, liberrimus, misericors &c.

Haec in omni invocatione cogitanda sunt, ut discernas Deum ab omnibus commenticiis numinibus, ab Idolis & a diabolis.

Sciamus igitur sancitam esse legem de castitate, ut agnoscamus Deum esse mentem castam, & in castitate invocemus, quia vult singulas familias Ecclesias esse invocantes, & celebrantes ipsum. Nec potest invocare mens polluta vagis libidinibus, perseverans in illa contaminatione. Sicut scriptum est, Sine sanctificatione nemo videbit Deum.

Hic etiam consideremus horribilia exempla poenarum, omnium temporum, & comminationem divinam, Scortatores & adulteros iudicabit Deus.

Sed cur inquit Salomon, Hauriet voluptatem a Domino?

Consolatio est in coniugio piorum, opponenda magnitudini & varietati periculorum publicorum & privatorum, quae nec prospici omnia, nec vitari humana diligentia possunt. Quia verum est quod Menander dixit, Ducens uxorem in medium pelagus saevissimis tempestatibus navigat.

Privata pericula sunt, ut morbi, inopiae, infelices partus, contumacia natorum. Publica, ut cum bella dissipant, aut delent familias, aut cogunt miseros parentes exulare cum parvis natis.

His tantis periculis monet Salomon opponendam esse praesentiam Dei, qui est custos, & tuae Ecclesiae & publicae Ecclesiae. Sicut scriptum est, Omnes capilli vestri numerati sunt. Item, Ero murus igneus in circuitu vestro. Item, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet.

Sic familias Zachariae, Annae, Mariae, & multorum aliorum defendit Deus, cum quidem in ludaea vagarentur pessimarum gentium exercitus, & assidua latrocinia fierent. Inter talia pericula aliquos coetus piorum semper tegit Deus. Ideo proponit Salomon hanc consolationem Ecclesiis coniugum, Haurient voluptatem a Domino. Et simul hoc sciamus, oportere familiam Ecclesiam esse, sonare vocem Dei, recte invocare Deum, & veris officiis ei servire &c.

Et talis Ecclesia sine ulla dubitatione curae erit Deo, inter medios fluctus, & quamlibet magnos tumultus imperiorum.

## 19:1 Argument
CHAPTER XIX.

Latin:

CAPUT XIX.

## 19:10
DELIGHTS do not befit a fool.

In chapter 33 of Ecclesiasticus, discipline is wisely and orderly established, and a measure is set, and the example can be widely transferred to governance. Fodder, a rod, and a burden for an ass, thus bread, discipline, and work for a servant, that is, governments should not only be punishments and butcheries of men, but should be salutary, established in a certain way, they should grant sustenance convenient for men, but they should also add certain labors, which may be exercises of virtue for men, and let an inspector and ἐργοδιώκτας, fear of punishments, and punishments of delinquents be added. Thus let us know that these three things must be observed by each of us regarding our bodies, our children, and our families. Let a tutor also observe these three things, let him have regard for health in a pupil, let him teach and punish. For those who only punish, and do not teach, accomplish nothing, etc. If anything from these three is omitted in governance, neither life nor discipline can be retained, because all these things are pernicious to life, Luxury, idleness, excessive servitude. Thus it is said, Delights do not befit a fool, that is, men must be ruled by labors and the fear of punishments. Just as many sayings warn that men become insolent in prosperous circumstances. Sallust, Prosperous circumstances fatigue even the minds of the wise. Item, Nothing is more intolerable than a fortunate fool.

A prudent man is slow to anger.

Pliny says, He who hates vices, hates men. Which is rightly said concerning the common frailty of men, not concerning enormous crimes, as the natures of men differ, of which some are more sluggish, others more irascible, others more morose, others more free, as each one's frailty is his own. We ought both to bear these diseases in this common conversation wisely, and to forgive them for the sake of common tranquility with prudent dissimulation. Concerning this kind, it is commanded: You shall know the customs of a friend, you shall not hate them. Item, Love covers all sins. Item, Forgive, and it shall be forgiven you. And the saying of the Emperor Frederick is recited, He who does not know how to dissimulate, does not know how to rule. And it is very often commanded concerning tolerating the weak in the Church.

Latin:

NON decent stultum delitiae.

IN capite 33. Ecclesiastici sapienter & ordinata est disciplina, & modus constituts, ac late ad gubernationem transferri exemplum potest. Pabulum, virga & onus asino, sic panis, disciplina & opus servo, Id est, imperia non tantum sint poenae & carnificinae hominum, sed sint salutaria certo modo constituta, concedant victum convenientem hominibus, Addant autem & certos labores, qui sint hominibus exercitia virtutis, Et accedat inspector & ἐργοδιώκτας, metus poenarum, & poenae delinquentium. Sic singuli de nostris corporibus, de filiis, de familiis haec tria servanda esse sciamus. Haec tria servet & paedagogus, habeat rationem valetudinis in discipulo, doceat & castiget. Nam qui tantum castigant, non docent, nihil proficiunt &c. Si quid ex his tribus in gubernatione omittitur, nec vita nec disciplina retineri potest, Quia haec omnia perniciosa sunt vitae, Luxus, ocium, nimia servitus. Ita dicitur, Non decent stultum deliciae, Id est, Laboribus & metu poenarum homines regendisunt. Sicut multa dicta monent, insolescere homines in rebus secundis. Salustius, Secundae res etiam sapientum animos fatigant. Item, Nihil insipiente fortunato intolerabilius est.

Prudens homo tarde irascitur.

Plinius inquit, Qui vicia odit, homines odit. Quod recte dicitur de communi imbecillitate hominum, non de enormibus delictis, ut differunt hominum naturae, quarum aliae sunt segniores, aliae iracundiores, aliae morosiores, aliae liberiores, ut sua cuiusque est imbecillitas. Hos morbos in hac communi conversatione & sapienter ferre debemus, & prudenti dissimulatione communi tranquillitati condonare. De hoc genere praeceptum est. Mores amici noveris, non oderis. Item, Universa delicta operit dilectio. Item, Remittite, & remittetur vobis. Et recitatur dictum Imperatoris Friderici, Qui dissimulare nescit, imperare nescit. Et de tolerandis infirmis in Ecclesia saepissime praecipitur.

## 19:29
When the scoffer is struck, the inexperienced will be more cautious.

There are four causes of punishments. The first, because it is the justice of God to punish contumacy.

The second, because God wishes the distinction between honorable and base things to be recognized, and that He Himself is a judge loving honorable things and punishing base things.

The third cause, that the human race may be preserved.

The fourth, that others may be warned by the example and be restrained by fear. Concerning this fourth degree, he says here, When the scoffer is struck, the inexperienced will be more cautious.

However, the distinction must be considered: To strike the mocker, he says, that is, the contumacious one, rushing into crimes with Epicurean contempt, because God defers punishments for a while; afterwards, when the contempt grows, more atrocious punishments come.

He therefore threatens punishment to those persevering in contempt, and at the same time signifies that some who are more unlearned are warned by those examples, so that they may become better, and because this divine order is not entirely useless, but some, having been warned, are amended.

Latin:

Derisore percusso, Imperitus erit cautior.

Quatuor causae sunt poenarum. Prima, quia iusticia Dei est punire contumaciam.

Secunda, quia Deus vult agnosci discrimen honestorum & turpium, & se esse iudicem amantem honesta, & punientem turpia.

Tertia causa, ut conservetur genus humanum.

Quarta, ut exemplo aliis commonefiant, & metu coherceantur. De hoc quarto gradu hic inquit, Derisore percusso, imperitus erit cautior.

Considerandum est autem discrimen, Percuti inquit subsannatorem, Id est, contumacem, Epicureo contemptu ruentem in scelera, quia Deus aliquantisper differt poenas, postea cum crescit contemptus, veniunt poenae atrociores.

Minatur itaque poenam perseverantibus in contemptu, & simul significat aliquos rudiores illis exemplis commonefieri, ut fiant meliores, & quia hic ordo divinus non prorsus inutilis est, sed aliqui commonefacti emendantur.

## 20:1 Argument
CHAPTER XX.

Latin:

CAPUT XX.

## 20:12
The hearing ear, and the seeing eye, the Lord makes both.

In a happy government, these two things must concur: Good counsels of the governors, and the obedience of the people, and he says that both are a gift of God. As Jeremiah was a good monitor, but he did not have King Zedekiah obeying, therefore Jerusalem was destroyed. Hezekiah obeyed Isaiah, therefore the city was saved. This is the principal sentiment of this most beautiful saying, The hearing ear, and the seeing eye, God gives both. He warns, however, that polities and economies are not to be ruled by human wisdom alone, but that the help of God is to be sought, both in giving counsel, and in bending the wills of men to obedience, and in ruling events. As Jeremiah says, I know, O Lord, that his way is not of man. And in the Psalm, Unless the Lord build the house, etc. Item, Commit thy way to the Lord, and he shall do it.

Furthermore, the saying of Solomon is also to be applied in general to all excellent craftsmen; their minds find and discover many things in the arts divinely, which others do not perceive.

There is a God in us, we grow warm when he stirs us, That spirit comes from ethereal seats.

It is bad, it is bad, they say when they hold it. But after they have lost it, they praise it.

Since there are many other common vices of men, which we bring with us when we are born?

then also a loathing of present goods, because minds are unstable, and offended by some inconveniences, they easily crave others; from this many sayings have arisen.

Likewise Horace, The lazy ox desires the saddle, the horse desires to plow.

And a fable exists in Aesop about the Ass, repeatedly changing his condition for the worse. And the verses of Sophocles are in Ajax, Foolish men do not recognize a good, while it is in their hands, before they lose it; afterwards, having lost it, they desire it.

Finally, individuals discover in themselves such loathings of their present state, just as he says in Terence, We are displeased with ourselves. And it is most common to bear present governments with difficulty and to crave others, as in Thucydides ἀεἰ τὸ παρὸν βαρὺ, That is, the present government is always burdensome.

Against this instability of minds, many precepts have been handed down. Isocrates says, στέργε τα παρόντα, That is, Be content with present things. Likewise, May you wish to be what you are, and prefer nothing else. And Pindar most charmingly advises that, since evils are mixed with goods, goods are to be selected and cherished, and evils are to be softened by art. According to one good, two losses are distributed to men, but fools cannot elegantly bear evils, whereas the honest bring forth the goods.

Let us therefore learn this art necessary in life. It belongs to justice to embrace goods with gratitude, then indeed to prudence and patience to bear certain inconveniences moderately, lest by foolish changes we summon more evil. As Periander advises, μὴ κακῷ κακὸιυ ἰῶ, That is, do not cure evil with evil.

To this doctrine let this sentence of Solomon also be referred, which properly taxes the loathing of present goods, and signifies that things lost are again desired and sought. Thus also Paul commands that we learn to understand present goods, and give thanks to God for them, Rejoice always, pray without ceasing. Likewise, Do not murmur against God, just as the greatest part of the human race lives in perpetual impatience and grumbling against God, when nevertheless we are assiduously gifted by God with many huge goods. But because we sustain some burdens at the same time, and this life is subject to many dangers, consolations are also added, namely divine promises, and commands concerning prayer. In the same place, therefore, Paul says, Pray always.

Let it be enough to have briefly advised these things concerning the saying of Solomon, which extends most widely. Let it be referred, however, to the common place, to the loathing of present goods, which fights against justice and tolerance, and arises from the mobility of the mind, craving now one thing and now another.

Latin:

Aurem audientem, & oculum videntem, Dominus facit utrumque.

Ingubernatione felici haec duo concurrere oportet, Bona consilia gubernantium, & Obedientiam populi, & utrumque dicit esse donum Dei. Ut leremias erat bonus monitor, sed non habebat regem Zedechiam obtemperantem, Ideo Hierosolyma deleta est. Esaiae obtemperabat Ezechias, Ideo civitas servata est. Haec est principalis sententia huius venustissimi dicti, Aurem audientem, & oculum videntem, Deus dat utrunque. Monet autem non sola sapientia humana regi politias & oeconomias, sed petendum esse auxilium Dei, & in dando consilio, & in flectendis voluntatibus hominum ad obedientiam, & in regendis eventibus. Sicut leremias inquit, Scio Domine, quod non est hominis via eius. Et in Psalmo, Nisi Dominus aedificaverit domum &c. Item, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet.

Caeterum & dictum Salomonis in genere accommodandum est ad omnes excellentes artifices, horum mentes inveniunt & deprehendunt multa in artibus divinitus, quae alii non cernunt.

Est Deus in nobis agitante calescimus illo,Sedibus aethereis spiritus ille venit.

Malum est, malum est, dicunt cum tenent. Sed postquam amiserunt, laudant.

Cum alia multa vicia communia sunt hominum, quae nascentes nobiscum asferimus?

tum etiam fastidium bonorum praesentium, quia animi sunt instabiles, & offensi aliquibus incommodis, facile appetunt alia, Inde multa dicta orta sunt.

Item Horatius, Optat ephippia bos piger, optat arare caballus.

Et Apologus apud Aesopum extat de Asino, subinde mutante conditionem in deteriorem. Et Sophosclis versus sunt in Aiace, Stulti homines non agnoscunt bonum, dum est in manibus, priusquam amittant, postea amissa, desiderant.

Denique singuli in seipsis deprehendunt talia fastidia praesentis status, Sicut & ille apud Terentium inquit, Nostri nosmet paenitet. Et usitatissimum est, praesentia imperia aegre ferre & appetere alia, sicut apud Thucyd. ἀεἰ τὸ παρὸν βαρὺ, Id est, semper praesens imperium est grave.

Adversus hanc instabilitatem animorum, multa praecepta tradita sunt. Isocrates inquit, στέργε τα παρόντα, Id est, Boni consule praesentia. Item, Quod sis, esse velis, nihilque malis. Et Pindarus venustissime monet, cum bonis mala admixta sint, bona excerpenda & fovenda esse, & arte lenienda mala. Iuxta unum bonum damna duo distributa sunt hominibus, non possunt autem stulti eleganter ferre mala, sed honesti bona foras proferunt.

Hanc igitur artem in vita necessariam discamus. Iusticiae est bona amplecti cum gratitudine, Deinde vero prudentiae ac pacidntiae quaedam incommoda moderate ferre, ne stultis mutationibus plus mali accersamus. Ut monet Periander, μὴ κακῷ κακὸιυ ἰῶ, Id est, malum malo mediceris.

Ad hanc doctrinam referatur & haec sententia Salomonis, quae proprie taxat fastidia praesentium bonorum, & significat amissa rursus desiderari & requiri. Sic & Paulus prrcepit, ut praesentia bona intelligere discamus, & pro iis Deo gratiae agamus, Semper gaudete, semper gratias agite. Item, Non murmurate adversus Deum, sicut maxima pars generis humani vivit in perpetua impacientia & fremitu adversus Deum, cum tamen multis ingentibus bonis a Deo assidue donemur. Sed quia simul onera aliqua sustinemus, & haec vita multis periculis obnoxia est, adiunguntur etiam consolationes, videlicet promissiones divinae, & mandata de precatione. Eodem igitur loco inquit Paulus, Semper orate.

Haec de dicto Salomonis, quod latissime patet, breviter monuisse satis sit. Referatur autem ad locum communem, ad fastidium bonorum praesentium, quod pugnat cum iusticia & tolerantia, & oritur ex mobilitate animi, subinde alia atque alia appetentis.

## 20:21
An inheritance, to which one hastens at the beginning, will finally lack a blessing.

There is a precept about awaiting a calling. Absalom could have become king, if he had awaited the death of his father, but because he seizes dominion before the time and without a calling, he is again cast out and oppressed with horrible punishments. The sons of Ephraim invaded Canaan before Moses, with carnal confidence in the divine promise, because the land of Canaan had been promised to the stock of Abraham, but because they wage war without a calling, by human persuasion, they are killed, and the whole plan was useless. 1 Chron. 7. Let us therefore learn to consider the calling, and to move nothing without a compelling calling.

Latin:

Haereditas, ad quam properatur initio, tandem benedictio-ne carebit.

Praeceptum est de expectanda vocatione. Absolon potuisset fieri rex, si expectasset mortem patris, sed quia ante tempus & sine vocatione rapit dominationem, rursus excutitur & opprimitur horribilibus poenis. Filii Ephraim ante Mosen invaserunt Cananaeam, carnali fiducia divinae promissionis, quia stirpi Abrahae terra Canaan promissa erat, Sed quia sine vocatione, persuasione humana movent bellum, interficiuntur, & totum consilium inutile fuit. 1. Paral. 7. Discamus igitur considerare vocationem, & nihil sine vocatione cogente movere.

## 20:25
It is ruin for a man to commit a holy sacrilege, and afterwards to make vows.

In the Latin writing and version there are errors, which have provided an occasion for foreign and fictitious interpretations, and for accommodation to monastic vows. Otherwise, the sentence of Solomon is clear. He taxes the most common superstition of the greatest part of men, who, polluted by impiety, by the persecution of the Gospel, and by other crimes against conscience, afterwards dream that they please God on account of ceremonies, and exalt themselves above others, as those who now defend the abuses of the Mass.

Latin:

Ruina est homini, sancta contumelia afficere, & postea vota facere.

IN latina scriptione & versione mendae sunt, quae praebuerunt occasionem alienis & commenticiis interpretionibus, & accommodationi ad vota monastica. Caeterum Salomonis sententia perspicua est. Taxat usitatissimam superstitionem maximae partis hominum, qui polluti impietate, persecutione Evangelii, & aliis sceleribus contra conscientiam, postea somniant se placere Deo, propter caeremonias, & se efferunt supra alios, ut qui nunc defendunt abusus Missae.

## 21:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXI.

Latin:

CAPUT XXI.

## 21:30-31
THERE is no wisdom, no prudence, no counsel against the Lord. The horse is prepared for the day of battle, but true victory proceeds from God.

This doctrine has been often repeated, that without the help of God counsels are not happy, nor are the labors of men fortunate, as in the Psalm, Unless the Lord build the house, etc. Item, Without me you can do nothing. Item, No man can take anything to himself, unless it has been given to him from heaven. These sayings do not mean that men do absolutely nothing, and that the human will has itself like a statue only passively. But these three precepts must be joined in every life, in all counsels, and in all business.

First, that each one understands his own calling, and performs the duties of his calling. This diligence is in some part in our power. And to this pertain the precepts concerning diligence and labor, As to the Ephesians, Walk wisely, not as fools. From the Thessalonians. Strive ambitiously to do your own things.

The second precept,

When we perform the duties of our calling, let the invocation of God and trust in divine help be added, and let us firmly establish that labors are neither happy without God, nor unhappy or sterile when we seriously ask to be helped, according to these sayings, Commit your way to God, and he will act. Item, Your labor will not be in vain in the Lord. 1. John 5. This is the confidence which we have toward God, that if we ask anything according to his will, he hears us, etc.

These consolations must be opposed to the difficulties which make spirits languish, doubting whether the labor will be profitable. And faith and invocation must be stirred up, and with the hope of divine help, labors must be performed diligently. And it must be known that these are the chief acts of worship of God, to ask for and expect the help of God in the duties of one's calling, as the Psalm teaches, Be subject to God, and pray to him.

The third precept is,

But let the distinction also be understood between calling and πολυπραγμοσύνην. God does not help evil actions, nor those undertaken by our curiosity outside of our calling. Often, however, even the wise sin, πολυπραγμοσύνη, as Pericles provoking the Lacedaemonians, Agesilaus and Lysander the Thebans, Demosthenes provoking the Macedonian king, Cicero Antony.

Here let us know that many sins concur, Businesses are sought outside of one's calling, and with God neglected, they are undertaken with trust in human wisdom and power. God punishes these sins, as unhappy outcomes in πολυπραγμοσύνη show.

Latin:

NON est sapientia, non prudentia, non consilium contra Dominum. Equus paratur ad diem belli, A Deo vera victoria proficiscitur.

SAepe repetita est haec doctrina, sine auxilio Dei non esse felicia consilia, nec labores hominum fortunatos, ut in Psalmo, Nisi Dominus aedisficaverit domum &c. Item, Sine me nihil potestis facere. Item, Nemo potest sibi sumere quidquam, nisi datum fuerit ei de caelo. Haec dicta non hoc volunt, homines prorsus nihil agere, & voluntatem humanam habere se sicut statuam tantum passive. Sed haec tria praecepta in omni vita, in omnibus consiliis, in omnibus negociis coniungi oportet.

Primum, ut quisque intelligat suam vocationem, & faciat officia suae vocationis. Haec diligentia aliqua ex parte in nostra potestate est. Et huc pertinent praecepta de diligentia & labore, Ut ad Ephesios, Ambulate sapienter, non ut fatui. Ex ad Thessalo. Ambitiose hoc agite, ut propria faciatis.

Secundum praeceptum,

Cum facimus officia vocationis, accedat invocatio Dei, & fiducia auxilii divini, & certo statuamus, nec felices esse labores sine Deo, nec infelices aut steriles, cum serio petimus nos iuvari, iuxta haec dicta, Commenda Deo viam tuam, & ipse faciet. Item, Labor vester non erit inanis in Domino. 1. Iohan. 5. Haec est siducia nostra, quam habemus ad Deum, quod si quid petimus secundum voluntatem eius, audit nos &c.

Hae consolationes opponendae sunt difficultatibus, quae languefaciunt animos, dubitantes an labor profuturus sit. Et fides & invocatio exuscitanda, & spe auxilii divini labores sedulo faciendi sunt. Et sciendum, hos esse praecipuos cultus Dei, in officiis vocationis petere & expectare auxilium Dei, sicut Psalmus docet, Subditus esto Deo, & ora eum.

Tertium praeceptum est,

Sed intelligatur etiam discrimen inter vocationem & πολυπραγμοσύνην. Non iuvat Deus malas actiones, nec susceptas nostra curiositate extra vocationem. Saepe autem & sapientes peccant, πολυπραγμοσύνη, ut Pericles irritans Lacedaemonios, Agesilaus & Lysandet Thebanos, Demosthenes irritans regem Macedonicum, Cicero Antonium.

Hic sciamus multa peccata concurrere, Quaeruntur negocia extra vocationem, & neglecto Deo suscipiuntur fiducia humanae sapientiae & potentiae. Haec peccata punit Deus, ut infelices eventus in πολυπραγμοσύνη ostendunt.

## 22:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXII.

Latin:

CAPUT XXII.

## 22:1
A good name is better than many riches.

This doctrine has also been handed down above. That a good reputation is a good thing and to be desired, and to be defended by virtue, because God instituted this order. First, he wants us to have integral consciences, and not wounded ones. Afterwards, he wants rightly done things to be approved by right judgments, and basely done things to be blamed, so that in the human race the distinction between honorable and base things may be clearly seen, through which God himself is distinguished from evil natures. Then, he also wants us for the sake of example to desire and defend a good reputation, so that others may be invited to the imitation of rightly done things, and so that the petulance of others may not be confirmed by evil examples, because life is in great part governed by examples. Therefore this old saying is often cited, I have need of a good conscience for myself, and a good reputation for my neighbor, That is, because of the judgment of God we have need of a good conscience for ourselves. Then because of our neighbors, for the sake of example, we have need of a good reputation.

He calls good grace the benevolence of men, which arises from a good reputation, That is, from the opinion of virtue, Because virtue invites mediocre minds not infected by envy to the love of itself. Just as Pindar cites the sentiment of Nereus, Excellent virtue is praiseworthy even in an enemy.

The rich & the poor, they dwell together, God makes them both.

Necessary wisdom is in the Church of God, to recognize that the rule of life is the revealed will of God, & not to scrutinize the unrevealed, According to that, Your word is a lamp to my feet Psalm 119. And the revealed will is expressed and set forth in the Law and the Gospel, The unrevealed is concerning the causes, why there is such a great dissimilarity in the fortune of men.

Here minds are in a tumult, such variety and ἄταξια, moves the Epicureans to abolish providence. Others run down to physical causes and fates. Others are angry at God. Against such furies, hearts must be most diligently fortified by the doctrine handed down by God, and by faith and invocation.

First, therefore, faith concerning providence must be confirmed, as in this little book many sentiments are inculcated, teaching this especially, that the variety of fortune does not happen by chance & without the counsel of God &c. as he says above, The blessing of the Lord makes rich. Item, God does all things for his own sake, he even ordains the wicked for punishment, so that his judgment may be recognized. But let the most plain testimonies be in sight. As Psal. 32 The Lord looks from heaven, he sees all the sons of men &c. And in the Lamentations of Jeremiah, Who is he that says, Do not good and evil come out of the mouth of the Lord? And in the Gospel it is said, One of these sparrows will not fall upon the ground without the will of your Father. Item, All the hairs of your head are numbered. The same thing is advised by this saying, The Lord is the maker of both, that is, Riches and poverty do not happen by chance, and without the counsel of God. The command also concerning the seeking of bodily goods, and the promises testify that bodily goods are also given divinely, God wills these to be sought, so that we may attribute this glory to him, and recognize that he also bestows these bodily goods.

Then it is necessary to know that it is the command of God that each one should obey in his own vocation even in hardships. As Peter says, Humble yourselves under the mighty hand of God, That is, Obey God, even then, when he presses you, whose hand is powerful for both, namely for pressing and for liberating.

This now pertains to the revealed will of God, to know that it is the command of God that each one should obey in his own place. And concerning the poor it is said by name, Blessed are the poor in spirit. Item, Roman. 12. Not minding high things, but consenting to the humble. Item, An abomination before the Lord is whatever is high in men, That is, exalting itself by human desire. And Ecclesiasticus 3. Humble yourself in all things, Seek not things higher than you, but always think on those things which God has commanded. I have briefly advised these things concerning this sentiment in the text.

The use of this doctrine, however, extends most widely. For it embraces the doctrine concerning patience in afflictions, concerning obedience in one's vocation, concerning the repressing of ambition &c. But just as it is commanded to the poor man that he should wisely bear his poverty for the sake of God, so also it is commanded to the rich that they should recognize the will of God, know that their riches are gifts of God, and that God wills some to be rich and others poor, and that he wills life to be associated by mutual duties, and does not will the poor to be oppressed by the rich, nor in turn the rich to be affected by injury from the poor, because injuries are universally forbidden.

Latin:

Melius est nomen bonum quam divitiae multae.

HAec doctrina etiam supra tradita est. Bonam famam, rem bonam & appetendam, & virtute tuendam esse, quia Deus hunc ordinem instituit. Primum vult nos habere conscientias integras, & non sauciatas. Postea vult recte facta rectis iudiciis approbari, & turpiter facta culpari, ut in genere humano clare conspiciatur discrimen honestorum & turpium, per quod Deus ipse discernitur a malis naturis. Deinde vult etiam exempli causa nos appetere & tueri bonam famam, ut alii invitentur ad imitationem recte factorum, & ne malis exemplis aliorum petulantia confirmetur, quia vita magna ex parte regitur exemplis. Ideo saepe citatur hoc vetus dictum, Bona conscientia mihi opus est, bona fama proximo, Id est, propter iudicium Dei opus est nobis ipsis bona conscientia. Deinde propter proximos, exempli causa, opus est bona fama.

Gratiam bonam vocat benevolentiam hominum, quae oritur ex bona fama, Id est, ex opinione virtutis, Quia mediocres animos non infectos livore, invitat virtus ad amorem sui. Sicut Pindarus citat Nerei sententiam, Egregia virtus etiam apud hostem laudabilis est.

Dives & pauper, simul versantur, Facit utrunque Deus.

Necessaria sapientia est in Ecclesia Dei, agnoscere regulam vitae esse voluntatem Dei revelatam, & non scrutari non revelatam, Iuxta illud, Lucerna pedibus meis verbum tuum. Ac revelata voluntas expressa & proposita est in Lege & Evangelio, Non revelata est de causis, cur tanta sit dissimilitudo fortunae hominum.

Hic tumultuantur ingenia, Epicureos movet tanta varietas & ἄταξια, ut tollant providentiam. Alii decurrunt ad physicas causas & fata. Alii succensent Deo. Adversus tales furores diligentissime munienda sunt pectora doctrina a Deo tradita, & fide ac invocatione.

Primum igitur confirmanda est fides de providentia, ut in hoc libello multae sententiae inculcantur, hoc praecipue docentes, non casu & sine consilio Dei varietatem fortunae accidere &c. ut supra inquit, Benedictio Domini divites facit. Item, Omnia facit Deus propter seipsum, ad poenam etiam malos ordinat, ut ipsius iudicium agnoscatur. Sed sint in conspectu planissima testimonia. Ut Psal. 32 De caelo respicit Dominus, videt omnes filios hominum &c. Et in Threnis leremiae, Quis est iste qui dicit, Ex ore Domini non egrediuntur bona & mala? Et in Evangelio dicitur, Unus de istis passeribus non cadet super terram, sine voluntate Patris vestri. Item, Omnes capilli capitis vestri numerati sunt. Idem monet & hoc dictum, Factor est utriusque Dominus, id est, Divitiae & paupertas non accidunt casu, & sine consilio Dei. Mandatum etiam de petendis bonis corporalibus, & promissiones testantur corporalia bona etiam divinitus dari, Vult haec peti Deus, ut tribuamus ei hanc gloriam, & agnoscamus, quod etiam haec corporalia bona tribuat.

Deinde scire oportet, mandatum Dei esse, ut in sua quisque vocatione obediat etiam in aerumnis. Ut Petrus inquit, Humiliamini sub potenti manu Dei, Id est, Obedite Deo, tunc quoque, cum vos premit, cuius manus potens est ad utrumque, videlicet ad premendum & ad liberandum.

Hoc iam ad revelatam voluntatem Dei pertinet, scire mandatum Dei esse, ut suo quisque loco obediat. Et de pauperibus nominatim dictum est, Beati pauperes spiritu. Item, Roman. 12. Non sublimia appetentes, sed humilibus contenti. Item, Abominatio coram Domino, quicquid sublime est in hominibus, Id est, efferens se humana cupiditate. Et Ecclesiastici 3. Humilia te in omnibus, Altiora te ne quaeras, sed quae Deus praecepit, cogita semper. Haec breviter monui de hac sententia in textu.

Usus autem huius doctrinae latissime patet. Complectitur enim doctrinam de patientia in afflictionib. de obedientia in vocatione, de reprimenda ambitione &c. Sicut autem pauperi praecipitur, ut sapienter ferat suam paupertatem propter Deum, sic & divitibus praecipitur, ut agnoscant voluntatem Dei, sciant suas divitias Dei dona esse, & velle Deum alios esse divites, & alios pauperes, & velle consociatam esse vitam mutuis officiis, non velle opprimi pauperes a divitibus, nec vicissim divites a pauperibus affici iniuria, quia universaliter iniuriae prohibitae sunt.

## 22:28
Do not remove the ancient landmarks, which your fathers have set.

The image is taken from boundaries, lest the boundaries be removed. It is established, however, by that the digging up of boundaries and the disturbance of borders is a great crime. But the saying is applied to the preservation of old laws, about which many political precepts have been written, which command that changes to laws be prohibited with the greatest care, unless evident truth and utility persuade the change. And political men also command that some small inconveniences be forgiven for the sake of custom, lest, with the Republic moved like a sick body, greater evils occur. As Xenophon says, πασαι μεταβολαὶ πολιτεῶν θανατηφοροι. Therefore that is also said, A well-established evil is not to be moved. And it is true that it is a mark of great wisdom and virtue not to oppose rashly laws approved by great authority. As Plato says, this honor is to be preferred to all triumphs and Olympic victories, to obey the laws diligently.

But here it is also necessary to add a correction. First, concerning the doctrine of the Church , it is necessary to say, If anyone teaches another Gospel, let him be anathema. Likewise, Flee idols, etc. Therefore, when laws or custom sanction the worship of idols, it is necessary to contradict them, and the glory of God is to be preferred to life, peace, and all bodily things.

But the true and non-sophistical answer is that, in the necessary amendment of doctrine and worship, the boundaries of the fathers are not removed by those who recall men to true doctrine, because this true doctrine, delivered by God, is the first, and these very boundaries were later moved from their place by the deceptions of the devil and human furies, and idols were proposed in place of true worship. And it was rightly said by Tertullian, if it is understood correctly, That which is first is most correct. But true antiquity must be diligently inquired into. And it is a great confirmation of faith, having known the series of times of the Church, to choose the testimonies of those who retained the sources. This consideration of antiquity and comparison of doctrines is necessary for students.

Latin:

Non transferas terminos antiquos, quos fecerunt patres tui.

Imago sumpta est a limitibus, ne transferantur limites. Constat autem per sele, limitum effossionem & conturbationem finium magnum scelus esse. Sed accommodatur dictum ad legum veterum conservationem, de qua multa praecepta politica scripta sunt, quae iubent summa cura prohiberi legum mutationes, nisi evidens veritas & utilitas mutationem suadeat. Ac iubent etiam viri politici aliqua parva incommoda condonare consuetudini, ne mota Repub. velut aegro corpore, maiora mala accidant. Ex Xenophon inquit, πασαι μεταβολαὶ πολιτεῶν θανατηφοροι. Ideo & illud dicitur, Malum bene conditum non est movendum. Et verum est, magnam sapientiam & virtutem esse, non temere adversari legibus, autoritate magna comprobatis. Sicut Plato inquit, Omnibus triumphis & Olympicis victoriis hoc decus anteferendum esse, diligenter parere legibus.

Sed hic necesse est etiam addi correctionem. Primum de doctrina Ecclesiae dictum necesse est, Si quis aliud Evangelium docet, anathema sit. Item, Fugite idola &c. Quare cum leges aut consuetudo sanciunt idolorum cultum, necesse est contradici, ac vitae, paci, & omnibus rebus corporalibus antferenda est gloria Dei.

Vera autem & non sophistica responsio est, in emendatione necessariae doctrinae & cultuum, Limites patrum non transferri per eos, qui revocant homines ad veram doctrinam, quia haec vera doctrina divinitus tradita, prima est, & hi ipsi limites postea praestigiis diaboli & furoribus humanis moti sunt suo loco, & pro veris cultibus idola proposita sunt. Et recte dictum est a Tertulliano, si dextre intelligatur, Primum quodque rectissimum est. Sed diligenter inquirenda est vera antiquitas. Et magna est confirmatio fidei, cognita serie temporum Ecclesiae, eligere testimonia eorum, qui fontes retinuerunt. Haec consideratio antiquitatis, & collatio doctrinarum studiosis necessaria est.

## 23:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXIII.

WHEN you sit to eat with a powerful man, be cautious, and put a knife to your throat, that you may save your soul.

It is a general admonition that familiarities with the powerful are dangerous, because since they wish to excel and to be worshipped, and no one is so circumspect and cautious that he does not sometimes say or do something against their will, they are often offended, and being angry, they loosen the reins to the desire for vengeance, as Alexander killed Clitus for contradicting him too freely.

Let the saying therefore be referred to avoiding the familiarities of the powerful, about which matter many admonitions exist, such as,

Live for yourself, and as much as you can, avoid the illustrious; the cruel lightning comes from the illustrious citadel. Likewise, The cultivation of a powerful friend is sweet to the inexperienced; the experienced fears it.

And Aesop said to Solon, who was going to Croesus, that one must speak with a king ἠ ὡς ἵκ---ἠ ὡς ἥδιστα. Either as little as possible, or as sweetly as possible. Likewise, Let him who wishes to be pious leave the court.

It is, however, customary to seek out courts and the familiarities of the powerful, because the nature of seeds is ambitious, and like fire tending upwards. But let good minds consider the magnitude of the dangers, and let them curb ambition, and not seek out courts; when it is necessary, let them obey their calling, and bring modesty, so that they may contain themselves within the bounds of their calling, patience and prudence, lest they invite unnecessary dangers. And in the text follows an admonition. Lest we be captured by the splendor or the allurements . And he calls these, the bread of falsehood, That is, painted and fleeting goods, neither is courtly friendship, nor stable power.

All things are turned, certainly loves are turned.

Do not weary yourself to become rich, cease from your own prudence.

This sentence does not prohibit labor and sedulity, according to one's calling, nor diligence in one's calling. For above he commands several times concerning diligence, and proposes the example of the ant. He adds, however, that these labors will be so happy, when God blesses. For he wishes these causes to be joined, the invocation of God, and our own sedulity. Here, however, he prohibits the desire that carries itself outside of one's calling, tormenting the mind, which then attempts to increase power into wealth by many evil counsels. Thus also Paul commands, that we do not obey prudence for the sake of desire. He affirms, however, that these evil counsels are vain, when he says, They make for themselves wings like eagles, and those riches will fly away. To this add the customary sentences, κακηβούλη τῷ βουλεύσαντι κακίστη.

This precept therefore recalls us to guarding the bounds of our calling, and to seeking the help of God, and to the diligence and prudence, which are divinely commanded

Do not eat bread with an envious man, nor desire his foods, because in his soul he is like a specter, and he says, Eat and drink, and yet his heart is not with you.

Above he said that the familiarities of the powerful or courtly ones are dangerous. Then he warned, lest captured by allurements and splendor we seek power and riches above our calling. Now he warns that envious men must be avoided. And this is the simplest narration of the sentence. As in giving a benefit a good will is most pleasing, so a benefit becomes grave, if good will is absent. This common sense judges, and the cause is manifest in philosophy, because the will is the principal cause of virtues and vices. Therefore, for benefits the Greek saying is most customary, χαρίτων δὲ τε θυμὸς ἄριστος, That is, in benefits is the best. Indeed, even the divine voice says, God loves a cheerful giver . And Christ values the small gift of the widow more than the greater gifts of others. And among Seneca is read the saying of Fabius who says, A benefit given proudly by a man is like stony bread. Solomon therefore warns both, First, that in giving benefits our will should be beneficent, eager to help, favoring eternal salvation, and on the contrary, that we should avoid those whose will is not beneficent, even if by the ostentation of beneficence, just as by the simulation of other virtues, they seek praise. Nor yet is simulation long-lasting, therefore here Solomon says, The morsel which you eat you will vomit up, and you will lose your good words.

Add, however, to envious men, the suspicious and malevolent. For also familiarities are dangerous and brief. And concerning the malevolent Phocylides says, Μὴ καιόμεν ἔρξης, πείρειν ἴσον ἐς ἐνι πόντῳ.

Do not do good to an evil man, because it is the same as if you were casting seeds into the water.

But the description in Solomon most especially fits the suspicious. Because in the soul of the envious or suspicious dwells a Schaar, that is, a Satyr or a specter. As a specter repeatedly puts on one form and another, so the suspicious person perpetually and fashions other suspicions by false reasonings.

But the suspicious sin against these precepts, Do not be a whisperer, nor a calumniator among the people. Item, Do not judge, that you may not be judged. And suspicion is opposed to and candor. And the habit of suspecting often distracts teachers and governors without a true cause, and afterwards from those distractions public dissensions and wars of dogmas arise.

But someone might ask, Is nothing to be judged by suspicions? I answer, A mind loving the truth does not assent to ambiguous conjectures, do not conclude unless moved by firm arguments. And although there are often probable conjectures in suspicion, and to guard against snares with moderate diligence is prudence worthy of , nevertheless it is candor not to harm another on account of suspicions, nor to condemn anyone, until certain arguments convince you. Thus Claudian praises the Emperor Theodosius, because he was not suspicious.

Latin:

CAPUT XXIII.

CUM sedes ut comedas cum potente, sis cautus, & ponas cultrum in gutture tuo, ut serves animam tuam.

Generalis admonitio est, periculosas esse familiaritates cum potntibus, quia cum velint excellere, & coli, nec quisquam adeo circumspectus & cautus sit, ut non interdum aliquid dicat aut faciat contra eorum voluntatem, saepe offenduntur, & irati laxant frenos cupiditati vindictae, ut Alexander Clitum liberius contradicentem interiecit.

Referatur ergo dictum ad vitandas familiaritates potentum, de qua re multae extant admonitiones, ut,

Vive tibi, quantumque potes, praelustria vita,Saevum praelustri fulmen ab arce venit.Item,Dulcis inexpertis cultura potentis amici,Expertus metuit.

Et Aesopus Soloni eunti ad Croesum dixit, cum rege loquendum ἠ ὡς ἵκ---ἠ ὡς ἥδιστα. Aut quam minimum, aut quam dulcissime. Item, Exeat aula qui vult esse pius.

Usitatum est autem appetere aulas & potentum familiaritates, quia natura seminum ambitiosa est, & velut ignea sursum tendens. Sed bonae mentes periculorum magnitudinem considerent, & frenent ambitionem, nec appetant aulas; cum necesse est vocationi obtemperent, & afferant modestiam, ut se intra vocationis metas contineant, pacientiam & prudenciam, ne accersant non necessaria pericula. Et in textu sequitur admonitio. Ne splendore vel illecebris capiamur. Et vocat haec, panem mendacii, Id est, fucosa & fugacia bona, nec amicitiae aulicae, nec potencia stabilis est.

Omnia vertuntur, certe vertuntur amores.

Non angaris animo ut fias dives, Prudentiam tuam omittito.

HAec sententia non prohibet laborem & sedulitatem, iuxta vocationem, neque diligentiam in vocatione. Supra enim aliquoties praecipit de diligentia, & ---micae exemplum proponit. Addit autem hos labores ita felices fore, cum Deus benedicit. Vult enim coniungi has causas, invocationem Dei, & nostram sedulitatem. Hic autem prohibet cupiditatem efferentem se extra vocationem, excruciantem animum, quae deinde multis malis consiliis augere potentiam in opes conatur. Ita & Paulus praecipit, ne prudentiae ad cupiditatem obtemperemus. Affirmat autem haec mala consilia irrita esse, cum inquit, Faciunt sibi pennas velut aquilae, & avolabunt divitiae illae. Huc adiungito usitatas sententias, κακηβούλη τῷ βουλεύσαντι κακίστη.

Revocat igitur nos hoc praeceptum ad metas vocationis tuendas, & ad petendum auxilium Dei, & ad diligentiam & prudentiam, quae divinitus praecepta

Ne cum invido comedas panem, neque concupiscas cibos eius, quia in anima eius tanquam spectrum est, ac dicit, Comede & bibe, & tamen cor non tecum est.

Supra dixit periculosas esse familiaritates potentum seu aulicas. Deinde monuit, ne capti illecebris & splendore appetamus potentiam & opes supra vocationem. Nunc monet fugiendos esse invidos. Et haec simplicissima enarratio est sententiae. Ut in dando beneficio gratissima est bona voluntas, sic beneficium fit ----ave, si absit bona voluntas. Haec communis sensus iudicat, & causa manifesta ----in philosophia, quia voluntas est principalis causa virtutum & viciorum. Ideo beneficiis usitatissimum est graecum dictum, χαρίτων δὲ τε θυμὸς ἄριστος, Id est, ----mus in beneficiis est optimus. Imo & vox divina inquit, Hilarem datorem di---. Deus. Et Christus pluris facit exiguum donum viduae, quam maiora dona aliorum. Et apud Senecam legitur dictum Fabii qui ait, Beneficium ab homine du--superbe datum simile esse pani lapidoso. Monet igitur Salomon utrumque, Primum, ut in dandis beneficiis nostra voluntas sit benefica, cupida iuvandi, faveris aternae saluti, & econtra, ut vitemus eos, quorum voluntas non est benefica, etiam si ostentatione beneficentiae, sicut simulatione aliarum virtutum, captant laudem. Nec tamen diuturna est simulatio, ideo hic dicit Salomon, Buccellam quam comedis evomes, & perdes tuos bonos sermones.

Adiungito autem ad invidos homines, suspicaces & malevolos. Nam & ho----n familiaritates periculosae & breves sunt. Et de malevolis Phocylides inquit, Μὴ καιόμεν ἔρξης, πείρειν ἴσον ἐς ἐνι πόντῳ.

Non benefacias malo, quia simile est, ac si semina abiiceres in aquam.

Maxime autem convenit descriptio in Salomone ad suspicaces. Quia in----, in anima invidi vel suspicacis habitare Schaar, id est, Satyrum vel spectrum. Venim spectrum subinde aliam & aliam formam induit, ita suspicax perpetuo atque alias suspiciones falsis ratio cinationibus fingit.

Peccant autem suspicaces contra haec praecepta, Non sis susurro, nec calum--tor in populo. Item, Nolite iudicare, ne iudicemini. Et opponitur suspitio ti & candori. Et consuetudo suspicandi saepe distrahit doctores & gubernatores sine vera causa, & postea ex distractionibus illis publica dissidia dogmatum bella oriuntur.

Quaerat autem aliquis, Nihil ne suspicionibus iudicandum est? Respondeo, Mens amans veritatis non assentitur ambiguis coniecturis, non statuas nisi motus firmis argumentis. Et quanquam saepe probabiles coniectae sunt in suspitione, & cavere insidias mediocri diligentia, prudentia est digna--de, Tamen candor est non propter suspiciones alteri nocere, nec condemnare neminem, donec certa argumenta te convincant. Sic Claudianus laudat Theodusium Imperatorem, quod non fuerit suspicax.

## 23:9
Do not speak in the ears of a fool.

He speaks especially of those teaching, who hand down doctrine piously and faithfully ἀργαλέον φονέοντα ἀφοσι πολλ' ἄγορεύειν. with a docile listener, and avoid contentions with the proud, the obstinate, those bewitched by the of their own wisdom, who neither seek nor love the truth. As Christ says, Do not give what is holy to dogs. And the Greek verse says,

But here it is asked, Is Confession not necessary? I answer briefly, let teachers teach rightly in their own place, and tax impious doctrine, whatever the listeners ought to be, and yet there are always some in the Church who are docile. If they are blasphemed and killed, it is necessary to bear injuries. Other men also interrogated let each one in his place issue a plain and true confession. But without a calling or interrogated, let them not thrust themselves in, where it is manifest that nothing is gained.

Latin:

Auribus stulti ne loquaris.

Praecipue de docentibus loquitur, qui doctrinam pie & fideliter tradant torio docili, & vitent certamina cum superbis, pertinacibus, fascinatis ad--tione propriae sapientiae, qui neque quaerunt neque amant veritatem. Sicut Christus inquit, Ne des sanctum canibus. Et graecus versus inquit, ἀργαλέον φονέοντα ἀφοσι πολλ' ἄγορεύειν.

Sed hic quaeritur, An non necessaria sit Confessio? Breviter respondeo, doctores suo loco recte doceant, & taxent impiam doctrinam, qualescunque debent auditores, & tamen semper in Ecclesia aliqui sunt dociles. Si a blasphem--interficiuntur, necesse est ferre iniurias. Caeteri etiam homines interroga--quisque loco confessionem planam & veram edant. Sed sine vocatione aut interrogati, non ingerant se, ubi manifestum est nihil profici.

## 24:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXIV.

Latin:

CAPUT XXIIII.

## 24:11
Deliver them who are drawn to death, and those who are driven to slaughter, etc.

This sentence pertains to the precept, You shall not kill, for it is necessary to separate that, You shall not communicate in the sins of others, and it begins that unjust cruelty is neither to be helped, nor to be confirmed. Nay, that one must oppose unjust by provocation. As when Jeremiah is condemned, the power ought to deliver him. Private individuals ought to testify by their own confession, when some even do oppress unjustly. Jer. chap. 26. And the Eunuch draws him out of the prison, etc.

Jonathan opposes his father Saul, and not only by testimony, but also , without sedition he defends David.

And Obadiah secretly fed the Prophets driven out by Jezebel. Nay, and the Egyptian midwives save the Israelite infants, etc.

These examples show how, even against the commands of superiors, others ought to act for the innocent, both by confession and by other duties, not seditiously. And the examples themselves contain divine approvals, and narrate great rewards divinely given for these duties.

On the contrary, flatterers sin, like Doeg, who manifestly approve of unjust cruelty against their conscience. And some wicked men invent sophistical pretexts by which they excuse themselves and Tyrants.

Afterwards, those also sin who by their own silence confirm the furies of tyrants, especially when, according to their calling, they ought to show what they think, so that when pious men are subjected to punishments on account of the profession of true doctrine, a preacher understanding the truth ought certainly to disapprove of such cruelty.

Latin:

Libera eos, qui trahuntur ad mortem, & qui impelluntur ad necem &c.

HAec sententia pertinet ad praeceptum, Non occidas, seiungi enim oportet illud, Non communices peccatis alienis incipit autem iniustam saeviciam, neque iuvandam, neque confirmandam esse. Imo provocatione adversandum esse iniustae fae--Ut cum damnatur Hieremias, potestas liberare eum debet Privati sua confessione testari debent, cum iniuste opprimere etiam aliqui faciunt. Hiere. cap. 26. Et Eunuchus extrahit eum ex carcere, etc.

Ionathas adversatur patri Sauli, & non solum testimonio, sed etiam ciis, sine seditione defendit Davidem.

Et Abdias clam pavit Prophetas pulsos a Iesabel.Imo & obstetrices Aegyptiae servant infantes Israeliticos &c.

Haec exempla ostendunt, quomodo, etiam contra mandata superiorum gere innocentes alii debeant, & confessione, & aliis officiis, non seditiose. Et ipsa exempla continent adprobationes divinas, & narrant magna praemia sim divinitus pro his officiis dari.

Econtra peccant adulatores, ut Doeng, qui iniustam saeviciam manifeste probant contra conscientiam. Et aliqui scelerati fingunt praetextus sophistici quibus se & Tyrannos excusant.

Postea peccant etiam, qui suo silentio confirmant furores Tyrannorum, praesertim, cum iuxta vocationem ostendere debeant, quid sentiant, ut cum suppliciis afficiuntur homines pii, propter verae doctrinae professionem, concionator intelligens veritatem, certe talem saevitiam improbare debebat.

## 24:16
Do not lie in wait wickedly for the dwelling of the just. For the just man shall fall seven times, and shall rise again.

He repeats the commandment, Do not kill, with the addition of a refutation and a vituperation of sarcasm. For because the just are often exercised by great calamities, on account of this very deformity the unjust become more spirited, and furthermore insult the miserable. Therefore, this sentence teaches that one must judge about men from the very truth of the cause, not from favorable or adverse circumstances. This whole discussion about the scandal of the cross has been often repeated in the doctrine of the Church, and has been copiously treated in Job, who says, Even if he kills me, I will hope in him. Nevertheless, I will argue my ways before him, and he himself will be my Savior, nor will all hypocrites enter before him. Indeed, the Poet also says,

The outcome proves the deeds; I pray that he lacks success, whoever thinks that deeds are to be judged by the event.

And yet the voice of the law is true, It will be well for the just, and ill for the unjust, namely according to the interpretation of the Gospel, which teaches that the Church in this life is subjected to the cross for many reasons, but is afterwards liberated. And the impious, although they have flourished for a long time, are nevertheless afterwards cast into eternal punishments.

Latin:

Non insidieris impie habitaculo iusti. Septies enim cadet iustus, & resurget.

Repetit praeceptum, Non occidas, adiecta refutatione & vituperatione sarcasmi. Quia enim saepe magnis calamitatibus exercentur iusti, propter hanc ipsam deformitatem fiunt animosiores iniusti, & insuper miseris insultant. Docet igitur haec sententia de hominibus iudicandum esse ex ipsa veritate causae, non ex secundis aut adversis rebus. Haec tota disputatio de scandalo crucis saepe repetita est in doctrina Ecclesiae, & in Iob copiose tractata est, qui inquit, Etiamsi occidat me, sperabo in eum. Veruntamen vias meas coram eo arguam, & ipse erit Salvator meus, nec intrabunt coram eo omnes hypocritae. Imo & Poëta inquit,

Exitus acta probat, careat successibus opto,Quisquis ab eventu facta notanda putat.

Et tamen vera est legis vox, lustis bene erit, iniustis male, scilicet iuxta Evangelii interpretationem, quod docet Ecclesiam in hac vita subiectam esse cruci, propter multas causas, postea vero liberari. Et impios, quanquam diu floruerunt, tamen abiici postea in aeterna supplicia.

## 24:16
The just man shall fall seven times.

Which is understood especially about calamities or about the evil of punishment, although sometimes falls and some evils of fault also concur, as in David. But here he especially vituperates sarcasm and ἐπιχεραικακίαν because God commands mercy, and does not want the miserable to be proudly mocked, not even in the just punishment of another.

Latin:

Septies cadet iustus.
