Quod praecipue intelligatur de calamitatibus seu de malo poenae, Quanquam etiam interdum lapsus & aliqua mala culpae concurrunt, ut in Davide. Maxime autem vituperat hic Sarcasmum & ἐπιχεραικακίαν quia Deus praecipit misericordiam, & ne quidem in poena iusta alterius superbe illudi vult miseris.

## 24:21-22
Fear God, my son, and the King, and do not mix with the seditious, because their destruction will come suddenly, and who knows the ruin of the seditious?

This sentence also is to be observed among the principal ones of this book, which commands obedience, and indeed orders it.

First, he commands to fear God, then the King, because all legitimate obedience, which is rendered to authority, ought to proceed from this source, so that it may be rendered on account of God, who established this order. And so that the obedience of the heart may not be feigned, he used the significant word, fear, by which is signified true reverence of the heart, and fear of the divine judgment, as it is commanded in Rom. 13., that honor and fear and tribute be rendered.

Secondly, he warns to prefer God, when the commands dissent, just as the rule of the Apostles also teaches, It is necessary to obey God rather than men. This doctrine is copiously handed down elsewhere, when it is spoken about magistrates.

But he clearly warns that the seditious are to be avoided, where it is necessary to judge in dissensions, who are the seditious. And in political affairs the judgment is easier, because the ordinary authority stands out, to which one must be obedient in legitimate matters, and indeed also in doubtful civil affairs, and many hard burdens must be borne. But when the injury is notorious, subjects have an excuse when they do not obey. There, however, when or to what extent defense may be lawful, the judgment is difficult, as Cassius and Brutus are not in a case of defense against Julius. In the Church, however, the rules are to be held universally , He who hears you, hears me. Likewise, If anyone teaches another Gospel, let him be anathema.

The second rule commands that the uncorrupted doctrine of the Gospel of those teaching be heard, as the voice of God sounding from heaven. Since therefore pastors or Bishops teach rightly, obedience is necessary, because all rational creatures owe obedience to the Gospel.

But when false dogmas and corruptions of the Gospel are taught, the teachers and defenders of false dogmas are by no means to be heard, which is said in the most atrocious words in the other rule, If anyone teaches another Gospel, let him be anathema.

They are therefore seditious, who unjustly refuse to obey their authority, and they sin more, when they even take up unjust arms against authority, as Absalom did, etc. And concerning punishments, clear sayings exist, He who takes the sword, shall perish by the sword. Item, He who resists authority, resists God, and brings punishment upon himself. Let us also add the sentiment of Nestor concerning punishments,

He is clanless, lawless, and hearthless who loves the horror of civil war.

And here there is a clear threat concerning the punishment of the seditious in Solomon, when he says that they will suddenly give punishment. Just as the histories of all times show, of Absalom, Cassius, Brutus, and the like.

Latin:

Time Deum, fili mi, & Regem, & cum sediciosis ne miscea-ris, quia subito veniet exitium eorum, & ruinam sedicioso-rum quis novit?

Est & haec sententia inter praecipuas huius libri observanda, quae praecipit obedientiam, & quidem ordinat eam.

Primum iubet Deo oberire, deinde Regi, quia omnis legitima obedientia, quae praestatur potestati, ab hoc fonte proficisci debet, ut propter Deum praestetur, qui sanxit hunc ordinem. Et ut sit obedientia cordis non fucosa, usus est verbo insigni, timoris, quo significatur vera cordis reverentia, & metus iudicii divini, ut Rom. 13. praecipitur, ut honos & timor & tributum praestentur.

Secundo monet Deum anteferre, cum dissentiunt mandata, sicut & regula Apostolorum docet, Oportet Deo magis obedire quam hominibus. Haec doctrina copiose alibi traditur, cum de magistratibus dicitur.

Diserte autem monet vitari sediciosos, ubi necesse est diiudicari in dissensionibus, qui sint sediciosi. Et in politicis negociis facilior est diiudicatio, quia eminet ordinaria potestas, cui in rebus legitimis, imo & in dubiis civilibus negociis obediendum est, & multa dura onera ferenda sunt. Sed quando iniuria est notoria, habent subditi excusationem cum non obediunt. Ibi autem, quando aut quatenus defensio licita sit, difficilis diiudicatio est, ut Cassius & Brutus non sunt in casu defensionis adversus Iulium. In Ecclesia vero universaliter regulae tenendae sunt, Qui vos audit, me audit. Item, Si quis aliud Evangelium docet, anathema sit.

Altera praecipit audiri vocem docentium incorruptam Evangelii doctrinam, tanquam vocem Dei de caelo sonatem. Cum igitur pastores aut Episcopi recte docent, necessaria est obedientia, quia omnes creaturae rationales Evangelio obedientiam debent.

Cum autem docentur falsa dogmata, & corruptelae Evangelii, nequaquam audiendi sunt doctores & defensors falsorum dogmatum, quod atrocissimis verbis dicitur in altera regula, Si quis aliud Evangelium docet, anathema sit.

Sunt igitur sediciosi, qui iniuste recusant obedire suae potestati, & plus peccant, cum etiam arma iniusta capiunt contra potestatem, ut fecit Absalon &c. Et de poenis extant clara dicta, Qui gladium accipiet, gladio peribit. Item, Qui potestati resistit, Deo resistit, & poenam sibi accersit. Addamus & Nestoris sententiam de poenis,

ἄφρητωρ ἄθεμιστος ἀνἐστιος ἐστὶν ἐκεῖνοςὅς πολεμου ἔραται ἐπιδιμου, ὀκρυόεντος.

Et hic de poena sediciosorum perspicua comminatio est in Salomone, cum dicit subito daturos esse poenam. Sicut historiae omnium temporum ostendunt, Absalonis, Cassii, Bruti, & similium.

## 24:26
A kiss is a right answer.

That is, to say, to warn, to persuade, to teach what is apt and true and salutary, is like a sweetest kiss, as it is said elsewhere, A word spoken in due time is like apples of gold in a setting of silver.

Latin:

Osculum recta responsio.

ID est, apta & vera & salutaria dicere, monere, suadere, docere, simile est dulcissimo osculo, sicut alibi dicitur, Poma aurea in lance argentea opportunus sermo.

## 24:27
First finish the work outside, etc.

It is an old reproach that it is customary that men do more πάρεργα ἔργα. And Seneca says, A great part of life slips away doing evil, a greater part doing nothing, the greatest part doing the things of others.

Latin:

Primum perfice opus foris &c.

Vetus reprehensio est, usitatum esse, quod homines magis faciant πάρεργαἔργα. Et Seneca dicit, Magna pars vitae elabitur mala agendo, maior nihil agendo, maxima aliena agendo.

## 25:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXV.

Latin:

CAPUT XXV.

## 25:2
It is the glory of God to conceal a word, But it is the glory of kings to search it out.

That is, the word of God is so proposed that fools not applying their mind do not immediately understand, but it is wisdom to search it out. He calls kings wise.

Latin:

Gloria Dei est verbum celare, At gloria regum est id inquirere.

ID est, verbum Dei ita propositum est, ut stulti non applicantes mentem, non statim intelligant, sed spaientia est id inquirere. Reges vocat sapientes.

## 25:20
Vinegar upon nitre, is he who sings songs to a wicked heart.

Above he warned several times that listeners must be distinguished, the weak and teachable from the wicked and stubborn and enemies of the truth, as Nicodemus was weak, Caiaphas was stubborn and an enemy of the truth. The weak are to be taught lovingly, just as Paul also says, Receive the weak in faith. And Solomon says, that admonition is profitable among those who listen. A wise rebuke is a gem among those who listen.

On the contrary, concerning the stubborn it is said, Do not give what is holy to dogs. And it was said above , Do not speak to the ears of a fool. This distinction must be greatly considered, so that we do not dispute before just anyone, and so that the wicked, when irritated, do not become more rabid. Concerning such he says here, who, irritated by good doctrine and good admonitions, are neither appeased nor healed, but are rather alienated and inflamed. Concerning whom it is commonly said, that hornets are not to be imitated. That lions are not to be goaded. Item, κακὸν ἔν κεἰμενον μὴ κινη ἐη.

But Solomon used most elegant figures, when for admonition or deprecation he says a song, and proposes the image of vinegar and nitre. The biting quality of nitre is either irritated or aided by vinegar and lime. As Pliny says, For phagedaenas and ulcers that spread, apply nitre with lime and vinegar. Thus the hatred in Saul against David becomes only more savage by the entreaties and admonitions of Jonathan. He warns, therefore, by this saying that disputes and admonitions are to be omitted in the presence of the obstinate, who, when irritated, become more rabid.

But someone might ask, is confession prohibited by this saying, or is silence indicated to the pastor? I answer, let the pastor say what is necessary in his own place, because he always has a teachable part of the audience. And yet, prudence must be applied in handling certain wounds. Just as Paul wishes, that the weak not be disturbed untimely. And these sayings are true,

He who warns untimely, he does harm. Likewise, We see that some things are made worse by curing Wounds, which it would have been better not to touch. Confession, however, is necessary when we are seriously questioned.

If he who hates you is hungry, feed him with bread &c.

Previously he said that the incurable and implacable are not to be irritated by admonitions. Now he adds a neighboring sentiment, which warns that we should not, however, omit the duties by which even the hardest are sometimes appeased, just as Homer says, ἀκεσται δὲ φρένες ἐσθλῶν. He commands, therefore, that we lay aside hatreds, and seek opportunities for healing offenses. And Solomon adds a promise.

Latin:

Acetum super nitrum, canens carmen pravo cordi.

Supra aliquoties monuit discernendos esse auditores, infirmos ac dociles a pravis ac pertinacibus et hostibus veritatis, ut Nicodemus infirmus erat, Caiphas erat pertinax & hostis veritatis. Infirmi amanter docendi sunt, sicut & Paulus inquit, Infirmum in fide assumite. Et Salomon inquit, prodesse admonitionem apud eos, qui auscultant. Gemma est sapiens obiurgatio apud auscultantem.

Econtra de pertinacibus dictum est, Ne date sanctum canibus. Et supra dictum est, Auribus stulti ne loquaris. Hoc discrimen valde considerandum est, ne apud quoslibet disputemus, & ne mali irritati fiant rabiosiores. De talibus hic dicit, qui irritati bona doctrina, & bonis admonitionibus, neque placantur, neque sanantur, sed magis alienantur, atque incenduntur. De quibus vulgo dicitur, Non imitandos esse crabrones. Non stimulandos esse leones. Item, κακὸν ἔν κεἰμενον μὴ κινη ἐη.

Sed Salomon elegantissimis figuris usus est, cum pro admonitione vel deprecatione dicit cantilenam, & imaginem aceti & nitri proponit. Mordacitas nitri vel irritatur, vel adiuvatur aceto & calce. Ut Plinius inquit, Phagedaenis & ulceribus, quae serpunt illini nitrum cum calce & aceto. Sic odium in Saule adversus Davidem, tantum fit saevius deprecationibus & admonitionibus Ionathae. Monet igitur hoc dicto omittendas esse disputationes et admonitiones, apud pertinaces, qui irritati fiunt rabiosiores.

Sed quaerat aliquis, an hoc dicto prohibeatur confessio, aut pastori indicatur silentium? Respondeo, Pastor suo loco dicat necessaria, quia semper habet auditorii partem docilem. Et tamen prudentia adhibenda est in contrectandis quibusdam vulneribus. Sicut vult Paulus, Non intempestive turbari infirmos. Et vera sunt haec dicta,

Intempestive qui monet, ille nocet.Item, Curando fieri quaedam peiora videmusVulnera, quae melius non tetigisse fuit.Confessio vero tunc necessaria est, cum serio interrogamur.

Si esurierit is qui odit te, pasce eum pane &c.

Antea dixit, insanabiles & implacabiles non irritandos esse admonitionibus. Nunc addit vicinam sententiam, quae monet, ne tamen nos officia omittamus, quibus non durissimi aliquando placantur, sicut Homerus dicit, ἀκεσται δὲ φρένες ἐσθλῶν. Praecipit igitur, ut odia deponamus, & quaeramus occasiones sanandi offensiones. Et addit Salomon promissionem.

## 25:22
The Lord will reward you.

As if he were saying, even if you do not bend the enemy, yet God will in turn do good to you for such benefits. According to that, Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the earth. It is said figuratively, You will heap coals upon his head. That is, when he sees that you do not contend with hatred and pride, he will be moved by shame, so that he himself might also remit something of his hatred and pride, if he is not of the worst nature, as Theognis says,

Ανδράσι τοῖς ἀγαθοῖς ἑπεται γνώμητε καὶ ἀιδὼς.

Latin:

Dominus reddet tibi.

Quasi dicat, etiamsi non flectis inimicum, tamen Deus vicissim tibi benefaciet pro talibus beneficiis. Iuxta illud, Beati mites, quia possidebunt terram. Figurate dictum est, Carbones congregabis super caput eius. Id est, Cum videbit te non certare odio & superbia, pudore movebitur, ut & ipse aliquid de odio & superbia remittat, si non est pessima natura, ut ait Theognis,

Ανδράσι τοῖς ἀγαθοῖς ἑπεται γνώμητε καὶ ἀιδὼς.

## 25:26
A troubled fountain, and a muddy spring, is a just man falling before the wicked.

The doctrine concerning the scandal of the cross is often repeated in the Church, as in Psalm 36. Do not envy, is recited at length.

Human wisdom does not sufficiently explain the causes why God allows the just to be oppressed by the unjust, and by foul monsters, as when Paul is killed by Nero. But God has revealed in the Church the causes why He wishes it to be subject to the cross, and at the same time He proposes consolation. It is necessary that this doctrine be most known to all. But human judgments proudly neglect the wisdom revealed by God, and from that confusion, when they see the good oppressed by the bad, they argue that all things are carried by chance, and that there is no providence.

Against such judgments it is necessary to fortify minds with firm faith. Therefore, in this little book, sentiments concerning providence have often been recited, and God shows examples in this life. For He keeps the rule, Atrocious crimes are punished by atrocious punishments in this life, even if sometimes the good are also afflicted, the other cause of which matter is known to the Church.

Heavenly doctrine, however, consoles the pious, so that they do not fail from God on account of calamities, even if they see the impious flourish for a time, and so that they are not broken in spirit on account of the sarcasms and triumphs of the impious. Such an admonition is also proposed in this saying, so that the pious are not broken in spirit on account of the ἐπιχαιρε κακίαν. of the impious. The well-known verses pertain here,

The outcome proves the deeds, I pray that he may lack success, Whoever thinks that deeds are to be judged by the event.

Although the final event will at last correspond to the deeds in the final judgment, yet in this life let us hold this rule, that all actions are to be judged principally according to the word of God. Afterwards, let it be considered which events are regular, which are irregular.

Latin:

Fons conturbatus, & scaturigo lutulenta, iustus cadens co-ram impio.

SAepe repetitur in Ecclesia doctrina de scandalo crucis, ut in Psalo 36. Noli aemulari, prolixe recitatur.

Humana sapientia non satis explicat causas, cur Deus sinat opprimi iustos ab iniustis, & tetris portentis, ut cum Paulus a Nerone interficitur. Sed Deus in Ecclesia patefecit causas, quare velit subiectam esse cruci, et simul consolationem proponit. Hanc doctrinam notissimam esse omnibus oportet. Sed humana iudicia sapientiam a Deo patefactam superbe negligunt, & ex illa confusione, cum vident bonos a malis opprimi, argumentantur omnia casu ferri, & nullam esse providentiam.

Adversus talia iudicia necesse est mentes firma fide munire. Ideo saepe in hoc libello recitatae sunt sententiae de providentia, & Deus ostendit exempla in hac vita. Servat enim regulam, Atrocia delicta puniuntur atrocibus poenis in hac vita, etiamsi interdum & boni adfliguntur, cuius rei alia causa est, nota Ecclesiae.

Consolatur autem doctrina caelestis pios, ne propter calamitates deficiant a Deo, etiamsi florere impios aliquandiu videant, Et ne frangantur animis propter Sarcasmos, & triumphos impiorum. Talis admonitio & in hoc dicto proposita est, ne pii frangantur animis, propter impiorum ἐπιχαιρε κακίαν. Pertinent huc noti versiculi,

Exitus acta probat, careat successibus opto,Quisquis ab eventu facta notanda putat.

Etsi autem ultimus eventus in postremo iudicio tandem respondebit factis, tamen in hac vita teneamus hanc regulam, Actiones omnes iudicandas esse principaliter iuxta verbum Dei. Postea consideretur, qui eventus sint regulares, qui irregulares.

## 26:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXVI.

Latin:

CAPUT XXVI.

## 26:1
As snow in summer, and as rain in harvest, so honor is not fitting for a fool.

It pertains to the precepts of modesty, and signifies that men not yet chastened or tamed in prosperous circumstances loosen the reins on all their desires. As Alexander, when he was most modest and mild at the beginning, afterwards wishes to be God, and becomes cruel, etc.

Moreover, this saying pertains to the place of things commonly happening, where this rule is also recited, That in prosperous circumstances men become worse, as, The people sat down to eat and to drink, and rose up to play, Item, Fortune makes him a fool whom she favors too much. Item.

Minds generally grow wanton in prosperous circumstances, Nor is it easy to endure favorable things with an even mind.

As a bird wanders, and a swallow flies away, so a false curse will not alight.

It is a consolation of a good conscience against false reproaches. Item against unjust imprecations, as Psal. 108. Let them curse, and you will bless. It warns, however, when we do right and necessary things with a good conscience, not to do the contrary on account of reproaches or unjust imprecations, and at the same time it consoles us with this admonition, that these darts do not harm, although it cannot be but that they wound the mind, but God afterwards heals such wounds. As David says concerning the reproaches of Shimei, God will give me good for these curses. And it was excellently said by Cyprian, He to whom it is cursed is not miserable, but he who curses. Alexander, It is kingly to hear ill while doing well.

It follows, These things must be borne wisely, and it must be judged where it is profitable for the Republic to answer, where it is not profitable, concerning which distinction he speaks in the following sentence.

Latin:

Sicut nix in aestate, & sicut pluvia in messe, sic non decet stul-tum gloria.

Pertinet ad praecepta modestiae, & significat homines nondum castigatos seu cicuratos in rebus secundis laxare frenos omnibus cupiditatibus. Ut Alexander cum initio modestissimus & mitissimus esset, postea vult esse Deus, & fit crudelis &c.

Pertinet autem hoc dictum ad locum communiter accidentium, ubi et haec regula recitatur, In rebus secundis homines fieri deteriores, ut, Sedit populus manducans & bibens, & surrexit ludere, Item, Fortuna quem nimium fovet, stultum facit. Item.

Luxuriant animi rebus plerunque secundis,Nec facile est aequa commoda mente pati.

Sicut avis fugit, & hirundo avolat, ita maledictum falsum non adhaerescet.

Consolatio est bonae conscientiae, contra falsa convicia. Item contra iniustas imprecationes, ut Psal. 108. Maledicent illi, & tu benedices. Monet autem, cum recta & necessaria, bona conscientia facimus, ne propter convicia aut iniustas imprecationes contraria faciamus, & simul consolatur nos hac admonitione, quod haec tela non noceant, quanquam fieri non potest, quin saucient animum, sed Deus postea sanat talia vulnera. Sicut inquit David de conviciis Semei, Deus dabit mihi bona pro his maledictis. Et praeclare dictum est a Cypriano, Non is, cui maledicitur, miser est, sed qui maledicit. Alexander, Regium est bene agentem male audire.

Sequitur, Sapienter haec ferenda sunt, & iudicandum, ubi prosit Reipublicae respondere, ubi non prosit, de quo discrimine in sequenti sententia dicit.

## 26:4
Do not answer a fool according to his folly, lest you also become like him.

Above he said that some are incurable, of whom he says, Vinegar upon nitre is one who sings songs to a wicked heart. The question is therefore, whether one must be silent altogether before such people? Here two conflicting sentences are recited, which differ for this reason, that they may warn that a choice must be applied, because sometimes one must answer, sometimes one must be silent. Moreover, the judgment is easy for pastors in the public duty of teaching, these always have dissimilar listeners, pious, teachable, and incurable. It is necessary, however, for them to serve the better part, where the very voice of doctrine uncorrupted refutes adversaries and the malevolent, and at the same time the pastor can modestly warn the listeners not to assent to the calumnies of the slanderers. But in other private meetings it is better to omit struggles, according to that, Do not give what is holy to the dogs. And often there are probable causes why one must be silent. As when rabid adversaries are irritated, and seem likely to stir up questions, which would be more likely to wound the minds of the weak, and to cause greater dissipations.

Thus David calls himself a mute dove, Psal. 55. signifying that he had to be silent somewhere. For it is most common for greater contentions to arise from small beginnings. As Homer says about strife,

Small at first through fear, soon it raises itself into the air, And walks on the ground, and hides its head among the clouds.

And Thucydides says, ἐξ ὀλίγου πολλα.

Then therefore, when it is more useful to be silent, let us follow the example proposed in the Psalm, They slandered me, But I was praying. And the consolation must be held. Let them curse, but you will bless.

And David proposes to us a most sweet consolation, saying, The Lord will give me something good for these curses. The thing itself also refutes many things while we are silent. Thus Scipio, relying on the goodness of his deeds, refused to answer the tribunes, saying in the assembly, On this day some years ago I defeated the enemies in a great battle. Let us therefore rather go into the temple, to give thanks to God, than to listen to the reproaches of this man. Sometimes, however, a refutation can be modestly opposed, just as here a distinction is established, that one must not answer a fool, unless you wish to become like him, if you wish to contend with reproaches, to indulge in hatred and anger, etc. But when probable causes persuade you to answer, a refutation can be modestly opposed, which contains useful things for pious readers, so that some may either be confirmed or healed.

Nazianzenus says most modestly, That he does not even seek this, that he may win, but that he may join the adversary to himself. Item, That not only are errors to be reproved, but also better doctrine is to be handed down, which is taken from Demosthenes, who says, ἐπιτιμᾷν παντὸς ἐστὶ, συμβουλένειν δ' ὀυ παντὸς.

An artisan forms a fool, he manages the thing, he is as if he were lame in his feet. It agrees with the Homeric,

An artisan forms a work. And hiring a fool, he hires one who corrupts the work.

THAT is, an artisan is to be chosen in any kind, and everyone is rightly the artisan of his own work and does the work, while sciolists and the unskilled corrupt it. As when physicians are to be used, let us seek the learned and the skilled, not veterinarians. In choosing leaders, not the nobility of birth, but skill, fidelity, and fortitude should be looked at. If you seek a musician, choose a suitable one, not some rude person, who might be an ass to the lyre.

But let us know that this admonition of Solomon not only commands prudence in choosing others, but also diligence or assiduity in exhorting ourselves, so that individuals may become the artisans of their own works, and may show fidelity and diligence in their own vocation.

Item, lest, like busybodies or impostors, we profess the arts of others. This admonition therefore pertains not only to prudence in choosing others, but also to the virtue which is called Assiduity, which faithfully and diligently performs the works of one's own office and vocation. And the use of this admonition extends widely in life, As Aristotle says, That life would be happy, if artisans were to judge about the arts. For it is manifest that great confusions of life arise properly from the fact that sciolists, who are not truly artisans, rule religions and other parts of government. Hence those sayings, Let everyone practice the art which he knows. Item, Let not the cobbler go beyond his shoe. Item, Können wir nicht alle richten / so wollen wir alle richten.

There is, however, in Solomon a singular emphasis on the word forming. For it signifies the native form of the work, and that perfection can be understood and thought of and effected only by artisans. And just as it is the part of art to form, so afterwards the sciolist does it by chance rather than by art, or certainly does it by perverse imitation.

Latin:

Ne respondeas stulto de stultitia eius, ne similis eius fias.

Supra dixit aliquos insanabiles esse, de quibus inquit, Acetum super nitrum canere carmen pravo cordi. Quaestio est igitur, an omnino tacendum sit apud tales? Hic duae pugnantes sententiae recitantur, quae ideo dissentiunt, ut moneant adhibendum esse delectum, quia aliquando respondendum est, aliquando tacendum. Facilis est autem pastoribus diiudicatio in publico munere docendi, hi semper habent auditores dissimiles, pios, dociles, & insanabiles. Necesse est autem eos servire meliori parti, ubi vox ipsa doctrinae incorrupta refutat adversarios & malevolos, & simul pastor modeste potest monere auditores, ne calumniis maledicorum assentiantur. Sed in aliis privatis congressibus melius est omittere certamina, iuxta illud, Ne detis sanctum canibus. Et saepe causae sunt probabiles, cur tacendum sit. Ut cum adversarii rabiosi irritantur, & videntur moturi quaestiones, quae magis sauciaturae essent mentes infirmorum, & facturae maiores dissipationes.

Sic David vocat se columbam mutam, Psal. 55. significans sibi alicubi tacendum fuisse. Est enim usitatissimum, ex parvis initiis maiores contentiones fieri. Ut Homerus de lite inquit,

Parva metu primo mox sese attollit in auras,Ingrediturque solo, & caput inter nubila condit.

Et Thucydides inquit, ἐξ ὀλίγου πολλα.

Tunc igitur, cum utilius est tacere, sequamur exemplum in Psalmo propositum, Detrahebant mihi, Ego autem orabam. Et tenenda consolatio est. Maledicant illi, at tu benedicas.

Et David dulcissimam consolationem nobis proponit, inquiens, Dabit mihi Dominus aliquid boni pro his maledictis. Multa etiam refutat res ipsa nobis tacentibus. Ita Scipio fretus rerum gestarum bonitate, noluit respondere tribunis inquiens in concione, Hoc die ante annos aliquot magno praelio vici hostes. Potius igitur eamus in templum, gratias acturi Deo, quma ut huius convicia audiamus. Aliquando tamen modeste refutatio opponi potest, sicut hic discrimen constituitur, non respondendum esse stulto, nisi velis illi similis fieri, si velis velis certare conviciis, indulgere odio & iracundiae &c. Sed cum probabiles causae suadent ut respondeas, modeste opponi potest refutatio, quae res utiles piis lectoribus contineat, ut aliqui vel confirmentur vel sanentur.

Nazianzenus modestissime inquit, Se nec hoc quidem quaerere ut vincat, sed ut adversarium sibi adiungat. Item, Non tantum reprehendenda esse errata, sed etiam doctrinam meliorem tradendam esse, quod a Demosthene sumptum est, qui inquit, ἐπιτιμᾷν παντὸς ἐστὶ, συμβουλένειν δ' ὀυ παντὸς.

Artifex format stultum rem gerit, est velit claudus pedibus.Congruit cum Homerico, Εσθλὸν καὶ τὸ τέτυκται ὅτ' ἂγγελος ἄισιμα ἔιδη.

Artifex format opus. Et conducens stultum, conducit cor-rumpentem opus.

ID est, artifex eligendus est in quolibet genere, & sui quisque operis artifex recte facit opus, scioli & imperiti corrumpunt. Ut cum medicis utendum est, quaeramus doctos & peritos, non veterinarios. In eligendis ducibus non generis nobilitas, sed peritia, fides, fortitudo spectentur. Si musicum quaeris, elige idoneum, non rudem aliquem, qui sit asinus ad lyram.

Sciamus autem hanc admonitionem Salomonis non solum praecipere de prudentia in aliis eligendis, sed etiam de diligentia seu sedulitate in nobis ipsis cohortandis, ut singuli fiant suorum operum artifices, & in sua vocatione praestent fidem ac diligentiam.

Item, ne, ut ardeliones aut impostores, alienas artes profiteamur. Pertinet igitur haec admonitio non solum ad prudentiam in eligendis aliis, sed etiam ad virtutem, quae vocatur Sedulitas, quae proprii officii & vocationis opera fideliter & diligenter facit. Et late patet usus huius admonitionis in vita, Sicut Aristoteles dicit, Vitam beatam fore, si artifices de artibus iudicarent. Manifestum est enim magnas vitae confusiones proprie inde oriri, quod religiones & alias partes gubernationis regunt scioli, qui non sunt vere artifices. Hinc illa dicta sunt, Quam quisque norit artem, hanc exerceat. Item, Ne sutor ultra crepidam. Item, Können wir nicht alle richten / so wollen wir alle richten.

Est autem in Salomone singularis emphasis in verbo formandi. Significat enim nativam formam operis, & perfectionem a solis artificibus, & intelligi ac cogitari & effici posse. Et sicut artis est formare, ita postea sciolus casu potius quam arte facit, aut certe Facit prava imitatione.

## 26:11
As a dog returns to its vomit, So the fool repeats etc.

It is a common saying, as far as prudence is concerned. It is not the part of a prudent man to strike twice against the same stone. But this sentence in Solomon is a sad rebuke of those who relapse after repentance. Just as Christ also says, That the last things of some become worse than the first, And that the devil returns having taken seven others into the idle house, by which description Christ signifies the cause of relapses. For when after repentance we are secure and idle and admit allurements again, many sad sins follow afterwards. Many things are said to this effect, Idle things are harmful to the secure. Sodom after liberation, when the citizens had been received by the virtue of Abraham, was not long after defiled by most terrible vices. He commands, therefore, that we be attentive and vigilant, after repentance, and that we do not admit allurements, but avoid the occasions of sins, according to that saying, To avoid sins is to avoid the occasions of sins, etc.

Latin:

Sicut canis redit ad vomitum, Ita stultus iterat &c.

Usitatum dictum est, quod ad prudentiam attinet. Non est prudentis bis ad eundem lapidem impingere. Sed haec sententia in Salomone est tristis obiurgario relabentium post paenitentiam. Sicut & Christus inquit, Novissima quorundam fieri deteriora prioribus, Et redire diabolum assumptis aliis septem in domum ociosam, qua descriptione significat Christus causam relapsuum. Cum enim post paenitentiam securi & ociosi admittimus rursus illecebras, sequuntur postea multa tristia peccata. In hanc sententiam multa dicuntur, Ocia securis insidiosa nocent. Sodoma post liberationem, cum cives recepti essent virtute Abrahae, non multo post tererrimis viciis inquinati sunt. Praecipit igitur, ut simus intenti & ugilantes, post paenitentiam, nec admittamus illecebras, sed vitemus occasiones peccatorum, luxta illud, Vitare peccata, est vitare occasiones peccatorum &c.

## 27:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXVII.

Latin:

CAPUT XXVII.

## 27:1
Do not boast about tomorrow, because you do not know what the present day will bring forth.

It agrees with the Roman saying, You do not know what the late evening may bring. Item, Divine power plays with human affairs,

And the present hour scarcely has certain faith. Item, All things of men hang by a thin thread, And those things which were strong fall by a sudden disaster, And he who was just now Croesus is suddenly Irus.

Finally, all books are full of such admonitions concerning the inconstancy of fortune, which are proposed for this reason, that they may admonish us of modesty, and prohibit these two great vices, trust in ourselves, and the rashness of undertaking unnecessary things through foolish hope, and that they may enclose us within boundaries, so that we may only perform the necessary duties of our vocation. And indeed, in the acknowledgment of our infirmity, let us join the invocation of God, just as Christ says, Without me you can do nothing. And Jeremiah says, I know, O Lord, that the way of man is not his own, that is, his vocation. For he does not say this, that men should do nothing, but that without the help of God, neither counsels nor actions are successful.

Therefore, let this rule always be in sight, that we may rightly do both the necessary and the present things, which befit our office, and that we may seek help from God, and not, trusting in our own strengths or by foolish hope, undertake unnecessary things, just as Julius hoped that, having subdued the Parthians, he would come into Egypt, and from there would sail on the Ocean as far as Gaul, and after such a circumnavigation of the world, would return to Rome. Today, namely on the third day of August, two thousand and 25 years ago, Mardonius was defeated at Plataea in a huge battle, and that very history contains many examples of such braggarts.

Mardonius had promised Xerxes that he would subdue Greece; in that battle , having lost his army, he was killed. And at that time, indeed, Pausanias was modest. Afterwards, elated by successful events, he coveted the kingdom of Greece, and being recalled, he was killed by hunger in the temple. Many great errors happen in life, for the reason that men, through trust in themselves, attempt unnecessary things, and through foolish hope promise themselves outcomes such as they dream of. This trust in ourselves, and these foolish hopes, are prohibited by this saying of Solomon: Do not boast about tomorrow.

A stone is heavy, and sand is weighty, But the anger of a fool is heavier than both.

This sentence is very worthy of consideration. For since anger is turbulent by itself, and full of dangers, even in wise men, as is said above, It works toward the evil of a man. Item, Anger is a short madness, etc., anger becomes much more savage in fools, who do not curb the motions of their minds, and are implacable. And it is said much more truly, Μωρὸς ἀνὴρ τάχακεν καἱ ἀναίτιον αἰτιοῶτο, than what Homer says about the more excellent, Δεινὸς ἀνὴρ τάχακεν καὶ ἀναίτιον ἀιτιῶτο. A vehement man blames one who should not be blamed. And Euripides says that there is some happiness in having a wise enemy, who, as a victor, knows how to spare, and to moderate the insolence of victory.

Latin:

Non glorieris de die crastino, quia nescis, quid pariet ho-dierna dies.

Convenit cum Romano dicto, Nescis quid vesper serus vehat. Item, Ludit in humanis divina potentia rebus,

Et certam praesens vix habet hora fidem.Item, Omnia sunt hominum tenui pendentia filo,Et subito casu quae valuere ruunt,Irus & est subito, qui modo Croesus erat.

Denique omnes libri pleni sunt talium admonitionum, de fortunae inconstantia, quae ideo proponuntur, ut nos de modestia admoneant, & prohibeant haec duo magna vicia, fiduciam nostri, & temeritatem movendi res non necessarias, stulta spe, & nos intra metas includant, ut tantum faciamus nostrae vocationis officia necessaria. Et quidem in agnitione nostrae infirmitatis, adiungamus invocationem Dei, sicut inquit Christus, Sine me nihil potestis facere. Et leremias inquit, Scio Domine, non est hominis via eius, id est, Vocatio. Non enim hoc dicit, nihil agere homines, sed sine auxilio Dei, neque consilia neque actiones felices esse.

Semper igitur in conspectu sit haec regula, ut & necessaria & praesentia recte faciamus, quae nostro officio conveniunt, & a Deo petamus auxilium, nec confisi nostris viribus, aut stulta spe moveamus non necessaria, sicut lulius sperabat se Parthis domitis venturum in Aegyptum, & inde in Oceano navigaturum esse, usque in Galliam, ac post tantam orbis terrarum circuitionem, Romam rediturum esse, Hodie, videlicet tertio die Augusti, ante duo millia annorum & 25. annos, Mardonius ad Plateas victus est ingenti praelio, ac illa ipsa historia multa exempla continet talium Thrasonum.

Mardonius promiserat Xerxi, se domiturum esse Graeciam, in ea pugna amisso exercitu, interfectus est. Ac tunc quidem Pausanias fuit modestus. Postea rebus secundis elatus appetivit regnum Graeciae, & revocatus, in templo fame necatus est. Multa magna errata in vita accidunt, propterea, quia homines fiducia Ivi, moliuntur non necessaria, & stulta spe promittunt sibi exitus, quales somniant. Haec fiducia nostri, & hae stultae spes prohibentur hoc dicto Salomonis. Non glorieris de die crastino.

Grave est saxum, & ponderosa arena,Sed ira stulti gravior est utraque re.

Valde digna est consideratione haec sententia. Cum enim ira per sese turbulenta sit, & plena periculorum, etiam in viris sapientibus, ut supra dicitur, In viri malum operatur. Item, Ira furor brevis est &c. multo fit saevior ira in stultis, qui non frenant animorum motus, & sunt implacabiles. Ac multo verius dicitur, Μωρὸς ἀνὴρ τάχακεν καἱ ἀναίτιον αἰτιοῶτο, quam quod Homerus dicit de praestantioribus, Δεινὸς ἀνὴρ τάχακεν καὶ ἀναίτιον ἀιτιῶτο. Vehemens vir culpat non culpandum. Et Euripides dicit aliquam esse felicitatem, habere hostem sapientem, qui victor scit par. cere, & moderari insolentiam victictoriae.

## 27:8
As a bird that wandereth from her nest, So is a man that wandereth from his place.

Since there is no kind of life, no function, without many troubles & dangers, fastidiousness quickly creeps into any kind of life, and the minds of many become weaker, so that they flee present troubles, they seek other kinds of life, and other functions, as we see daily many either deserting Churches entirely, or seeking quieter ones, Many also desert marriages, And these desertions and changes are customary in friendships, in opinions, and in many things, according to that,

The lazy ox desires the saddle, the horse desires to plow.

Against these fastidiousnesses & this mobility, precepts have been handed down, 1. Corinth. 7. Let every man abide in the same calling wherein he was called. Let the life of men be ruled by the word of God, not by our softness, not by our desires, which are dissimilar and vagabond. Therefore they must be curbed by a certain norm handed down by God. If you are a husband, do not desert your spouse because of the hardships that accompany that kind of life. If you preside over a Church, do not desert it, without a grave cause. Therefore also Isocrates rightly commands, στέργε τα παρόντα.

And here Solomon censures the mobility of minds, in those wanderers who easily desert their station, and says they are like birds, flying here and there on any occasion. Proverbs also pertain to this. A plant often moved does not grow. Item, A stone often rolled is not covered with moss.

These things warn privately that frequent useless changes are for the head of a household. But the Republic must be considered much more. The change of teachers often harms Churches, because other teachers immediately bring another method of teaching. Places also and diverse familiarities bring great changes to judgments, Because there are great contagions of judgments & opinions in dissimilar familiarities.

Iron sharpeneth iron, And a man sharpeneth the countenance of his friend.

He censures the arrogance of those who wish to be wise alone, and do not confer their judgments & counsels with others, as it is said, Everyone is the worst master to himself. Item, One man does not see everything. Conversely, he warns that collation with others is useful, as Euripides says, Conversation has produced arts. And Cicero says, It is most profitable in learning συζὴτησις.

As in water face answereth to face, So the heart of man to man.

That is, As faces appearing in water are slippery, so hearts are inconstant & deceitful. And by this saying the inconstancy of the human heart is censured, both in friendships & in opinions, and in other affairs even rightly undertaken. And since this is a common evil, and is often the cause of great calamities, it is accused by the complaints not only of writers, but also of the people. To this pertain those things, Nowhere is faith safe. Item, And faith stands and falls with fortune. And hold that saying of Epicharmus, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν, to be the nerves and joints of wisdom. Item, Whom you believe to be faithful, avoid, and you will be safe. And in Jeremiah, Cursed is he that trusteth in man.

These sayings contain admonitions about many great things. First in doctrine, that we may not approve dogmas about God moved by human authority alone, but seek the fountains in the doctrine handed down by God. Then, that we may not, trusting in human defenses, move unnecessary things. Finally, that we may not, trusting in friendships, commit secrets or dangerous affairs to others.

Latin:

Sicut avis deserens nidum suum, Sic est vir, deserens locum suum.

Cum nullum vitae genus, nulla functio sit, line multis molestiis & periculis, cito in quolibet vitae genere obrepunt fastidia, & multorum animi fiunt inbecilliores, ut fugiant praesentes molestias, quaerunt alia vitae genera, & alias functiones, ut quotidie videmus multos vel omnino deserere Ecclesias, vel quaerere tranquilliores, Multi etiam coniugia deserunt, Et usitatae sunt hae desertiones & mutationes in amicitiis, in opinionibus & multis rebus, luxta illud,

Optat ephippia bos piger, optat arare caballus.

Contra haec fastidia & hanc mobilitatem praecepta tradita sunt,) 1. Corinth. 7. Unusquisque in qua vocatione vocatus est, in ea maneat. Regatur hominum vita verbo Dei, non nostra mollicie, non nostris cupiditatibus, quae sunt dissimiles & vagabundae. Ideo frenandae sunt certa norma a Deo tradita. Si es maritus, non deseras coniugem propter aerumnas, quae comitantur illud vitae genus. Si praees Ecclesiae, non deseras eam, sine gravi causa. Ideo & Isocrates recte praecipit, στέργε τα παρόντα.

Et hic Salomon mobilitatem animorum taxat, in erronibus illis, qui facile deserunt suam stationem, & dicit similes esse avibus, quavis occasione passim volitantibus. Huc & proverbia pertinent. Planta saepe translata non adolescit. Item, Saxum saepe volutatum non obducitur musco.

Haec monent privatim crebras mutationes inutiles esse patrifamilias. Sed multo magis consideranda est Respublica. Mutatio doctorum saepe nocet Ecclesiis, quia alii doctores subinde aliam rationem docendi afferunt. Loca etiam & faemiliaritates diversae magnas mutationes iudiciis afferunt, Quia magna sunt contagia sudiciorum & opinionum in dissimilibus familiaritatibus.

Ferrum ferro acuitur,Et homo acuit faciem amici sui.

Taxat arrogantiam eorum, qui soli sapere volunt, nec conferunt sua iudicia & consilia cum aliis, sicut dicitur, Quisque sibiipsi pessimus magister est. Item, Unus vir non videt omnia. Econtra monet utilem esse collationem cum aliis, sicut Euripides ait, Conversatio peperit artes. Et Cicero inquit, Plurimum prodest in discendo συζὴτησις.

Sicut facies in aquis,Sic cor hominis ad homines.

ID est, Sicut facies in aquis apparentes lubricae sunt, ita corda sunt inconstantia & fallacia. Et taxatur hoc dicto inconstantia humani cordis, cum in amicitiis & in opinionibus, tum in aliis negociis etiam recte susceptis. Et cum hoc vulgare malum sit, & saepe sit causa magnarum calamitatum, non modo scriptorum, sed etiam populi querelis accusatur. Huc illa pertinent, Nusquam tuta fides. Item, Et cum fortuna statque caditque fides. Et Epicharmium illud teneto, μέμνησο ἀπιστεῖν, nernos atque artus esse sapientiae. Item, Quos credis fidos effuge tutus eris. Et in Ieremia, Maledictus qui confidit in homine.

Haec dicta continent, admonitiones de multis magnis rebus. Primum in doctrina, ne dogmata de Deo sola humana autoritate moti approbemus, sed fontes quaeramus in doctrina a Deo tradita. Deinde, ne confisi humanis praesidiis moveamus res non necessarias. Denique ne confisi amicitiis arcana aut negocia periculosa aliis committamus.

## 27:23
Be thou diligent to know the state of thy flocks.

It is an economic saying, similar to that which Aristotle recites, The horse is best fattened by the eye of the master. However, the sentence can be transferred in general to diligence in each one's own office, opposed to sloth & meddlesomeness.

Let a prince diligently rule his own country, and omit the affairs of others. Let teachers in the Church and in schools teach diligently; let a pastor not have one foot in the temple and the other in the court.

The histories of all times show that the outcomes of those who stir up affairs outside their calling are unhappy, and yet there are many natures so restless that they do not wish to contain themselves within their own boundaries. A Greek verse taxes this curiosity with a notable figure.

Καὶ που ἐων ὀλιγος μνᾶτει ἐυτηχειν ἀθηναν .

Let us learn, therefore, that among the chief rules of life is that each person should contain himself within the boundaries of his own calling, and diligently perform the duties of his own office. As Paul says, Study to be quiet, and to do your own business. To this common doctrine, this saying of Solomon can also be adapted, which indeed also warns that we should do everything in its own time.

Latin:

Diligenter agnosce faciem pecoris tui.

Oeconomicum dictum est, simile isti, quod recitat Aristoteles, Equum optime saginari oculo domini. Potest autem in genere sententia transferri ad diligentiam in suo cuiusque officio oppositam ignaviae, & polypragmosynae.

Princeps suam patriam diligenter regat, & omittat aliena negocia. Doctores in Ecclesia & in scholis diligenter doceant, non habeat pastor alterum pedem in templo, alterum in curia.

Ostendunt autem omnium temporum historiae infelices esse exitus moventium negocia extra vocationem, & tamen multae sunt naturae adeo inquietae, ut se intra suas metas continere nolint. Versiculus graecus insigni figura taxas hanc curiositatem.

Καὶ που ἐων ὀλιγος μνᾶτει ἐυτηχειν ἀθηναν.

Discamus igitur inter regulas vitae praecipuas esse, continere se quemque intra metas vocationis suae, & diligenter proprii muneris officia facere. Sicut Paulus inquit, Hoc studiose expetire, ut quieti sitis, & propria agatis. Ad hanc communem doctrinam accommodari & hoc dictum Salomonis potest, quod quidem etiam monet, ut suo quaeque tempore faciamus.

## 28:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXVIII.

For the transgressions of the land, princes are often changed, And by a prudent and intelligent man, the government is more durable.

There is a common complaint about the negligence and vices of princes. And the people accuse the princes, according to that saying, Whatever the kings rave, the Greeks are punished. But Solomon here accuses not only the princes, but both, the people and the princes. For he says, because of the sins of the land, that is, of the people and of the princes. And often both have many sad sins, just as it is also said in Hosea, Like people, like priest. Let both, therefore, acknowledge their own sins. Let the people also be warned, so that many may acknowledge the causes of public calamities, that they are not only the transgressions of princes, but also the many frenzies of others; indeed, often because of the stubbornness of the people, God gives unhappy princes.

Here, therefore, Solomon says that frequent changes of governors happen because of the sins of the land, that is, of the princes and the people. And it is a brief description of huge calamities when he says that frequent changes of princes occur. For these changes do not happen without seditions, wars, devastations, slaughters, the destruction of laws, and the confusion of religions and discipline. Therefore, many very sad complaints about changes are read everywhere. Xenophon says,

Πᾶσαι μεταβολαι πολιτειῶν θανατηφόροι . And Thucydides, Πᾶσα ἰδέα κακοῦ ἐστιν ἐν στάσει .

An antithesis is added, which contains a singular admonition, necessary for all men, princes and people. He says that governments are more durable because of a prudent and intelligent man, as because of Zechariah, Simeon, and the like, it is consistent that many seditions were repressed among the Jewish people, because there is no doubt that then also many argued that the Romans should be cast out, just as once Antiochus had been cast out. This Zechariah and Simeon and the like advised that seditions should not be stirred up, but that this however hard servitude should be endured, because they knew that in the last time the people would be in this servitude. They also knew that happy liberations only happen in such a way when God raises up pious leaders and provides opportunities and the concord of governors, not when restless men stir up wars only with human counsels.

Solomon therefore warns both the governors and the people, that each one, being understanding in his own place, should see what things must be tolerated wisely and modestly, and that we should not ourselves invite changes through impatience or restlessness of mind, as happens and will happen in all empires that are destined to fall. As in Judea, new movements arose from time to time against Herod, and then against the Romans, because there were many restless and provocative natures, and being such, they later sought pretexts. Similar things, however, have been done, are being done, and will be done in all empires. Let us therefore consider the causes. And although individuals and private persons can rarely prevent such ruins, yet so that our consciences may not be wounded, let us be modest, lest by our impatience, when it is not necessary, we attract destructive movements.

At the same time, however, with true groans and vows, let us pray to God, the guardian of the Church and of human society, that He Himself may rule us and the governors, and preserve the Church, the light of doctrine, discipline, and the hospices of the Churches. As the Prophets cry out so often, as in Isaiah, We are your clay, etc. Do not be angry too much, etc. And in Jeremiah, Do not reduce us to nothing, etc. And Psalm 7, Arise, O Lord my God, to the commandment which you have commanded, that the Synagogue of the peoples may surround you, that is, Preserve your law, lest a universal dissipation occur.

When the just are preferred, there is great glory; when the impious arise, changes occur.

That is, the Church and empires flourish when the authority of the just is valid, that is, when Doctors teach rightly, and in administration do not serve private affections, avarice, ambition, the desire for vengeance, malevolence, and other diseases of the mind. So also in empires, when men who love public concord and safety are in charge, empires flourish longer. According to that, Justice establishes the throne. Item, The good is congregative and communicative of itself, and conversely, evil is destructive of itself.

But when the impious are raised to government, great dissipations and distraction follow, as when false dogmas are scattered, or when greedy men hold empires, there rapines provide an occasion for war, when the ambitious arm themselves with factions and oppress those who dissent, there Republics are distracted into diverse parts, and each part grants license to its own, which is not granted in a quiet and well-mannered Republic. As it is said, ἐν δὲ διχοστασίῃ καὶ πάγκακος ἐμμορε τιμῆρ. Thus changes occur, that is, dissipations, which are universally a destruction to the human race. Hence many complaints are read among writers. Juvenal, The fatherland also once lost, the glory of a few and the desire for a title of praise. Item Claudian,

For other kingdoms, luxury with vices, and pride with hatreds, overturns.

In the Hebrew text there is a word that signifies change, sometimes also dismissal from servitude, which also fits here, because it signifies that the impious loosen the reins of discipline and grant immoderate license to the common people and their own, that is, not to punish offenses. But impunity is always the beginning of the ruin of cities, as appears in the history of Benjamin and many other examples.

Warned by these sayings, let us ask God for salutary governors and salutary Doctors, and let us ourselves endure the reins of discipline, and let us not imagine that liberty is infinite license. But let us remember that saying of Crassus, We are servants of the laws, so that we may be able to be free. For Liberty is the faculty of doing what is right, of using one's own body and one's own things rightly, according to honest laws, which restrain those who do not act rightly and protect the weaker against injuries.

He who covers his sins will not be happy, but to him who confesses and abandons them, the Lord will show mercy.

Others persist in atrocious crimes with manifest stubbornness, which men understand; that these rush into punishments, there is no doubt, according to that saying, Atrocious crimes are punished with atrocious punishments.

But here mention is made of those who cover their crimes, that is, who sophistically excuse them, as those who establish impious cults, and adorn false dogmas with specious excuses; he threatens these with punishments, who even if they cannot be restrained by men, yet will at length be restrained divinely, as the Jews were restrained when their whole republic was destroyed. Likewise, as the Arians and the like were restrained.

But as it was said above, the just man in the beginning is his own accuser, so here he requires confession of error and crime, and indeed such a one as also shows amendment, as when, having acknowledged the truth, we cast away false opinions, and embrace the truth, and acquiesce in it, and give thanks to God. It must be diligently considered in this sentence that it demands such a conversion in which those opinions are left behind which the conscience acknowledges to be false, and evil purposes against the conscience. For it is necessary in conversion that the evil purpose be left behind, nor is there conversion or repentance as long as perseverance in the evil purpose remains, nor can faith and invocation exist together with an evil purpose.

Latin:

CAPUT XXVIII.

Propter delicta terrae saepe mutantur principes, Et propter virum prudentem & intelligentem durabilius est imperium.

Usitata querela est, de principum negligentia & viciis. Et populus accusat principes, luxta illud, Quicquid delirant Reger, plectuntur Achivi. Sed Salomon accusat hic non solos principes, sed utrosque, populos & principes. Inquit enim, propter peccata terrae, id est, populi ac principum. Ac saepe utrique multa tristia peccata habent, sicut & apud Oseam dicitur, Qualis populus, talis sacerdos. Agnoscant igitur utrique sua peccata, Moneatur & populus, ut multi agnoscant publicarum calamitatum causas, non sola principum delicta, sed etiam multos aliorum furores esse, imo saepe propter populi contumaciam Deus das principes infelices.

Hic igitur Salomon dicit accidere crebras mutationes gubernatorum, propter terrae peccata, id est, principum & populi. Ac brevis descriptio est ingentium calamitatum, cum dicit, crebras accidere mutationes principum. Non enim fiunt hae mutationes sine seditionibus, bellis, vastationibus, caedibus, legum interitu, religionum & disciplinae confusione. Ideo multae tristissimae quaerelae de mutationibus passim leguntur. Xenophon inquit,

Πᾶσαι μεταβολαι πολιτειῶν θανατηφόροι.Et Thucydides, Πᾶσα ἰδέα κακοῦ ἐστιν ἐν στάσει.

Addita est autem Antithesis, quae singularem commonefactionem continet, omnibus hominibus, principibus & populo necessariam. Imperia dicit durabiliora esse, propter hominem prudentem & intelligentem, ut propter Zachariam, Si meonem, & similes consentaneum est multas seditiones in populo iudaico repressas esse, quia non dubium est, tunc quoque multos disputasse, Romanos eiiciendos esse, sicut olim Antiochus eiectus erat. Hic Zacharias & Simeon ac similes consuluerunt non movendas esse seditiones, sed tolerandam esse hanc quamvis duram servitutem, quia sciebant postremo tempore populum in hac servitute futurum esse. Sciebant etiam ita tantum fieri felices liberationes, cum Deus excitares pios duces, & praeberet occasiones & concordiam gubernatorum, non cum tantum humanis consiliis homines inquieti bella moverent.

Monet igitur Salomon & gubernatores & populum, ut suo quisque loco sit intelligens, videat quae sapientet & modeste toleranda sint, nec impacientia aut animi inquietudie mutationes ipsi accersamus, sicut in omnibus imperiis ruituris accidit & accidet. Ut in Iudaea subinde novi motus orti sunt contra Herodem, & deinde contra Romanos, Quia multae erant inquietae & irritatrices naturae, & tales cum essent, postea quaerebant praetextus. Similia autem facta sunt, fiunt & fient in omnibus imperiis. Causas igitur consideremus. Et quanquam raro tales ruinas prohibere singuli ac privati possunt, tamen ne vulnerentur nostrae conscientiae, simus modesti, ne nostra impatientia, cum non necesse est, attrahamus exitiales motus.

Simul autem veris gemitibus & votis oremus Deum custodem Ecclesiae, & societatis humanae, ut ipse regat nos & gubernatores, & servet Ecclesiam, lucem doctrinae, disciplinam, & Ecclesiarum hospitia. Sicut toties clamant Prophetae, ut apud Esaiam, Nos lutum tuum sumus &c. Non irascaris nimium &c. Et apud leremiam, Non ad nihilum redigas nos &c. Et Psal. 7. Exurge Domine Deus meus ad praeceptum, quod mandasti, ut Synagoga populorum circumdet re, id est, Conserva legem tuam, ne fiat universalis dissipatio.

Quando iusti praeferuntur, magna est gloria,Quando exurgunt impii, fiunt mutationes.

ID est, Ecclesia & imperia florent, quando valet autoritas iustorum, id est, quando Doctores recte docent, & in administratione non serviunt privatis affectibus, avariciae, ambitioni, cupiditati vindictae, malevolentiae, & aliis morbis animi. Sic & in imperiis, quando praesunt homines amantes publicae concordiae & salutis, imperia diutius florent. Iuxta illud, Iusticia stabilit thronum. Item, Bonum est congregativum & communicativum sui, & econtra malum est destructivum sui.

Sed cum ad gubernationem evehuntur impii, sequuntur magnae dissipationes & distractio, ut cum falsa dogmata sparguntur, aut cum tenent imperia homines avari, ibi rapinae praebent occasionem belli, cum ambitiosi armant se factionibus, & praemunt dissentientes, ibi distrahuntur Respublicae in diversas partes, & quaelibet pars suis concedit licentiam, quae in quieta & bene morata Republica non conceditur. Sicut dicitur, ἐν δὲ διχοστασίῃ καὶ πάγκακος ἐμμορε τιμῆρ. Ita fiunt matationes, id est, dissipationes, quae universaliter sunt exitio generi humano. Hinc multae querelae leguntur apud scriptores. Iuvenalis, Patriam quoque perdidit olim, Gloria paucorum laudis titulique cupido. Item Claudianus,

Nam caetera regnaLuxuries viciis, odiisque superbia vertit.

In haebraico textu verbum est, quod significat mutationem, interdum & dimissionem ex servitute, quod etiam huc congruit, quia significat impios laxare stenos disciplinae, & concedere vulgo & suis immodicam licentiam, hoc est, non punire delicta. Semper autem impunitas initium est interitus civitatum, ut in historia Beniamin & aliis multis exemplis apparet.

Moniti autem his dictis, petamus a Deo salutares gubernatores, & salutares Doctores, & nos ipsi disciplinae frenos patiamur, nec fingamus libertatem esse infinitam licentiam. Sed illud Crassi dictum meminerimus, Legum servi sumus, ut liberi esse possimus. Est enim Libertas facultas recta faciendi, suo corpore & suis rebus recte utendi, iuxta honestas leges, quae coërcent non recte facientes, & muniunt imbecilliores contra iniurias.

Qui tegit peccata sua, non erit felix, Confitenti autem & relinquenti propiciabitur Dominus.

Alii manifesta pertinacia perseverant in sceleribus atrocibus, quae homines intelligunt, Hos ruere in poenas, nihil dubium est, luxta illud, Atrocia delicta puniuntur atrocibus poenis.

Sed hic mentio fit de his, qui tegunt scelera, Id est, qui sophistice excusant, ut qui stabiliunt impios cultus, & fucosis excusationibus ornant falsa dogmata, his minatur poenas, Qui etiamisi ab hominibus reprimi non possunt, tamen tandem divinitus reprimentur, ut repressi sunt Iudaei deleta tota ipsorum republica. Item, ut repressi sunt Arriani & similes.

Ut autem supra dictum est, Iustus in principio est accusator sui, ita hic requirit confessionem erroris & delicti, & quidem talem, quae etiam ostendit emendationem, ut cum agnita veritate abiicimus falsas opiniones, & veritatem amplectimur, in ea acquiescimus, & Deo gratias agimus. Diligenter considerandum est, in hac sententia, quod postulat talem conversionem, in qua relinquantur opiniones, quas conscientia agnoscit falsas esse, & proposita mala contra conscientiam. Necesse est enim in conversione relinqui malum propositum, nec est conversio seu paenitentia, donec manet perseverantia in malo proposito, nec fides & invocatio cum malo proposito simul esse possunt.

## 28:14
Blessed is the man who is always fearful, but he who hardens his heart will fall into evil.

This sentence does not fight against faith, against the consolation of consciences, against joy in the Mediator, but both must exist together. Let there be fear acknowledging the judgment and wrath of God against sins, let there be solicitude in avoiding evil opinions and lapses. As Paul says, Work out your salvation with fear and trembling. Let there also be grief deploring our stubbornness and uncleanness, doubts, and many evils which cling to us. But let faith govern fear and grief, which may establish us, although unworthy and miserable, yet to be pleasing to God on account of the Mediator. This saying therefore censures security and carnal pride, trust in one's own wisdom, justice, and power. Just as Sennacherib rushed into destruction by trust in his own wisdom, power, and justice. Nebuchadnezzar rushed into punishments on account of the admiration of his own wisdom and power. Against this security it is said, Where shall the Lord dwell, if not in a spirit contrite, humbled, and trembling at my words? Likewise, The beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord.

Latin:

Beatus est homo, qui semper est pavidus, Indurans vero cor suum cadet in malum.

Non pugnat haec sententia de fide, de consolatione conscientiarum, de laetitia in Mediatore, sed utrumque simul esse oportet. Sit timor agnoscens iudicium & iram Dei, adversus peccata, sit sollicitudo in cavendis malis opinionibus & lapsibus. Sicut Paulus inquit, Operamini salutem cum timore & tremore. Sit etiam dolor deplorans nostram contumaciam & immundiciam, dubitationes & multa mala, quae in nobis haerent. Sed gubernet timorem & dolorem fides, quae statuat nos, quanquam indignos & miseros, tamen placere Deo propter Mediatorem. Taxat igitur hoc dictum securitatem & superbiam carnalem, fiduciam propriae sapientiae, iusticiae & potentiae. Sicut Sennaherib ruit in exitium fiducia suae sapientiae, potentiae & iusticiae. Nabuchodonosor ruit in poenas propter admirationem suae sapientiae & potentiae. Adversus hanc securitatem dictum est, Ubi habitabit Dominus, nisi in spiritu contrito, humiliato, & tremente sermones meos? Item, Initium sapientiae timor Domini.

## 29:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXIX.

Latin:

CAPUT XXIX.

## 29:12
A prince loving lies has all his ministers impious.

These are customary sayings, which signify that the morals and judgments of the people imitate the examples of the governors.

Forsooth, the examples of the powerful flow into the common people, The whole world is composed according to the example of the King, And as the camps follow the trumpets of the leaders, so they follow their morals.

And that of Hosea, Like priest, like people. And the matter shows that the examples of governors have the greatest weight in both directions. But they are most powerful in those whose domestic familiarity and assiduity with the Lord is greatest. And it is true that, Like is delighted by like. Therefore, the opinions and morals of princes and courtiers are for the most part similar. The Greeks express these things in these common sayings, Like mistresses, like little dogs. Likewise, Like mistresses, like handmaids. And here Solomon says, A prince loving lies has all impious ministers. As truth or veracity is a distinguished virtue in a governor, fighting against vanity, perfidy, calumnies, superstitions, etc. So on the contrary, the love of lies is a very pernicious evil in any governor whatsoever.

And that evil embraces many vices, just as there are many kinds of lies. A manifest kind is Vanity, or perfidy, in not keeping promises, in treaties, etc. As the perfidy of Alcibiades is censured.

Another kind of lies is the Love of calumnies. That is common in courts. For very many take delight in detraction, and have ears open to all gossip. Concerning whom are the well-known verses of Menander,

The court takes the greatest delight in the wicked, The first and most blessed is the flatterer. The sycophant holds the second parts, The third is κακοθης . And in the Pseudolus of Plautus it is said, Men who carry, and who listen to crimes, If it were allowed by my judgment, let them all be hanged. The carriers by their tongues, the listeners by their ears.

And the calumnies themselves are adapted to the affections of the masters, concerning the pursuits and parties of factions, concerning Religion, concerning the affectation of power, and other opportune arguments. And sycophants beautifully know the precept of a certain flatterer of Alexander, who said, Calumniate boldly, something always sticks, even if the falsehood is detected at the beginning, nevertheless a scar remains, which may cause harm later.

The third kind of lies are suspicions, which unfaithful natures fashion from themselves, which very often provide an occasion for great evils. As Tyrants remove many only on account of suspicions. And suspicious natures listen most greedily to calumnies. Therefore they have lying courtiers, and very many calumniators.

The fourth kind is Sophistry, which defends false opinions and bad causes with specious pretexts for the favor of princes. This kind of lies brings forth the greatest admiration of wisdom and power.

This sentence of Solomon therefore narrates that which happens, that the courts of masters are similar. He warns, however, silently, that we should consider these evils, fear the snares, be more vigilant and circumspect, etc.

When Prophecy ceases, the people are scattered. And he who keeps the law is blessed.

He calls prophetic governance prophecy, that is, in which God gives a governor strong in counsel and fortunate, so that as long as Elisha presided, both kingdoms of Israel remained, as long as Isaiah was heard, the status of Jerusalem was moderate, with him killed Manasseh was captured, and great public calamities followed. In the Churches concord remained while the Apostles lived, afterwards immediately many and foul lacerations were made, because curious and ambitious natures feared no one's authority. And generally when Republics are moved, whether justly or unjustly, internal struggles follow between successors, who all desire Monarchy, when neither virtue nor good fortune is similar to the first leader, whose authority previously prevailed. As with Alexander dead, many desire the kingdom. This was signified in the fable of Cadmus, he sows the teeth of the dragon, that is, he has restless descendants, who then fight among themselves. Solomon therefore warns that we should learn that human counsels do not suffice for retaining good governance, but there is need for a prophetic voice, that is, which keeps itself within the bounds of the precepts of God, and at the same time is helped by God.

For Solomon adds what kind of thing Prophetic government is, namely, that which guards the law of God, that is, it keeps itself within the boundaries of divine precepts, and does not depart from the word of God, does not disturb the Churches with curiosity, or ambition, or sycophancy, does not move the Republic πολυπραγμοσύνη, with avarice, with ambition. As after the death of Constantine his successors disturbed the empire and the Church in many ways.

Latin:

PrInceps amans mendacia, habet omnes ministros impios.

Usitata dicta sunt, quae significant mores & iudicia populi imitari exempla gubernatorum.

Scilicet in vulgus manant exempla potentum,Regis ad exemplum totus componitur orbis,Utque ducum lituos, sic mores castra sequuntur.

Et illlud Oseae, Qualis sacerdos, talis populus. Et res ostendit, plurimum exempla gubernatorum in utramque partem videre. Sed maxime valent in his, quorum est domestica familiaritas & assiduitas cum Domino. Et utrum est, Similes similibus delectari. Ideo plerunque sunt similes principum & aulicorum opiniones & mores. Id his bellis dictis Graeci efferunt, Quales dominae, tales catellae. Item, Quales dominae, tales ancillae. Et hic Salomon inquit, Princeps amans mendacia, habet omnes ministros impios. Ut insignis virtus in gubernatore est veritas seu veracitas, pugnans cum vanitate, perfidia, calumniis, superstitionibus &c. Ita econtra valde perniciosum malum est in gubernatore quocunque amor mendaciorum.

Et multa vitia complectitur id malum, sicut mendaciorum genera multa sunt. Manifestum genus est, Vanitas, seu perfidia, in non servandis promissis, in foederibus &c. Ut Alcibiadis perfidia vituperatur.

Aliud genus est mendaciorum, Amor calumniarum. Id commune est in aulis. Plurimi enim delectantur obtrectatione, & habent patulas aures ad omnes fa bellas. De quibus sunt versiculi noti Menandri,

Aula maxime delectatur improbis,Primus & beatissimus est assentator.Secundas partes habet Sycnphanta,Tertius est κακοθης.Et in Pseudolo Plauti dicitur,Homines qui gestant, quique auscultant crimina,Si meo arbitratu liceat, omnes pendeant.Gestarores linguis, auditores auribus.

Et calumniae ipsae accommodantur ad affectus dominorum, de factionum studiis & partibus, de Religione, de affectatione potentiae, & aliis argumentis opportunis. Ac pulchre norunt Sicophantae praeceptum cuiusdam assentatoris Alexandri, qui dixit, Audacter calumniare, semper aliquid haeret, etiamsi falsitas initio deprehenditur, tamen manet cicatrix, quae postea noceat.

Tertium genus mendaciorum sunt suspiciones, quas naturae infidae ex seipsis fingunt, quae saepissime magnis malis occasionem praebent. Ut Tyranni tantum propter suspiciones multos tollunt. Ac naturae suspicaces avidissime audiunt calumnias. Habent igitur mendaces aulicos, & calumniatores plurimos.

Quartum genus est Sophistica, quae falsas opiniones & malas causas fucosis praetextinbus in gratiam principum tuetur. Hoc genus mendaciorum praecipuam affert admirationem sapientiae & potentiae.

Haec igitur sententia Salomonis narrat id quod fit, Similes esse aulas dominorum. Monet autem tacite, ut haec mala consideremus, metuamus insidias, simus vigilantiores & circumspectiores &c.

Cum cessat Prophetia, dissipatur populus.Et custodiens legem, beatus est.

Prophetiam vocat gubernationem propheticam, id est, in qua Deus dat gubernatorem valentem consilio & felicem, ut donec Elisaeus praefuit, mansit utrumque regnum Israel, donec Esaias audiebatur, Hierosolymae mediocris status erat, eo interfecto Manasses captus est, & magnae calamitates publicae secutae sunt. In Ecclesiis mansit concordia utcunque viventibus Apostolis, postea statim multae & tetrae dila cerationes factae sunt, quia nullius autoritatem verebantur naturae curiosae & ambitiosae. Et plerumque motis Rebuspub. seu iuste seu iniuste, sequuntur intestina certamina inter successores, qui omnes appetunt Monarchiam, cum nec virtus nec felicitas similis sit primo duci, cuius antea valuit autoritas. Ut mortuo Alexandro multi appetunt regnum. Hoc significatum est in Cadmi fabula, hic serit dentes draconis, id est, habet posteros inquietos, qui deinde inter se dimicant. Monet igitur Salomon, ut discamus humana consilia non sufficere ad retinendam bonam gubernationem, sed opus est prophetica voce, id est, quae intra metas praeceptorum Dei se continet, & simul a Deo iuvatur.

Addit enim Salomon, qualis sit Prophetica gubernatio, scilicet, quae custodit legem Dei, Id est, continet se intra metas prae ceptorum divinorum, nec discedit a verbo Dei, non turbat Ecclesias curiositate, vel ambitione, vel assentatione, non movet Rempub. πολυπραγμοσύνη, avaritia, ambitione. Ut post Constantini interitum imperium & Ecclesiam turbarunt eius posteri multipliciter.

## 30:1 Argument
CHAPTER XXX.

At the beginning of the thirtieth chapter a new Epigraph has been added. And it is possible, as is customary in collecting maxims, that other sentences were also added later to the sentences of Solomon by principal men, just as there were always excellent Doctors among that people. Nor are these following sentences vulgar admonitions, or about small moments of life.

The first contains the highest rule of all, that we should acknowledge the darkness of our mind, and wish to be ruled by the word of God, and include ourselves within the boundaries of the word of God, that is, with firm assent embrace the doctrine concerning the essence and will of God, and concerning our morals, which God has revealed, and not invent other opinions concerning the essence and will of God, concerning worships, or concerning our morals, as the Gentiles did most audaciously, who subsequently invented new deities, and granted license to wandering lusts. Likewise heretics, Pharisees, Monks, and other Idolaters, invented opinions and worships concerning God against the voice handed down by Him.

Let this rule therefore be in sight, repeated most often, in the whole heavenly doctrine, As in the Psalm, Thy word is a lamp to my feet. And in Isaiah, To the law and to the testimony. And the eternal Father says concerning the Son, Hear him. Thus God wishes to gather to Himself an eternal Church, and not otherwise, by the voice of His doctrine, As it is said, Sanctify them in the truth, thy word is truth. Thus He wishes the Church to be associated, and coupled to one head, namely to the Son of God.

But the history of the world shows how great dissipations have always been made, and are being made, in the human race, when men depart from the word of God, impelled by the Devil and their own levity, as Adam and Eve.

Let us consider these most sad examples, and let us be firm in retaining the purity of the doctrine handed down by God.

This is the sum of this most grave antithesis placed here. In the beginning the writer confesses his own foolishness, that is, he warns that we should acknowledge our darkness. As Paul says, The world did not know God in wisdom through wisdom. He condemns human thoughts, and the trust of human wisdom, asserting something concerning the essence and will of God, concerning worships and our morals, without the voice handed down by God.

Afterwards he leads us to the divine voice, Every word of God is purified, it is a shield to the believer. He says expressly purified, so that he may warn that he is speaking about the word of God without corruptions. And afterwards he adds, It is a shield to the believer, this demands that we embrace with firm assent, and acquiesce and trust in God, thus revealing Himself.

Afterwards he prohibits additions, that is, all corruptions, all idols, all impious worships, all assertions concerning God, and concerning our morals, which do not conform to the norm of the word of God.

The second sentence speaks about the mediocrity of life. It shows that there are two principal goods , the true knowledge of God, and a moderate degree in the common labors or offices of life, Because God wishes man not to be idle, and useless.

a burden to the earth, but in his own place in his own calling to help the common society, as it is written, In the sweat of thy face shalt thou eat thy bread. Item, Thou shalt eat the labors of thy hands. Item to the Thessal. Do your own business &c.

He desires, however, a moderate station, Because for the most part men in prosperous circumstances become more negligent. As it is written, The people sat down to eat & to drink, & rose up to play &c. Negligent people call upon God less anxiously, they seek pleasures, they become proud, curious, stirring up unnecessary things. Just as David & Solomon in leisure became more negligent. And Josiah, elated by previous successes, stirred up an unnecessary war.

Finally, daily examples show that both are dangerous, excessive wealth & extreme poverty, because the needy, since they have not learned honest labors, flee to flatteries & to evil arts. But because he asks from God for truth, & a mediocrity of means, he testifies that both are a benefit of God, & are to be sought in their own order, & that labors ordained by God are to be undertaken. Just as here he names the bread that is measured out.

Latin:

CAPUT XXX.

Initio trigesimi capitis addita est nova Epigraphe. Ae fieri potest, sicut in colligendis gnomis usitatum est, ut Salomonis sententiis etiam aliae postea additae sint a prae cipuis viris, ut in isto populo semper excellentes Doctores fuerunt. Nec sunt vulgares admonitiones, aut de parvis vitae momentis hae sequentes sententiae.

Prima continet regulam omnium summam, ut agnoscamus nostrae mentis caliginem, & velimus regi verbo Dei, & includamus nos metis verbi Dei, hoc est, firma assensione amplectamur doctrinam de essentia & voluntate Dei, & de nostris moribus, quam Deus patefecit, nec fingamus alias opiniones de essentia & voluntate Dei, de cultibus, aut de nostris moribus, ut audacissime fecerunt Gentes, quae subinde nova numina finxerunt, & concesserunt licentiam vagis libidinibus. Item haeretici, Pharisaei, Monachi, & alii Idolatrae, finxerunt opiniones & cultus de Deo contra vocem ab ipso traditam.

Sit igitur in conspectu haec regula saepissime repetita, in tota doctrina caelesti, Ut in Psalmo, Lucerna pedibus meis verbum tuum. Et apud Esaiam, Ad legem & ad testimonium. Et aeternus Pater de Filio dicit, Hunc audite. Ita vult Deus sibi colligere aeternam Ecclesiam, & non aliter, voce suae doctrinae, Ut dictum est, Sanctifica eos in veritate, sermo tuus est veritas. Ita vult consociatam esse Ecclesiam, & copulatam ad unum caput, scilicet ad Filium Dei.

Sed historia mundi ostendit, quantae dissipationes semper factae sint, & fiant, in genere humano, cum a verbo Dei discedunt homines, impulsi a Diabolo & sua levitate, ut Adam & Heva.

Haec tristissima exempla consideremus, & simus in retinenda puritate doctrinae a Deo traditae firmi.

Haec est summa huius gtavissimae antithesis hic positae. Initio fatetur scriptor suam insipientiam, id est, monet, ut agnoscamus tenebras nostras. Sicut Paulus inquit, Non cognovit mundus Deum in sapientia per sapientiam. Damnat humanas cogitationes, & fiduciam humanae sapientiae, asseverantis aliquid de Del essentia, & voluntate, de cultibus nostris moribus, sine voce a Deo tradita.

Postea deducit nos ad vocem divinam, Omne verbum Dei purgatum clypeus est credenti. Diserte inquit purgatum, ut moneat se loqui de verbo Dei sine corruptelis. Et postea addit, Credenti clypeus est, hic postulat, ut firma assensione amplectamur, & acquiescamus & confidamus in Deo, sic se patefaciente.
